# The Dead Down Under
## The Red Continent Quarantine
### By Gabriel Simon

---


THE DEAD DOWN UNDER
-------------------

A Consolidated Master Anthology of Grimdark Zombie Outbreak Stories

The Rules of the Dead

In this world, the apocalypse conforms strictly to the classic Romero rules:
1. Unknown Origin: No one knows why the dead are rising. There is no patient zero, no viral lab leak, no biological explanation given to the survivors. The dead simply get back up.
2. Relentless but Slow: The zombies do not run. They shamble, drag their limbs, and move slowly. However, they never tire, they never stop, and their collective weight can breach almost any barricade.
3. The Infection: A bite or deep scratch from a zombie introduces a mysterious, terminal pathogen. The victim will inevitably develop a burning fever, die, and reanimate.
4. Universal Reanimation: Anyone who dies—regardless of the cause of death (blood loss, heart attack, starvation)—will reanimate as a zombie, unless their brain is destroyed before or immediately after death.
5. The Only Kill: The only way to stop a zombie permanently is to destroy its brain or sever the brainstem.

STORY 1: AUSSIE TWITCH CON
--------------------------

Chapter 1: Wholesome Vibes

The pastel peach-shaped neon sign hummed, casting a warm, comforting glow over the plushies lined up on the table. Peach smiled, her cheeks aching slightly from a full day of meet-and-greets, and handed a signed squishy peach back to a young girl wearing oversized cat-ear headphones. 

"There you go, mate. Thanks so much for coming out," Peach said, her voice cozy and soft, exactly as it was on her streams. 

"I watch your cozy Minecraft runs every single night!" the girl beamed, clutching the plushie to her chest. "They help my anxiety so much."

"Aw, that’s so sweet. I'm glad we can make a little safe space for you. Have a great Twitch Con!" Peach waved as the girl moved along, guided by the convention volunteers in their bright purple crew shirts.

Melbourne Convention and Exhibition Centre—affectionately known as the Jeff’s Shed by locals—was absolutely packed. The air was a thick mix of body heat, stale convention center pies, hot chips, and the unmistakable ozone scent of hundreds of high-end gaming rigs running at full capacity. Over at the adjacent booth, Nathan—better known to his fans as Crayator—burst into a booming, infectious laugh that cut through the low rumble of thousands of chatting attendees. He was currently trying to balance a foam sword on his nose for a cheering crowd. A few rows down, Kathleen—Loserfruit—was surrounded by a massive queue, smiling for a selfie while holding her custom streaming camera on a gimbal.

It was bright, vibrant, and completely normal. 

Peach leaned back in her ergonomic chair, taking a sip from her water bottle. Behind her, the chat moderator monitor she’d set up to display her live Discord feed flickered. A username caught her eye in the VIP channel: DeadHead_Rex. 

DeadHead_Rex: Peach, hope the con is going well! Remember, if the crowd gets too hectic, locate your nearest emergency exit. Convention halls are death traps if a stampede starts. Always look for the service doors, they lead to the loading docks.

Peach let out a soft snort. Rex was one of her longest-serving moderators, a guy from Brisbane who spent ninety percent of his free time watching classic horror cinema. He was particularly obsessed with George Romero's zombie movies. Over the last three years, under the guise of "streamer safety," Rex had treated her stream chat to endless, highly detailed monologues about what to do in a zombie outbreak. 

PeachKachu: Rex, you worry too much, haha. The only monsters here are the queues for the toilets.

She tapped out the reply quickly. 

A sudden, sharp scream cut through the convention hall. 

It wasn't the usual fan-girl shriek of excitement. It was high, ragged, and ended in a wet, choking gasp. 

Peach sat up, her brow furrowing. Across the aisle, Nathan stopped laughing, the foam sword dropping to the floor. The ambient noise of the hall—the click of keyboards, the thud of game audio from the main stage, the chatter—began to die down, replaced by a strange, sweeping silence that rolled across the exhibition floor like a cold front.

"What was that?" a volunteer murmured, looking toward the far end of the hall, near the food court. 

"Probably some viral prank," a fan in Peach's queue muttered. "Some TikToker doing a flash mob."

Then came another scream. And another. They erupted in rapid succession, a chain reaction of pure, raw terror moving closer. 

Through the wide aisle between the booths, Peach saw the crowd start to shift. People weren't walking; they were pushing. The casual stride of con-goers morphed into a frantic, desperate press. In the distance, a massive purple banner showcasing a new indie game came tearing down as people scrambled over the display stands. 

"Hey, everyone, just calm down!" a crew member shouted through a megaphone, his voice cracking. "Please exit in an orderly—"

His voice was cut off. 

Through the fleeing crowd, Peach saw them. 

They weren't running. That was the first detail her brain struggled to process. They walked with a slow, heavy, hitching gait, their limbs dragging as if fighting against intense gravity. But there was a terrifying weight to their movement. One of them—a man in a torn business suit, his face pale and slick with dark, coagulated blood—shambled forward. He collided with the crew member with the megaphone. 

The crew member tried to push him back, yelling something, but the shambling man simply reached out, his fingers digging into the crew member’s neck with unnatural, crushing strength. 

With a sickening, wet tear, the shambler bit down. 

A fountain of dark crimson sprayed over the purple convention carpet. 

Peach’s breath caught in her throat. Her hands began to shake violently. The cozy, pastel world she had built around herself evaporated in an instant, replaced by the metallic stench of fresh blood and the terrifying, deafening roar of a crowd realizing they were trapped.

Chapter 2: The Breach

The scream of the crew member ended in a sickening gurgle. 

For a second, the crowd near the food court froze, their brains failing to compute the blood pooling on the purple carpet. But when the shambling man tore a chunk of flesh from the crew member's shoulder, swallowing it with a jaw that seemed to hinge too wide, the illusion of safety shattered completely.

A wave of screaming, high-pitched and animalistic, swept through the hall. 

"Oh my god, he's actually killing him!" someone screamed.

"Move! Out of the way!" 

A massive surge of bodies pressed forward. Fans abandoned their queues, sprinting toward the main exhibition entrance. In their panic, they knocked over tables, metal stanchions, and expensive gaming setups. The sound of breaking glass and plastic cracked through the room.

From her booth, Peach watched in horror as Crayator—Nathan—stepped forward. He was a big guy, and he clearly thought he could stop what he assumed was a violent brawl. 

"Whoa, mate! Back off!" Nathan yelled, pushing through the back of his booth. He grabbed the shoulder of a shambling teenager in a torn hoodie who was crawling over a demo kiosk. "Hey! Stop!"

The teenager turned. His eyes were milky white, filmed over with cataracts, and his mouth was slick with blood. With a speed that didn't match his slow shuffle, he lunged, his jaw snapping shut. 

Nathan let out a guttural cry of surprise, trying to pull his arm back, but the turned teen clamped his teeth down onto Nathan’s forearm. 

"Nathan!" Peach screamed, her voice cracking. 

She wanted to run to him, but the stampede was already between them. A deluge of terrified people swept past her booth. A young guy fell, and before he could get up, three people tripped over him, trampling him into the floor. His ribs cracked under the weight of the crowd. 

Peach’s mind flashed back to a Discord call from six months ago. Rex’s voice was clear and urgent in her memory: “If you’re ever in a crowd panic, Peach, do not go with the flow. If the main exits are jammed, people will crush each other. Look for the service doors. The ones marked staff only. They lead backstage to the loading bays. Nobody ever runs for those.”

She looked toward the main entrance. Rex was right. Hundreds of people were jammed into the narrow bottleneck of the front glass doors. They were pressing so hard against the glass that it was groaning under the pressure. The dead were closing in from behind, dragging down anyone who fell or lagged. 

At the other side of the hall, Kathleen—Loserfruit—was backing away, her eyes wide with terror, shouting into her phone, trying to warn someone. A shambling woman with a gaping wound in her chest grabbed Kathleen's hair. Kathleen screamed, throwing a punch, but the dead woman didn’t even flinch. She pulled Kathleen down into the swarm.

Tears blurred Peach’s eyes. She couldn’t save them. She couldn’t save anyone. 

"Rear service doors," Peach whispered to herself, her heart hammering against her ribs like a trapped bird. "Rear service doors."

She looked behind her booth. There was a heavy gray door labeled Staff Only - Authorized Personnel Only. 

She scrambled over her pastel-colored desk, knocking over a display of stickers. As she hit the floor on the other side, she heard a slow, wet dragging sound. 

She spun around. 

A con-goer, a young man who had been at the front of her queue just seconds ago, was shambling toward her. His shirt was soaked in blood from a bite on his neck, but his eyes were already milky. He moved with a stiff, mechanical gait, his hands reaching out, his fingers twitching.

He was slow. But he was relentless. 

Peach didn't hesitate. She lunged for the gray service door, grabbed the metal handle, and pushed. It clicked open. She slipped inside, slamming the door shut behind her, cutting off the deafening noise of the dying convention hall.

Chapter 3: Backstage Retreat

The heavy service door slammed shut with a dull, echoing thud. Instantly, the cacophony of the main hall—the screaming, the smashing glass, the wet tearing sounds—was muffled, reduced to a low, vibrational hum. 

Peach stood in the concrete backstage corridor, her chest heaving as she sucked in the cool, sterile air. The fluorescent lights overhead flickered, casting long, jittery shadows against the grey cinderblock walls. 

Thump.

A heavy, sluggish weight hit the metal door behind her. 

Thump. Thump.

Peach scrambled back, her sneakers squeaking on the polished concrete. Through the small, wire-reinforced glass window in the door, she saw a pale, blood-smeared face press against the pane. It was the young man from her queue. His eyes were wide, vacant, and glazed over with a milky white film. He smeared his bloodied forehead against the glass, his teeth gnashing pointlessly. 

He was trying to get in. But he didn't know how to turn a door handle. 

"Oh, god," Peach whispered, backing away down the hall. 

She turned a corner and nearly collided with a group of people. 

"Whoa! Watch out!" a familiar voice yelled. 

Peach gasped, raising her hands defensively. It was Bazza Gazza. The popular Australian streamer looked completely disheveled, his usual neat hair pointing in random directions, his face drained of color. With him were two others: a young female con volunteer named Sarah, wearing a torn purple staff shirt, and a teenage boy named Toby, who was clutching a blood-soaked paper towel to his left forearm.

"Peach!" Bazza breathed, his eyes wide. "Did you see it? What the hell is going on out there? People are... they're eating each other!"

"It's a riot," Sarah said, her voice shaking as she frantically tapped her phone. "It has to be. But the reception is completely dead. I can't get emergency services. Nothing is dialing through."

"It's not a riot," Peach said, her voice trembling but strangely hollow. 

She stared at Toby's arm. The blood was soaking through the paper towel, dripping onto the concrete floor. 

"What happened to your arm, Toby?" Peach asked, her heart dropping. 

"I... I just tripped," Toby stammered, his eyes darting away. He was shivering, despite the warmth of the corridor. "Someone grabbed me in the crowd. Just a scratch. I'm fine. I just need a band-aid."

Peach felt a cold dread settle in her stomach. Rex’s rants, once a running joke during her Sunday cozy streams, echoed in her mind with terrifying clarity. 

“If you see a bite, Peach, or even a deep scratch from one of them—it’s over. The pathogen, whatever it is, enters the bloodstream. The fever starts, and then they die. And when they die, they get back up. Doesn’t matter who they were. They become one of them.”

"Toby," Peach said, stepping back. "Did one of those... those things bite you?"

"I told you, it's just a scratch!" Toby yelled, his voice cracking with panic. 

"Peach, leave the kid alone, he's in shock," Bazza said, stepping between them. "We need to find a way out of here. The loading dock should be down this hall. We grab a car, get out of the city, and—"

"Bazza, listen to me," Peach interrupted, her voice rising. "They aren't just crazy people. They're dead. They’re zombies, Bazza. Like in the movies."

Sarah let out a nervous, hysterical laugh. "Zombies? Are you serious? This is real life, Peach. It's probably some new drug, or a terror attack. The police will be here soon. We just need to wait for them."

Peach looked back at the gray door. The thumping had stopped, but she could hear other noises now. Wet, dragging footsteps echoing from the adjacent corridors. The building was massive, and the service halls connected everything. 

"We can't wait," Peach said, her eyes locked on Toby, whose face was already beginning to turn a sickly, feverish gray. "And we need to get away from him."

Chapter 4: The Rule of Resurrection

The corridor felt increasingly narrow as they hurried forward, their footsteps slapping against the concrete. Toby lagged behind, his breath coming in ragged, wheezing gasps. His skin had gone a translucent, waxy white, dotted with beads of cold sweat. 

"I... I can't," Toby whimpered, his knees buckling. He slithered down against the wall, clutching his stomach. "It burns. My chest... it burns so bad."

"Toby!" Sarah knelt beside him, checking his forehead. "Oh my god, he's burning up. Bazza, help me get him up."

Bazza walked over, his face etched with worry. "Kid, you've got to keep moving. We’re almost to the loading dock."

"Wait!" Peach yelled, staying a few paces back. She saw the rapid deterioration. It was happening too fast. Rex had mentioned this: “In some people, the reaction to the bite is violent. Their body shocks. The fever spikes, kills them in minutes, and then the reanimation process takes over.”

"Peach, what is your problem?" Bazza snapped, turning on her. "He's a kid! We can't just leave him here to die!"

"He is already dying, Bazza!" Peach shouted back, tears of frustration hot on her face. "Look at him!"

Toby let out a long, rattling gasp. His eyes rolled back into his head, showing only the whites. His body went rigid, back arching off the concrete, before he collapsed entirely, limp and motionless. 

Sarah pressed two fingers to Toby’s neck. Her face fell. "There's... there's no pulse. He’s not breathing!"

"No, no, no," Bazza muttered, running a hand through his hair. "Sarah, do CPR! Do something!"

Sarah positioned her hands on Toby’s chest, leaning over him. "Toby, come on, breathe..."

"Sarah, get away from him!" Peach screamed, her voice echoing off the walls. She reached out, trying to grab Sarah's shoulder. "Get back! Now!"

"Peach, shut up!" Bazza yelled, pushing Peach's hand away. "She's trying to save him!"

"He's dead, Bazza! Anyone who dies comes back! Don't you get it? Anyone!"

Before Bazza could respond, Toby’s chest didn't rise from a breath, but his eyelids snapped open. 

The eyes that stared up at Sarah were no longer brown. They were milky, dead, and staring into nothingness. 

With a sudden, snapping motion, Toby’s head lunged forward. His teeth clamped shut on Sarah’s throat. 

A choked, wet shriek tore from Sarah’s lips. Blood erupted from the wound, spraying across Toby’s pale face and Sarah’s purple staff shirt. Toby didn't let go; he shook his head side to side, tearing the flesh like a rabid dog. 

"Oh my god! Oh my god!" Bazza screamed, falling backward onto his hands. He scrambled away, his eyes wide with a horror so profound it seemed to break his mind. 

Sarah fell sideways, clawing at Toby’s face, but Toby felt no pain. He crawled on top of her, his teeth snapping again, burying his face in her neck. Her legs kicked weakly against the concrete, her eyes locking onto Bazza in silent, desperate plea. 

Then, her eyes went dull. The kicking stopped. 

"Bazza, get up!" Peach grabbed Bazza by the collar of his jacket, dragging him to his feet. "Get up! We have to go!"

"She... he... they..." Bazza stammered, his body shaking so hard he could barely stand. 

"They're gone! We have to run!"

Behind them, Sarah’s corpse began to twitch. Her fingers curled, and her head rolled slowly toward them, her jaw slack. 

Peach pulled Bazza down the hall. They ran past the loading dock sign, but as they rounded the corner, they saw the heavy double doors to the loading bay were chained shut from the outside, with a cluster of dark, shambling shapes scratching at the glass from the loading dock. 

They were trapped in the corridor. 

Peach looked to her left. A steel fire door led to a stairwell. 

"Up!" Peach yelled, pushing Bazza toward the stairs. "Go up!"

Chapter 5: Pinned on the Gantry

Peach and Bazza bolted up the concrete steps, the sound of their heavy breathing echoing in the narrow stairwell. Behind them, from the bottom of the stairs, came the scraping of shoes and the unmistakable, wet grunting of Toby and the newly turned Sarah. 

Peach pushed open the door at the top of the stairs. They emerged onto a high, industrial catwalk—a metal gantry suspended forty feet above the main exhibition hall. It was used by technicians to adjust lighting rigs and banners, constructed of open steel mesh. 

"Look down," Bazza whispered, his voice trembling as he peered through the grate beneath his feet. 

Peach forced herself to look. 

The vibrant, purple-lit wonderland of Aussie Twitch Con was gone. In its place was an open-air abattoir. The main hall was a shifting sea of bodies—thousands of them. The slow, shambling dead moved in packs, cornering terrified con-goers against the glass doors and the stage setups. Peach could see the remnants of her own booth, the peach neon sign shattered, pastel plushies soaked in dark blood. 

The collective sound of the hall was a low, vibrating drone—a chorus of guttural groans, wet chewing, and the desperate, fading screams of those being torn apart. 

"There's... there's thousands of them," Bazza whimpered, clutching the metal handrail. "They're all dead. Everyone is dead."

"We need to keep moving," Peach said, her voice hard. "The gantry connects to the administration wing. There’s a security hub there. Heavy doors."

Suddenly, the door at the far end of the catwalk burst open. Three con-goers—two guys in gaming jerseys and a girl holding a camera—sprinted onto the narrow metal walkway. They were screaming, running blindly. 

Behind them, three shambling figures emerged from the doorway. One was a security guard in a high-vis vest, his face half-torn away. 

"Hey! Stop running!" Peach warned, but it was too late. 

The panicking group charged down the narrow, three-foot-wide gantry. The lead runner, a tall guy with wild eyes, shoved past Bazza to get to the stairs. Bazza, still in a state of shock, was caught off guard. His foot slipped on the smooth metal mesh. 

"Whoa!" Bazza gasped. 

He pitched sideways over the railing. The handrail caught him at the waist, but his momentum carried his legs over. He slipped through the gap in the safety rails, his hands snapping out to grab the bottom rung of the guardrail. 

"Bazza!" Peach lunged forward, dropping to her knees. 

Bazza was dangling forty feet in the air. Below him, directly beneath his kicking legs, a dozen shamblers in the hall looked up, attracted by the sudden movement. They gathered slowly, raising their blood-soaked arms toward him like fans at a concert. 

"Peach! Don't let go! Please, mate, don't let me fall!" Bazza screamed, his eyes wide with absolute terror. 

"I've got you! Grab my wrists!" Peach reached down, her small hands locking onto Bazza's forearms. She pulled with everything she had, but she was a variety streamer, not an athlete. Bazza’s dead weight dragged her chest against the cold metal edge of the catwalk. 

To make matters worse, the runners had jammed themselves at the stairwell door behind Peach. One of them had tried to open it, only to find the stairs below now filled with the dead that had followed Peach and Bazza up. 

"They're coming up the stairs!" the girl with the camera screamed. 

From the far end of the gantry, the turned security guard and his companions were shuffling forward, their footsteps clanking rhythmically on the metal mesh. They were only twenty feet away. 

From the stairwell door behind Peach, the dead were pushing through, forcing the remaining runners back onto the catwalk. 

Peach was trapped in the middle of a collapsing bridge. The dead were closing in from both ends. Bazza was dangling over the edge, his grip slipping from her sweat-slicked hands. 

"Peach..." Bazza sobbed, his fingers twitching. "I can't hold on."

Chapter 6: Into the HVAC

The screams of the con-goers jammed at the stairwell door rose to a deafening pitch as the dead began to tear into them. 

"Pull! Bazza, kick your feet up!" Peach screamed, her muscles burning. 

One of the fleeing runners, desperate for any escape, stumbled over Peach. In his panic, his boot stamped directly onto Bazza’s left hand. 

Bazza let out a sharp cry of pain, his grip failing. His hand slipped from Peach’s. He hung by a single hand, his body swinging wildly over the drop. 

"Bazza!" Peach grabbed his wrist again, her shoulder socket popping with a agonizing click. 

The runner who had stepped on Bazza was grabbed from behind by the reanimated security guard. The guard's teeth sank into the runner's shoulder. The runner shrieked, falling backward, his flailing hand catching Bazza’s belt. The extra downward force threatened to drag Peach over the rail. 

"No!" Peach gritted her teeth, locking her legs under the bottom bar of the guardrail. 

With a final, desperate burst of adrenaline, Bazza managed to swing his foot up, catching his heel on the edge of the catwalk. He scrambled, rolling himself over the bottom rail and onto the metal mesh floor just as the runner was dragged into the swarm. 

But there was no time to celebrate. The dead were now only feet away on both sides of the narrow catwalk. 

Peach looked at the wall of the exhibition hall. Suspended just above the gantry was a massive, rectangular aluminum duct—the main HVAC intake. A square, slatted ventilation register was mounted to the wall, its screws loose from years of vibration. 

Rex’s voice flashed through her head: “Large commercial buildings, Peach. The air ducts are practically tunnels. If you can get into the main trunk, you can move across the entire building. They’re steel-reinforced, too. The dead can’t chew through sheet metal.”

"Help me!" Peach yelled, slamming her foot against the metal grate. 

The sheet metal bent. She kicked it again, harder. The bottom screws sheared off, and the grate swung outward, leaving a dark, gaping square in the wall. 

"Get in!" Peach shoved Bazza toward the opening. 

Bazza didn't hesitate. He scrambled headfirst into the dark aluminum pipe. Peach climbed in right behind him, her hips scraping against the sharp metal edge. 

Behind her, the last remaining runner screamed as the dead swarmed over him. A pale hand reached into the ventilation shaft, fingers clawing blindly at the air, just inches from Peach's ankle. 

Peach grabbed the loose metal grate, pulled it back into place, and kicked the locking latch from the inside, jamming it. 

She turned and began to crawl. 

The ventilation duct was four feet wide and three feet high. It was pitch black, freezing cold, and smelled of decades of accumulated dust. Every movement they made resulted in a loud, booming clank-clank-clank that echoed through the entire system. 

"Keep moving," Bazza’s voice came from the darkness ahead, hollow and shaking. "We have to keep moving."

They crawled for what felt like hours, their knees bruised and raw against the sheet metal. Eventually, the duct sloped downward, and they reached a wide junction where several smaller pipes met. 

Peach stopped, sitting cross-legged in the cramped space. Her heart was still racing. She pulled out her phone, using the screen's pale blue light to illuminate the space. 

Bazza was sitting opposite her, leaning against the metal wall. His skin was deathly pale, and he was shivering violently despite the sweat plastering his hair to his forehead. He had his right arm wrapped tightly around his waist. 

"Bazza," Peach said, her voice dropping to a whisper. "Let me see your side."

Bazza flinched, pulling his arm tighter. "I'm fine. Just... scratched it on the rail when I fell. It's nothing."

"Bazza, show me. Please."

"I said I'm fine, Peach!" Bazza snapped, his voice echoing loudly in the metal chamber. He glared at her, his eyes wild and defensive. "Don't look at me like that. I'm not like the kid. I'm fine."

Peach stared at him. Through the tears in his gaming jacket, she could see a dark, wet stain spreading on his shirt. 

Before she could speak, a sound echoed from the duct behind them. 

Scratch. Scratch. Clank.

Something was crawling in the vents with them. And it was moving closer.

Chapter 7: Claustrophobic Turning

The scratching sound grew louder, accompanied by the hollow, metallic clang of elbows dragging over sheet metal. Whatever was behind them in the dark was moving with a slow, mechanical rhythm. 

"Bazza, we have to move," Peach whispered, her voice trembling. "It's right behind us."

Bazza didn't answer. He was crawling on all fours, but his movements were erratic, his head hanging low. He let out a low, wet groan that sounded dangerously like the monsters outside. 

"Bazza!" Peach nudged his heel. 

Suddenly, Bazza collapsed. He rolled onto his side, his back slamming against the aluminum wall of the duct with a loud boom. He was panting, his mouth open, a thick, dark froth bubbling at the corners of his lips. His skin was gray, his veins standing out like black branches beneath his pale flesh. 

Peach shone her phone light on him. His jacket had fallen open. On his right flank, a massive, jagged bite mark was visible, the flesh torn away to expose the dark, congealed blood beneath. 

"You... you lied to me," Peach breathed, tears spilling over her cheeks. 

"I... I didn't want... to be left behind," Bazza whispered, his eyes glazing over. "Peach... I'm so cold. I'm so..."

His eyes rolled back. His body went rigid, his chest freezing mid-breath. A long, final rattle escaped his throat. 

He was dead. 

Peach scrambled backward, her back hitting the junction walls. She knew what was coming. She knew the rules. 

"No, please, Bazza, not you," she sobbed. 

It took less than five seconds. 

Bazza’s fingers twitched, his nails scratching against the metal floor. His head snapped up, his jaw unhinging with a wet crack. The eyes that opened were completely milky, reflecting the harsh white LED of her phone screen. 

He didn't recognize her. There was no trace of the funny, easy-going streamer she had shared stages with. There was only a ravenous, dead animal. 

With a guttural snarl, Bazza lunged forward, his hands clawing at her legs. 

In the tight, four-foot-wide tube, there was nowhere to run. Peach kicked out, her boot catching Bazza in the chest, but he didn't feel it. He kept crawling forward, his teeth snapping inches from her knees. 

The scratching from behind was getting louder. The other shambler was closing in. 

Peach’s hand frantically searched her backpack. Her fingers wrapped around the heavy, double-walled stainless steel water bottle she had been carrying all day. 

“Destroy the brain, Peach. Through the eye socket or the temple. It’s the thinnest part of the skull.” Rex’s voice was a scream in her mind. 

"I'm sorry!" Peach shrieked. 

She swung the heavy metal bottle with both hands, driving the thick, steel bottom directly into Bazza's temple. 

CRACK.

Bazza’s head slammed against the metal side of the vent, but he didn't stop. He lunged again, his teeth grazing the fabric of her pants. 

Peach screamed, a raw, primal sound, and swung the bottle again. And again. 

CRACK. CRACK. CRUNCH.

She felt the bone give way. The metal bottle deformed in her hands, slick with dark, foul-smelling blood. Bazza’s body suddenly went limp, collapsing face-down onto the sheet metal with a heavy thud. 

Silence fell over the duct, broken only by Peach’s ragged sobbing and the approaching scrape of the second zombie behind her. 

She was covered in Bazza's blood. The cozy, wholesome streamer was gone, replaced by a shell of a person. 

Peach wiped her face with her sleeve, smeared with crimson, and dragged herself over Bazza's motionless corpse, pushing deeper into the dark, narrow tunnel.

Chapter 8: Scavenging the Underbelly

Peach crawled blindly through the dark metal maze, her elbows barking against the rivets, her breath a jagged, panicked rattle. She didn't look back. She didn't want to see the empty, milky eyes of the thing that had once been Bazza Gazza, or the other shape scraping along in the dark behind it. 

She turned one corner, then another, dragging herself through a long, straight run of ductwork. Up ahead, a faint vertical column of light cut through the darkness from a metal vent in the floor of the pipe. 

Peach dragged herself to it and looked down. 

It was a maintenance workshop. Steel workbenches, tool racks, rolls of wire, and stacks of unused cardboard boxes were scattered around the concrete floor. The room was dark, lit only by a buzzing emergency light. It was quiet. 

She pressed her face to the grate, listening. No shuffling. No groaning. 

Using her heels, she kicked the metal grate. It popped loose and fell, clattering loudly against a steel workbench below. Peach didn't wait. She slipped through the opening, dropping six feet onto the metal table, then sliding down to the cold concrete floor. 

She collapsed against the table legs, her body shaking in violent, uncontrollable waves. She looked down at her hands. They were stained dark, Bazza's blood dried under her fingernails. 

“They bite, Peach. Teeth and nails. That's all they've got. If you've got duct tape and some thick magazines or cardboard, wrap your forearms. Wrap your calves. They can't bite through three inches of paper and plastic. It's the cheapest armor you'll ever make, and it'll save your life.”

Rex's voice was a quiet anchor in the chaos of her mind. 

Peach wiped her nose on her sleeve, took a deep breath, and stood up. Her legs were weak, but she forced them to move. 

She scanned the workshop. On a nearby shelf, she found a heavy, three-inch-wide roll of silver duct tape. Next to it was a box of thick, gloss-laminated official Twitch Con Australia guides—over a hundred pages of dense paper. 

She grabbed a guide, wrapped it tightly around her left forearm, and began winding the duct tape around it, pulling it tight until it was a rigid, silver cylinder. She did the same to her right arm. She wrapped her calves next, binding them in layers of glossy paper and thick adhesive. 

Next, she needed a weapon. The bent metal water bottle was useless now. 

On a pegboard above the main workbench, she spotted a heavy-duty, two-foot-long steel pipe wrench. She took it down. It was cold, heavy, and balanced poorly, but it was solid iron. 

Thump.

Peach froze. 

At the far corner of the workshop, a door marked HVAC Room 3 swung slowly inward. A man shambled out. He was dressed in a blue maintenance uniform, his chest torn open, his intestines spilling out over his belt. He dragged his left foot, his jaw clicking open and closed. 

He caught her scent, or heard the rustle of the tape. His milky eyes locked onto her, and he let out a low, wet hiss. 

Peach backed up, her heels hitting the workbench. There was no vent to climb back into. She had to fight. 

The turned maintenance worker shuffled forward. He was slow, but the space was small. He lunged, his blood-slicked hands reaching for her throat. 

Peach raised her left arm defensively. The zombie’s jaw clamped down onto her forearm. 

She braced for the pain, but there was none. She heard the screech of teeth tearing through the silver duct tape and the crunch of the glossy con guides, but the bite stopped short of her flesh. The armor held. 

"Get off me!" Peach screamed. 

She raised the heavy iron wrench with her right hand and swung it down with all her weight, targeting the side of the shambler's head. 

CRACK.

The wrench struck his temple. The maintenance worker didn't fall; he merely stumbled, his teeth still snagged on the tape. 

Peach gritted her teeth, pulled her arm back, and swung again, aiming for the center of his skull. 

CRUNCH.

The iron wrench split the bone. The zombie's eyes went completely vacant, and his knees buckled. He collapsed to the floor like a sack of wet cement, his jaw releasing her arm. 

Peach stood over him, panting, the wrench dripping dark, thick fluid. Her left arm was covered in scratch marks on the silver tape, but her skin was unbroken. 

She looked at the maintenance worker’s belt. A ring of keys hung there. She knelt, ignored the stench of decay, and ripped the keys free. 

A map of the MCEC was pinned to the corkboard by the door. She looked at it, finding her position. The Master Broadcast Suite—the room that controlled the convention center's external satellite links and emergency broadcast systems—was on the third floor of the administration wing. 

To get there, she had to cross the open loading dock.

Chapter 9: Crossing the Loading Dock

Peach unlocked the heavy metal door at the back of the workshop, the lock clicking open with a loud, echoey snap. She pushed the door open a crack, peering out into the cold night air. 

The loading dock was a massive, semi-open concrete expanse. Rows of white delivery vans, massive flight cases for audio equipment, and wooden shipping crates were scattered across the yard. Overhead, the sky was a bruised purple-black. In the distance, the Melbourne skyline was dotted with fires, thick columns of black smoke rising into the air, illuminated by the orange glow of the burning streets below. The night was filled with a distant, chaotic symphony: wailing sirens, the rattle of automatic gunfire, and the faint, collective roar of a city tearing itself apart. 

There was no help coming. The world was ending. 

In the immediate yard, six shambling figures wandered between the trucks. They moved with that same slow, dragging gait, their heads lolling, their arms hanging limp. 

On the far side of the concrete yard, about eighty yards away, was the glass-and-steel entrance to the MCEC administration wing. That was where she needed to go. 

Peach gripped the heavy pipe wrench, her knuckles white beneath the duct tape. She couldn't fight six of them in the open. 

“Zombies are stupid, Peach. They don’t have brains anymore—not really. They respond to sensory input. If they see you, they’ll walk toward you. If they hear something, they’ll walk toward the noise. If you get stuck, throw a rock. Throw a bottle. Create a distraction and move while their backs are turned.”

Peach looked around the doorway. On a metal shelf next to the door was a heavy, rusted steel toolbox filled with spare bolts. 

She picked it up, her muscles straining. She took a deep breath, stepped slightly out onto the concrete loading platform, and threw the toolbox with a desperate, two-handed heave. 

It sailed through the air and crashed violently against a large metal skip on the far left side of the yard, the bolts scattering across the asphalt with a loud, ringing din. 

CLANG-CLATTER-CLATTER.

Immediately, the six shambling figures in the yard stopped. Their heads snapped toward the noise. With a collective, low groan, they turned and began to shuffle toward the skip, their dragging footsteps moving away from the administration entrance. 

Peach didn't waste a second. She dropped low, using the large black flight cases as cover. She darted from case to case, her boots making soft, urgent taps on the wet asphalt. Her heart hammered in her ears. 

She was halfway across the yard when a figure stepped out from the gap between two delivery vans, right in her path. 

It was a massive man, easily six-foot-four, wearing a hi-vis vest over a flannel shirt. His jaw was hanging loose, dangling by a shred of flesh, and his chest was soaked in dark, dried blood. 

He locked his milky eyes on her. A wet, rattling gasp escaped his throat. 

Peach stopped. The other zombies in the yard heard the gasp and began to turn back. 

She couldn't run. She had to put him down, fast. 

Peach lunged forward, swinging the pipe wrench. But the massive shambler was heavy, his momentum carrying him forward. He ignored the swing, his large hands reaching out and grabbing her shoulders, slamming her back against the side of a delivery van. 

The impact knocked the wind from her lungs. The pipe wrench slipped from her grasp, clattering to the concrete. 

The giant zombie leaned in, his ruined mouth snapping shut, his teeth aiming for her face. 

Peach raised her left arm, shoving her duct-tape-and-brochure-wrapped forearm directly into his gaping maw. His teeth sank into the thick layers of paper, grinding against the glossy con guides, but they couldn't reach her skin. 

With her right hand free, Peach scrambled for her backpack pocket. Her fingers found the heavy steel pen she used for signing autographs. 

She gripped the pen like a dagger, raised it, and drove the steel tip directly into the giant's right eye socket. 

SQUELCH.

The pen sank deep, piercing the thin bone at the back of the orbit. 

The giant zombie froze. The strength suddenly drained from his limbs, and he collapsed forward, pinning Peach against the van for a second before sliding down to the concrete in a heap. 

Peach gasped for air, pulling her arm free. She grabbed the pipe wrench from the floor, sprinted the remaining twenty yards, and shoved open the glass door of the administration building. 

She ran inside, slamming the door, and bolted up the concrete stairs, heading for the third floor. 

At the end of the third-floor hallway, she found the door marked Master Broadcast Suite. It was a heavy, reinforced steel door with a digital keypad and a key backup. 

Her hands shaking violently, Peach sorted through the key ring she had stolen from the maintenance worker. She found a small, brass key labeled Admin-Suite. 

She shoved it into the lock, turned it, and pushed. 

The door opened. She slipped inside, slammed the heavy steel door shut, and threw the manual deadbolts, locking herself inside the windowless, soundproof studio.

Chapter 10: The Beacon

The Master Broadcast Suite was dead silent. The acoustic foam cladding the walls absorbed every sound, creating an eerie, sensory-deprivation chamber. The air was cool, smelling of ozone and clean carpets. Row after row of black equipment racks stood hummed softly, their green and red LED lights blinking in the dim space. 

Peach collapsed onto the floor, her back against the reinforced steel door. She pulled her knees to her chest, buried her face in her arms, and wept. She cried for Nathan, for Kathleen, for Bazza, and for the thousands of fans who had just wanted to play games and meet their favorite creators. 

Her tears ran through the blood and grime smeared across her cheeks. 

After several minutes, she forced herself to stand. She had to move. 

She walked over to the massive production console. A bank of security monitors flickered to life as she tapped the keyboard. The screens displayed the horrors of the Melbourne Convention and Exhibition Centre:
- Camera 4 showed the main hall, a dark, churning sea of reanimated corpses shambling over ruined booths.
- Camera 12 showed the gantry catwalk, empty save for the bloodied remains of the runners.
- Camera 19 showed the hallway outside her door. Three zombies, dressed in con volunteer uniforms, were wandering the carpeted corridor, their heads turning aimlessly. 

Peach sat in the high-backed production chair. Her forearms and calves were still bound in silver duct tape and official Twitch Con brochures. She looked at her reflection in the dark glass of the main monitor. She looked unrecognizable. Her cozy pastel aesthetic was gone, replaced by a traumatized, blood-spattered survivor. 

She looked at the main control board. The convention center’s emergency satellite uplink was active, powered by a massive backup diesel generator on the roof. The internet lines were down, but the dedicated satellite transponder was still functional. 

Peach booted up the master encoder. She routed the studio's professional 4K camera and microphone to the main outbound feed. She opened her browser and logged into her Twitch account. 

She overrode the official Twitch Con main page stream—which was currently displaying a static "Technical Difficulties" card—and linked it directly to her studio feed. 

She hit the button marked ON AIR. 

A red light illuminated above the camera lens. 

The stream title updated: PEACHKACHU: A WARNING FROM MELBOURNE. THE DEAD ARE WALKING.

For a second, the viewer count stayed at zero. Then, it exploded. The platform’s servers, struggling under global load, began to funnel millions of panicked users searching for news. 
- 5,000 viewers.
- 50,000 viewers.
- 250,000 viewers.
- 1,000,000 viewers.

The chat box on her secondary monitor began to scroll so fast it was a blur of colorful usernames and crying emojis. 

Peach leaned toward the microphone. Her voice was thin, shaking, but clear. 

"If anyone is watching this... if anyone is still out there. Listen to me," she said, her eyes locked on the camera lens. "Melbourne has fallen. I think... I think the whole country is falling. Maybe the world. We don't know why. It just started."

She took a ragged breath, wiping her eyes. 

"They aren't sick people. They're dead. The dead are getting back up. I survived... I only survived because of a friend in my chat. His name was Rex. He loved zombie movies, and he taught me the rules. You need to write these down. You need to remember them if you want to live."

She pointed to her wrapped arms. 

"First: A bite or a scratch is a death sentence. There is no cure. You will get a fever, you will die, and you will turn."

"Second: The dead are slow. They don't run. But they never stop. If you see them, run the other way. Do not get trapped in a crowd. Do not go to the main exits. They will bottleneck, and you will get crushed."

"Third: Anyone who dies—for any reason—will rise as one of them. If someone has a heart attack, if someone bleeds out, they will turn. You have to destroy their brain before they wake up."

"Fourth: The only way to kill them is to destroy the brain. A shot to the body won't stop them. A stab to the chest won't stop them. Smash the skull. Drive something into the eye socket. Break the brainstem. That is the only way."

The chat was a waterfall of panic, denial, and terror. Peach ignored it, looking at her security monitors. 

On Camera 19, the three zombies in the hallway suddenly stopped. They turned their heads toward the Master Broadcast Suite door. The audio from her broadcast was leaking slightly through the studio's ventilation ducts. 

They began to shuffle toward the door. 

Thump.

Peach flinched. The sound was muffled by the acoustic foam, but she felt the vibration through the floorboards. 

"They're outside," she whispered, looking back at the camera. 

Thump. Thump.

The door handle began to jiggle. The heavy, reinforced steel door groaned as more weight pressed against it from the hallway. More shamblers were coming up the stairs, drawn by the sound. 

Peach looked back at the lens, her expression cold and resolute. 

"Don't panic," she said, her voice dropping to a whisper. "Wrap your arms. Find a weapon. Keep moving. Don't die... because if you die, you join them."

Thump. Thump. Thump.

The rhythmic, relentless hammering of the dead began to shake the heavy steel frame. 

"Good luck," Peach said. 

She reached out and cut the feed. The screen went black.

================================================================================

STORY 2: THE TRIAGE WARD
------------------------

Chapter 1: The White Noise

The air in the pediatric ward was a suffocating blend of harsh bleach, stale sweat, and the metallic tang of blood drifting up from the lower floors. Under the flickering fluorescent lights, the hum of the backup generators vibrated through the linoleum floor, a constant, low-frequency rumble that did nothing to soothe the raw nerves of the survivors inside. 

Chloe pressed the heels of her hands into her eye sockets, trying to rub away the gritty, burning exhaustion. She had been on shift for thirty-six hours. The concept of "shifts" had collapsed three days ago, along with the city’s power grid. 

"Nurse Chloe?" a small, quiet voice piped up from Bed 3. 

Chloe forced a tired smile onto her face and turned. It was Max, a seven-year-old boy with a mop of curly brown hair, clutching a faded blue teddy bear. He was admitted for acute asthma just before the madness started. 

"Hey, buddy. Still awake?" she asked, walking over to check his IV line. 

"I can't sleep," Max whispered, his eyes wide. "The noises outside are scary. And the TV isn't working anymore."

Chloe glanced at the wall-mounted television. The screen was static, a blizzard of black and white noise. The last broadcast they’d received, twelve hours ago, was a frantic government announcement advising everyone to remain indoors, accompanied by shaky footage of fires consuming the Melbourne CBD. 

"It's just the wind, Max," Chloe lied softly, adjusting his oxygen mask. "And the soldiers are downstairs. They're keeping us safe, okay? Nothing is getting in here."

Outside the high, reinforced glass windows of the second-floor ward, the reality was far grim. Column after column of greasy black smoke rose from the surrounding suburbs of Parkville. In the distance, the flat, rhythmic pop-pop-pop of military rifles echoed off the concrete buildings, followed by the occasional, heavy boom of a grenade or gas explosion. 

At the entrance to the ward, the heavy double doors were flanked by Corporal Vance. The Australian Defence Force soldier was dressed in dusty, sweat-stained camouflage. His F88 Austeyr rifle was held across his chest, his finger hovering near the trigger guard. He looked as sleep-deprived as Chloe, his eyes bloodshot, his jaw clenched tight. 

Vance was there to keep the desperate out. The hospital’s main lobby had been overwhelmed on Day 3, forced into lockdown as thousands of injured, sick, and panicking citizens tried to breach the doors. Now, RMH was a fortress—or a prison, depending on how you looked at it. 

"Hey, Chloe," Vance muttered, his voice raspy as she walked toward the nursing station to log Max's vitals. "Got any of that instant coffee left?"

"Just the decaf," she said, pulling a crumpled sachet from her scrubs pocket. "Emergency stash."

Vance let out a dry, humorless chuckle. "Decaf. In a bloody warzone. Figures." 

Before he could take the sachet, the hospital's power grid failed completely. 

The lights cut out. The hum of the generator died, plunging the ward into a thick, suffocating darkness. For three seconds, the only light came from the emergency exit signs casting a sickly red glow over the row of children's beds. Max let out a soft whimper. 

Then, with a choking cough, the backup generator on the roof kicked back in. The overhead lights flickered, returning at half-brightness, casting long, jittery shadows. 

Simultaneously, a shrill, rhythmic alarm began to blare from the nursing console. 

Beep. Beep. Beep.

Chloe’s heart jumped. It was a Code Blue. Room 204. The adult ICU ward directly adjacent to their wing.

Chapter 2: The Code Blue

Chloe sprinted down the concrete-walled corridor, her rubber-soled nursing clogs squeaking against the linoleum. Behind her, the heavy double doors of the pediatric ward remained under Corporal Vance's watch, but the shrill alarm of the Code Blue echoed loudly in the quiet wing. 

She pushed open the door to Room 204. 

The adult ICU room was cramped, smelling of vomit, rubbing alcohol, and the heavy, humid heat of a Melbourne winter night. On the bed lay Mr. Henderson, a seventy-eight-year-old retired rail worker who had been admitted two days ago following a massive, debilitating stroke. He had no bites, no scratches. Just a failing brain in a worn-out body. 

Dr. Aris, a young ER physician with dark circles under his eyes and his scrub top soaked in sweat, was already leaning over the bed, his arms locked as he delivered rhythmic, heavy chest compressions. 

"One, two, three, four..." Dr. Aris muttered under his breath, his chest heaving. 

At the head of the bed, Nurse Julia was bag-valve-masking the elderly man, squeezing the blue plastic balloon to force oxygen into his lungs. 

"What's the status?" Chloe asked, immediately stepping in to check the monitor. 

"Flatline," Julia gasped, her face pale. "He went into respiratory arrest three minutes ago. Dr. Aris has been on compressions since."

Chloe looked at the cardiac monitor. The green line was flat, registering only the artificial spikes caused by Dr. Aris’s manual compressions. 

"Defib is offline," Dr. Aris grunted, not stopping the compressions. "The backup generator isn't throwing enough voltage to charge the capacitors. We have to do this manually. Julia, prep another milligram of epi."

Chloe grabbed the syringe, checking the label before handing it to Julia, who injected it directly into Mr. Henderson's IV line. 

"Come on, mate," Dr. Aris whispered, his forehead dripping sweat onto the patient’s sheets. "Don't do this. Not tonight."

For another three minutes, they fought. Chloe took over compressions, her own muscles screaming with fatigue as she pressed down on the elderly man's sternum, feeling the brittle ribcage crack and give way under the pressure. It was a sickening, crunching sensation, but she kept going. 

"Stop," Dr. Aris finally said, his voice flat. He reached out and touched Chloe’s shoulder. 

Chloe stopped, her chest heaving as she stood back. 

Dr. Aris leaned over Mr. Henderson, shining a small penlight into his eyes. The pupils were wide, fixed, and completely unresponsive. He pressed two fingers to the carotid artery. Nothing. 

On the monitor, the green line remained stubbornly, silently flat. 

"Call it," Dr. Aris sighed, running a blood-smeared hand over his face. "Time of death... two-twenty-five AM."

Julia let out a shaky breath, stepping back and dropping the blue bag-valve mask onto the tray. "We need the bed. The triage deck downstairs has three more criticals waiting."

"Yeah," Dr. Aris muttered, his shoulders slumping. "Chloe, help me clear the lines. Julia, go tell the family in the waiting room. If they're still there."

Chloe reached for the tape securing the IV line in Mr. Henderson’s right hand. The skin was already cooling, turning a waxy, translucent gray. 

The room was silent, save for the distant, dull thud of an explosion somewhere in the city. 

As Dr. Aris leaned over the bed to unplug the ventilator tubing, Mr. Henderson’s chest suddenly hitched. 

It wasn't a normal breath. It was a sharp, spasming contraction, like a fish out of water. 

Julia, who was halfway to the door, froze. "Did... did he just breathe?"

Chloe looked at the monitor. The green line was still flat. 

"Residual air in the lungs," Dr. Aris said, though his voice sounded uncertain. He reached out to touch Mr. Henderson's chest. "It's just a post-mortem spasm. It happens."

Before his hand could make contact, Mr. Henderson’s eyes snapped open. 

They were not the eyes of a stroke victim. They were filmed over with a thick, milky white membrane, vacant and dead. 

With a speed that defied his age and the stroke that had paralyzed him, Mr. Henderson's arms shot up. His cold, waxy fingers clamped onto Dr. Aris’s wrists with a crushing, unnatural strength.

Chapter 3: From Within

Dr. Aris let out a sharp, choked cry of surprise as the dead man’s fingers dug into his flesh, bruising his wrists. 

"Henderson! Let go!" Dr. Aris yelled, trying to pull back, but the elderly man's grip was like an iron vise. 

With a wet, guttural snarl, Mr. Henderson lunged off the bed. His teeth clamped down on Dr. Aris’s left cheek. The doctor shrieked, a high-pitched sound of pure agony, as Henderson shook his head violently, tearing away a chunk of flesh. 

Blood erupted, spraying across Chloe’s scrubs and the clean white sheets. 

"Help! Get him off!" Dr. Aris screamed, falling backward onto the floor, dragging the reanimated man down with him. 

Julia let out a terrified shriek, dropping her notepad. She scrambled backward, threw open the door, and fled into the corridor, her panicked screams fading down the hall. 

"Dr. Aris!" Chloe lunged forward. She grabbed the heavy steel tray from the bedside table, raising it high and slamming the edge down onto Mr. Henderson’s back. 

CLANG.

The blow did nothing. The dead man didn't even flinch. He crawled further on top of the bleeding doctor, his snapping jaw aiming for Dr. Aris's throat. 

The door burst open. Corporal Vance ran in, his rifle raised, his boots clicking on the floor. 

"Get back! Get away from him!" Vance yelled, aiming his Austeyr at the thrashing shapes on the floor. 

"Vance, shoot him!" Chloe cried, backing away. 

BANG! BANG! BANG!

The three shots were deafening in the small tiled ICU room, the muzzle flashes illuminating the space like lightning. The bullets tore through Mr. Henderson’s back, leaving gaping, bloodless holes in his hospital gown. 

But Henderson didn't stop. He didn't drop. He merely jerked from the impact and kept snapping, finally burying his teeth deep into Dr. Aris’s neck. 

Dr. Aris’s screams turned into a wet, bubbly gurgle. He thrashed weakly, then lay still, his eyes wide and vacant. 

"What the hell?" Vance whispered, his face turning gray. "I hit him in the center mass. Why isn't he down?"

Vance stepped forward, trying to grab Henderson’s shoulder to pull him off the dead doctor. But Henderson spun around with a growl. His blood-soaked hand shot out, catching Vance by the ankle and pulling the soldier to the floor. 

Vance fell hard, his rifle clattering away. Before he could recover, Henderson crawled up his legs, his jaw snapping. Vance kicked out with his free boot, but Henderson bypassed the defense, sinking his teeth deep into Vance's right thigh. 

Vance screamed, a guttural roar of pain. "Agh! He bit me! The bastard bit me!"

Chloe watched in absolute horror. In the span of two minutes, the quiet ICU room had become a slaughterhouse. Dr. Aris was dead on the floor. Vance was pinned, bleeding. And Henderson was still clawing, unfazed by three bullet wounds to the chest. 

She remembered a scrap of information from an emergency broadcast she'd seen on her phone before the cellular towers died—a young woman with a blood-smeared face, streaming from a dark studio, warning the world: “A shot to the body won’t stop them. Smash the skull. Drive something into the eye socket. Break the brainstem. That is the only way.”

Chloe looked around the room. On the floor next to Dr. Aris’s body was a heavy, stainless steel bone saw from an orthopedic tray. 

She lunged for it.

Chapter 4: The Lockdown Trap

Chloe’s hands shook as they wrapped around the cold, heavy handle of the stainless steel bone saw. She didn't think. She didn't let herself feel the horror of what she was about to do. 

She lunged forward, raising the heavy instrument, and swung it down with all her strength into Mr. Henderson's skull. 

CRACK.

The blade bit deep into the bone. Henderson let out a dry, rattling hiss, his fingers releasing Vance's ankle as his body jerked. 

Chloe pulled the saw back, a spray of dark, congealed blood hitting her face. She screamed, a raw, terrified sound, and drove the heavy edge down again, right into the center of his forehead. 

CRUNCH.

The skull split. The milky white eyes rolled back, and Mr. Henderson collapsed sideways onto the concrete floor, his limbs instantly going limp and silent. 

Chloe dropped the saw, her chest heaving, her stomach churning as she gagged. The smell of copper and decay was overwhelming. 

"Chloe..." Vance groaned from the floor. He was clutching his right thigh, where blood was pumping through his fingers, staining his camouflage trousers a deep, dark crimson. "Help... my leg..."

Chloe forced her medical training to take over. She dropped to her knees beside the soldier. "Keep pressure on it! I need to apply a tourniquet."

She grabbed Vance's webbed tactical belt, threading it around his upper thigh. She pulled it tight, ignoring his screams of agony as she twisted it to stop the arterial flow. 

Vance’s face was white, his lips turning blue. He was shivering violently. He knew what a bite meant. He’d seen the reports from the other sectors. 

"It's... it's inside," Vance stammered, his eyes darting around the room. "The outbreak... it started inside. The radio... they said the quarantine was holding. They lied."

He reached for the tactical radio on his shoulder, his blood-slicked fingers pressing the button. "Base, this is Vance. Sector 2-B is compromised. Repeat, compromised. We have an active outbreak within the ward. I'm... I'm initiating containment protocol."

"Vance, no!" Chloe yelled. "What are you doing?"

Vance ignored her. He dragged himself toward the wall near the door, where a red, metal-cased panel was mounted. It was the hospital's newly installed emergency lockdown system, designed to isolate individual wards in case of a chemical leak or civil unrest. 

He punched a four-digit military code into the keypad. 

"Vance, stop!" Chloe lunged to grab his hand, but she was too late. He slammed his palm against the large, red LOCKDOWN button. 

Immediately, a deep, mechanical hum echoed through the ceiling. 

CLANG. CLANG. CLANG.

Outside the room, in the corridors, heavy steel security shutters began to slide down from the ceiling, sealing the exits. The glass observation windows of the pediatric ward were covered by thick, interlocking metal plates. The heavy double doors of their wing clicked, their electronic deadbolts locking into place with a sound like a gunshot. 

They were sealed in. 

"Containment..." Vance whispered, his head slumping against the wall. His eyes were glazed, the fever already taking hold. "Nobody... leaves. We don't... let it spread."

"You idiot!" Chloe screamed, slamming her hands against the locked door. "We're trapped in here with..."

She stopped. 

She turned slowly, looking back at the floor. 

Dr. Aris’s body lay in a pool of his own blood, his throat torn open. 

Chloe’s breath caught in her throat. She remembered the rules. The warning she’d seen on her phone. “Anyone who dies—for any reason—will rise. You have to destroy their brain.”

On the floor, Dr. Aris’s fingers began to twitch.

Chapter 5: The Rules of the Dead

Dr. Aris’s chest heaved with a sudden, violent gasp. His head snapped to the side, his neck flexing with a wet click. 

"Chloe..." Vance whispered, his hand reaching out weakly toward his rifle, which lay out of reach. "Help me up..."

But Chloe wasn't looking at Vance. She was looking at Dr. Aris. The doctor's eyes rolled down, locking onto her. They were cloudy, dead, and entirely devoid of the kind, overworked man who had spent his last days trying to save the hospital's patients. 

With a low, clicking snarl, Dr. Aris scrambled to his feet. He didn't rise like a normal person; his limbs moved with a stiff, jerky motion, as if his muscles were resisting his own commands. 

"Get back!" Chloe screamed. 

She lunged for the door, threw it open, and stumbled out into the concrete corridor of the pediatric ICU. Dr. Aris lunged, his fingers clawing at her hair, but she slipped past the door frame, grabbed the heavy wood handle, and slammed it shut. 

She turned the lock from the outside. 

"Chloe! Chloe, open the door!" Vance’s terrified voice came through the glass observation panel. He was sitting on the floor, dragging his paralyzed, tourniqueted leg, trying to reach the door handle. 

Behind Vance, Dr. Aris turned. The scent of Vance's fresh blood was too strong to ignore. 

"Vance, I'm sorry!" Chloe sobbed, backing away, her hands pressed against the glass. "I can't! He’ll get out!"

Vance turned his head, his face contorting in terror as he saw his former colleague shambling toward him. He tried to crawl, to fight, but his strength was spent. Dr. Aris dropped onto Vance’s back, his teeth burying into the soldier's shoulder. 

Vance’s screams echoed through the thick glass, muffled and distant, before ending in a wet, choking silence. 

Chloe covered her mouth to stifle her own screams. She forced herself to turn away. She had to protect the children. 

She ran into the main pediatric nursery. Max was sitting in his bed, tears streaming down his face, clutching his teddy bear. Beside him, four-year-old Lily and two-year-old Ben were crying, their small voices blending into a chorus of panic. 

"Chloe!" Max sobbed. "Where's the doctor? What's that sound?"

"Shh, it's okay, Max," Chloe said, her voice shaking as she ran to the nursery’s double glass doors. "We're going to play a game. The quietest game in the world. I need you all to climb under your beds and hold your breath, okay? Like hide-and-seek."

"Is... is the monster coming?" Lily whimpered. 

"No monsters, sweetie. Just a game. Go, quickly!" 

Chloe watched as the children scrambled under their steel cribs. Then, she began to move. She grabbed a heavy steel filing cabinet, dragging it across the linoleum, the metal screeching loudly. She pushed it against the glass double doors. She piled desks, heavy medical carts, and plastic chairs on top of it, building a crude barricade. 

In her right hand, she gripped the bloody orthopedic bone saw. It was the only weapon she had. 

Thump.

Chloe froze. 

The lock on the door to Room 204 rattled. Then, the wood splintered as something threw its weight against it. 

CRACK.

The door gave way. 

Through the gaps in her barricade, Chloe saw them enter the corridor. 

Dr. Aris was first, his face slick with blood. Behind him came Corporal Vance. The soldier's camouflage uniform was torn, his throat ripped open. He walked with a stiff, hitching limp from the tourniquet still tied around his leg, but he didn't stop. 

The two dead men shambled down the corridor, their milky eyes searching the shadows, drawn by the sound of the screeching filing cabinet.

Chapter 6: The Security Key

The heavy double doors of the nursery rattled. Through the gaps in the piled filing cabinets and chairs, Chloe saw Dr. Aris and Corporal Vance press their faces against the glass pane. Their milky white eyes stared blankly into the room, their fingers scratching uselessly at the wooden frame. 

Under the cribs, Max, Lily, and Ben huddled together, crying silently into their hands. 

Chloe backed away, her hands slick with sweat on the handle of the bone saw. She looked around the room. The air was already starting to feel warm and stuffy. When Vance initiated the containment protocol, the steel shutters closed, and the primary ventilation system in this ward shut down to prevent airborne contagion. In a few hours, the oxygen in the sealed room would run out. 

She needed to get out. But the steel shutters blocking the emergency stairs could only be overridden by the electronic keycard on Vance's tactical vest. 

She had to get that card. And the only way to get it was to bring Vance close enough to touch. 

"Max," Chloe whispered, her voice barely a breath. "Stay under the bed. No matter what you hear, do not make a sound."

Max nodded weakly, clutching his teddy bear. 

Chloe walked to the side of the nursery. Adjacent to the main doors was a small medication prep room. It had a solid wooden door that opened into the same corridor, equipped with a metal sliding hatch—a "pill window"—used for passing medicine to staff without opening the ward. 

She slipped into the prep room, leaving the nursery door closed behind her. She walked to the pill window and unlocked the sliding metal latch. 

She slid it open a crack. 

The corridor outside was dimly lit in red. She could see Dr. Aris and Vance at the main nursery doors, still scratching at the barricade. 

Peach had said they responded to sound. 

Chloe took a breath, reached for a metal kidney dish on the counter, and tapped it against the wooden door frame. 

Clink. Clink.

The two figures in the hall stopped scratching. Their heads snapped toward the prep room door. 

Slowly, their limbs dragging, they shuffled toward the sound. Vance was leading, his right leg dragging in a heavy, mechanical hitch. The black keycard was clipped to the front of his tactical vest, dangling just above his magazine pouches. 

Chloe waited, her heart hammering. 

Vance reached the door. His pale, blood-smeared face appeared in the narrow window of the pill hatch, his gnashing teeth snapping at the air. The smell of his decay rolled through the opening, making Chloe gag. 

He lunged at the opening, his arms shoving through the hatch. His fingers, tipped with dirt-caked nails, clawed wildly, trying to grab her scrubs. 

The keycard was inches away, but Vance’s thrashing arms blocked her from reaching it. 

"Come on," Chloe whispered. 

She raised the bone saw, aiming for Vance’s left hand. With a swift, heavy strike, she slammed the metal edge down onto his forearm. The bone cracked, and his arm went limp, but Vance didn't even yell. He just kept clawing with his right hand, his face pressing harder against the steel hatch. 

Chloe reached out with her left hand, her fingers stretching through the gap. She caught the edge of the plastic keycard holder. 

Vance’s right hand snapped sideways. 

His fingers closed around her wrist.

Chapter 7: The Security Station

Vance’s grip was ice-cold and incredibly tight, his fingers digging into Chloe’s flesh. With a low, wet grunt, he pulled her arm forward, dragging her shoulder against the steel frame of the pill hatch. Through the narrow opening, his blood-smeared face pressed close, his snapping jaw inches from her bare wrist. 

Chloe screamed, her boots slipping on the linoleum as she pulled back with all her weight. 

"Let go! Let go of me!" she shrieked. 

Through the hatch, she saw Dr. Aris shambling closer, drawn by her screams. If Aris reached her, she would be pinned. 

With her free right hand, she raised the bone saw. The cramped space of the prep room made a full swing impossible, so she reversed her grip, holding the heavy steel saw like a dagger. 

She drove the pointed metal tip of the saw straight through the open hatch, aiming for Vance’s face. 

SQUELCH.

The steel point pierced the corner of Vance’s left eye socket, sinking deep into the brain. 

Vance’s jaw immediately snapped shut, his body going rigid. The strength evaporated from his fingers. His hand slid off Chloe’s wrist, and his body slumped backward, sliding down the wooden door into a heap in the red-lit corridor. 

Chloe pulled her arm back through the hatch, her skin bruised but unbroken. In her hand, she was clutching the black plastic keycard holder she had managed to tear from Vance’s vest. 

Dr. Aris reached the hatch, his face slamming against the metal frame as he snarled, trying to squeeze his head through the opening. 

Chloe slammed the sliding metal hatch shut, turning the latch. 

She stood in the dark prep room, her chest heaving as she stared at the keycard in her hand. It was stained with Vance’s blood, but the magnetic strip was intact. 

She walked back into the nursery. Max, Lily, and Ben were still huddled under the cribs, shivering. 

"I've got the card," Chloe whispered, helping Max out. "We're going to leave now. But you have to be very, very quiet. We’re going to walk past the doctor, and he mustn’t hear us. Okay?"

Max nodded, his face stained with tears. He grabbed Lily’s hand, while Chloe picked up two-year-old Ben, cradling him against her shoulder. 

They walked to the prep room door. This door was separate from the nursery entrance and wasn't blocked by the barricade. Chloe swiped the card against the wall-mounted reader. The green LED flickered to life. 

She cracked the door open. 

In the red-lit hallway, Dr. Aris was still at the nursery door, his back to them, scratching at the steel filing cabinet. The corpse of Corporal Vance lay motionless on the floor, the bone saw still jutting from his eye socket. Vance was dead for good. 

Chloe waved her hand, signaling the kids to follow. They slipped out of the prep room, keeping close to the wall, their bare feet silent on the floor. They bypassed Dr. Aris, who was too obsessed with the noise behind the barricade to notice them. 

They reached the end of the corridor, where the heavy steel security shutter blocked the stairwell. 

Chloe swiped the card again and pressed the override button. 

With a loud, grinding metallic squeal, the heavy steel shutter began to rise, winding up into the ceiling. 

The sound was deafening. Behind them, Dr. Aris stopped scratching and spun around, his milky eyes locking onto them. He let out a loud snarl and began his slow, dragging shuffle toward the stairs. 

"Go! Go!" Chloe shoved Max and Lily through the half-opened gap under the rising shutter. 

She scrambled under the steel plate last, pulling her legs through just as Dr. Aris reached the opening. 

They emerged onto the third-floor landing of the main hospital. 

The stairwell was dark. But as the shutter rose fully behind them, the dim red light from the ward illuminated the main corridor. 

It was a graveyard. 

The walls were splattered with dried blood. The floor was littered with abandoned medical carts, smashed gurneys, and dozens of bodies—doctors, nurses, patients, and soldiers—lying silent in the dark. 

And from the shadows at the far end of the hall, the low, collective groan of the dead began to rise.

Chapter 8: The Descent

The service stairwell was a concrete throat, cold and smelling of damp dust. The only light was the faint, pulsing green of the emergency exit signs. Chloe held two-year-old Ben tightly against her chest, her other hand guiding Max and Lily down the metal steps. 

Clack. Clack. Clack.

From the landing above, the sound of Dr. Aris's shoes on the metal stairs echoed. He was coming down. And he wasn't alone. From the third-floor corridor they had just left, other dragging footsteps were joining the descent. 

"Keep moving," Chloe whispered, her voice cracking. "Watch your step."

They reached the second-floor landing. The heavy fire door was locked from the inside, its small window showing only darkness. 

They continued down. As they approached the first-floor landing, Chloe stopped. 

Through the wire-reinforced glass of the door, she saw the main lobby. It was lit by the flickering orange glow of fires outside the building. The lobby was a nightmare. The barricades the military had built out of desks and sandbags had been breached. The glass doors of the entrance were shattered. Dozens of zombies—former civilians who had fled to the hospital for safety—wandered the debris-strewn lobby. 

Worse, several turned ADF soldiers, still clad in body armor and tactical helmets, shambled among them. One of them, a sergeant, still had a combat knife stuck in his shoulder. They had died from their wounds in the triage area, only to reanimate and turn the lobby into a trap. 

Max gasped, clutching Lily’s hand tighter. "Chloe... the soldiers. They're like the doctor."

"Don't look, Max," Chloe said, her heart freezing. "Keep your eyes on the stairs. We're going to the basement. The laundry area. There's a service exit there."

Suddenly, the first-floor stairwell door rattled. 

A zombie—a woman in a bloody hospital gown—had pressed her face against the glass. She saw them. She let out a wet, whistling groan and began to throw her weight against the door handle. 

Thump. Thump.

The latch was old and worn from days of heavy use. With a sharp crack, the lock gave way. The door swung open, and the woman shambled onto the landing, her arms reaching out. 

"Run!" Chloe yelled, pushing Max and Lily down the final flight of stairs. 

They bolted down the concrete steps toward the basement level. Behind them, the shambling woman fell down the first few steps, tumbling heavily and landing in a heap on the concrete, her bones cracking. But she didn't stop. She immediately began to crawl, dragging her broken legs, her teeth still snapping. 

From the open door on the first floor, more shamblers began to pour into the stairwell. 

They reached the basement door. It was a heavy, grey metal door with a brass handle. Chloe grabbed it, pushed, and they spilled into the dark, steam-filled basement of the hospital.

Chapter 9: The Waste Chute

The basement laundry room was a vast, subterranean cavern of concrete and steam. Rows of massive, industrial-sized washing machines and cylindrical dryers stood like sleeping iron giants in the dim emergency lighting. The air was thick, hot, and smelled of damp cotton and mold. 

Chloe led the children between the rows of machines. Ben was quiet now, his thumb jammed into his mouth, his small body shivering. Max and Lily kept close, their eyes darting to the steam pipes overhead that hissed and dripped. 

"The exit to the ambulance bay is just past the boiler room," Chloe whispered, pointing toward a heavy set of swinging doors at the far end of the room. 

Suddenly, those doors creaked open. 

A figure shambled out. It was a woman in a stained kitchen apron, her arms covered in severe burns, her head hanging at an unnatural angle. Behind her came three more—hospital kitchen staff and a laundry worker wearing a heavy rubber apron. They sniffed the air, their milky eyes locking onto the group. 

Chloe spun around to lead the kids back to the stairwell. 

But the door they had just entered was already rattling. Through the glass pane, she saw the crowd of shamblers from the first floor pushing through, their bodies jamming the narrow doorway. 

They were trapped. The dead were closing in from both ends of the laundry room. 

Chloe’s eyes searched the room, panic rising in her chest. There were no windows. No side doors. Only concrete walls. 

Then, she saw it. 

In the wall near the massive industrial washers was the steel door of the waste and linen compactor chute. It was a wide, square opening that led down to the sub-basement boiler and incinerator room, designed to drop heavy bags of trash and waste directly to the furnaces. 

She ran to it, throwing open the heavy steel latch. The chute was a steep, smooth slide of galvanized steel, sloping down into pitch darkness. 

"Max, Lily, listen to me," Chloe said, her voice fast and desperate. "You have to slide down. It’s like a slide at the park. It’ll take you to the basement floor below this one. I’ll be right behind you."

"It's dark, Chloe," Lily whimpered, backing away. "I'm scared."

"I know, sweetie, but you have to go. Now!" 

Chloe grabbed Lily, lifting her into the opening, and pushed her gently. Lily let out a short gasp as she slid down the metal tube, her shoes squeaking before disappearing into the dark. 

"Max, you next. Take Ben."

Chloe handed two-year-old Ben to Max. The boy hugged the toddler tightly, his eyes wide with fear but filled with a brave determination. He climbed into the chute. 

"See you at the bottom, Chloe," Max whispered. 

He let go. The two boys disappeared down the slide. 

Chloe turned to climb in herself, but she was out of time. 

The turned laundry worker in the rubber apron had closed the distance. With a speed that caught her off guard, he lunged across a sorting table, his hands snapping out. 

His fingers, slick with grease and blood, clamped onto the collar of her scrubs. 

Chloe gasped as she was yanked backward, her feet slipping on the wet floor. She fell hard against the edge of a sorting table, the breath knocked from her lungs. The zombie crawled over the table, his snarling face pressing close, his teeth snapping inches from her throat. 

She thrashed, trying to find her weapon, but she had dropped the keycard and had no saw. 

From the chute below, she heard Max’s distant scream.

Chapter 10: The Ambulance Bay

Chloe writhed on the sorting table, the heavy rubber apron of the turned laundry worker pressing down on her chest. The zombie’s teeth snapped, strings of black saliva dripping onto her collarbone. 

With her right arm pinned, she swept her left hand across the metal table, searching for anything. Her fingers brushed against a heavy, cast-iron tape dispenser. She grabbed it, raised it, and slammed the solid metal block into the side of the zombie's skull. 

CRACK.

The zombie didn't stop, but the impact knocked him sideways. Chloe used the opening to drive the corner of the heavy dispenser directly into his eye socket. 

SQUELCH.

The laundry worker went limp, collapsing onto her. Chloe shoved his heavy, dead body off her chest, scrambled to her feet, and dove headfirst into the waste chute. 

She slid down the steep metal tube, the friction burning through her scrubs. A second later, she shot out of the bottom of the chute, landing heavily in a mountain of dirty, blood-stained bedsheets. 

"Chloe!" Max’s voice cried out from the darkness. 

Chloe sat up, spitting out lint. Max was huddled nearby, holding Ben, while Lily was crying into Max's shoulder. Max's scream had been from the shock of the drop into the dark sub-basement. 

"I'm here, I'm here," Chloe gasped, crawling out of the pile of linens and hugging them. "Are you hurt? Is everyone okay?"

"We're okay," Max sobbed. "But it's so dark."

They were in the incinerator room. The ceiling was low, lined with heavy concrete beams and massive boiler tanks that hissed with residual pressure. A flight of metal stairs in the corner led up to a security door labeled Ground Level - Ambulance Bay. 

"Come on," Chloe said, pulling them toward the stairs. 

They ran up the steps. Chloe pushed the door's panic bar. It clicked open, and they emerged into the cool night air of the Royal Melbourne Hospital’s ambulance bay. 

The bay was a covered concrete driveway, littered with abandoned triage tents, discarded medical supplies, and several vehicles. At the far end, the gates of the hospital were visible, blocked by a tangled mass of crashed sedans and a burned-out military transport truck. 

In the center of the bay, an ambulance stood idling, its diesel engine rumbling softly. The driver's side door was wide open, the interior lights casting a pale yellow glow.
