"Get in the back!" Chloe told Max and Lily, opening the rear doors of the ambulance. She helped them climb inside, then placed two-year-old Ben into the passenger seat of the cabin, buckling him in as she jumped into the driver's seat. 

She slammed the door. The keys were in the ignition. The fuel gauge showed half a tank. 

"Hold on back there!" Chloe yelled, shifting the transmission into drive. She slammed her foot onto the accelerator. 

The heavy ambulance surged forward, its tires squealing on the concrete. She steered toward the perimeter fence, aiming for a small gap where a section of chain-link gate had been knocked down by a civilian car. 

But she didn't see the black ice or the slick pool of transmission fluid on the asphalt. 

The ambulance skidded. The steering wheel spun wildly in her hands. 

CRASH.

The front bumper of the ambulance slammed violently into the side of an abandoned military Humvee. The impact shattered the windshield, sending a spiderweb of cracks across the glass, and deployed the passenger-side airbag with a loud bang. 

The engine shuddered, coughed, and died. 

Chloe’s head hit the steering wheel, leaving her dazed. She shook her head, trying to clear her vision. Beside her, Ben was screaming. 

Through the cracked windshield, she saw them. 

The crash had alerted every zombie in the area. From behind the crashed cars at the gates, from the shadows of the triage tents, dozens of figures turned. Turned civilians, patients in torn gowns, and ADF soldiers in full combat gear began their slow, rhythmic march toward the stalled ambulance. 

"No, no, no," Chloe whispered, her hands shaking as she turned the key. 

Chug-chug-chug-chug.

The starter motor groaned, but the engine didn't catch. 

"Come on, please!" she begged, turning the key again. 

Chug-chug-chug-chug-cough.

The dead were closing in. The first one—a turned soldier with his helmet cracked—reached the hood of the ambulance. He slammed his blood-slicked palms against the cracked windshield, his milky eyes staring at her through the glass. 

Behind him, twenty more shamblers pressed against the sides of the vehicle, their fingers clawing at the doors, the metal skin of the ambulance groaning under their collective weight. 

"Chloe!" Max screamed from the back. "They're at the windows!"

Chloe gripped the key, her tears blurring the dashboard, and turned it one last time. 

Chug-chug-chug...

================================================================================

STORY 3: THE BLUE MOUNTAINS BLOCKADE
------------------------------------

Chapter 1: The Last Stand at the Pass

The blue haze that usually blanketed the valleys of the Blue Mountains was gone, replaced by a greasy, sulfurous smog that drifted up from the ruins of Sydney. It clung to the damp eucalyptus leaves and coated the sandstone cliffs of the Great Western Highway cutting in a layer of gray soot. 

Senior Constable Cooper sat on the hood of a highway patrol sedan, his fingers working with mechanical precision to clean the slide of his Glock pistol. The battery on the patrol car was dying, its red and blue lights flashing with a dim, sluggish pulse that cast jittery shadows against the towering rock walls. 

They were the last line. 

Behind them, further up the winding mountain pass, lay the small township of Glenbrook and the mountain sanctuaries where thousands of terrified refugees had fled. In front of them, the highway sloped down toward the plains—and the dead. 

The blockade was a messy, desperate wall of steel and concrete. They had dragged concrete roadworks barriers across the two-lane road, reinforcing the gap with crashed civilian SUVs and three police vehicles. It was a solid wall, but it felt incredibly small against the scale of the mountains. 

"Any luck on the wire, Davies?" Sergeant Briggs barked, his voice raw from smoke and shouting. He stood near the center barrier, his binoculars pressed to his eyes. 

"Still nothing, Sarge," Davies replied from a concrete utility niche. He was surrounded by loose wires and a portable generator that coughed out a thin stream of blue exhaust. "The remote transmitter is dead. If we want to detonate the landslide charges on the cliffs above, someone is going to have to do it manually from the junction box down in the cutting."

Cooper didn't look up from his gun. "That junction box is fifty yards past the barricade, Sarge. It's right in the open."

"We hold them here," Briggs said, his jaw tight. "We buy Davies enough time to splice a hardline, or we blow it manually when they hit. Either way, this road gets buried."

Beside Cooper, Miller—a grizzled veteran cop with only six months left until his retirement—blew a thin stream of roll-your-own cigarette smoke into the damp air. "Six months, Coop. I was going to buy a boat. Now look at us. Playing soldiers in the scrub."

"Shut up and load your twelve-gauge, Miller," Cooper muttered, snapping the slide back onto his Glock with a sharp clack. 

At the far edge of the barrier, Constable Kelly—the rookie, barely twenty-one—was pacing back and forth, his hands shaking as he clutched an F88 Austeyr rifle they had scavenged from an abandoned military checkpoint. His knuckles were white. 

"They're coming," Kelly whispered, his voice cracking. "Listen."

The silence of the mountain pass was broken by a low, vibrating hum. It wasn't the sound of engines or the wind. It was a massive, collective drone—a chorus of dry throats groaning in unison, accompanied by the sluggish, dragging scrape of thousands of boots on wet asphalt. 

Briggs lowered his binoculars, his face turning the color of the sandstone. 

"Kelly, get to your post," Briggs ordered, his voice dropping. "Miller, toss that cigarette."

Emerging from the thick mountain mist at the bottom of the cutting came the front of the tide. They filled the highway from rock wall to rock wall. Thousands of them—former city dwellers, office workers, tourists—their clothes torn, their skin gray. They shambled slowly, their feet dragging, but their collective weight moved forward with the terrifying, unstoppable momentum of a glacier. 

"Lord help us," Miller muttered, racking his shotgun. 

Cooper stepped down from the patrol car's hood, raised his Glock, and lined his sights up with the front of the approaching horde.

Chapter 2: The Severed Wire

"It's not the transmitter, Sarge," Davies called out, his face illuminated by the spark of a wire stripper. "The remote system is fried, but the backup copper line... it’s been torn to shreds."

Cooper squinted down the asphalt. About thirty yards ahead of their barricade, a massive, rusted delivery truck had crashed into the ditch, its cabin smashed against the sandstone wall. 

"The truck that crashed yesterday," Cooper realized. "It must have dragged the conduit down when it hit the ditch."

"Exactly," Davies grunted, wiping sweat from his brow. "The main cable running to the blasting caps on the upper ledge is severed. The only place where the circuits still connect is the manual override junction. In the utility bunker."

Every eye in the squad turned to look down the road. 

The utility bunker was a small, reinforced concrete structure built into the side of the sandstone cliff. It lay fifty yards ahead of the barricade, positioned right in the middle of the narrow cutting. To reach it, someone would have to climb over their defensive wall, run down the open highway, and enter a dead-end bunker in the direct path of the oncoming dead. 

"We can't run a new wire," Miller said, his voice flat as he loaded red shells into his Remington shotgun. "Not with them only a hundred yards out. We'd be overrun before we cleared the spool."

"So what's the play, Sarge?" Kelly asked, his voice rising, his eyes darting between Briggs and the massive wall of bodies shambling toward them. "We just stand here and wait to get eaten?"

"No," Briggs said, his voice hard. "We hold the barricade. We draw them in. Once the cutting is packed with them, someone has to make a break for the bunker. They slip inside, hit the manual plunger, and blow the cliff."

"Sarge, that's a suicide run," Cooper said, his voice remarkably calm. "Whoever goes into that bunker is never coming out. The landslide will bury the entrance, and the horde will pin them inside."

"I know," Briggs said quietly. He didn't look at Cooper. "But if we don't blow this pass, the horde reaches Glenbrook in two hours. There are ten thousand people up there. Families. Kids. We hold."

Cooper checked his belt. He had three spare magazines for his Glock. In the back of the patrol car, they had a single metal ammo crate of 5.56mm for Kelly’s Austeyr, and two boxes of shotgun shells. Against a tide that stretched back to the horizon, it was nothing. 

The front of the horde was eighty yards away now. The slow, rhythmic scraping of their feet on the asphalt sounded like waves hitting a shingle beach. Cooper could see their faces clearly now. The lead shambler was a teenage girl in a blue school uniform, her throat a jagged, blackened cavern of dried blood. Her arms were stretched out, her fingers twitching with a mindless, insatiable hunger. 

"Briggs," Miller said, raising his shotgun. "They're crossing the line."

Briggs raised his service pistol, lining up the sights. "Hold your fire. Wait for the fifty-yard marker. Headshots only. Don't waste a single bullet."

Kelly’s rifle barrel was shaking so hard the muzzle was drawing small circles in the air. 

"Steady, kid," Cooper murmured, leaning his shoulder against a concrete barrier. "Just squeeze. Don't pull."

The schoolgirl zombie stepped over the yellow road line they had spray-painted on the asphalt. Fifty yards. 

"Fire," Briggs said. 

Cooper squeezed the trigger. The Glock barked, a sharp, flat report that echoed off the high sandstone cliffs. A small, neat hole appeared in the schoolgirl’s forehead. Her knees buckled, and she fell face-first onto the road, silent and still. 

Behind her, a hundred more stepped over her body.

Chapter 3: The Tide Hits

The cutting transformed into a firing range, the deafening cracks of the rifles and shotguns echoing off the stone walls in a continuous, ear-shattering roar. Smoke, thick and grey, pooled in the humid air, stinging Cooper's eyes. 

BOOM. BOOM.

Miller’s twelve-gauge barked, the spray of buckshot tearing the face off a turned businessman and shattering the shoulder of a woman behind him. She stumbled but didn't stop, her dragging steps carrying her forward. 

"Headshots, Miller!" Briggs roared, firing his Glock through a gap in the concrete barriers. "We don't have the ammo to wound them!"

"Easy to say, Sarge!" Miller grunted, shoving two more red shells into the tube. "They're crowding the cars!"

The front of the horde had reached their steel wall. They didn't try to climb it with athletic grace; they simply pressed their weight against the patrol cars. The metal of the doors groaned, and the glass of the windshields spiderwebbed under the pressure of dozens of bodies pushing from behind. 

Kelly was firing in rapid, panicked bursts. The empty brass casings from his Austeyr rained down onto the asphalt, clinking loudly. 

"I'm out!" Kelly screamed, his voice cracking as the bolt of his rifle locked back. "I'm dry!"

"Grab a mag from the crate, kid!" Cooper yelled, not taking his eyes off his sights. He squeezed the trigger twice, dropping a turned hiker and a nurse. He was down to his last two Glock magazines. 

The weight of the dead was shifting the patrol cars. The heavy sedan Cooper was leaning against shifted two inches, the tires scraping against the road. 

Miller stood next to the front wheel arch of the sedan, racking his shotgun. He was focused on the shambler trying to climb over the hood. He didn't look down. 

From beneath the chassis of the patrol car, a pale, blood-smeared hand shot out. 

It belonged to a turned mechanic in greasy overalls who had crawled under the vehicle hours ago, hidden in the shadows. His rotting fingers clamped onto Miller’s ankle. 

"Agh!" Miller yelled, his balance failing. He crashed down onto the asphalt, his shotgun clattering away. 

"Miller!" Cooper spun, raising his Glock, but the angle was blocked by the car's wheel. 

Before Miller could scramble back, the weight of the crowd pushed a turned woman over the hood of the car. She fell heavily, landing directly on top of Miller's chest. Her teeth clamped down onto his throat, tearing away a chunk of flesh. 

Miller let out a wet, bubbling shriek. 

"Miller!" Kelly screamed, aiming his rifle, but the rookie’s hands were shaking too violently to shoot. 

Two more shamblers fell over the hood, dropping onto the grizzled veteran. The sound of tearing fabric and wet chewing filled the gap between the concrete barriers. Miller's hand reached up, clawing at the sky, before dropping limp onto the asphalt, his blood pooling around the empty shotgun shells. 

"Back off!" Briggs yelled, grabbing Kelly by the shoulder and dragging him back. "They're over the wall! Retreat to the cutting!"

Chapter 4: The Turn of a Friend

"Fall back! Fall back!" Briggs roared, firing his last pistol rounds into the face of a shambler that was sliding over the hood of the patrol car. 

Cooper grabbed Kelly’s tactical vest, dragging the paralyzed rookie backward into the narrow sandstone cutting. Davies followed, clutching his wire cutters and a rifle he had barely fired. They retreated fifteen yards, their boots scraping on the loose gravel, until they were hemmed in by the towering sandstone walls on either side. 

Behind them, the barricade was failing. The collective weight of the horde pushed the patrol cars aside with a loud, metallic grind. The dead began to spill through the gaps. 

Among the first to climb over the hood of the lead cruiser was a figure in a torn, blood-soaked police uniform. 

Miller. 

His throat was a mangled ruin, his shirt soaked in dark, arterial red. But he didn't lie dead. He stood, his limbs moving with a stiff, hitching rhythm as he stepped down from the bumper. His chin was slick with blood, and his eyes had already glazed over with that milky, dead membrane. 

"Miller..." Kelly whispered, his voice cracking. He raised his Austeyr, the barrel waving erratically. "Oh god, Miller. I'm sorry."

"Kelly, shoot!" Briggs yelled, his voice echoing off the sandstone. "That's not him! Shoot!"

The turned veteran locked his milky eyes onto Kelly. With a low, rattling grunt, Miller shambled forward, his hands reaching out, his fingers twitching in a grotesque parody of the man who had just been complaining about his retirement boat. 

Kelly froze. His finger was on the trigger, but his brain refused to let him fire on the man who had taught him how to handle a patrol car. 

Miller lunged, his jaw snapping shut. 

Cooper stepped forward, shoving Kelly out of the way. He raised his Glock, aligning the front sight post with Miller’s forehead, and squeezed the trigger. 

BANG.

The Glock barked. A small hole appeared in Miller’s forehead, and a spray of dark fluid hit the rock wall. Miller’s knees buckled instantly, and he collapsed to the asphalt, sliding into a silent heap. 

Kelly fell back against the sandstone wall, sobbing, his rifle dropping to his side. "He... he didn't even get bitten. They just tore his throat. How did he turn so fast?"

"Because it doesn't matter how you die," Cooper said, his voice cold, though his hand shook slightly as he reloaded his second-to-last magazine. "If you die, you get back up. My moderator, Rex, warned us on his stream. It's the rules of the dead. Everyone who dies joins them."

Briggs looked down the road. The dead were pouring through the gap in the barricade. A wall of shambling flesh, fifty wide, was marching down the cutting toward them. 

The concrete utility bunker was now only thirty yards ahead of them, but the front of the horde was already passing it. 

"Davies, is the plunder ready?" Briggs asked, his jaw set. 

"The plunger is in the bunker, Sarge," Davies said, his voice trembling. "But the line is dead. I have to splice it at the bunker's exterior terminal box. In the open."

Briggs drew his combat knife. "We make a push. Cooper, Kelly, keep the sides clear. Davies, you're on the splice. I'll lead."

Chapter 5: The Fall of Briggs

The squad moved down the cutting, their boots crunching on the gravel as the towering sandstone walls squeezed them closer to the incoming dead. 

"Keep moving!" Briggs yelled, his voice bouncing off the rock. He fired his Glock at point-blank range, the bullet dropping a turned commuter in a business shirt, then stepped forward and drove his combat knife into the eye socket of a second shambler. 

Cooper and Kelly fired in tandem, clearing a narrow path. Cooper's Glock clicked empty. He dropped the magazine, slapped in his last spare, and racked the slide. He had seventeen rounds left. 

They reached the concrete utility bunker. It was a blocky, windowless tomb built into the cliff face. A heavy, rusted steel grate door was the only entrance, locked with a thick padlocked chain. 

"Davies, get the tools!" Briggs shouted, shielding the tech officer with his body. 

Davies scrambled forward, his wire cutters clipping the lock chain with a sharp, metallic snap. He threw the chain aside and pushed the steel grate door open. 

But the noise of the clanking metal had drawn the closest shamblers. 

From behind the crashed delivery truck in the ditch, a cluster of dead emerged. One of them—a turned highway patrol officer, his face bloated and unrecognizable—lunged at Briggs. 

Briggs raised his left arm to block the bite, driving his knife into the policeman’s temple. But as the officer fell, another zombie—a woman in a yellow raincoat—lunged from the blind spot, her teeth sinking deep into Briggs’s exposed shoulder. 

Briggs let out a sharp grunt of pain. He spun, driving his elbow into her face, but the flesh of his shoulder was already torn, blood soaking through his blue shirt. 

"Sarge!" Cooper lunged forward, his Glock barked once, and the woman in the raincoat dropped. 

Briggs stumbled back against the steel grate. He looked at his shoulder, then at the horde that was now only ten yards away, their dead faces illuminated by the dim red lights of the patrol cars behind them. He knew the rules. 

"Get in!" Briggs yelled, his voice cracking with a mix of pain and resolve. 

He grabbed Cooper by the tactical vest and shoved him violently into the dark concrete interior of the bunker. He did the same to Kelly and Davies, pushing them inside. 

"Sarge, no! We can get you inside!" Cooper yelled, scrambling to his feet. 

Briggs didn't answer. He slammed the heavy steel grate shut. 

CLANG.

He threaded the broken padlock chain through the handles from the outside and snapped the padlock shut, locking them in. 

"Sarge!" Kelly screamed, throwing his weight against the iron bars. "Unlock it! Sarge, please!"

Briggs turned his back to the grate, leaning against the cold metal bars. He pulled his combat knife from his belt. His breath was coming in ragged gasps, his skin already turning a feverish, waxy gray. 

"The plunger is inside, Cooper," Briggs said, not looking back. "The line... you have to splice it. Do it quick."

"Briggs!" Cooper yelled, grabbing the bars. 

"Blow the pass, Cooper," Briggs whispered. "That's an order."

Through the bars, Cooper watched as the horde reached the bunker. Briggs lunged forward, driving his knife into the skull of the first zombie. He fought with a silent, desperate fury, but there were too many. Within seconds, a dozen hands grabbed his arms, his legs, his throat. 

They dragged him down onto the wet asphalt, their bodies covering him in a writhing, snapping mass. 

Cooper turned away, his stomach turning, and shone his flashlight into the dark interior of the bunker.

Chapter 6: Pinned at the Bunker

The concrete bunker was a dark, narrow vault. The air was thick with the scent of damp concrete, rust, and the exhaust fumes from Davies’s portable generator. Outside the steel grate, a wall of gray faces and grasping hands pressed against the iron bars. The metal groaned under the collective weight, and the sound of fingernails scratching against the steel was a deafening, metallic hiss. 

Cooper shone his flashlight around the room. In the center was the heavy, wooden-cased blasting machine—a classic manual plunger. 

Davies dropped to his knees in front of it, his hands shaking as he traced the copper wires coming from the wall. 

"The line is dead here too," Davies gasped, his voice cracked with panic. "The conduit must have shattered when the truck hit the ditch. The copper core is severed right inside the wall."

"Can you fix it?" Cooper asked, standing near the grate, his Glock raised, keeping his eyes on the zombies scratching at the bars. 

"I... I have to strip the main cable coming from the cliff, pull it through the conduit, and splice it directly to the plunger’s firing terminals," Davies said, his fingers fumbling with his wire strippers. "It’s thick, industrial wire. It's going to take a few minutes."

"Make it quick, mate," Cooper muttered. He had twelve rounds left in his Glock. Beside him, Kelly was pressed against the back wall, his Austeyr held limp in his hands, his chest heaving as he hyperventilated. 

Suddenly, a loud thud echoed from above. 

Cooper snapped his flashlight beam to the ceiling. A square ventilation hatch, used for venting lead-acid battery gases, had been left open. 

Through the opening, a shape wriggled. 

It was a turned surveyor, wearing a harness and a high-vis vest, who had been working on the cliffs earlier. He must have fallen from the ledge above, landing on the bunker's roof, and crawled into the shaft. 

Before Cooper could raise his gun, the zombie slipped through the hatch, dropping five feet down. 

He landed directly on top of Davies. 

Davies let out a sharp cry of terror as the zombie’s dead weight pinned him to the concrete floor. The surveyor’s jaw snapped, his teeth burying deep into Davies’s collarbone, tearing through the fabric of his shirt. 

"Agh!" Davies shrieked, his wire strippers clattering away. 

Cooper lunged forward, pressing the muzzle of his Glock against the surveyor's temple, and squeezed the trigger twice. 

BANG. BANG.

The zombie’s head jerked, and he went limp, his body collapsing onto Davies. Cooper grabbed the heavy corpse by the harness and dragged it off the technician, throwing it into the corner. 

"Davies!" Cooper knelt beside him. 

Davies lay on the floor, his hand pressed to his shoulder. Dark, thick blood was pumping through his fingers, staining his blue shirt. His eyes were wide, filled with a sudden, waxy glaze. He was shivering, his breath coming in shallow, ragged gasps. 

"He... he bit me," Davies whispered, looking up at Cooper. "It burns. Cooper... my chest is on fire."

In the corner, Kelly let out a sob, covering his face. "We're dead. We're all dead."

Davies looked at the severed wires, then at his wire strippers lying on the floor. "I... I can't do it. My hands... they’re shaking too much. I can’t strip the wire."

Chapter 7: Kelly's Sacrifice

"Tell me what to do, Davies," Cooper said, his voice flat as he picked up the wire strippers. 

Davies was lying on the concrete floor, his eyes rolling back, his skin slick with sweat. "Strip... strip the thick black cable. Inside... three wires. Red, blue, green. Connect... connect the red and blue to the copper terminal posts. Ignore... ignore the green."

His voice trailed off into a dry, rattling whisper. His eyes went wide, staring at the concrete ceiling, and then his body went completely limp. 

Davies was dead. 

In the corner, Kelly let out a sharp, hysterical sob. "He's gone. He's going to get up, Cooper. He's going to turn."

CRACK.

Outside, the steel grate groaned. A massive, bloated zombie had shoved its head and shoulders through the gap where the chain had broken, its face pressing against the iron bars. The concrete anchors securing the door frame to the sandstone cliff were beginning to crack under the relentless pressure of the horde. 

Cooper stood over Davies's body, his Glock raised, waiting for the first sign of movement. He felt a cold, empty weight in his chest. Everyone was dying. 

"I'll do the splice," Cooper said, not looking at Kelly. "Get ready to shoot him."

"No," Kelly said. His voice was suddenly quiet, the panic replaced by a strange, hollow calm. 

Cooper looked over. Kelly was looking up at the open ventilation hatch in the ceiling. The F88 Austeyr was clutched in his hands, a single magazine left on his vest. 

"The grate is going to fail, Cooper," Kelly said, his eyes reflecting the dim emergency light. "If they break through, you won't finish the splice. And there's no way out."

"Kelly, don't—"

"I'm going up," Kelly interrupted. He grabbed the iron rungs of the maintenance ladder built into the concrete wall. "There's a rocky path that leads from the highway up to the roof of this bunker. If I make noise on the roof, they’ll turn. They'll go up the path to get to me. It'll buy you the time."

"Kelly, you won't survive that. There’s no exit on the roof."

Kelly let out a soft, dry chuckle. "I'm a cop, Coop. I'm supposed to protect people. Let me do my job."

Before Cooper could stop him, Kelly scrambled up the iron rungs. He hauled himself through the narrow ventilation hatch, disappearing into the dark night. 

A second later, his voice boomed from the roof above, echoing off the sandstone cliffs. 

"Hey! Over here, you rotting bastards! Come and get me!"

Kelly opened fire. The rapid, deafening pop-pop-pop-pop of the Austeyr cut through the mountain night. 

Through the bars of the grate, Cooper saw the crowd of zombies freeze. Drawn by the loud gunfire and screams directly above them, they began to turn away from the bunker door. They shuffled slowly toward the narrow, rocky path that wound up the side of the cliff to the roof. 

Cooper dropped to his knees beside the plunger box, his hands shaking as he grabbed the wire strippers. 

Above his head, the sound of Kelly’s rifle continued, a desperate, rhythmic defense. 

"Come on," Cooper muttered, his teeth grinding as he stripped the thick black insulation. 

From the roof came a sudden, sharp scream. Kelly's rifle fired in a single, continuous burst, then fell silent. 

Cooper didn't look up. He kept working in the dark, the tears finally starting to blur his vision.

Chapter 8: The Bunker Siege

Cooper knelt in the pitch darkness of the concrete vault, the beam of his flashlight shaking as it illuminated the raw copper cores of the main cable. 

Behind him, a dry, rasping sound scraped against the concrete floor. 

He spun around, dropping the wire strippers. 

Davies was crawling. His body moved with a stiff, hitching gait, his head tilted back. The eyes that opened were completely milky white, reflecting the harsh light of Cooper's torch. His jaw clicked, a string of bloody saliva trailing onto the concrete. 

Cooper raised his Glock, his finger tightening on the trigger. 

"I'm sorry, mate," Cooper whispered. 

BANG.

The gunshot was deafening inside the small concrete room. Davies’s head jerked, and he collapsed sideways, his body sliding into a silent heap in the corner. 

Cooper took a deep, shuddering breath and turned back to the plunger box. He had nine rounds left in his magazine. 

Outside the steel grate, the thumping began again. 

The horde on the roof, having finished with Kelly, was shuffling back down the narrow, rocky path. Drawn by the gunshot inside the bunker, they swarmed the steel door once more. The metal bars groaned, the concrete frame cracking further as the weight of dozens of bodies pressed against it. 

Cooper looked through the bars. 

At the front of the crowd, pushing his face against the cold iron, was Sergeant Briggs. The sergeant's blue shirt was soaked in dark red, his throat torn open. Beside him was a mangled, bloody shape—a turned Kelly, his rookie face unrecognizable save for the reflective police patch on his shoulder. 

They were both staring at him, their jaws snapping, their dead hands reaching through the bars. 

"Cooper..." a voice seemed to echo in the wind, but it was just the whistling of the mountain draft through the vents. 

Cooper blocked it out. He grabbed the red and blue copper wires, his fingers slick with sweat. He tried to thread them around the positive and negative terminal posts on the plunger casing, but the screws were tight. 

He didn't have a screwdriver. 

He reached for his belt, pulled his folding pocket knife, and used the flat edge of the utility blade as a crude screwdriver. His hands shook violently as he turned the brass screws, the metal scraping in the dark. 

Outside, the steel grate gave a loud, snapping sound. One of the iron bars bent, creating a six-inch gap. Briggs’s arm shoved through the opening, his fingers clawing at the air, his nails scratching against the shoulder of Cooper’s jacket. 

Cooper scrambled back, his heels hitting the wall. He had to finish it. He had to connect the last wire.

Chapter 9: The Splice

The concrete frame around the steel grate cracked with a sound like a gun report. A spiderweb of fractures shot across the rock wall, and the top anchor bolt sheared off, the metal door sagging inward. 

Briggs’s hand clawed at the air, his bloodied fingers catching the sleeve of Cooper’s jacket, dragging him toward the gap. 

Cooper raised his Glock with his left hand and fired once. 

BANG.

The bullet struck Briggs in the forehead. The sergeant’s head snapped back, and he fell, his hand releasing Cooper’s sleeve. But Kelly was right behind him, his mangled jaw snapping, his fingers reaching through the widening gap. 

Cooper scrambled back to the plunger box, his knees scraping on the concrete. The red wire was secured, but the blue wire’s copper core was bare, dangling inches from the positive terminal. 

He grabbed the pocket knife, trying to loosen the brass screw. His fingers were slick with his own blood from a scrape on the rock wall. The blade slipped, slicing his thumb. He gritted his teeth, ignoring the stinging pain, and forced the blade into the screw’s slot. 

CRACK-CRUNCH.

The entire steel grate collapsed inward, crashing onto the concrete floor in a cloud of gray dust and rust. The barrier was gone. 

The narrow entrance of the bunker was immediately flooded with the dead. They poured over the fallen grate, their dragging feet clanking on the metal. Turned civilians, soldiers, and Kelly shambled into the room, their arms outstretched, their collective groan a deafening, suffocating wall of sound. 

"Get back!" Cooper screamed. 

He raised his Glock and fired in a rapid, desperate chain. 
BANG. BANG. BANG. BANG.

Four zombies dropped, their bodies creating a temporary barrier on the floor. He had four rounds left. Kelly shambled over the pile, his dead eyes locked onto Cooper. Cooper raised the gun, aimed at the rookie's face, and squeezed the trigger. 

BANG.

Kelly collapsed. 

Cooper dropped the gun. There was no time to reload. He threw himself onto the plunger box. 

He grabbed the bare blue copper wire with his bare, bleeding hand. He didn't try to screw it down; there wasn't time. He pressed the raw copper core directly against the brass terminal post, his thumb holding it tight against the metal. 

He felt the sharp sting of the electrical current from the capacitor bank tingling against his raw skin. 

With his right hand, he grabbed the wooden T-bar of the plunger, pulling it upward until it locked into the ready position. 

A turned commuter in a torn suit tripped over Kelly’s body, falling forward. His dead hands closed around Cooper’s left ankle, his fingers digging into the leather of his boot with crushing force. Another zombie—a woman with a shattered jaw—reached over the box, her fingers clawing at Cooper’s collar, her teeth snapping inches from his face. 

Cooper gritted his teeth, his thumb pressing the blue wire harder against the brass post. 

He looked up at the ceiling, then down at the plunger. 

He slammed his right hand down on the wooden T-bar.

Chapter 10: The Landslide

Cooper slammed his palm down on the wooden T-bar of the plunger. 

For a fraction of a second, nothing happened. Then, the electrical current surged through the spliced wires, completing the circuit. 

Outside the bunker, a series of sharp, deafening cracks ripped through the sandstone cliffs above. The charges of industrial dynamite, laid deep within the rock face, detonated in a synchronized chain. 

The concrete floor beneath Cooper buckled violently. The shockwave hit him like a physical blow, throwing him backward onto the concrete. His flashlight flew from his hand, bouncing across the floor and smashing into darkness. 

Then came the roar. 

It was a deep, grinding sound of rock crushing rock—the sound of a mountain collapsing. Thousands of tons of ancient red sandstone slid down the cliff face. Through the open entrance of the bunker, the night sky was obliterated by a rushing wall of dirt, dust, and massive boulders that cascaded onto the highway. 

The landslide buried everything. The patrol cars, the concrete barriers, the delivery truck, and the thousands of shambling dead were crushed and entombed under a twenty-foot-deep mountain of rock. 

The entrance of the bunker was hit with a deafening crash as a massive slab of sandstone slammed against the door frame, sealing the opening completely. 

Then, silence fell. 

The roar of the landslide faded, replaced by the soft, settling hiss of dust. 

Cooper lay in the pitch darkness, his lungs burning as he coughed, breathing in the thick, chalky air. The air was warm and heavy, suffocatingly dry. He reached down, feeling his legs. They were bruised and scratched from falling debris, but unbroken. He was alive. 

He struggled to a sitting position, his hands brushing against the cold concrete floor. He reached for his pocket, pulled out his phone, and tapped the screen. 

The screen was cracked, but it flickered to life, casting a pale, weak blue glow over the room. 

The dust was so thick it looked like fog in the light of the screen. In the corner, the bodies of Davies and the turned surveyor lay still under a layer of red sandstone dust. 

Cooper crawled toward the entrance. 

The steel grate was gone, completely crushed under a solid wall of fractured sandstone. Boulders the size of cars were jammed together, sealing the entrance as effectively as a concrete plug. There was no way out. The air in the tiny, windowless bunker would only last a few hours. 

Cooper slumped against the rock wall. He had done it. The Great Western Highway was blocked. The pass was sealed. The mountain sanctuaries were safe. 

He leaned his head back against the cold sandstone, closing his eyes to save his breath. 

Scratch.

Cooper’s eyes snapped open. 

Scratch. Scritch.

The sound was faint, muffled by tons of rock, but it was rhythmic. Relentless. 

From the other side of the sandstone barrier, from deep within the rubble pile where the landslide had buried them, came the scraping of fingernails. 

The dead didn't breathe. They didn't suffocate. Pinned under the weight of the collapsed mountain, their broken limbs were still moving. They were slowly, rhythmically scratching their way through the gaps in the rubble. 

Toward the bunker. Toward him. 

Cooper held his phone closer to his chest, the pale blue light illuminating his waxy, dust-covered face in the dark. 

Scratch. Scratch. Scratch.

The screen flickered, and died.

================================================================================

STORY 4: THE GORGE
------------------

Chapter 1: The Sanctuary

The red sandstone walls of the gorge rose a hundred feet straight up into the cloudless desert sky, their faces painted in deep ochre, orange, and blood-red. Inside this geological fortress in the West MacDonnell Ranges, the air was cool, scented with the sweet, damp smell of ancient palm trees and the fresh water of the permanent spring. 

Down by the waterhole, the community of a hundred and twenty people went about their afternoon quietly. Children played in the shallows without splashing, their voices kept to a low murmur. Auntie Lena sat near a cooking fire, turning damper on the hot coals, while two younger women sorted wild bush tomatoes they had gathered from the spinifex plains outside. 

It was peaceful. But it was an alert, defensive peace. 

When Alice Springs fell on Day 4, the community had retreated deep into the ranges, bringing their families back to the country their ancestors had walked for sixty thousand years. They had lived off-grid for generations; they didn't need the power grid or the supermarkets that had collapsed in the cities. 

At the mouth of the gorge, the sandstone walls narrowed to a bottleneck just twenty feet wide. Across this gap, the community had built a formidable wall. They had stacked heavy red boulders, interlocking spinifex logs, and sheets of corrugated iron, reinforcing the base with packed sand. 

High above the wall, on the northern clifftop, Birrani leaned against the warm sandstone. He wore his dusty green park ranger uniform, the telescopic sight of his .223 bolt-action hunting rifle pressed to his eye. He scanned the red sand plains that stretched out toward the distant Stuart Highway. 

Beside him sat Uncle Jarli. The Elder’s face was a map of deep wrinkles, his hair and beard white as salt. He wasn't looking through a glass lens. His dark, weathered eyes were locked on the horizon, reading the country. 

"They're coming, Birrani," Jarli said softly. His voice was like dry leaves scraping over stone. "The country feels heavy today. The wind is shifting, carrying the smell of the dead."

Birrani adjusted the focus of his scope. The hot air shimmered over the spinifex grass, creating a mirage of water on the horizon. But through the heat waves, he saw the movement. 

It was a line of dust, miles away, moving slowly toward the ranges. 

"I see them, Uncle," Birrani said, his thumb flicking the safety on his rifle. "Refugees from the highway. Or what's left of them."

"Not refugees anymore," Jarli murmured, standing up. He grabbed his spear thrower—his woomera—which he carried alongside a heavy steel crowbar. "The bad spirit has taken them. Tell the boys at the wall. The wind is bringing the dead."

Chapter 2: The Desert Walkers

Through the telescopic sight, the incoming dead looked like a procession of ghosts. 

There were about forty of them, walking in a loose, shambling column across the red spinifex plains. The harsh, baking sun of Central Australia had not been kind to them over the last week. Their skin was cracked, blackened, and stretched tight over their cheekbones like dry leather. Some were tourists, still wearing wide-brimmed sun hats and carrying cameras that clanked against their hollow chests. Others were truckies and grey nomads who had been caught on the Stuart Highway when the world ended. 

They moved with a slow, heavy drag, their bare feet burning on the hot red sand, but they didn't seem to feel it. 

"They're coming straight for the mouth, Birrani," Uncle Jarli’s voice crackled over the small two-way radio at Birrani’s hip. The Elder had descended the cliff path to the rock wall below. 

"I copy, Uncle," Birrani said, his voice calm. He lay flat on the rocky ledge, the metal of his rifle barrel warm under his hand. "Got forty of them. Slow walkers. No runners."

"The boys are ready at the wall," Jarli said. "But we don't let them get close. We use the rocks."

Birrani looked down. At the clifftops directly above the narrow twenty-foot bottleneck, the younger men of the community—Jason, Kaelen, and Marcus—were stationed. They had spent the last three days moving heavy sandstone boulders to the edge of the cliffs, securing them behind thick eucalyptus logs that acted as release levers. 

The geological advantage of the gorge was absolute. To enter the sanctuary, the dead had to walk through a narrow canyon cutting with vertical sandstone walls. There were no side trails. No foothills. 

Birrani opened his ammo box, pulling out a handful of brass .223 rounds. He loaded the rifle's five-round magazine, pushed the bolt forward, and locked it into place. 

The first zombie—a turned grey nomad in a dirty singlet, his arms covered in deep, dry bite marks—shambled to the edge of the rocky wash leading to the gorge entrance. His mouth was open, dry and black, his tongue swollen. His milky eyes stared blankly at the red rock walls. 

The wind died down. The desert became dead silent. 

Birrani lined up the crosshairs of his scope with the nomad's forehead. He inhaled the dry, hot air, held it in his lungs, and slowly squeezed the trigger. 

crack.

The rifle report was a sharp, dry snap that echoed down the canyon. Through the scope, Birrani saw the nomad's head jerk back as the bullet struck. His knees buckled instantly, and he collapsed into the red dust, his body silent. 

Behind him, the rest of the column stopped. Their heads turned, their milky eyes scanning the high cliffs, drawn by the sound. 

Then, with a low, collective groan that sounded like the wind through the sandstone caves, they began to shamble faster, their dragging feet kicking up red dust as they pressed toward the mouth of the gorge.

Chapter 3: The Red Wall

The sand-packed wash leading into the gorge bottleneck was narrow, funneling the slow-moving column of the dead into a tight crowd. 

Below, behind the barricade of boulders and corrugated iron, Uncle Jarli raised his arm. Beside him stood the young men of the community, holding steel crowbars and heavy wooden hunting spears. They waited, their faces tense but resolute. 

On the cliffs above, Jason and Marcus stood by the heavy eucalyptus logs that held back the stacks of sandstone boulders. 

"Wait," Jarli’s voice came over the radio, soft and steady. "Let them fill the cutting. Wait..."

The crowd of shamblers pressed into the bottleneck, their dry, rasping groans rising in a chorus that echoed off the vertical stone walls. The smell of their sun-baked decay was thick. 

"Now!" Jarli shouted. 

Jason and Marcus slammed their crowbars under the release logs, leveraging them upward. 

With a deep, grinding roar that shook the cliff face, the massive sandstone boulders—some the size of oil drums—cascaded over the edge. They tumbled down the vertical walls, striking the stone with sparks before crashing into the narrow bottleneck. 

The geological defense was devastating. 

The heavy rocks crushed the shamblers beneath, the sheer weight of the stone smashing skulls, fracturing bones, and pinning limbs under hundreds of pounds of red sandstone. The bottleneck became a grinder. 

From his high ledge, Birrani leaned over, picking off the ones that had escaped the rockfall. 

Crack. Crack. Crack.

Each shot was deliberate. He dropped a turned truckie, then a young woman, and then a tourist in a floral shirt. 

Only three zombies made it through the dust and falling rocks to the barricade itself. Kaelen, a young hunter of nineteen, stepped forward, his heavy steel-tipped spear raised. With a quick, practiced thrust, he drove the spear through the eye socket of a turned nomad who had reached the base of the wall. 

"Good job, Kaelen!" Jason yelled, leaning over the corrugated iron to smash the skull of another with his crowbar. 

But the last zombie—a mangled woman with her arm broken—reached out blindly. Her dry, dirt-caked hand clawed at Kaelen’s arm as he pulled his spear back. Her jagged, broken fingernails caught the skin of his forearm through a tear in his sleeve, leaving a three-inch scratch that immediately began to bead with bright red blood. 

Kaelen flinched, pulling his arm back, his heart jumping. He quickly drove the butt of his spear into her temple, dropping her. 

"Kaelen! You alright, brother?" Jason asked, turning to him as the dust began to settle. 

Kaelen quickly pulled his sleeve down, covering the scratch. He forced a grin, though his face had gone pale. "Yeah, mate. All good. Just... scratched my arm on a sharp edge of the corrugated iron when I lunged. It's nothing."

Jason clapped him on the shoulder. "Good stuff. We held them. Not one of the bastards got through."

Below the cliffs, the mouth of the gorge was quiet again. The forty shamblers lay crushed and motionless under the massive pile of red rocks, their blood soaking into the thirsty desert sand. 

But on the wall, Kaelen clutched his forearm, the skin around the scratch already starting to throb with a strange, unnatural heat.

Chapter 4: The Fire in the Scrub

The celebration was short-lived. 

Through the rising heat waves on the plains, Birrani saw a second, much larger dust cloud. It was a massive wave of the dead—easily eighty or ninety shamblers—spilling off the red sand dunes. They were moving in a dense, tight pack, drawn by the sound of the rifle shots and the grinding roar of the rockfall. 

"We've got more, Sarge!" Birrani called over the radio, using the old police term he’d picked up in the Alice. "A lot more. They're going to crawl over the rock pile."

"We don't have enough rocks left on the north ledge, Birrani!" Jason’s voice came back, tight with worry. 

"Uncle Jarli," Birrani said, looking at the large, black metal drum positioned near the cliff edge. "It's time for the ranger's special."

"Do it, son," Jarli replied. 

The drum was a forty-four-gallon barrel of diesel fuel, scavenged from the ranger station's generator depot. Birrani and Marcus used their steel crowbars as levers, wedging them under the heavy steel barrel. 

With a grunt of effort, they rolled the drum over the lip of the cliff. 

It plummeted down the vertical sandstone wall, striking a jutting ledge and splitting open with a loud, metallic CLANG. A torrent of thick, dark diesel sprayed across the pile of crushed sandstone and the incoming horde below, soaking the shamblers in highly flammable oil. 

Down at the wall, Kaelen stood shivering, his forehead beaded with sweat despite the hot desert air. He clutched his arm, his vision starting to blur, but he forced himself to focus. He grabbed a dried spinifex torch, held it to the cooking fire, and handed it to Jason. 

Jason threw the burning grass torch over the barricade. 

WHOOSH.

The fuel ignited instantly. A wall of bright orange fire erupted in the narrow sandstone bottleneck, the flames rising thirty feet into the air, casting a flickering, hellish light against the towering red cliffs. The heat was immense, forcing the defenders back into the shadow of the gorge. 

The slow-moving dead walked directly into the inferno. They didn't feel the pain, they didn't scream, but the intense heat consumed them. Their dry, sun-baked flesh burned like paper, and their muscles shriveled, their legs collapsing under them. The narrow canyon acted as a natural chimney, channeling the draft and incinerating the horde to black ash. 

Behind the wall, the young men watched in awe. 

"It's a furnace," Jason laughed, wiping soot from his forehead. "They can't get through that. Not in a million years."

"Country defended us today," Uncle Jarli said, looking up at the towering cliffs. "The old ways and the new ways. We held."

From the clifftop, Birrani let out a breath, leaning his forehead against the cool steel of his rifle barrel. It was a complete, decisive victory. The geological sanctuary had proven impenetrable. 

But down at the wall, Kaelen backed away into the shadows of the gorge, his body shaking with a violent, freezing chill. He wrapped his arms around himself, his teeth chattering as the fever began its rapid, silent assault.

Chapter 5: The Silent Transition

The desert night fell fast, dropping the temperature of the gorge into a bone-chilling cold. The fire at the center of the camp had burned down to a mound of glowing red coals, casting a warm, flickering light over the sleeping tents. 

In a canvas wall tent near the back of the gorge, Uncle Tommy lay on his cot, wrapped in three heavy wool blankets. He was eighty-two, his heart worn out from a lifetime of hard work and the chronic diabetes that had plagued his later years. Over the last two days, his breathing had become shallow and wet. 

The tent was quiet, smelling of eucalyptus oil, damp canvas, and the sweet smoke of the coals. 

Matriarch Auntie Lena stepped into the tent, holding a tin mug of warm lemon-grass tea. She walked to the side of the cot, her face soft in the shadows. 

"Tommy?" she whispered. 

Tommy didn't move. His face looked remarkably peaceful, the deep lines of his forehead smoothed out in sleep. His hands were folded over his chest. 

Lena reached down, placing a gentle hand on his forehead. It was cold. 

She pressed two fingers to his wrist, then to the side of his neck. 

Nothing. 

Lena let out a long, trembling breath and closed her eyes. She bowed her head, murmuring a quiet prayer in her language. Tommy had passed away peacefully in his sleep, his tired heart finally giving out. It was the way an Elder was supposed to go—surrounded by his country and his people, not torn apart in the streets of the cities. 

"Sleep now, old man," Lena whispered, pulling the blanket up over his chin. 

She walked out of the tent, closing the canvas flap behind her. She needed to find Uncle Jarli and the other Elders to prepare for the burial ceremony. 

Inside the tent, Uncle Tommy’s body lay silent on the cot. 

For three minutes, there was no sound but the wind rustling the palm leaves outside. 

Then, Tommy’s chest gave a sudden, sharp contraction. 

His fingers, curled under the wool blanket, twitched. His head rolled slowly to the side, his neck flexing with a soft, dry click. 

The blanket shifted as his eyes snapped open. 

They were no longer the dark, wise eyes of the Elder who had taught the young boys how to find water in the dry sand. They were filmed over with a thick, milky white membrane, vacant and dead. 

With a slow, jerky motion, Uncle Tommy sat up.

Chapter 6: The Watchers

On the high northern cliff, the dry desert wind whistled through the crevices in the sandstone. Below, the fire in the bottleneck was dying down, leaving only a glowing bed of ash and the faint, sweet smell of burnt spinifex. The threat from the plains had been neutralized. 

Birrani sat by a small, shielded campfire, nursing a tin mug of black tea. The warmth of the metal cup felt good against his cold hands. 

"We did it, Uncle," Birrani said, looking out over the dark expanse of the plains. "The canyon is safe. We have water, we have bush tucker, and the walls are too high. We can hold this place forever."

Uncle Jarli didn't answer. He sat cross-legged on the red dirt, staring up at the canopy of stars that wheeled across the desert sky. His face was etched with a deep, heavy sadness. 

"We hold the front door, Birrani," Jarli said quietly. "But you cannot build a wall against the wind."

"What do you mean, Uncle?"

Jarli picked up a stick, poking the coals. "This sickness... it’s not like the flu. It’s not something that travels in the water or the air. I watched them closely today. The way they walk. The way they look at us. There is no spirit left in them. The spirit has been pushed out, but the body... the body refuses to rest."

He looked at Birrani, his dark eyes solemn. 

"It is a curse on death itself, son. The natural cycle has been broken. The ancestors are quiet because the path to them is blocked. I feel it in the country. It is a weight. A sickness of the land."

Birrani set his mug down. "If it's a curse on death..."

"It means anyone who dies, Birrani. Anyone. Even if they never saw one of those things, even if they never took a bite. When the breath leaves the body, the bad spirit takes over. They will rise."

Birrani’s heart skipped. His mind immediately went to the inner camp. He thought of the kids, the pregnant mothers who had fled from the town, and especially Uncle Tommy, who had been failing for days. 

"Uncle," Birrani said, standing up, his rifle in his hand. "If that's true, we can't leave the sick alone. Uncle Tommy... we need to put a guard in his tent."

Before Jarli could stand, a sharp, piercing shriek tore through the silence of the gorge. 

It was Auntie Lena’s voice, coming from the inner camp. 

It was a scream of pure, sudden terror, echoing off the sandstone walls of the sanctuary. 

Birrani didn't wait. He grabbed his rifle and bolted down the steep, narrow cliff path, his boots sliding on the loose red gravel as he ran toward the camp, Jarli following close behind.

Chapter 7: Reanimation in the Camp

Auntie Lena had returned to Uncle Tommy's tent, her heart lighter after hearing the shouts of victory from the bottleneck. She stepped through the canvas flap, the interior of the tent lit only by the faint orange glow of the camp coals outside. 

"Tommy," she said softly, walking toward the cot. "We held them, old man. Birrani and the boys set a fire. The gorge is safe."

The shape under the blankets shifted. 

"Tommy?" Lena reached out. 

With a sudden, snapping movement, the blankets were thrown aside. The figure of Uncle Tommy lunged off the cot, throwing his weight directly into her. The impact sent Lena crashing to the dirt floor, the tin mug of lemongrass tea clattering away. 

Lena gasped, her hands pressing against Tommy’s chest to push him back. "Tommy! What are you doing? It's me, Lena!"

But the man on top of her didn't speak. He let out a wet, clicking growl, his jaw unhinging. His face was a mask of cold, vacant rage, his milky eyes staring blindly into hers. Before she could scream, his teeth clamped down onto her shoulder, tearing through her blouse into the flesh. 

Lena shrieked—a high-pitched wail of agony and disbelief. 

She thrashed on the dirt floor, her legs kicking out. Her heel caught the small wooden bedside table, knocking it over. The portable gas lantern on the table shattered, the glass casing breaking and the pressurized fuel leaking out. 

A single spark from the overturned heater caught the fuel. 

WHOOSH.

A sheet of bright yellow flame raced up the canvas wall of the tent. Within seconds, the dry fabric was consumed, the fire spreading to the cotton blankets and the dry straw mats on the floor. 

Lena, fueled by pure adrenaline, managed to wedge her knee under Tommy’s chest and kick him off. She scrambled on hands and knees out of the burning tent, collapsing into the dirt of the central camp. 

"Help!" Lena screamed, clutching her bleeding neck, the blood staining her hand and the red dirt. "Tommy... Tommy has the bad spirit!"

The camp was instantly thrown into chaos. Tents unzipped, and families emerged into the cold night air, their faces illuminated by the growing fire of Tommy’s tent. Women grabbed their children, and men reached for their knives and clubs. 

From the blazing canvas structure, a dark silhouette emerged. 

Uncle Tommy shambled into the firelight. His clothes were beginning to catch fire, but he didn't feel it. He walked with a stiff, heavy gait, his arms outstretched, his mouth wet with Lena's blood. 

In a nearby tent, Kaelen lay on his sleeping bag, his body burning with a searing, feverish heat. He heard the screams, but he couldn't move. His vision was gone, replaced by a red haze, and his heart was beating with a rapid, erratic flutter. The scratch on his arm was black, the veins around it swollen and dark. 

He closed his eyes, his breathing stopping as the fever claimed him.

Chapter 8: The Alarm

Birrani and Uncle Jarli vaulted down the final ledge of the cliff trail, their boots throwing up red sand as they sprinted into the inner camp. 

The scene was a chaotic nightmare. Uncle Tommy’s tent was a roaring pillar of canvas and fire, the thick black smoke rising to choke the narrow gorge. The flames illuminated the terrified faces of the families who had fled Alice Springs, their screams echoing off the sandstone walls. 

Uncle Tommy—his clothes smoldering, his flesh blistering from the heat—was shambling toward a group of children who were huddled near the waterhole. 

Jason stood in his path, a heavy wooden club raised in his hands. But he was frozen, his face twisted in anguish. "Tommy! Please, old man, lie down!"

"Get back, Jason!" Jarli’s voice boomed as he ran into the firelight. He didn't hesitate. He swung his heavy steel crowbar with a practiced, powerful sweep, driving the iron tip directly into the side of Tommy’s skull. 

CRACK.

The bone collapsed. The turned Elder’s eyes went vacant, and his body slumped into the red dirt, the smoldering blankets coming to rest beside him. 

Jason dropped his club, his chest heaving as he stared at the body. "He... he just died in his sleep, Uncle. He didn't have a scratch. He just stopped breathing, and then..."

"I told you, son," Jarli said, his voice heavy as he wiped the crowbar on his trousers. "The land is sick. The curse is on everyone who dies."

"Lena!" a woman screamed from the center of the camp. 

Birrani ran toward the cry. Matriarch Auntie Lena lay on the dirt, her head cradled in a young girl’s lap. Blood was pumping through Lena's fingers as she clutched her neck, her skin already turning a pale, feverish gray. She was shivering violently, her breathing turning into a wet, rattling gasp. 

"She's burning up, Birrani," the girl sobbed. "The old man... he bit her deep."

Birrani felt a cold hand wrap around his stomach. He knew how fast the fever took them. 

"Jason, Marcus, get the kids into the storage dugout!" Birrani ordered, his voice rising in panic. "Lock the steel door from the inside! Go!"

He looked around the camp. "Where's Kaelen? He was supposed to be on the inner perimeter."

"I haven't seen him since the wall," Jason called out as he gathered the children. "He went to his tent."

Birrani ran toward Kaelen’s small dome tent at the edge of the clearing. The fabric was dark, silent. 

"Kaelen!" Birrani threw the zipper open and stepped inside, shining his phone light into the cramped space. 

Kaelen lay on his back, his body rigid, his skin waxy and covered in cold sweat. His eyes were closed. 

"Kaelen, brother, wake up," Birrani said, kneeling beside him. He reached out, placing his fingers against Kaelen's throat. 

There was no pulse. Kaelen’s skin was already cooling. 

Then, Kaelen's sleeve shifted. In the blue light of the phone screen, Birrani saw the three jagged, blackened scratches on his forearm, surrounded by swollen, dark veins. 

"No," Birrani whispered. 

Kaelen's eyes snapped open. 

They were milky white. With a guttural hiss, Kaelen’s hands shot up, his fingers locking onto the collar of Birrani’s ranger shirt.

Chapter 9: The Chase in the Red Rocks

Kaelen’s hands were like cold steel around Birrani’s collar, pulling the ranger down. In the cramped, dark space of the dome tent, Kaelen's snarling mouth snapped inches from Birrani's face. The smell of his sweat was already sour with the scent of the grave. 

Birrani thrashed, shoving his forearm against Kaelen's throat to keep the snapping teeth away. His .223 rifle was trapped beneath their bodies, useless. 

"Kaelen! Stop!" Birrani grunted, his boots tearing the fabric of the tent. 

Suddenly, a shadow cut through the tent opening. Uncle Jarli stepped in, his expression grim in the blue light of the phone screen. He raised the heavy steel crowbar and drove the iron tip down with a single, sharp stroke. 

SQUELCH.

The crowbar pierced the top of Kaelen's skull. The turned boy went limp, his hands releasing Birrani’s collar. 

Birrani scrambled backward out of the tent, his chest heaving, his face covered in red dirt and sweat. He lay on his back, looking up at the smoke-choked sky of the gorge. 

"He... he hid it," Birrani gasped, pointing to the tent. "He got scratched at the wall and didn't tell us."

"Fear makes men do foolish things, son," Jarli said, helping Birrani up. His old face was set like stone. "But we have no time to mourn. Look."

The central camp was a burning maze. The dry canvas of Tommy’s tent had collapsed, sending sparks that ignited two adjacent shelters. The red sandstone walls of the gorge reflected the orange, dancing flames, creating a hellish amphitheater. 

At the back of the gorge was the storage dugout—a cave carved deep into the soft sandstone cliff, reinforced with concrete and secured by a heavy, military-surplus steel door. It was where they kept their emergency food and where the children had been sent. 

Auntie Lena—now fully turned, her neck covered in black, dried blood—was shambling toward the dugout. 

Jason was at the steel door, trying to push the last of the children inside. He was frantic, his eyes wide. "Get in! Lock it from the inside!"

Lena lunged with a speed that didn't match her age. She tackled Jason from behind, her teeth sinking deep into his shoulder. Jason let out a guttural scream of pain, falling against the steel frame. 

Marcus, a young hunter, ran to help, swinging a wooden club, but Lena spun around and clawed his face, her teeth catching his wrist. 

"No!" Birrani screamed. 

He raised his .223 rifle, aiming through the smoke and the flickering firelight. He lined up the crosshairs with Lena's head. 

crack.

The rifle snapped. Auntie Lena's head jerked, and she collapsed onto the dirt, silent. 

But it was too late. 

Jason lay against the steel door, his hand clutching his mangled shoulder. Marcus sat on the ground, staring in horror at the deep, bleeding bite on his wrist. 

"Jason! Marcus!" Birrani ran toward them, but Jarli grabbed his shoulder, holding him back. 

"They are already gone, Birrani," Jarli said, his voice cracking with a rare, devastating grief. "You know the rules."

Jason looked up at Birrani. His face was already turning that waxy, waxy gray. He let out a wet, painful cough. With his remaining strength, he reached inside the dugout, pulled two-year-old Ben through the door, and slammed the heavy steel plate shut. 

"Lock it, Max!" Jason screamed, banging his bloody palm against the metal. "Lock it and don't open it for anyone!"

From inside, they heard the heavy iron deadbolt click into place. 

The children were safe. But Jason and Marcus were trapped on the outside.

Chapter 10: The Inner Threat

The inner gorge was silent, save for the crackle of the burning canvas and the hiss of steam from the spring. The red sandstone walls reflected the orange firelight, casting long, dancing shadows across the dirt clearing. 

Jason and Marcus lay against the heavy steel door of the dugout, their breathing shallow, their eyes glazed with a waxy film. 

Birrani stood in the center of the clearing, his hands shaking as he held his rifle. He reached into his pockets. Empty. He had fired his last rounds. 

Uncle Jarli walked to the side of the boys, his steel crowbar held in a white-knuckled grip. His face was weathered, old, and filled with a devastating weight. 

"We must do it now, Birrani," Jarli whispered, his voice cracking. "Before the bad spirit takes them. We keep them clean."

Birrani looked away, his chest heaving with silent tears. "They're my cousins, Uncle. Jason... he just saved the kids."

"I know, son. But it is the law of the land now."

Before Jarli could raise the crowbar, Jason’s chest stopped rising. He let out a final, rattling sigh. 

His eyes snapped open. 

They were milky white, reflecting the dancing flames of the tents. Instantly, his body twitched, and he scrambled to his feet with a guttural, wet hiss. A second later, Marcus’s breathing stopped, and he too rose, his face contorted in a vacant, dead rage. 

The two turned hunters lunged. They were young, strong, and their dead limbs moved with a terrifying, jerky speed. 

Marcus tackled Jarli, knocking the old man to the ground. The steel crowbar clattered away into the dirt. 

Jason charged Birrani. With no ammo, Birrani used the stock of his .223 rifle to block Jason’s lunging jaw. The wooden stock cracked under the force of the bite. Jason shoved him back, throwing Birrani against the hard rock wall. The impact knocked the wind from his lungs, and his rifle fell, breaking in two. 

"Uncle!" Birrani gasped. 

On the ground, Jarli was struggling, his hands pressed against Marcus’s throat as the zombie's teeth snapped inches from his face. 

Birrani scrambled across the dirt. He grabbed a heavy, burning branch of eucalyptus from a collapsed tent, the wood glowing red and spitting sparks. He lunged forward, driving the charred, flaming point directly into Jason's eye socket. 

SQUELCH.

The wood pierced the brain. Jason’s body went limp, collapsing onto the red dirt. 

Birrani spun, driving the burning branch into the back of Marcus’s head as Jarli held him down. The skull cracked, and Marcus collapsed, silent. 

The two survivors lay in the dirt, panting, the smell of burning wood and blood thick in the air. The fires of the camp were starting to die down, leaving only smoking ruins and the cold, dark silence of the gorge. 

The mouth of the gorge was still secure. The dead outside were buried under tons of sandstone boulders. The geological fortress had held. 

But inside, everyone was dead. Only Birrani and Jarli remained. 

Suddenly, a muffled scream cut through the steel door of the dugout. 

"Birrani! Uncle Jarli!" Max’s voice was high, frantic, and filled with a terror that made Birrani’s blood run cold. 

Birrani scrambled to his feet and ran to the steel door, banging his hands against the cold metal. "Max! Max, what's wrong? Open the door!"

"I can't! The lock is jammed! Uncle, help!" 

Birrani pressed his face to the small, wire-reinforced glass peephole in the center of the door. 

Inside, the dugout was dark, lit only by the green glow of a chemical lightstick on the floor. 

Two-year-old Ben was standing in the center of the room. But he wasn't crying. His small head was tilted back, his limbs moving with a stiff, jerky hitch. He had been bitten on the leg by Auntie Lena during the panic, a wound hidden under his trousers. 

The toddler’s eyes were milky white. 

"Ben! Get away!" Max screamed from the corner, clutching Lily to his chest as the turned toddler shambled slowly toward them in the green light, his small mouth clicking. 

"Max! Unlock it!" Birrani screamed, throwing his shoulder against the heavy steel door. The military-grade lock was deadbolted from the inside, and the key was lying on the floor inside the room, out of the children's reach. 

"Max! The key! Pick up the key!" 

Through the glass, Birrani watched as the turned toddler closed the distance. Max squeezed his eyes shut, holding Lily tight, as the screams from inside began to echo through the steel. 

Birrani clawed at the iron frame, his fingers bleeding, helplessly screaming into the cold, silent stone of the gorge.

================================================================================

STORY 5: THE MURRAY RIVER
-------------------------

Chapter 1: The Lazy River

The wide, muddy-brown waters of the Murray River wound lazily through the ancient forests of giant red gums, their massive, twisted trunks casting long, dark shadows over the surface. The afternoon was silent, save for the hum of the houseboat's small diesel generator and the occasional, lonely cry of a whistling kite overhead. 

Dave stood on the rear deck of The Lazy River, a double-decked tourist houseboat that had been their sanctuary for the last thirty days. He lifted the red plastic jerry can, tilting it. A hollow, sluggish slosh was the only reply. 

"Less than two liters," Dave muttered, his voice flat. 

He stepped through the sliding glass door into the cramped, wood-paneled cabin. His wife, Sarah, was sitting at the small dining table, gently wiping their ten-year-old son Billy's forehead with a damp cloth. The cabin was warm, the air conditioning long dead to save power. 

"How's the fuel?" Sarah asked, her eyes tired and lined with worry. 

"Generator's going to sputter out by sunset," Dave said, leaning against the kitchen counter. "And that means the electric water filter goes offline. We've got two bottles of clean water left, Sarah. After that, we're boiling river water, but we've been out of cooking gas since Tuesday."

Sarah looked out the window. The riverbanks were lined with thick scrub and fallen logs, completely empty. "We can't drink the raw river water. Not with the run-off. We'll get cholera, or worse. In this heat, Billy won't last three days."

Billy looked up, clutching a drawing pad. "Can't we just find another boat, Dad? One with fuel?"

"We've been floating down from Mildura for a week, mate. Every boat we’ve seen is either burned out or jammed in the reeds," Dave said, placing a gentle hand on his son's shoulder. 

Dave walked to the steering console and picked up his binoculars. He looked downriver, where the water bent around a wide, sandy point. 

Emerging from the red gum trees was the silhouette of a town. The white-painted houses and historic storefronts of Renmark lined the high banks. At the main wharf, the historic paddle steamer, The PS Industry, lay half-sunken, its paddle wheel smashed, its timber charred black. 

The town's riverfront was completely silent. No smoke rose from the chimneys. No cars moved along the street. 

"Renmark," Dave said, adjusting the focus. "There’s an IGA supermarket just two blocks from the main wharf. And a service station near the caravan park."

Sarah stood up, her face tight. "Dave, the towns are death traps. You know what happened in Mildura."

"I know," Dave said. "But we don't have a choice. We dock, we scavenge what we can, and we get back on the water. We lock Billy inside the cabin. We'll be in and out in an hour."

He turned the wooden steering wheel, guiding the heavy houseboat toward the weathered timber pylons of the Renmark wharf.

Chapter 2: The Silent Port

The houseboat’s diesel engine gave a final, choking sputter and died just as the steel hull glided alongside the weathered timber pylons of the Renmark wharf. The silence that followed was heavy, almost suffocating, broken only by the gentle lapping of the river against the hull. 

Dave threw the mooring ropes over a wooden cleat, pulling them tight and securing the boat. They were officially out of fuel. If they couldn't find diesel here, The Lazy River would remain anchored to this dock forever. 

Inside the cabin, Sarah was strapping a heavy canvas backpack onto her shoulders. She carried two empty plastic fuel cans in one hand and a heavy, fire-hardened wooden club in the other. Dave checked his belt, making sure his heavy steel crowbar and his long hunting knife were secure. 

"Billy, look at me," Dave said, kneeling in front of his son in the dim cabin. 

Billy gripped his drawing pad tightly, his bottom lip trembling. "Don't go, Dad."

"We have to, mate. We’ll be right back," Dave said, his voice gentle but firm. "I need you to do exactly what we practiced. Slide the glass doors shut, turn the security latch, and draw the curtains. Do not open them for anyone. Not even if you think you hear us, unless we use the code word on the radio."

"The code word is Billabong," Billy whispered. 

"Good lad. Keep the two-way radio in your hand. If you see anything out on the deck, you press the button and talk quietly. Don't make any noise in here, okay? Just draw your pictures."

Billy nodded, stepping back into the shadow of the cabin. 

Dave and Sarah stepped out onto the rear deck. Dave slid the heavy glass door shut, and they heard the click of the internal lock. 

They climbed the splintered wooden steps of the wharf, emerging onto the riverfront park. The lawn was dead, the grass turned to yellow straw, littered with abandoned picnic rugs, plastic cups, and the rusted remains of a bicycle. The hot sun beat down on the empty street, the asphalt baking. 

"Look," Sarah whispered, pointing to a wooden bench under a massive Moreton Bay fig tree. 

A man in a faded blue council uniform sat on the bench, his head slumped against his chest. He was motionless. But as their boots clicked on the concrete path, his head rolled slowly upward. 

His eyes were milky, his skin gray and peeling from sunburn. A thick, dried crust of black blood covered his shirt. He let out a low, dry whistle of air through his throat but didn't stand. He lacked the energy, or perhaps his legs were broken. He just stared at them with a vacant, dead hunger. 

"Keep moving," Dave murmured, gripping his crowbar. "Ignore him. He's too slow."

They left the park behind, slipping past the shuttered tourist information center. As they rounded the brick corner of the building, they looked down Murray Avenue, the town's main shopping street. 

The road was a graveyard of abandoned cars, their doors open, their interiors stripped. A few blocks down, near the red brick facade of the supermarket, several shambling figures wandered the middle of the road, their shadows long in the afternoon heat.

Chapter 3: Renmark's Streets

The heat reflecting off the asphalt of Murray Avenue was like a physical barrier, baking the sweat onto Dave’s neck. The silence was absolute, save for the rhythmic scuff-scuff of their boots and the hot wind rattling a loose metal sign on a bakery storefront. 

Dave and Sarah moved in a crouch, using the rusted flanks of abandoned cars as cover. 

"How many do you see?" Sarah whispered, her fingers tight around the wooden club. 

Dave peeked over the hood of a dusty sedan. Thirty yards ahead, a turned elderly woman in a faded sun dress was shuffling aimlessly around a fire hydrant, her head hanging at an angle. Further down the block, near a chemist shop, two more shapes in dirty clothing dragged their feet. 

"Three in the open," Dave whispered back. "They're slow, Sarah. Just stay in the shadows of the storefronts. If we don't make any noise, they won't look."

They moved from shopfront to shopfront, passing locked doors, dusty window displays, and signs of panic: dropped shopping bags, shattered windshields, and dried, black blood stains on the concrete pavement. The town had been abandoned in a hurry, but many hadn't made it out. 

They reached the parking lot of the IGA supermarket. 

The lot was a graveyard of rusted trolleys, their plastic wheels melted onto the asphalt. Two sedans had crashed head-on near the entrance, their engines cold and covered in dust. 

The supermarket's automatic sliding glass doors were broken. One pane was shattered, leaving a wide, jagged opening. From the dark interior, a wave of rancid, humid air rolled out—the unmistakable, sickening stench of rotting fruit, spoiled meat, and decades-old dust. 

Sarah gagged, pulling her shirt over her nose. "God, the smell."

"The refrigeration died weeks ago," Dave muttered, his eyes scanning the parking lot. 

At the far edge of the lot, near the trolley bay, a turned man in a faded polo shirt was scratching at a metal post. His back was to them, his movements slow and mechanical. 

"We go in, grab the water, find the canned goods aisle, and get out," Dave said, gripping his iron crowbar. "Keep your eyes on the corners. The dark inside is where they'll be hiding."

He stepped through the shattered glass entryway, his boots crunching softly on the fragments. Sarah followed him, the darkness of the IGA swallowing them whole as they left the baking sun behind.

Chapter 4: The IGA Supermarket

Inside the supermarket, the darkness was thick and cavernous. The only light was the bright shafts of afternoon sun cutting through the broken front entryway, illuminating millions of dust motes dancing in the rancid air. 

Dave and Sarah moved down Aisle 4. The shelves were mostly stripped, a testament to the initial panic weeks ago. Smashed jars of mayonnaise, torn cereal boxes, and rotting produce littered the concrete floor, dried into a sticky, black crust. 

They reached the drinks aisle. Most of the soda and juice bottles had been taken, but on the bottom shelf of a display unit, Dave spotted three intact plastic-wrapped slabs of spring water. 

"Water," Dave breathed, kneeling down. He sliced the plastic wrap with his hunting knife, packing six large bottles into Sarah’s canvas backpack and another six into his own. The weight was heavy, but it was life. 

Sarah moved to the next aisle, searching for canned goods. "Dave! Over here. There’s still some tinned fruit and baked beans on the top shelf."

"Don't try to carry too much," Dave whispered, walking over to join her. "We need to stay light."

Sarah reached up for a cardboard shipping box on the top shelf, intending to use it to carry the cans. The box was wedged tight between two rusted metal brackets. She gave it a firm pull, but it didn't budge. She braced her feet, gripping the cardboard with both hands, and yanked. 

The box came free, but the force of the pull dragged the top bracket forward. The entire metal shelving unit—unbolted and top-heavy with leftover tins of soup—began to wobble. 

"Sarah, get back!" Dave yelled. 

Before Sarah could react, the tall metal rack tilted. It crashed forward with a massive, deafening roar. 

CRASH. CLANG-CLANGER-CLANG.

Tins of soup and peaches erupted onto the concrete floor, scattering in all directions. The metal shelving unit collapsed onto the opposite rack, the steel brackets shearing off with a loud, ringing din that echoed through the entire hollow building like a series of gunshots. 

Silence fell again, but it was a tense, vibrating silence. 

Dave scrambled over the fallen tins, grabbing Sarah’s arm. "Are you alright? Did it hit you?"

"I'm... I'm fine," Sarah gasped, her eyes wide, her hands shaking as she clutched her wooden club. "I'm so sorry, Dave. I just pulled the box..."

"It’s okay. We need to go. Now."

From the dark, shadowed rear of the store—behind the heavy double doors marked Stockroom - Employees Only—came the response. 

A low, collective groan rolled out of the dark. It was a hollow, wet sound, the voices of dozens of throat-damaged dead waking up. The heavy stockroom doors began to rattle, their plastic windows smudged with black, greasy handprints from the other side. 

Thump. Thump. Thump.

The doors swung open. Out of the darkness shambled the dead, their milky eyes locking onto the bright shafts of light in the main store, their dragging footsteps moving toward Aisle 4.

Chapter 5: Cornered in the Aisles

Dave gripped Sarah’s wrist, pulling her toward the front entrance. "Run! Out the front!" 

They sprinted down the aisle, but as they rounded the corner near the checkout registers, Dave froze. 

The loud crash of the metal shelves had not just alerted the stockroom. The three zombies from the parking lot outside—including the turned man in the polo shirt—were already shuffling through the shattered glass doors. Their milky eyes locked onto Dave and Sarah, and they raised their thin, sunburned arms, blocking the narrow exit. 

Behind them, the double stockroom doors crashed open fully. A dozen shambling dead, their skin gray and sloughing off, spilled into the back aisles. Among them was the turned store manager, still wearing a faded blue tie, and a checkout girl with her red name tag pinned to a blood-soaked vest. 

They were surrounded. The dead were closing in from both the front doors and the rear stockroom, their dragging feet scraping on the linoleum. 

"Dave, where do we go?" Sarah gasped, her back hitting a candy display.
