Dave’s eyes darted around the dark rafters. Near the bakery counter in the back corner of the store, he spotted a vertical steel ladder bolted to the brick wall. It led up to a narrow maintenance mezzanine and a wooden access door to the roof. 

"The ladder! Go!" Dave shouted. 

They bolted toward the bakery. Dave knocked over a stack of plastic bread crates to slow the advance of the stockroom group. They scrambled behind the counter, the smell of sour yeast and mold thick in the air. 

"Up, Sarah! Go!" Dave shoved her toward the steel rungs. 

Sarah grabbed the cold metal, climbing frantically. Dave followed right behind her, his heavy backpack shifting on his shoulders. 

Below them, the manager zombie reached the counter. His dead hands clawed at the air, and he grabbed the bottom rung of the ladder. Behind him, a turned teenage stock boy in a green apron lunged, his fingers catching the heel of Dave's hiking boot. 

The zombie pulled down with a sluggish, heavy weight. 

"Agh!" Dave gritted his teeth, his grip on the ladder slipping. 

He swung his free leg, driving his boot heel directly into the stock boy's face. 

CRUNCH.

The nose collapsed, and the zombie fell backward, crashing into a metal display of bread tins. Dave scrambled up the remaining rungs, pulling his legs onto the narrow wooden mezzanine platform. 

Directly in front of them was the heavy wooden access door. 

Sarah slid the rusted metal bolt back and threw the door open. Bright, hot sunlight flooded the dark mezzanine, blinding them for a second. They scrambled through the opening, collapsing onto the flat, gravel-strewn roof of the IGA. 

Dave grabbed the heavy door, slammed it shut, and jammed his iron crowbar through the metal handles from the outside, wedging it tight. 

Thump. Thump.

From the other side of the wood, the dead began to scratch and thump, their groans muffled by the thick timber. 

Dave lay on the gravel, his chest heaving, the baking sun of the Murray lands hot on his face. They were on the roof. They were safe for the moment, but they were still trapped forty feet in the air, with no way down.

Chapter 6: The Leap

Dave ran to the edge of the flat, gravel roof, peering over the low brick parapet. 

The alleyway below was narrow, shaded, and littered with plastic crates and rotting cardboard boxes. Directly beneath them, parked close to the wall, was a white delivery van. The drop from the roof to the van’s metal roof was about twelve feet. 

Behind them, the wooden access door creaked violently. The steel crowbar Dave had wedged through the handles was beginning to bend, the wood around the lock splintering under the weight of the dead pressing from inside. 

"We have to jump," Dave said, turning to Sarah. "The van roof will break our fall. I'll go first."

"Dave, it's too high," Sarah whispered, backing toward the edge, her face pale. 

"We don't have a choice!" 

Dave scrambled over the brick parapet. He lowered himself, hanging by his fingers from the brick edge for a second, his boots dangling in the air. He let go. 

THUD.

Dave landed hard in the center of the van's roof. The thin sheet metal buckled inward with a loud, hollow boom, absorbing most of the impact. Dave rolled to the side, sliding off the hood onto the concrete floor of the alley. He stumbled, his knees barking with pain, but he stood up. 

"I'm good! Come on, Sarah! Lower yourself and drop!" Dave called out, his voice a harsh whisper. 

CRACK.

On the roof, the access door splintered. The turned manager and two other shamblers burst out onto the gravel, their milky eyes locking onto Sarah. 

Sarah let out a shriek. She scrambled over the brick parapet, her fingers clawing at the rough brick. The turned manager lunged, his hand scraping the gravel just inches from her face. 

She let go. 

In her panic, Sarah didn't drop straight. Her body twisted in the air, and her right boot caught the steel bar of the van's roof rack. The momentum flipped her sideways, sending her tumbling off the side of the vehicle. 

She hit the hard concrete of the alleyway with a sickening, wet crack. 

Sarah let out a sharp, ragged scream of pure agony, her body curling into a fetal position as she clutched her right leg. 

"Sarah!" Dave lunged forward, dropping to his knees beside her. 

Her right ankle was ruined, bent outward at a horrific, unnatural ninety-degree angle. The skin was stretched white over the fractured bone, blood starting to pool beneath the surface. She was shivering violently, her teeth grinding as she tried to stifle her screams. 

"I... I can't," she sobbed, her fingers digging into the dirt. "Dave, my leg... I can't stand."

From both ends of the narrow alleyway, the response was immediate. 

The loud crash of Dave's landing and Sarah’s scream had echoed off the brick buildings. From Murray Avenue and the riverfront park, the slow, shambling shapes of the dead turned. They entered the alleyway from both ends, their dragging footsteps closing the trap.

Chapter 7: The Slow Retreat

The alleyway exits were narrowing, the slow-moving dead shuffling in from both sides. Dave had to act. He threw the two empty plastic fuel cans onto the concrete; they were useless now. He kept the backpacks—the water bottles inside were too precious to lose. 

"Sarah, get on my back," Dave said, dropping to one knee in front of her. "Grab my shoulders. Tight."

Sarah sobbed, wrapping her arms around his neck, her knuckles white. She kept her wooden club clutched in her right hand. Dave stood up, his thighs screaming with the sudden, heavy load of her weight. 

He lunged forward, moving toward the southern exit of the alley. It was blocked by only two shamblers—a turned teenage girl in a school uniform and a man with his jaw hanging loose. 

Dave walked as fast as he could, his boots slapping the concrete. The heat in the shaded brick alleyway was suffocating, and sweat ran into his eyes, blinding him. 

The turned girl reached out, her fingers clawing at Sarah’s dangling foot. Sarah screamed, swinging her wooden club with a desperate, two-handed strike. The club struck the girl’s temple with a hollow crack, knocking her sideways against the brick wall. 

Dave used his elbow to push the second shambler out of the way, the dead man’s teeth snapping inches from his shoulder, before they burst out of the alley onto the side street. 

They were back in the blinding sunlight. The riverfront park lay just two blocks away. 

Static.

The small two-way radio at Dave’s hip crackled to life, the speaker spitting out a high, frightened whisper. 

"Dad? Dad, are you there?" 

Dave reached down, pressing the talk button, his breath coming in ragged gasps. "Billy. We're here. We're coming back. Code word: Billabong. Are you okay?"

"Dad, there are people on the pier," Billy whispered, his voice trembling. "They came out of the trees. They're... they're walking slowly toward the boat. I’m scared."

"Keep the doors locked, Billy! Do not make a sound. We’re almost there."

Dave let go of the button, his heart dropping. The dead were at the boat. 

He forced his exhausted legs to move, carrying Sarah across the dusty, sun-bleached grass of the park. The weight on his back felt like lead, and his chest burned with every breath. They rounded the final cluster of gum trees, and the wide, brown expanse of the Murray River came into view. 

The Lazy River was still floating, moored to the timber wharf. 

But the wooden jetty leading to the boat was no longer empty. A dozen shambling dead, drawn by the sound of the boat's engine earlier, had gathered on the narrow timber planks, blocking their path back to the safety of the river.

Chapter 8: The Pier Blockade

The wooden pier of Renmark was thirty yards long, its weathered gray planks extending out into the deep, muddy-brown river. Moored at the very end was The Lazy River. 

But the planks were covered. A dozen shamblers, their sunburned skin peeling and their clothes rags, wandered the narrow pier. Some had their arms hanging over the side, looking down at the water. They were blocking the only dry path back to the boat. 

Behind Dave and Sarah, the sounds of the town were growing louder. The zombies they had bypassed on Murray Avenue were entering the park, their dragging steps rustling the dry grass. 

"Dave," Sarah whispered, her chin resting on his sweaty shoulder. "We can't fight them on the pier. It's too narrow. If they crowd us, we'll fall into the river."

Dave looked down at the riverbank. The bank was a steep, clay slope that slid down into a thick, tangled wall of reeds and muddy shallows. 

"The water, Sarah," Dave said, his breath raspy. "The river is our only path. We go through the reeds, slip into the channel, and swim to the back deck of the boat. The current will help us."

"I can't swim, Dave," Sarah sobbed, her fingers digging into his collar. "Not with this ankle. The pain... if I try to kick, I'll pass out."

"I'll carry you in the water. Just hold onto my shoulders. We can do this."

Dave slid down the clay bank, his boots losing traction, and they slid five feet into the thick, dark mud at the water's edge. The reeds were tall, over six feet, enclosing them in a green, humid wall. The mud was deep, sucking at Dave's boots with a wet schlop every time he tried to lift his feet. 

Clack. Clack.

Directly above them, the zombies on the pier stopped. The sound of the sliding clay and the rustling reeds had drawn their attention. 

A turned woman in a yellow sundress walked to the edge of the pier. Her milky eyes rolled down, locking onto the movement in the reeds below. She let out a sharp, wet hiss. 

She didn't hesitate. She stepped over the edge of the pier. 

She fell ten feet, landing heavily in the mud with a wet splat. Her legs snapped under her, but she didn't feel it. She immediately began to crawl, her mud-caked hands clawing toward Dave's legs. 

From the bank behind them, three more shamblers began to slide down the clay slope, their bodies tumbling and rolling into the reeds. 

Dave was stuck. The thick, black river mud had swallowed his boots up to the shins, anchoring him in place as the dead closed in through the reeds.

Chapter 9: The Distraction

The mud was a living thing, sucking at Dave's boots, locking his shins in a cold, heavy vise. 

The turned woman in the yellow dress was crawling closer, her fingers digging into the muck, her teeth snapping inches from his trousers. From the clay bank behind them, three more shamblers rolled into the reeds, their bodies thrashing. 

"Dave, they're on us!" Sarah screamed, raising her wooden club, but she was pinned on his back. 

Dave looked at the river. Just three feet away, the reeds ended, and the dark, deep water of the river channel began. The current was slow, but it swept directly past the back swim platform of The Lazy River. 

He knew they wouldn't make it together. Not in the mud. 

"Sarah, listen to me," Dave said, his voice flat and urgent. He reached behind his back, unbuckling her arms. 

"Dave, no!" 

"I'm going to push you into the channel," Dave said, his hands locking under her arms. "You don't need to kick. Just float on your back. The current will carry you straight to the swim platform of the boat. Billy will see you. He’ll pull you up."

"What about you?" Sarah sobbed, grabbing his shirt. 

"I'm drawing them away. If they're looking at the water, they'll see you. I have to go up the bank."

With a final, desperate heave that popped his shoulder joint, Dave lifted Sarah, throwing her body over the wall of reeds into the deep, dark water of the river channel. 

Sarah hit the water with a splash. She sank for a second, then surfaced, coughing. She rolled onto her back, her face barely breaking the surface, her arms paddling weakly as the slow current began to carry her downriver. 

Dave turned back. The crawling woman’s hand closed around his ankle. 

He didn't try to pull his foot out of the mud. He reached down, unlaced his heavy hiking boots, and pulled his feet straight out of them, leaving the leather boots behind in the black muck. 

Barefoot, he scrambled up the slippery clay bank, his toes clawing at the mud. He emerged onto the grassy riverbank, completely exposed. 

He raised his steel crowbar, waving his arms, and began to scream at the top of his lungs. 

"Over here! Come on, you dead bastards! I'm right here!" 

The dozen shamblers on the pier stopped. The ones in the reeds turned. Drawn by the loud shouting on the open bank, they turned away from the water. They shambled slowly, rhythmically, toward the clay slope, climbing up the grass toward Dave. 

In the river, Sarah floated past the wooden pylons of the pier, keeping her body low, her head barely visible among the floating red gum leaves. The current carried her twenty yards downriver, guiding her straight to the white steel swim platform at the stern of The Lazy River. 

She grabbed the metal ladder, a sharp scream of agony tearing from her throat as the movement wrenched her fractured ankle. 

"Billy!" she cried, her voice cracking. "Billy, open the door! Billabong! Billabong!"

Inside the cabin, the glass door slid open with a sharp clack. Billy’s terrified face appeared. He reached down, grabbing his mother's wet hands, and together they hauled her shivering, exhausted body onto the white fiberglass deck. 

Sarah lay on the deck, gasping for air, her eyes locking onto the riverbank. 

Dave was running barefoot along the grassy edge, his shadow long in the setting sun, with twenty shamblers shuffling in a slow, relentless line behind him.

Chapter 10: The Riverbed

Dave ran. His bare feet cut on the sharp stones and dry spinifex of the riverbank, leaving a trail of dark red smears on the dirt, but he didn't feel it. Behind him, the low, vibrating drone of the dead was close, their dragging footsteps a constant, scraping rhythm. 

The bank ended. Directly ahead of him was a narrow timber jetty used by the local rowing club. It extended twenty yards out into the wide, brown expanse of the river. 

It was a dead end. 

Dave scrambled onto the jetty, his heels thumping on the gray planks. He reached the very end, where the water was deep and the current ran swift. He turned. 

The first of the shamblers—the turned council worker—stepped onto the jetty, his arms outstretched, his milky eyes locked on Dave. Behind him came a dozen more, filling the narrow wooden deck. 

The sun was sinking below the tree line, casting a blood-red light across the wide Murray River, painting the water in hues of crimson and black. 

Dave looked at the river. He took a deep, final breath, raised his steel crowbar, and dove headfirst into the deep channel. 

The water swallowed him, cold and silent. The light of the setting sun faded into a murky, brown darkness as he sank into the depths. 

Dave opened his eyes. He kicked, trying to swim back to the surface, intending to let the current carry him down to the boat. 

But the riverbed was not empty. 

Under the water, in the dark, silent silt of the river bottom, a pair of cold, white-filmed eyes snapped open. 

Several drowned bodies—civilians who had jumped from the bridge to escape the panic on Day 4, their lungs filled with river water—lay on the muddy bottom. They didn't rot normally in the cold water, and they didn't drown. They walked. 

A turned woman, her clothes covered in green river weed, reached up from the silt. 

As Dave kicked, her cold, bloated hands shot out of the darkness. Her fingers clamped onto his bare ankle with a crushing, dead grip. 

Dave gasped, a plume of silver bubbles escaping his lips. He thrashed, kicking wildly with his free leg, but the weight of his water-logged clothes and the suction of the mud below made it impossible to break the grip. 

A second set of hands reached out of the weeds, grabbing his waist, pulling him down into the dark, silent silt of the riverbed. 

Above, on the deck of The Lazy River, Sarah used her hunting knife to slice through the mooring ropes. The thick hemp parted with a wet snap. 

The houseboat, free from the wharf, began to drift slowly with the current, moving downriver away from the infested pier. 

Billy stood at the rear rail, the binoculars pressed to his eyes, tears streaming down his face as he looked back at the rowing jetty. 

"Mom," Billy sobbed, his voice cracking. "Dad jumped. I saw him jump near the jetty."

Sarah dragged herself to the rail, her fractured ankle throbbing with a sickening heat, and took the binoculars. She focused on the dark, red-lit water near the end of the jetty. 

A sudden, violent burst of bubbles broke the surface of the river. 

Then, the bubbles stopped. 

A second later, a hand broke the surface. 

It was Dave's hand. The fingers were curved into claws, scratching at the air with a slow, mechanical twitch. The current caught his body, rolling him over. His head broke the surface for a brief second—his eyes milky white, staring blankly at the red sky—before the weight of the water pulled him back under. 

The houseboat drifted silently down the river, carrying Sarah and Billy into the dark, empty forest, leaving the riverbed behind.

================================================================================

STORY 6: COOBER PEDY
--------------------

Chapter 1: The Heavy Air

The air inside the sandstone dugouts of Coober Pedy was thick, warm, and smelled of dry earth, old sweat, and stagnant carbon dioxide. Ninety days ago, this underground sanctuary—carved into the red desert stone to escape the blistering Outback heat—had seemed like a miracle. Now, it felt like a tomb. 

Marcus sat on a wooden crate in the main communal chamber, his eyes squinted in the dim glow of a battery-powered lantern. In his hand, he held a portable gas monitor. The digital screen flickered, displaying a number that made his jaw clench: CO2: 1.8%. 

Beside him, Dr. Patel leaned against the rough, hand-carved sandstone wall, letting out a dry, rattling cough. 

"We're losing pressure, Marcus," the elderly doctor said, his voice weak. "The ventilation is failing. The CO2 is rising by a decimal point every two hours. If we don't clear the main intake tower on the surface, the air in these lower chambers will turn lethal in less than forty-eight hours."

"The backup shafts are all sealed," Marcus muttered, running a rough hand over his stubbled chin. "The main shaft is the only one that draws enough air down. Something must be blocking the surface intake."

"Probably the wind," Patel sighed. "Or them."

"Them" was the only word they used for the shambling corpses that wandered the desert. In the scorching 45-degree heat of the South Australian outback, the zombies didn't rot normally. The dry heat mummified them, turning their skin into tough, leathery parchment. They didn't run, but they never stopped, and they were drawn to any source of cool air or noise. 

The heavy curtain at the entrance of the chamber was pulled back. Chloe stepped in, her boots clicking on the stone floor. She was a young miner, twenty-five, with her dark hair tied back in tight braids. She carried a heavy, double-sided pickaxe over her shoulder, and a worn rubber respirator hung around her neck. 

"The spiral shaft is clear, Marcus," Chloe said, checking the seal on her water canteen. "But the air up there is hot enough to burn your lungs. We need to move before the midday heat hits."

Marcus stood up, grabbing his own pickaxe from the wall. He buckled his tactical belt, securing a heavy wrench and his hunting knife. 

"Dr. Patel," Marcus said, looking at the doctor. "Seal the doors to the nursery and the elderly dugouts. Tell everyone to lay flat and move as little as possible. Conserve the air we have left."

"Good luck, son," Patel said, his hand trembling as he patted Marcus’s shoulder. 

Marcus and Chloe walked down the dark, narrow tunnel toward the service shaft—a vertical, sixty-foot chimney carved into the rock, with rusted metal rungs acting as a ladder leading up to the surface hatch.

Chapter 2: The Ascent

The air inside the vertical service shaft was even hotter than in the chambers below, rising in wavy, invisible currents from the depths of the mine. 

Chloe climbed first, her boots finding the narrow metal rungs built into the rough, pink sandstone wall. Marcus followed directly beneath her, his heavy pickaxe strapped to his back, his hands gripping the sweat-slicked metal. Every breath was a struggle, their lungs burning as they climbed higher, leaving the cool depths of the earth behind. 

"Rungs are loose up here!" Chloe called down, her voice echoing hollowly in the narrow, four-foot-wide chimney. 

"Keep going," Marcus grunted, his knuckles scraping against the abrasive sandstone. "Don't look down."

As they neared the top, the temperature spiked. The air coming down from the surface felt like the blast of an open oven. Sweat poured into Marcus’s eyes, stinging, and his shirt was soaked, sticking to his back. The sixty-foot climb felt like miles in the stagnant, oxygen-deprived air. 

At the top of the shaft, the ladder ended at a narrow concrete platform directly beneath the surface hatch—a circular plate of heavy, reinforced steel, locked with a central threaded wheel-bolt. 

Chloe hauled herself onto the platform, her chest heaving as she sat cross-legged, wiping her face with her sleeve. Marcus climbed up beside her, the space so tight their shoulders pressed together. 

"Listen," Chloe whispered, her eyes wide. She pointed up at the steel hatch. 

Marcus held his breath, leaning his head toward the metal plate. 

Through the thick steel, he heard a dry, scraping sound. It was the slide of leathery skin against the hot metal, accompanied by a faint, rhythmic clink-clink of fingernails scratching at the seams. 

"There's something sitting on the hatch," Chloe said, her fingers tightening around the handle of her pickaxe. "A walker."

"We can't clear the vent from down here, Chloe," Marcus said, his voice flat. "If we go back, we suffocate. We open the hatch, we put it down, and we make a run for the vent tower. Understand?"

Chloe swallowed hard, then nodded. She reached up, grabbing the heavy iron wheel-bolt in the center of the hatch. 

"Ready?" she asked. 

Marcus gripped his pickaxe with both hands, positioning himself at the edge of the ladder rungs, ready to swing. "Do it."

Chloe braced her feet and turned the wheel. The rusted thread groaned, then spun free with a sharp, metallic clack. She pushed the heavy steel plate upward, and the blinding, white-hot light of the South Australian desert blasted into the shaft.

Chapter 3: The Surface Hatch

The hatch pushed open, and the white-hot, blinding glare of the South Australian sun hit them. Along with the light came a wave of dry, searing heat that instantly sucked the moisture from Marcus’s lips. 

As the steel plate swung back, a heavy, leathery weight rolled off the top, sliding across the metal and landing in the red gravel with a dry, hollow thump. 

It was the walker they had heard. The desert sun had mummified it over the last ninety days. Its skin was blackened, stretched tight over its ribs like old parchment, and its lips were shriveled back, exposing yellowed teeth in a perpetual, silent snarl. It wore only a pair of faded denim shorts and a single, melted plastic sandal. 

The mummy rolled onto its chest, its joints cracking loudly in the heat as it tried to push itself up, its milky eyes locking onto Marcus’s boots. 

Marcus didn't hesitate. He scrambled out of the hatch, his boots crunching on the gravel, raised his double-sided pickaxe, and drove the pointed steel pick directly through the center of the mummified skull. 

CRUNCH.

The dry bone split like old wood. The mummy collapsed, silent and still. 

Chloe climbed out behind him, squinting in the glare. She grabbed the steel hatch and pulled it shut, turning the external wheel-bolt to lock it from the surface, preventing anything from stumbling down the shaft. 

They stood in the classic, scarred landscape of Coober Pedy. The earth was a flat expanse of red gravel and white clay, dotted with hundreds of cone-shaped mounds of dirt excavated from opal mining shafts. In the distance, the town’s main street was silent, a collection of rusted tin roofs and abandoned machinery shimmering in the heat waves. 

"The tower," Chloe said, pointing twenty yards to their left. 

The ventilation tower was a six-foot-tall concrete pipe rising from the gravel, capped with a steel mesh dome to filter out debris. A shallow, three-foot-deep concrete trench surrounded the base to divert flash-flood rainwater. 

The problem was immediately obvious. 

Five mummified bodies lay piled inside the narrow trench, their dried, leathery limbs tangled together. They had fallen in, probably drawn by the cool draft of the intake during the night, and had become wedged against the steel mesh. The suction of the massive intake fan below had pulled their dried clothes and bodies tight against the grate, completely sealing the airflow. 

As they watched, a low, whistling sound echoed from the tower, the fan below straining against the blockage. 

"We need to pull them out," Marcus said, walking toward the trench, his pickaxe held ready. "Chloe, grab the hook. We clear the grate first."

Before they could take a step, the gravel shifted behind a rusted dump truck parked nearby. 

The dry, rattling hum of dragging feet echoed in the heat.

Chapter 4: Clearing the Shaft

Marcus and Chloe dropped into the three-foot-deep concrete trench. 

The heat was suffocating, trapped within the concrete walls, and the draft from the massive intake fan below was like a furnace exhaust, drawing the hot desert air down with a low, vibrating hum. 

"Get the crowbar under his shoulder!" Marcus yelled over the roar of the fan, his respirator mask muffled. 

Chloe wedged the tip of her pickaxe beneath the armpit of a turned tourist whose dried, leathery body was jammed sideways against the steel mesh. The suction was strong, holding the corpse tight like a leaf against a pool drain. 

"One, two, three—heave!" Chloe grunted, leveraging the tool. 

With a wet, tearing sound, the mummy’s dried flesh parted from the steel mesh. Marcus grabbed the body by the collar of its sun-bleached polo shirt and hauled it out of the trench, throwing it onto the red gravel. 

They worked with frantic, exhausting speed. The sun beat down on their backs, and the sweat pooled inside their respirator masks, making it hard to breathe. One by one, they dragged the mummified corpses out: a truck driver, an elderly woman, a young boy. 

The physical toll was immense. Marcus's hands were raw, his fingers cramping around the handle of his pickaxe. 

Finally, only one remained—a turned opal miner, his chest pierced by a heavy steel drill-bit that had rusted into his ribs, wedging him firmly between the concrete wall and the intake grate. 

"Clear the drill-bit, Marcus!" Chloe gasped, leaning against the concrete wall, her face red, her breath coming in ragged pants. 

Marcus swung the flat side of his pickaxe, striking the rusted steel rod. 

CLANG.

The blow sheared the rusted mount. With a dry crack, the miner’s body released its grip on the grate. Marcus grabbed the collar of his uniform and hoisted the corpse out of the trench. 

Immediately, the whistling sound of the restricted intake stopped. It was replaced by a deep, clean, resonant roar as the massive fan below drew a huge column of air down into the subterranean dugouts. 

The airflow was restored. The people below would breathe. 

But the loud clanging of their tools and the roaring fan had done its job. 

Marcus climbed out of the trench, wiping the sweat from his eyes. He froze. 

Emerging from the shadow of the massive, rusted dump truck parked thirty yards away came the dead. Seven mummified shamblers, their skin dried to a dark cardboard color, their limbs cracking loudly as they moved. They shuffled in a loose, flanking line, their milky eyes locked on the two living miners. 

They were blocking the path back to the service hatch.

Chapter 5: The Ambush

"Hatch is blocked," Chloe said, her voice tight as she backed away from the concrete trench. "They're spread out too wide. If we try to run past them, they'll corner us in the open."

Marcus looked at the approaching dead. They walked with a slow, heavy drag, their bare feet cracking on the hot clay. But they were organized in their pursuit, their blank, milky eyes locked on the two living miners. Behind them, from the white clay hills, three more silhouettes appeared, descending toward the noise. 

"The truck," Marcus said, pointing to the massive, yellow Caterpillar dump truck parked thirty yards away. 

It was a twenty-ton behemoth, a relic of the mining operations that had been left to rust. The chassis sat high off the ground, the tires twice as tall as a man, with a steel catwalk and a heavy ladder leading up to the elevated cab. 

"Go!" Marcus yelled. 

They sprinted across the red gravel, their boots kicking up clouds of white clay dust. 

The mummified dead turned, shifting their course toward the truck. Their joints cracked loudly, a dry, popping sound like dry sticks snapping underfoot. 

Chloe reached the steel ladder of the dump truck, her pickaxe clattering against the metal as she scrambled up the rungs. Marcus followed right behind her, his breath a hot, dry rasp in his throat. 

As Marcus reached the third rung, a shadow detached itself from the massive, six-foot-tall rubber tire of the truck. 

It was a turned tourist, his skin dried to a dark cardboard color, wearing the remnants of a faded Hawaiian shirt. With a sudden, snapping lunge, he reached out, his bony fingers grabbing the leg of Marcus’s trousers. 

"Marcus!" Chloe screamed from the catwalk above. 

Marcus swung his free leg, driving his boot heel directly into the tourist's chest. 

CRUNCH.

The dried, hollow ribs collapsed under the impact. The zombie let out a dry whistle of air and fell backward onto the gravel, its fingers releasing his leg. Marcus scrambled up the remaining rungs, pulling himself onto the elevated metal catwalk. 

Chloe had already thrown open the heavy steel door of the truck cab. It was dusty, hot, and smelled of old grease, but it was empty. 

They scrambled inside, and Marcus slammed the heavy door shut, throwing the mechanical lock. 

Thump.

A second later, the first of the mummified dead reached the bottom of the ladder, his bony fingers scratching at the metal rungs. Below them, the rest of the pack gathered around the massive yellow chassis, their milky eyes rolling upward toward the cab. 

Inside, the heat was suffocating, the sun baking the metal cabin, but they were safe behind the reinforced glass and steel. For now.

Chapter 6: Pinned in the Dump Truck

Inside the cab, the heat was rising with terrifying speed. The metal roof of the Caterpillar dump truck acted like a greenhouse, trapping the midday sun and raising the temperature to a suffocating fifty degrees. Marcus pulled off his respirator mask, gasping for air, but the air he inhaled felt like liquid fire. 

Outside, the mummified dead surrounded the vehicle. 

One of them—a turned tourist whose skin was completely black and cracked from sunburn—reached the catwalk, his dry, leathery face pressing against the side window. He scratched at the glass, leaving a greasy streak, his jaw clicking pointlessly. 

"We need to get this thing moving," Chloe gasped, her face flushed red, sweat dripping from her nose. "If we stay here, we’ll bake to death in an hour."

Marcus scrambled to the driver's seat. He checked the dashboard. The keys were still hanging from the ignition column on a wire ring. 

"Come on," Marcus muttered, his hand shaking as he gripped the key. 

He turned it. 

The dashboard glowed weakly, the analog needles flickering. He pressed the heavy black starter button on the floorboards. 

Click. Click. Click.

The starter motor clicked weakly, but the massive diesel engine remained silent. 

"Dead battery," Marcus said, slamming his hand against the steering wheel. "It’s been sitting in the sun for three months. The charge is gone."

"So that's it?" Chloe sobbed, leaning her head against the dusty passenger window. "We're trapped in an oven."

"No," Marcus said, his eyes locking onto the metal hatch on the side of the chassis, visible from the catwalk window. "The battery box is under the catwalk. If we jump the starter solenoid terminals with a wrench, we can bypass the ignition and force the starter to turn. But someone has to go out there."

He looked through the window. The turned tourist was still scratching at the glass, and below the catwalk, three more shamblers were climbing the steel ladder, their bony hands reaching up. 

"I'll do it," Marcus said, grabbing a heavy, fifteen-inch steel wrench from the floor pocket. "I’ll slip out, jump the terminals, and you hit the starter button from inside. We run them over and get to the hatch."

Chloe grabbed his arm. "Marcus, the catwalk is narrow. If they grab you..."

"If I stay here, I die anyway," Marcus said. He gripped the door handle, took a deep breath, and pushed it open, stepping out into the blinding, white-hot glare.

Chapter 7: The Battery Box

Marcus stepped onto the metal grating of the catwalk, the intense heat radiating from the yellow steel baking through the soles of his boots. 

The turned tourist at the window spun, his dried, leathery face snarling. Marcus raised the fifteen-inch iron wrench and swung it with a short, powerful arc. 

CRUNCH.

The iron struck the side of the mummified skull, splitting the dry bone. The tourist collapsed, rolling off the catwalk and falling ten feet onto the gravel below. 

"Keep the door shut, Chloe!" Marcus shouted, dropping to his hands and knees on the narrow metal walkway. 

He crawled toward the side of the chassis, where the heavy, rectangular battery box was mounted. The metal cover was secured by two rusted T-handles. The heat of the metal burned his palms, but he ignored the pain, prying the handles upward. 

They were rusted solid. 

Clank. Clank.

From the bottom of the ladder, the turned miner in the green apron was climbing fast, his dry joints cracking with every step. Below him, the other mummified dead were clawing at the massive rubber tires, their bony fingers scratching uselessly. 

Marcus jammed the flat edge of his wrench under the battery cover, prying with all his weight. 

SCREECH.

The rusted metal gave way, the cover popping open to expose four massive, heavy-duty mining batteries. The terminals were covered in a thick layer of blue-green corrosion. 

He needed to bridge the main positive lead directly to the starter solenoid post to spark the starter motor. 

As he raised the wrench to bridge the terminals, a dry, clicking snarl sounded directly behind him. 

The turned miner had reached the top of the ladder. His bony hand shot through the gap in the safety rail, his fingers locking around Marcus’s left ankle with a crushing, dead grip. 

Marcus flinched, his leg locking. He tried to kick back, but the narrow catwalk offered no leverage, and his boot slipped on the smooth metal grating. 

"Chloe!" Marcus screamed, his chest pressed flat against the hot metal. 

The zombie pulled, dragging Marcus’s leg backward toward the ladder, its snapping teeth aiming for his boot.

Chapter 8: The Jump-Start

The door of the cab threw open. Chloe leaned out over the safety rail, her face tight with concentration, and swung her pickaxe downward. 

The sharp steel point pierced the back of the turned miner’s neck, severing the brainstem. The zombie’s fingers released Marcus's ankle, and it fell, slipping down the rungs into the gravel. 

"Marcus! Get back inside!" Chloe screamed, the heat of the open door hitting her. 

"No! Solenoid!" Marcus gasps, scrambling back to the open battery box. 

He positioned the heavy steel wrench across the corroded positive lead and the starter solenoid terminal post. 

ZAP.

A massive, blinding shower of bright blue sparks erupted, crackling loudly in the desert air. The metal of the wrench heated up instantly, the intense current blistering the skin of Marcus's palms, but he gritted his teeth and held it in place, his muscles locking from the heat. 

"Chloe! Hit the starter! Now!" 

Inside the cab, Chloe slammed her boot onto the black floorboard button. 

The starter motor groaned, drawing massive power directly from the bridged connection. With a deep, rattling cough, the massive 12-cylinder diesel engine of the Cat dump truck roared to life. A plume of black exhaust soot belched from the vertical pipes above the cab, and the entire chassis vibrated with a deafening, metallic rumble that shook the red gravel ground. 

The engine was running. 

But the roaring diesel had drawn the rest of the pack. 

As Marcus pulled the wrench off the terminals, breaking the connection, a turned mechanic in greasy overalls—who had climbed up the massive, six-foot rubber tire—lunged over the safety rail onto the catwalk. 

He landed directly on top of Marcus. 

Before Marcus could raise the wrench, the mechanic’s jaw clamped down onto his right shoulder. The teeth tore through the fabric of his shirt, sinking deep into the flesh. 

Marcus let out a guttural shriek of agony. 

He raised the iron wrench with his left hand and drove the flat side into the mechanic's temple. 

CRUNCH.

The zombie went limp, sliding off his shoulder. Marcus scrambled to his feet, clutching his bleeding collarbone, and threw himself into the cab, slamming the heavy steel door behind him. 

He locked it, falling back into the passenger seat, his chest heaving, his face white. 

Chloe stared at him, her eyes wide, her hand on the steering wheel. "Marcus... your shoulder."

Marcus looked down at his shirt. A dark, wet patch of crimson was already spreading across the blue fabric.

Chapter 9: The Run

"Marcus..." Chloe whispered, her hands shaking on the steering wheel. "Your shoulder. It's... it's a bite."

Marcus clutched his collarbone, the blood warm and sticky between his fingers. He felt a sudden, icy chill radiating from the wound, a cold front moving through his chest. The fever was starting. 

"I know," Marcus said, his voice flat. He reached over, grabbed the massive gear lever, and slammed it into reverse. "Move over. I'm driving."

Chloe scrambled into the passenger seat as Marcus took the wheel. He slammed his boot onto the accelerator. 

The twenty-ton dump truck surged backward, the giant, six-foot rubber tires rolling over the gravel. The mummified zombies below were crushed under the immense weight, the sound of their dried bones snapping like dry twigs under a boot. Marcus shifted into drive, steering the behemoth across the gravel yard. 

He drove toward the service hatch they had climbed out of. 

The hatch was located on a flat clay clearing, twenty yards from the ventilation tower. Marcus steered the truck carefully, lining up the massive rear chassis, and parked the behemoth directly over the circular steel plate, leaving just enough clearance under the side catwalk for someone to slide down. 

With the truck parked there, nothing on the surface could ever breach the hatch. The twenty-ton vehicle was an impenetrable block. 

Marcus shut the transmission, leaving the diesel engine idling with a deafening rumble. He reached down, unclipped the master keys of the service hatch from his belt, and handed them to Chloe. 

"Go," Marcus said, his eyes glazing over with a feverish sweat. "Go down the hatch. Lock it from the inside."

"Marcus, come with me," Chloe pleaded, tears running down her cheeks. "We have the medical bay. We can—"

"No, Chloe," Marcus interrupted, his voice dropping to a harsh whisper. "You know the rules. I'm infected. In a few hours, the fever will kill me. If I'm down there when I turn, I'll kill Dr. Patel, the kids, everyone. I'm staying up here."

"Marcus..."

"That's an order, Chloe. Go. Tell Patel the vent is clear. The air is coming down."

Chloe stared at him, her chest heaving. She knew he was right. There was no cure. 

She took the keys, threw open the passenger door, and stepped onto the catwalk. The hot wind hit her, carrying the smell of the burning diesel exhaust. She climbed down the steel ladder, dropped onto the gravel beneath the truck’s massive chassis, and knelt by the steel hatch. 

She shoved the key into the lock, turned the wheel-bolt, and pulled the heavy steel plate open. 

She looked up through the gaps in the truck’s metal underbelly, seeing Marcus’s silhouette in the elevated cab.

Chapter 10: The Tomb

Chloe slid down the hatch into the dark, vertical service shaft. She grabbed the heavy steel plate, pulled it shut, and spun the internal locking wheel until the steel bolts locked into the rock frame. 

She was back in the cool, dark chimney. The draft coming down from the surface was no longer stagnant; it was a strong, cool wind, rushing past her face into the depths of the mine. The ventilation was working. 

She climbed down the metal rungs, her boots clanking in the dark. At the bottom of the shaft, Dr. Patel was waiting, holding a lantern. 

"Chloe," the doctor said, his voice raspy. "We felt the draft. The CO2 is dropping. We can breathe."

He looked past her, his eyes searching the dark shaft. "Where is Marcus?"

Chloe hit the stone floor, her knees buckling. She let out a dry, silent sob, her shoulders shaking. "He... he stayed up. He parked the Cat truck over the hatch. Nothing is getting down here, Doctor."

"And Marcus?" Patel asked, his face falling. 

"He's gone," Chloe whispered. 

On the desert surface, Marcus sat in the driver's seat of the yellow dump truck. 

The sun was sinking below the white clay hills of Coober Pedy, painting the empty desert in a blood-red glow. The heat of the day was finally starting to break, but Marcus didn't feel the cool wind. His body was shivering violently, wrapped in a freezing, internal winter. 

He reached out, his blistered fingers trembling, and turned the key. 

The massive diesel engine spluttered and died, the deafening rumble replaced by the vast, heavy silence of the outback. 

Outside, the mummified dead crawled over the massive rubber tires, their bony fingers scratching uselessly at the steel doors of the cab. A turned surveyor pressed his mummified face against the passenger glass, his jaw clicking, but the reinforced window didn't budge. 

Marcus leaned his head back against the vinyl seat. His chest rose in a shallow, final breath. 

His heart stopped. His head rolled to the side. 

For three minutes, the cab was silent, the only sound the scratching of the dead outside. 

Then, Marcus’s fingers twitched against the steering wheel. 

His eyelids snapped open. 

The eyes that stared out at the red desert sunset were completely milky white, vacant and dead. His jaw unhinged, letting out a low, dry grunt of hunger as his fingers began to scratch at the glass from the inside of the locked cab. 

Beneath the twenty-ton truck, the community slept in the cool sanctuary of the earth, safe, but forever sealed in their sandstone tomb.

================================================================================

STORY 7: GORDON DAM
-------------------

Chapter 1: The Arch

The Gordon Dam rose like a colossal, curved gray tombstone out of the misty wilderness of Southwest Tasmania. For one hundred and eighty days, the world had been dying, but here, in the cold, wet heart of the Gordon-Splinter Forest, it felt as though it had already passed. 

Brody stood at the western abutment, his fingers frozen inside thin, grease-stained work gloves. The wind, howling off Lake Gordon, carried the scent of rotting vegetation and damp granite. Below him, the concrete wall bowed in a sweeping arch, a vertical drop of one hundred and forty meters into the black, jagged jaws of the Gordon River gorge. 

"Visibility is getting worse," Tara muttered, pulling the collar of her oilskin coat tighter around her neck. She stood near the safety railing, peering through a pair of binoculars. The lenses were fogged, and she wiped them with a dirty thumb. "The mist is rolling up from the valley. I can’t see the eastern end of the wall anymore."

"Doesn't matter if we can see it or not," Sergeant Harris said. He was leaning against the steel door of the western gatehouse, his hands resting on the grip of a bolt-action rifle. A weathered, graying man, Harris had spent twenty years in the police force before the world turned. Now, his eyes were hollow, reflecting the cold gray of the Tasmanian sky. "The dead don't care about the mist. They'll follow the road. They always follow the road."

"Are we sure the transmission lines are still intact?" Brody asked, his voice shaking slightly from the cold. He was only twenty-four, a mechanical engineer who had been hired at the power station just three months before the collapse. Now, he was the only person left who knew how the massive turbines deep inside the rock actually functioned. "If the lines to the Huon Valley sanctuary are down, we're doing this for nothing."

"They're intact," Harris replied, his voice firm, though there was a trace of desperation in it. "The radio tower in the valley confirmed they're getting the signal. The emergency grid is primed. But they need juice. They've got three hundred survivors in the settlement, and their diesel generators died last week. If we don't get these turbines spinning, they won't survive the winter. They'll freeze, or the dark will let the dead slip past their walls."

Brody looked down at the massive concrete intake structure jutting out of the dark water on the lake side of the dam. The water was still—too still. Usually, the intake pulled thousands of cubic meters of water down the penstocks to feed the underground power station. 

But today, the water was a mirror. The turbines were silent. 

"We need to clear the screens," Brody said, pointing toward the intake gantry crane. "If the trash rakes don't clear whatever is blocking the grates, we won't get enough flow to start the turbines."

"Then let's get to work," Harris said, racking the bolt of his rifle. "Tara, keep your eyes on the eastern road. Brody, get to the winch."

They began to walk out onto the narrow concrete crest of the dam, their boots hollow against the concrete. The wind whipped at their clothes, threatening to pull them into the black abyss on either side.

Chapter 2: The Blocked Intake

The intake structure of the Gordon Dam was a massive concrete tower rising from the dark waters of the reservoir, connected to the dam crest by a narrow steel catwalk. Brody led the way, his hands gripping the freezing metal handrails. Every gust of wind shook the catwalk, sending a shudder through his boots.

He reached the control platform of the trash rake—a heavy, motorized crane designed to lower giant metal claws along the intake screens to scrape away logs and debris. 

Brody unlocked the control box, the rusted hinges groaning. He flipped the master switch. A low hum vibrated through the metal casing, and a red light flickered to life. 

"Power's up," Brody said, his breath fogging in the cold air. "I'm lowering the rake."

He pushed the lever forward. The winch groaned, steel cables unwinding as the heavy rake slid down the vertical guide rails into the dark water of Lake Gordon. For a moment, there was only the hum of the electric motor and the splash of water. Then, the cables went slack. The motor whined, straining, before a circuit breaker tripped with a loud clack. 

"Jammed," Brody muttered, resetting the breaker. "It's only down ten meters."

Harris walked to the edge of the platform and shone a heavy tactical flashlight down into the reservoir. The beam sliced through the murky water, illuminating the concrete intake screens. 

Tara gasped, stepping back. "Oh, God."

Brody leaned over the rail, squinting. 

Beneath the surface, pinned against the metal trash grates by the immense suction of the lake's natural pressure, was a tangled wall of human bodies. They were refugees from the early days of the collapse—hundreds of them who had tried to flee into the rugged wilderness of the Southwest National Park. Many had tried to cross the dam, only to be pushed over the edge, or had drowned trying to swim the cold reservoir. 

In the freezing, oxygen-poor depths of Lake Gordon, the bodies hadn't decomposed. They were gray, bloated, and preserved like waxen figures. 

And they were moving. 

Dozens of waterlogged heads turned toward the light. Their pale, milky eyes stared blankly through the water. Hands with missing fingers clawed weakly at the steel bars of the intake screens. Jaws snapped, releasing bubbles of trapped gas that floated to the surface. 

"They're wedged in the screens," Brody said, his voice trembling. "The suction has pulled them so tight against the steel bars that the rake can't slide past them. It's locked up."

"Can we clear them?" Harris asked, his face pale but expressionless. He had seen too much death to be shocked, but the sight of hundreds of drowned, snapping corpses under the dark water was a new horror. 

"I have to override the rake's safety limit and force the claw down," Brody said, looking at the control panel. "But if the motor burns out, we're done. Or... someone has to go down to the lower platform with a pole and push the bodies free of the guide rails."

"We don't have time for that," Tara called out from the main dam crest. Her voice was sharp with panic. "Look at the road!"

Chapter 3: The Valley Horde

Through the shifting curtains of gray mist, the eastern end of the dam wall was no longer empty. 

A single, dark figure emerged from the fog, dragging its left leg. Then another. Then five more. Within seconds, a dense, shuffling mass of bodies began to pour out of the forest road, stepping onto the narrow concrete crest of the dam. 

"How many?" Harris asked, his voice low as he walked back to the main wall, his rifle raised.

"Too many," Tara said, her hand shaking as she held her binoculars. "They must have been drawn by the sound of our truck, or the hum of the gantry crane. They're coming from the Strathgordon camp. The whole place must have turned."

The Gordon Dam crest was a two-lane road, barely six meters wide, bordered on both sides by concrete barriers and steel railings. The horde filled the road from edge to edge, a slow-moving, choked corridor of the dead. Some were clad in heavy wilderness parkas, others in the high-visibility vests of forestry workers, their flesh torn and blackened by frostbite and decay. 

They moved with the agonizing, halting gait of the Romero dead. There was no running, no screaming. Only the dry, collective scraping of thousands of boots on concrete, and a low, guttural wheezing that drifted across the reservoir. 

"They're a bottleneck," Harris said, his tactical mind assessing the situation. "With the drop on both sides, they can only come at us a few at a time. But there's no end to them. If they reach the western gatehouse, they'll block our only way down to the power station."

"How long do I have?" Brody called out, his hands working frantically to rewire the trash rake's control panel, bypassing the safety sensors. 

"Ten minutes before they reach the gatehouse gates," Harris said. He walked toward the heavy metal security gates at the western end of the wall, where the road met the solid rock of the mountain. "Tara, get the secondary rifle. We hold them at the gate. Brody, get those damn turbines started!"

"I'm working on it!" Brody shouted back. He grabbed a pair of pliers, cutting the wire that governed the automatic weight limit. The gantry crane was old, and the override meant he was forcing the electric motor to run at its absolute maximum capacity. 

On the dam wall, the first of the dead had reached the halfway point. One zombie, wearing a tattered Tasmanian Parks backpack, tripped over a crack in the concrete. The dead behind it didn't stop. They stepped on its back, crushing its ribcage, until the weight of the crowd pushed the fallen corpse over the edge. It plummeted silently through the mist, a tiny speck falling one hundred and forty meters before hitting the rocky riverbed below with a distant, sickening smack.

The rest kept coming.

Chapter 4: The Gatehouse Defense

Crack!

Harris's rifle barked, the sound echoing like a thunderclap between the sheer rock walls of the gorge. Fifty meters away, a zombie in a faded flannel shirt collapsed, its head jerking back as the bullet shattered its skull. It fell, instantly becoming an obstacle for the dead behind it.

Crack!

Beside him, Tara fired her lighter sporting rifle. A female zombie wearing a rusted headlamp took the round in the chest, stumbling but continuing to shuffle forward, its dead eyes locked on the gatehouse.

"Headshots, Tara!" Harris yelled over the wind. "Don't waste the ammo on their chests. They don't bleed out. You have to destroy the brain!"

"I'm trying!" Tara gasped, chambering another round. Her hands were shaking so hard she dropped a brass cartridge onto the concrete. It rolled off the edge, disappearing into the mist. "They're just... they're so close."

The gatehouse at the western end of the dam was a solid concrete bunker that housed the controls for the spillway gates and the entrance to the elevator shaft. Harris and Tara had closed the chain-link security gates, locking them with a heavy steel chain. But the chain-link fence was only a temporary barrier. 

The first wave of the dead reached the gate. Their cold, gray fingers slipped through the metal mesh, clawing at the air. Their faces pressed against the wire, distorting their features into grotesque masks of decaying flesh. 

Crack!

Harris fired point-blank into the face of a man who had been a surveyor, the bullet passing through the eye socket. The body went limp, hanging from the fence, held up by the pressure of the crowd behind it. 

"They're piling up!" Tara screamed. 

The weight of the dead was immense. With thousands of bodies shuffling forward from the eastern side of the dam, the force was cumulative. The steel posts of the gate began to groan, bending inward. The concrete anchor points cracked, releasing tiny puffs of stone dust. 

"Brody!" Harris roared, not looking back. "We can't hold this gate! The collective weight is going to tear the fence right out of the rock!"

Brody didn't answer. He was kneeling beside the winch motor on the intake platform, his hands covered in grease and copper wire. He could hear the gunshots, and he could hear the terrifying, metallic groaning of the gatehouse fence. He knew that if the fence gave way, the horde would sweep over the gatehouse, sealing the elevator and the stairs. 

"Just two more minutes!" Brody screamed back, his voice cracking. "I have to force the winch cycle!"

Chapter 5: The Winch Run

The electrical override hadn't been enough. The control box hummed and buzzed, but the winch drum remained locked. 

"The mechanical brake," Brody muttered, his stomach dropping. "It's rusted solid. It won't release automatically."

He looked at the gantry crane. The winch drum was located at the very end of the crane's steel boom, extending ten meters out over the dark, freezing waters of the reservoir. To reach it, he would have to climb out onto the narrow, wet steel girders of the crane's arm. There were no handrails out there—only the cold Tasmanian wind and a direct drop into the lake.

"Harris! I have to go out on the boom!" Brody yelled.

"Do it!" Harris fired another round. The gate was leaning at a thirty-degree angle now. "We're running out of lead!"

Brody scrambled up the ladder of the gantry crane, his boots slipping on the wet metal rungs. He reached the base of the boom. The wind here was a physical force, screaming off the lake, pushing against him like a solid hand. 

He gripped the cold steel girder and swung his leg over. He began to crawl forward on his hands and knees along the top of the narrow I-beam. 

Directly below him, the dark water of Lake Gordon churned. Ten meters down, he could see the pale, bloated faces of the drowned dead, their arms waving in the currents, their jaws snapping as if they could taste his sweat in the wind. On his left, the sheer face of the dam dropped one hundred and forty meters into the rocky canyon. One slip, and he would either drown in the freezing, zombie-infested lake or shatter on the rocks below.

"Don't look down," Brody whispered to himself, his teeth chattering. "Don't look down."

He dragged himself forward, inch by inch. His fingers were so cold they felt like wooden blocks. He reached the end of the boom, where the heavy cable drum sat. The mechanical brake was a thick steel lever locked in place by a rusted pin. 

Brody pulled his heavy iron pipe wrench from his belt. His hands were trembling so badly he almost dropped it. He gripped the wrench with both hands and slammed it against the rusted pin. 

Clang!

The sound echoed across the water. The pin didn't budge. 

Behind him, he heard a loud, ripping sound of tearing metal. The gatehouse fence was beginning to fail. 

"Come on!" Brody screamed, raising the wrench again. He swung with all his remaining strength, hammering the pin. 

Clang!

The pin sheared off, flying into the mist. The brake lever snapped back with a violent snap, and the cable drum began to spin, the steel cable screaming as it rapidly unwound.

Chapter 6: The Whine of the Turbines

Brody scrambled backward along the wet steel I-beam, his chest pressing flat against the metal as the gantry crane shook. Below, the heavy steel rake claw had plunged deep into the water, striking the wall of corpses with a muffled, watery impact. 

He slid off the boom and dropped onto the control platform. The electric motor was smoking, a thin wisp of acrid black smoke rising from the housing. Brody grabbed the manual override lever and slammed it into reverse. 

The motor shrieked, a high-pitched whine that set Brody's teeth on edge. The steel cables tautened, hummed like guitar strings, and then began to wind back onto the drum. 

Through the clear surface of the water, Brody watched the heavy steel claws scrape upward along the intake grates. The claws tore through the wall of bloated, waxy corpses. Limbs were severed, chests ripped open. The sudden, violent disruption broke the seal of bodies pressed against the grates. Without the collective barrier blocking the flow, the immense suction of the dam's intake penstocks took over. 

The remaining waterlogged dead were pulled violently through the grates or dragged down into the deep bypass channels. The water around the intake began to swirl, forming a dark, foaming whirlpool.

"It's flowing!" Brody yelled, pointing at the vortex. "The intake is clear!"

Beneath their feet, deep within the solid quartzite mountain where the power station's cavern sat, a low, subsonic rumble began. It started as a vibration in the soles of their boots, a deep hum that traveled up through the concrete foundation of the dam. 

The rumble grew louder, transforming into a massive, metallic roar as thousands of tons of water rushed down the vertical penstocks, striking the runner blades of the Francis turbines. 

Inside the gatehouse, the dark control panels suddenly came alive. Indicator lights flashed green. Analogue needles swung wildly to the right. The digital voltage meters began to climb, stabilizing at 220 kilovolts. 

"The generators are online!" Tara cried out, looking back through the gatehouse window. "We did it! The power is grid-locked!"

"Harris! We got power!" Brody shouted, running toward the catwalk. 

But his victory was cut short by the sound of tearing metal. 

The primary support post of the chain-link gate snapped at the base, the concrete holding it crumbling into gravel. The steel chain holding the gates shut shattered, links flying like shrapnel. 

The gatehouse defense had failed.

Chapter 7: The Gate Fails

The collapse of the gate was not a sudden crash, but a slow, crushing fold. The metal posts bent like warm wax under the relentless pressure of hundreds of bodies pushing from behind. 

With a final, metallic snap, the gate swung inward. 

The horde poured into the gatehouse courtyard. The dead at the front were crushed and trampled, their limbs snapping under the feet of the ones behind them, but they didn't care. They kept shambling forward, their mouths open in silent, hungry snarls. 

"Fallback!" Harris roared, firing his last three rounds into the center of the mass. He tossed the empty rifle aside and drew a heavy semi-automatic pistol from his holster. "Back to the wall! Now!"

Tara fired one last shot, her bullet grazing a zombie's cheek, before she turned and ran. Brody met them at the base of the gantry catwalk. 

Behind them, the gatehouse was completely overrun. The dead swarmed the entrance to the elevator shaft and the stairs leading down the mountain to where their escape truck was parked. The exit was gone. They were cut off. 

"We're trapped," Tara gasped, her eyes wide with terror as she looked at the swarming courtyard. "We can't get back to the valley."

"We go across," Harris said, pointing toward the eastern end of the dam wall. 

"But the other horde is coming from that way!" Brody said. 

He was right. Through the swirling mist, the eastern group of zombies—now numbering in the hundreds—was still shuffling across the narrow crest road of the dam. They had reached the center of the arch. 

Brody, Harris, and Tara were stuck in the middle. Behind them, the gatehouse horde was spilling out onto the western side of the dam wall. In front of them, the eastern horde was closing in. 

The dam wall was only six meters wide. To their left was the sheer, 140-meter vertical drop into the rocky riverbed of the gorge. To their right was the freezing, deep water of Lake Gordon, where the whirlpool of the intake was still spinning. 

"We don't have a choice," Harris said, his face hardening as he reloaded his pistol. "Move to the center. Use the crane base as a barrier. We fight them in the middle."

Chapter 8: Pinned on the Catwalk

The space on the narrow concrete arch was shrinking rapidly. From the west, the gatehouse horde spilled outward, a choking wave of grasping hands and hollow eyes. From the east, the secondary horde shuffled forward, blocking the road to the mainland. 

Brody, Harris, and Tara retreated to the center of the arch, where the massive steel frame of the gantry crane straddled the road. 

"Up the ladder!" Harris shouted, firing his pistol at the nearest zombie—a drowned woman whose waterlogged skin was peeling from her skull. The bullet took her in the forehead, and she fell backward, her body tumbling over the concrete barrier into the gorge. 

Tara scrambled up the iron ladder leading to the crane's elevated maintenance catwalk, which sat five meters above the road. Brody followed right behind her, the metal steps freezing under his hands. Harris came last, firing downward with his free hand until the pistol clicked empty on an empty chamber. 

"I'm dry!" Harris growled, holstering the useless weapon and pulling a heavy steel crowbar from the crane's tool rack. 

They stood on the narrow, grated catwalk of the crane. Below them, the two waves of the dead met. 

The sound was horrifying—the wet, packing crunch of cold bodies colliding, bones snapping as the sheer pressure of the two hordes compressed them against each other in the bottleneck. The dead didn't feel pain; they simply mashed together, their arms reaching upward toward the catwalk where the survivors stood. 

The grated floor of the catwalk allowed Brody to look straight down. Hundreds of gray, rotting hands poked through the gaps in the steel mesh, clawing at their boots. The smell of the dead—rotting fish, stagnant lake water, and decayed flesh—rose through the grates, choking them.

"They're climbing the frame!" Tara screamed, pointing at the massive steel legs of the crane. 

The zombies weren't intelligent, but they were relentless. As the crowd packed tighter at the base of the crane, the bodies piled on top of one another. The dead at the bottom were crushed into a mound of broken limbs, and the ones behind them used the pile as a ramp, dragging themselves up the steel struts of the crane.

A zombie wearing a torn yellow rain jacket reached the level of the catwalk. Its dead eyes locked onto Tara. 

Crack!

Harris swung the crowbar, shattering the zombie's temple. The body fell back, but another immediately took its place, its cold, wet fingers wrapping around the metal railing. 

"We need to get to the cab!" Brody said, pointing to the enclosed steel operator's cabin situated at the top of the crane structure, another three meters up. "It's reinforced steel! We can lock ourselves in!"

"Go!" Harris yelled, slamming the crowbar into another skull. "Tara, move!"

Tara turned toward the vertical ladder leading to the cabin. But as she stepped forward, a gray, waterlogged hand shot through the gap in the railing. 

It gripped her ankle.

Chapter 9: Dropping Like Flies

Tara screamed, her boot slipping on the wet metal grates. The waterlogged hand gripped her ankle with terrifying, cold strength. She fell backward, her spine hitting the steel railing of the catwalk with a dull thud. 

"Harris! Brody!" she shrieked, kicking wildly with her free leg. 

From below the catwalk, a second zombie—a bloated body with its face half-eaten by lake fish—lunged forward, its jaws clamping shut on the flesh of her calf. Tara let out a piercing, agonized shriek as blood sprayed across the gray steel.

"No!" Harris roared. He lunged forward, swinging the crowbar down onto the skull of the zombie holding her ankle. The bone cracked, and the grip released, but it was too late. 

Three more zombies had crawled up the steel legs of the crane. They threw their weight onto the railing, their cold hands grabbing Tara’s coat, her hair, her arms. Under their collective weight, the rusted safety railing of the catwalk groaned and snapped. 

With a deafening tear of metal, the railing gave way. 

Tara, along with the cluster of dead clawing at her, tumbled over the edge. She fell into the gray, swirling mist. Her scream echoed down the sheer face of the dam, growing fainter and fainter until it was silenced by the distant, crushing impact on the rocks below. 

"Tara!" Brody cried, his stomach turning. 

Harris didn't have time to mourn. As he watched Tara fall, a zombie in a forestry uniform lunged from the ladder behind him. Teeth sank deep into the meat of Harris’s shoulder. Another zombie grabbed his jacket, teeth tearing into the side of his neck. 

Harris gasped, his eyes widening. He slammed the crowbar into the face of the zombie biting his neck, breaking its jaw, but the damage was done. Blood gushed from his collarbone, staining his oilskin coat a dark, hot crimson. 

He stumbled back, leaning against the broken edge of the catwalk. He looked at Brody, his face pale, his breath rattling. 

"Harris..." Brody took a step toward him, but Harris held up a blood-slicked hand.

"They bite you, you die," Harris whispered, his voice thick with blood. "You die, you get back up. That's the rules."

"I can help you—"

"No," Harris said, looking down at his chest, then at the vertical ladder leading to the crane cabin. "I'm not turning on this wall. I'm not becoming one of them."

He dropped the crowbar. It clattered against the grates. 

Harris looked at Brody one last time. "Get to the cabin, kid. Lock the door."

Without waiting for Brody to answer, Harris turned and threw himself over the broken edge. 

He didn't fall into the gorge. He plunged into the deep, dark water of Lake Gordon, disappearing beneath the churning foam near the intake vortex. Brody knew that in the freezing depths, Harris would drown, and his body would reanimate, joining the silent, snapping legion pinned against the grates. But he wouldn't be on the wall. 

Brody didn't look back. He scrambled up the final ladder, the dead scratching at his heels. He shoved the steel door of the crane cabin open, threw himself inside, and slammed it shut, sliding the heavy iron bolt into place.

Chapter 10: The Single Light

The cabin of the gantry crane was a cramped, steel cell. It smelled of old grease, hydraulic fluid, and Brody's own terrified, gasping breath. 

Thud.

A body slammed against the heavy steel door. Outside, the scraping began—fingernails on metal, a dry, rhythmic scratching that vibrated through the steel walls. 

Brody backed away from the door, his heart hammering against his ribs. He fell back into the operator's leather seat. Through the reinforced glass windows of the cabin, he could see them. The dead had climbed the ladders. They packed the maintenance catwalks, their bodies pressed tight against the glass. 

A face smeared against the front pane—a young woman, her nose flattened against the glass, her jaw hanging open at an unnatural angle. Her cloudy, milky eyes stared directly at Brody. She began to slap her palms against the glass, leaving greasy streaks of decay. 

Thud. Thud.

More of them joined her. The glass was thick, designed to withstand Tasmanian winter storms, but the cumulative pressure of dozens of bodies leaning against it was terrifying. A tiny, hairline fracture appeared in the corner of the front window, spiderwebbing slowly outward. 

Brody squeezed his eyes shut, pulling his knees to his chest. He was alone. Harris was gone. Tara was gone. Everyone who had come with him from the valley was dead. 

Suddenly, a console on the dashboard beeped. A row of indicator lights flickered, switching from dull red to bright, glowing green. The cabin's overhead bulb flared to life, casting a harsh, yellow light over the controls. 

The hydro generators were at full capacity. The transmission lines were hot. 

Brody opened his eyes and looked out the side window, wiping away the condensation with his sleeve. 

To the east, past the curved concrete arch of the dam, the dense, black canopy of the Tasmanian wilderness stretched out for miles. It was a dark, lifeless sea of trees under a starless sky. 

But far on the horizon, nestled in the fold of the Huon Valley, a point of light flickered. 

Then another. 

Within seconds, a warm, electric grid of orange and white lights bloomed in the distance. The valley sanctuary—the last beacon of civilization in Tasmania—had lit up. The powerful halogen floodlights along their perimeter fences cut through the forest, securing their borders. The heat grids in their communal halls warmed the three hundred souls huddled inside. 

They were safe. The power had restored their lives. 

Brody watched the distant lights glow, a tear tracing a clean line through the grease on his cheek. 

Directly in front of him, the crack in the reinforced glass spread with a soft tink. A cold draft of wind slipped through the fracture, carrying the smell of the dead. 

The woman zombie slapped the glass again. The spiderweb pattern widened. The glass groaned, bending inward. 

Brody sat in the warm glow of the console lights, his eyes locked on the distant, shining valley, listening to the glass begin to shatter.

================================================================================

STORY 8: THE PILBARA PIT
------------------------

Chapter 1: The Red Pit

The midday heat of the Pilbara was a physical weight, thick with iron dust that coated the throat and burned the eyes. Day two hundred and seventy of the collapse, and the red expanse of Western Australia felt like another planet. 

Jase crouched at the edge of the secondary crusher platform, thirty meters above the pit floor. He was twenty-eight, his face lined with sunburn and grease, wearing a faded high-visibility shirt stiff with sweat. He lifted a plastic jerrycan, shaking it. A hollow, sloshing sound echoed. 

"Less than two liters," Jase said, his voice raspy. "For the three of us. We won't last another forty-eight hours up here, Kev. The heat will kill us before the dead do."

Kev sat on an upturned grease bucket, cleaning the action of a pump-action shotgun with an oily rag. He was fifty, a former site safety supervisor who had spent half his life in remote mines. His skin was leather, his gray beard trimmed short. 

"We're not dying of thirst, Jase," Kev said without looking up. "Not when there's an entire airfield three kilometers west. But we aren't walking there either."

He gestured toward the open-cut pit below. 

The mine was a vast, terraced amphitheater of red earth, nearly two kilometers wide. Spread across the tiered benches and the dusty floor of the pit were thousands of figures. In the dry, scorching 48-degree heat of the Pilbara, the dead didn't rot normally. They had mummified. Their skin was stretched tight and leathery over their bones, stained the same deep rust-red as the iron ore. 

They shuffled aimlessly, their boots dragging in the dust. There were former dump truck drivers, drillers, geologists, and FIFO contract workers who had been trapped here when the evacuation flights stopped. They moved with a slow, mechanical gait, their heads hanging low until a sudden sound or movement made them raise their pale, desiccated faces. 

"Sarah's still trying to ping the comms tower," Jase said, looking toward the small steel switchroom at the back of the platform. "If the autonomous grid is completely dead, we won't even be able to release the parking brakes on the CAT 797. The onboard computers will keep it locked down."

"She'll get it," Kev said, standing up and racking the shotgun. The sharp clack-clack of the slide was loud in the dry air. 

Below, in the pit, a dozen red-dusted heads snapped up in unison. They turned toward the crushing plant, their milky eyes staring toward the platform. They began to shuffle toward the steel stairs, their dry joints creaking in the silence. 

"We need to move," Kev muttered, checking his holster. "Before they start crowding the base of the tower. Jase, get your tools. We're going down to the workshop."

Chapter 2: The Silent Giants

The descent down the steel grating of the crushing plant was an exercise in absolute silence. Every step had to be measured, every handhold carefully placed to avoid a metallic clang that would alert the pit. 

Sarah met them at the bottom of the ladder. She was thirty, the mine's primary systems coordinator. Her short dark hair was damp with sweat, and she carried a ruggedized laptop in a dust-proof case slung over her shoulder. 

"The local wireless network is still broadcasting, but it's faint," Sarah whispered, her face pale. "I can see the haul fleet status on my monitor. The truck in Bay 4 is our best bet. Its fuel tanks are eighty percent full. But the autonomous lock is active. It thinks there’s an unsafe obstacle in the path—which is true, considering the thousands of dead out there."

They slipped through the side door of the primary maintenance workshop—a hangar so large it could house four two-story haul trucks side by side. 

In the center of Bay 4 stood the CAT 797. 

It was a mechanical mountain of steel. The tires alone were four meters tall, twice the height of a man. The dump bed was raised slightly, throwing a massive shadow across the concrete floor. A boarding ladder led up to the main deck and the cabin, which sat nearly seven meters above the ground. 

"She's beautiful," Jase whispered, climbing the steel access ladder to the engine platform. 

He popped the latch on the massive fiberglass engine cowling, revealing the twenty-cylinder diesel engine. The air inside the bay smelled of grease and stale air. 

"Batteries are drained," Jase called down quietly, looking at the voltage indicator. "The computer systems must have stayed on standby until the cells went dry. We need two heavy-duty industrial batteries to jump-start the main computer board, or we aren't going anywhere."

"Where are the spares?" Kev asked, his eyes scanning the dark corners of the hangar. The workshop was filled with massive toolboxes, racks of spare parts, and giant crane hooks hanging from the ceiling. 

"In the electrical storage donga, across the yard," Jase said. 

Before Kev could reply, a dry, shuffling sound echoed from the dark back-office section of the hangar. 

Scritch. Scritch.

It was the sound of leather skin rubbing against sheet metal. 

From behind a row of giant tires, a figure emerged. It was a former mechanic, still wearing a grease-covered jumpsuit. Its face was a dried, mummified skull, the lips shrunk back to reveal stained teeth. It raised its head, its nostrils flaring as it caught their scent. 

Then it opened its mouth and let out a dry, rattling hiss.

Chapter 3: The Scavenge Run

Kev didn't use the shotgun. The blast would have turned the workshop into a death trap within minutes. 

Instead, he stepped forward, his heavy safety boots silent on the dusty concrete. As the mummified mechanic hissed, Kev swung a heavy steel tire lever he had grabbed from a workbench. The metal bar caught the zombie flush in the side of the temple. The dried, leathery skull cracked like a hollow gourd. The figure collapsed instantly, going limp on the floor. 

"We need to move fast," Kev whispered, wiping the bar on his jeans. "Jase, let's go. Sarah, stay on the truck deck. Keep the ladder up."

Jase and Kev slipped out of the workshop's side door, stepping into the blinding glare of the Pilbara sun. The heat hit them like a furnace blast. The red dirt yard was filled with parked excavators, light vehicles, and mounds of scrap metal. 

Fifty meters away stood the electrical storage donga—a white, rectangular transportable building resting on concrete blocks. 

They ran low to the ground, using the massive tracks of a parked Liebherr excavator for cover. The air was dead, and the glare off the red earth was dizzying. Several mummified bodies wandered the far side of the yard, their clothes tattered, their dried skin blackened by the sun. They didn't see the two men, their slow heads turned toward the distant hills. 

Jase reached the donga door. It was unlocked. They slipped inside, the metal walls radiating heat like an oven. 

"Over here," Jase muttered, wiping sweat from his forehead. 

On the lower shelves of a steel rack sat the massive, heavy-duty lead-acid batteries used for the mine's heavy fleet. Each one weighed nearly forty kilograms. 

"I'll take one, you take the other," Kev said, gripping the rope handle of a battery. His muscles strained as he lifted it. 

Jase grabbed the second battery, his breath catching. It was incredibly heavy, and the metal edges dug into his hands. They turned toward the door. 

Through the small glass window of the donga door, Jase stopped dead. 

Three mummified zombies—drawn perhaps by the slight creak of the donga door or the scent of living sweat—had wandered into the narrow path between the storage containers. They were shuffling directly toward the donga steps, their dry, leathery hands reaching out. 

"Kev," Jase whispered, his fingers slipping on the heavy battery handle. "We have a problem."

Chapter 4: The Control Room

"Hold the door, Jase," Kev muttered, setting his battery down. 

Before Jase could react, Kev kicked the donga door open with a violent shove. The heavy metal edge caught the lead zombie square in the face, knocking it backward off the steps.
