Kev stepped out, the steel tire lever swinging in a brutal, tight arc. The bar smashed into the temple of the second zombie, sending it sprawling into the red dirt. 

The first zombie was already trying to stand, its jaw broken and hanging loose. Kev brought the bar down with a heavy, vertical strike, crushing the skull. 

"Go! Run!" Kev hissed. 

Jase scrambled down the steps, the heavy battery straining his arms and shoulders. He ran toward the workshop, his boots kicking up red dust. Kev grabbed his own battery and followed, his face flushed red with exertion. 

Inside the workshop, Sarah had climbed the stairs to the elevated control room—a glass-fronted office overlooking the hangar bays. A single solar-powered backup battery hummed in the corner, providing power to the main console. 

Her fingers flew across the keyboard of the site's primary server terminal. The screen flickered, displaying the mine's automation layout. 

CAT 797 - Fleet ID: UNIT 04
STATUS: SYSTEM OK / IGNITION LOCKED
REASON: COLLISION AVOIDANCE ACTIVE - MULTIPLE OBSTACLES IN PATH

"Come on," Sarah muttered, her sweat dripping onto the keyboard. "Bypass the safety protocols. Override the proximity sensors."

The mining company had installed strict safety software to prevent these 400-ton behemoths from crushing light vehicles or workers. To the software, the zombies were just "obstacles." Sarah had to access the system's root directory and disable the entire sensor array, converting the truck from an autonomous vehicle back to a fully manual machine. 

Down in the bay, Jase and Kev threw the heavy batteries onto the truck's engine deck. Jase worked like a man possessed, bolting the heavy terminals in place, sparks flying as the connection was made. 

"Sarah!" Jase yelled, his voice echoing in the hangar. "We're connected! Reset the board!"

Sarah hit the enter key on her console. 

OVERRIDE ACCEPTED. MANUAL MODE ACTIVE.

"Done!" Sarah shouted back, grabbing her laptop and running down the stairs. "Start it up!"

Jase climbed into the massive cab, turning the ignition key. 

The heavy starter motor began to crank—a massive, rhythmic wuh-wuh-wuh that shook the entire workshop. But the engine didn't catch. 

Across the open yard, the noise of the starter was like a dinner bell. Hundreds of mummified heads turned toward Bay 4.

Chapter 5: The Ignition

"Come on, you bastard," Jase growled, pumping the throttle pedal. 

The starter motor groaned under the strain, the heavy cables heating up. Sarah and Kev scrambled up the steel stairs of the truck, boarding the high deck just as Jase turned the key again. 

Wuh-wuh-wuh—ROAR.

The twenty-cylinder, 4,000-horsepower diesel engine caught. 

The sound was not just loud; it was a physical force. The concrete floor of the hangar vibrated, and dust rattled down from the corrugated steel roof. A thick plume of black soot coughed out of the high exhaust stacks, filling the upper rafters of the workshop with acrid, gray smoke. The deep, rhythmic thrum of the engine drowned out everything else, vibrating through their bones and teeth. 

"She's alive!" Kev yelled, pulling himself into the passenger seat of the spacious cab. 

Sarah climbed in behind him, slamming the heavy cabin door shut. The double-glazed glass muffled the engine's roar slightly, but the vibrations remained intense. 

Outside the workshop, the reaction was immediate. 

The silence of the Pilbara was shattered. In the open-cut pit, the shuffling hordes stopped. As if pulled by a single string, thousands of dry, mummified bodies turned toward the maintenance hangar. They began to march. Not running, but moving with a terrible, unified purpose, a red-dusted wave of death flowing up the dirt ramps. 

"Jase! The side doors!" Sarah screamed, pointing down. 

Through the lower glass windows of the truck cab, they could see the workshop's pedestrian entrances. The dead were already pushing through, their thin, dry bodies squeezing through the doors. They swarmed the workshop floor, circling the massive tires of the CAT 797 like ants around a boot. 

"We need to get out of here now!" Kev shouted, racking his shotgun. "They'll climb the maintenance stairs!"

Jase gripped the thick steering wheel, his knuckles white. He depressed the clutch and shoved the heavy gear selector into first. The transmission engaged with a deep, hydraulic clunk that shook the cabin. 

"Hold on!" Jase yelled. 

He slammed his foot down on the accelerator. The massive engine screamed, and the 400-ton steel giant began to roll forward, heading straight for the closed sheet-metal hangar doors.

Chapter 6: The Descent

The CAT 797 hit the sheet-metal workshop doors at fifteen kilometers an hour. The impact was barely a shudder inside the two-story-high cabin. The steel doors tore off their tracks with a screeching rip, buckling like tinfoil before being dragged under the massive front tires. 

They rolled out into the blinding Pilbara sun, surrounded by a cloud of red dust and shredded insulation. 

"The main road to the airstrip goes straight through the center of the pit," Sarah said, pointing her finger at the dusty, winding dirt ramp that cut down the terraced tiers of the mine. "It's the only path wide enough for a truck of this size."

"Then that's where we go," Jase said, turning the wheel. 

The truck rolled onto the main descent ramp. 

The ramp was fifty meters wide, carved into the red iron rock. Standing directly in their path was the vanguard of the pit horde. Hundreds of mummified zombies stood in a dense pack, their arms raised, their dried mouths open in silent screams. 

Jase didn't slow down. He couldn't. 

Thump-thump. Thump-thump.

The front tires, four meters in diameter and weighing over five tons each, met the first line of the dead. The sensation inside the cab was a series of heavy, rhythmic jolts. The massive tires rolled over the dry bodies, crushing leathery skin, brittle bones, and dried organs into the red dirt. 

"God," Sarah whispered, closing her eyes as the truck ran over a cluster of three zombies in high-vis shirts. 

"Keep your eyes open, Sarah!" Kev barked. "We need to watch the sides!"

The red dust kicked up by the tires was thick, mixing with the black diesel exhaust to create a choking, dark fog that reduced visibility to a few meters. Jase could only steer by looking at the top edge of the pit wall through the haze. 

The ramp grew steeper. The density of the horde increased. 

Now, they weren't just hitting individual zombies; they were driving through a solid wall of them. The engine began to labor, its pitch shifting to a deep, straining groan. 

Shudder.

The steering wheel jerked violently in Jase's hands, almost tearing itself from his grip. 

"The steering!" Jase gasped, fighting the wheel. "It's pulling to the left!"

"What is it?" Kev asked, leaning forward.

"The steering linkage," Jase said, his forearms straining as he forced the wheel back to the center. "The bodies. The leathery skin and the bones... they're getting caught in the front axle. They're binding the steering arms. If we hit the next terrace too fast, we'll lose control and go over the edge."

Chapter 7: The Airstrip Run

Jase threw his entire weight against the steering wheel, his muscles tearing as he tried to force the wheels to straighten. The hydraulic pump shrieked, straining against the mass of crushed bone and leathery flesh packed into the steering knuckle. 

They reached the flat floor of the pit. Ahead, through the swirling red haze, lay the pit exit—a wide cutting in the rock that led directly to the flat gravel airstrip. 

"Almost there!" Kev shouted, pointing through the dust. "The hangar is just past the gate!"

But as Jase turned the wheel to align with the exit, a loud, metallic bang echoed from beneath the cab. The steering wheel spun freely in his hands. 

"The linkage snapped!" Jase yelled. 

The massive front tires, locked at a sharp left angle, caught the soft gravel at thirty kilometers an hour. The 400-ton steel giant veered instantly off the road. 

"Brace!" Kev roared, grabbing the dashboard. 

The CAT 797 crashed headlong into the massive steel support pillars of the primary overland conveyor system. The impact was deafening—a screech of tearing iron as the truck's heavy steel bumper sheared through the columns. The conveyor line, carrying thousands of tons of iron ore overhead, collapsed. 

A deluge of heavy rocks and steel beams rained down on the truck. A massive truss crashed directly onto the cab's safety canopy, buckling the steel beams but sparing the occupants. The dump bed was pinned beneath a mountain of collapsed steel. 

The engine shuddered and died, steam and black smoke pouring from the radiator. 

Inside the cab, the silence was sudden and terrifying, broken only by the hiss of escaping coolant. 

Sarah opened her eyes, coughing in the dust. "Is everyone okay?"

"I'm whole," Kev grunted, kicking his door open. 

Jase looked out the side window. The airstrip hangar was barely fifty meters away. Through the open hangar doors, he could see the white wings of the mine's twin-prop Beechcraft King Air charter plane. 

But the yard between the crashed truck and the hangar was rapidly filling. 

The crash had drawn every zombie in the vicinity. Thousands of mummified bodies were spilling out of the pit cutting, their dry eyes locked on the wreckage. 

"We can't stay in the cab," Kev said, looking down at the deck. The ladder had been ripped away in the crash. "We have to jump to the conveyor frame and run for it."

Chapter 8: The Fuel Tanker

They scrambled out of the buckled cab door, stepping onto the collapsed steel frame of the conveyor belt. Below them, a dozen mummified figures clawed at the air, their dry fingernails scratching against the steel girders. 

Jase swung down first, landing hard on the red gravel. He reached up, catching Sarah as she dropped. Kev came last, his boots crunching on the stone, his shotgun held tight. 

The hangar was fifty meters away, but a wall of the dead was already beginning to fan out across the gravel yard, separating them from the hangar doors. 

"We need to refuel the plane," Jase said, his voice tense as they ducked behind a parked service ute. "The fuel tanker is parked next to the hangar, but it takes time to hook up the hose and pump the aviation gas. If the horde surrounds the plane while we're refueling, we'll never get off the ground."

Kev looked at the approaching crowd. There were at least three hundred zombies within fifty meters, and thousands more were pouring out of the pit. 

He reached into his pocket and pulled out a red emergency road flare. He looked at Jase, then at Sarah. 

"You two get to the hangar," Kev said, his voice flat. "Get the doors open. Hook up the fuel hose."

"Kev, no," Sarah said, grabbing his arm. 

"site safety rules, Sarah," Kev said, a faint, grim smile touching his lips. "In an emergency, you secure the high-value assets. That's the plane. Now go."

He struck the flare. 

A brilliant, blinding red light hissed to life, casting long, dancing shadows across the red dirt. Kev raised his shotgun and fired a round into the sky. The blast was deafening. 

"Hey! You dry bastards! Over here!" Kev roared, waving the burning flare. 

The horde stopped. Hundreds of dry, mummified heads turned toward the red glare and the booming sound. Kev fired again, blowing the head off a zombie that had gotten too close, and began to run toward the generator station on the opposite side of the yard. 

The crowd followed him like a tide, their scraping footsteps shifting away from the hangar. 

"Go!" Jase yelled, grabbing Sarah's hand. 

They ran. Behind them, the shotgun barked again. 

Crack!

Then another blast, followed by a pause. The red flare bobbed in the distance, surrounded by a swirling mass of dark figures. Then came the sound of a final, empty click. 

Kev's voice raised in a defiant shout, instantly choked off as the red light of the flare fell into the dirt and was smothered by a mass of tattered, rust-colored bodies.

Chapter 9: The Hangar Stand

Jase shoved the hangar's heavy steel rolling door. It groaned on its tracks, moving inch by inch until it was open wide enough for them to squeeze through. 

Inside, the hangar was cool and smelled of aviation fuel and rubber. The Beechcraft King Air sat in the center of the concrete floor, its white paint covered in a thin layer of red dust. 

"Open the fuel cap on the wing!" Jase yelled, running back outside to the parked fuel tanker. 

He grabbed the heavy black rubber refueling hose, dragging it across the gravel and through the gap in the hangar door. He connected the nozzle to the plane's wing tank. 

"I have to start the tanker's auxiliary pump!" Jase called out, running back to the truck. 

Sarah stood by the rolling hangar door. She looked out through the gap. The red flare had gone out, and the yard was dark with shadows. But she could hear them. The dry, rustling sound of thousands of boots on gravel was returning, heading straight for the hangar. 

"Jase! They're coming back!" she screamed. 

She began to pull the heavy steel door shut, sliding it along its track. The metal groaned. The gap narrowed to a few feet, just wide enough for the fuel hose to pass through. 

Outside, Jase hit the starter on the fuel tanker's pump. The small engine sputtered, then caught, humming as it began to force aviation fuel through the hose. 

The first of the dead reached the hangar. 

A mummified hand, its skin like dried beef jerky, shot through the gap in the door. It grabbed Sarah's shirt. She gasped, slamming her body weight against the door to force it closed, but the hose was blocking the final seal. 

More hands reached through the gap, clawing wildly in the dark. 

Sarah leaned her shoulder against the steel frame, straining with all her might. "Jase! Hurry!"

A zombie—a former site cook, his face half-crushed—wedged his head through the opening. His dried, yellow teeth snapped in the darkness, inches from her face. Sarah screamed, trying to push him back with her hands. 

Before she could pull away, the cook's hand shot forward. His jagged, dry fingernails tore deep furrows through the flesh of her forearm. Almost simultaneously, his jaws clamped down on her left wrist. 

The pain was a hot, searing spike. 

Sarah let out a choked cry, but she didn't let go of the door. Using her right hand, she grabbed a heavy steel pin from the door track and drove it into the zombie's eye socket. The figure went limp. 

With a final, desperate surge of adrenaline, Sarah kicked the body out, pulled the fuel hose inside, and slammed the door shut, throwing the heavy iron bolt lock. 

She slumped against the cold metal door, panting, her left hand gripping her bleeding wrist. In the dim light of the hangar, the red blood looked black.

Chapter 10: The Last Flight

The twin turboprop engines of the Beechcraft King Air coughed, then whined into life. The propellers spun, first in slow, heavy sweeps, then blurrring into shimmering discs of light that blew the red dust off the hangar floor. 

Jase leaned out of the cockpit door, his face covered in sweat. "Sarah! Refueling is done! Get in! We have to taxi out before they block the runway!"

Sarah walked slowly toward the plane. Her left arm was tucked against her chest, wrapped in a grease-stained rag. The white fabric was already soaked through with dark, spreading crimson. 

She stopped at the base of the airstep door. She didn't climb. 

"Sarah, come on!" Jase yelled, the engines roaring behind him. "Get on the plane!"

Sarah looked up at him, her eyes calm but wet with tears. She slowly unwrapped the rag, exposing her wrist. The bite mark was deep, a jagged, blue-ringed crescent of torn flesh. 

"I'm not getting on, Jase," she said, her voice barely audible over the hum of the engines. 

"No," Jase whispered, his hand shaking on the door frame. "No, we can clean it, we can—"

"You know the rules," Sarah said, shaking her head. "We've seen it a hundred times. The fever starts in a few hours. Then I die. If I'm in the air with you, I'll turn. You'll be locked in a cockpit with a corpse that wants to eat you. The plane will crash. Both of us die for nothing."

Behind her, the steel hangar doors groaned as the horde pushed against them. The metal panels were buckling inward, the iron bolts straining. 

"Sarah..." Jase's voice broke. 

"Fly the plane, Jase," she said. 

She turned and ran toward the wall where the hangar's emergency manual chain-winch was located. She grabbed the heavy chain and began to pull it down with her right hand, her muscles straining. 

The sliding hangar doors slowly rolled open, exposing the blinding glare of the runway. Standing just outside were dozens of mummified bodies. They saw Sarah. They lunged forward, their dried hands reaching for her. 

Sarah didn't run. She stood by the winch, holding the doors open, her eyes locked on the plane. 

"Go!" she screamed. 

Jase slammed the cockpit door shut and locked it. He grabbed the throttles, shoving them forward. 

The Beechcraft surged forward, rolling out of the hangar. The propellers sliced through the first two zombies in their path, spraying red dust and dark fluid across the concrete. The plane bounced onto the gravel runway, Jase steering with the rudder pedals as he lined up for takeoff. 

He pushed the throttles to the firewall. The engines screamed, the turboprops clawing at the hot, thin air. The plane accelerated down the strip. 

Through the side window, Jase looked back at the hangar. 

Sarah was gone, lost beneath a swarming, writhing mound of rust-colored bodies near the winch chain. 

The Beechcraft lifted off the gravel, climbing steeply into the hazy, orange Pilbara sky. The vibration of the runway disappeared, replaced by the smooth, steady hum of flight. 

Jase banked the plane, looking down one last time. 

The vast red pit of the mine was a silent, open scar in the earth, surrounded by miles of empty desert. Below, the gravel runway was covered in tiny, crawling specks of red dust. Near the hangar, the mound of bodies was still moving. 

Jase turned the nose of the plane toward the coast, flying alone into the empty blue, leaving the dead behind in the red dirt.

================================================================================

STORY 9: THE FARE
-----------------

Chapter 1: The Pick-Up

The neon sign of the Hilton Hotel blurred in the rain-slicked asphalt of George Street. It was 9:00 PM, and Sydney's CBD was bustling with the usual Friday night crowd—drunken revelers spilling out of pubs, couples walking arm-in-arm, and the endless hiss of traffic. 

Frank sat in the driver's seat of his yellow Ford Falcon taxi, his fingers tapping anxiously on the steering wheel. His dashboard was a mess of unpaid bills, overdue notices from a local loan shark, and a half-empty cup of cold coffee. He owed thirty thousand dollars, and the men he owed it to had made it clear that his knees were the next payment. 

He looked at his rearview mirror. Clara was sitting in the back seat, her attention locked on her smartphone. She was young, early twenties, wearing a designer trench coat and carrying a pristine leather handbag that probably cost more than Frank made in a month. He had watched her check out of the hotel, her father—some wealthy property developer, if the lobby gossip was true—shaking her hand and telling her to take a cab straight to her apartment in Vaucluse. 

Frank had taken the fare. But he wasn't going to Vaucluse. 

"Traffic on the Eastern Distributor is blocked up, mate," Frank said, his voice flat, rehearsed. "I'm going to take the backstreets through Alexandria and Botany. It'll save us twenty minutes."

Clara didn't look up from her phone. "Sure, whatever is fastest. Thanks."

Frank nodded, his heart hammering against his ribs. He reached down to the door panel and flipped the master lock switch. A soft, hydraulic thunk echoed through the cab. He had spent the afternoon disabling the manual release pins in the rear doors. The child safety locks were engaged. Clara was locked in. 

He turned the wheel, guiding the taxi away from the bright lights of the CBD, heading south toward the dark, quiet industrial grid of Botany. The city lights began to fade behind them, replaced by the towering, windowless silhouettes of shipping warehouses and empty container yards.

Chapter 2: The Detour

The yellow cab turned onto a dark, narrow stretch of Hale Street. The towering concrete walls of a shipping container terminal flanked both sides of the road, casting long shadows under the flickering yellow streetlights. 

Clara finally looked up from her phone. She frowned, peering out the rain-streaked window. 

"Excuse me," she said, her voice tightening. "Vaucluse is north. We've been driving south for fifteen minutes. Why are we in Botany?"

Frank didn't answer. He turned the taxi down an unlit service lane, parking beside a rusted chain-link fence. He killed the headlights, plunging the cab into near-total darkness. 

"Hey! I asked you a question," Clara said, her voice rising in panic. She reached for the door handle and pulled it. The handle clicked, but the door remained locked. She tried the lock pin, but it was recessed flat into the trim, impossible to grip. "Open the door! Let me out!"

Frank turned around in his seat. In his hand, he held a heavy, black replica semi-automatic pistol. In the dim light of the dashboard, it looked real enough to kill. 

"Shut up," Frank said, his voice shaking slightly despite his best efforts. "Keep your voice down, and you don't get hurt."

Clara froze, her face turning pale. She stared at the gun, her breath coming in short, ragged gasps. "Please... I have money. My father—"

"I know who your father is," Frank cut her off. "That's why you're here. Hands behind your back. Now."

He reached over the seat, tossing a set of thick plastic zip-ties into her lap. Clara trembled, her tears finally spilling over as she slowly pulled her hands behind her back. Frank reached into the rear, grabbed her wrists, and pulled the zip-tie tight until it clicked lock, binding her hands behind her back. 

He climbed back into the driver's seat, his forehead slick with sweat. He turned the key, restarting the engine. 

As the motor rumbled, the radio flickered. The late-night jazz station was interrupted by a frantic news bulletin. 

"...repeat, police are advising all residents to remain indoors," the announcer's voice was fast, strained. "Reports of bizarre, highly violent assaults are spreading from the CBD to the inner west. Emergency services are currently overwhelmed. Witnesses describe attackers who appear to be..."

Frank hissed, hitting the power button on the radio. The cabin fell silent again. 

"Just crazy druggies," Frank muttered to himself, shifting the cab into drive. "Just another Friday night in Sydney."

Chapter 3: The First Sign

The taxi crawled deeper into the industrial grid. Frank kept his eyes glued to the road, his hands gripping the steering wheel so hard his knuckles turned white. In the back seat, Clara was weeping silently, her bound wrists twitching as she tried to ease the bite of the plastic ties. 

Suddenly, a blinding flash of red and blue light illuminated the dark cabin. 

A police paddy wagon screamed past them, driving on the wrong side of the road. It didn't have its siren on—only its flashing lights—and it was moving at a terrifying speed, its tires screaching as it fish-tailed around a corner toward Botany Road. 

"Frank... please," Clara whispered, using his name from the driver ID badge on the dashboard. "Something is happening. Let me go. We need to get help."

"Shut up," Frank snapped, but his heart was racing. 

He drove past a closed freight depot. Near the entrance, under the glare of a security floodlight, a woman in a torn silk nightgown was running. She was barefoot, her legs covered in dark mud, her face and chest smeared with a wet, dark red stain. She didn't look at the taxi; she was screaming, her eyes wide with a blind, primal terror as she ran toward the main road. 

Frank slowed the cab, watching her disappear into the shadows. "What the hell..."

He reached out and clicked the radio back on. 

There was no music. No announcer. Only a wall of static, followed by the high-pitched, warbling screech of the Emergency Broadcasting System. 

"This is an emergency alert," a digitized, monotone voice announced. "A state of emergency has been declared for the Greater Sydney Area. All citizens are ordered to shelter in place. Lock all doors and windows. Do not, under any circumstances, approach individuals exhibiting violent behavior or apparent physical trauma. If you encounter—"

The radio signal cut out, dissolved into a low, rumbling hiss. 

Frank stared at the dashboard. A cold sweat broke out across his back. He turned the taxi onto a wider arterial road, hoping to speed up and reach the warehouse quickly. 

But as he rounded the bend, his headlights caught a solid wall of red taillights. 

Southern Cross Drive was at a complete standstill.

Chapter 4: The Bottleneck

The traffic gridlock stretched as far as the eye could see. Cars were packed bumper-to-bumper, their horns blaring in a discordant, deafening chorus. But the drivers weren't waiting in their vehicles. 

Doors were flying open. People were climbing out, abandoning their cars on the asphalt. They were running north, back toward the city, their faces twisted in panic. Some carried children, others carried nothing, their screams rising over the sound of the horns. 

"What is going on?" Clara cried, leaning forward as far as her bound arms would allow. "Frank! Let me out!"

Frank didn't answer. He stared through the windshield. 

Three cars ahead of him, a man in a gray business suit stepped out of a black Mercedes. He looked angry, waving his arms as if to confront the drivers around him. 

From between two parked delivery vans, a figure shambled. It was a young man, his clothes shredded, his shoulder torn open to reveal gray bone. He walked with a heavy, dragging limp, his head lolling to one side. 

"Hey! Stay back!" the businessman yelled, putting a hand out to push the limping man away. 

The attacker didn't hesitate. He lunged forward with surprising, jerky speed. His hands grabbed the businessman's lapels, and he buried his teeth directly into the side of the man's neck. 

A fountain of bright red blood sprayed across the hood of the Mercedes. 

The businessman let out a high-pitched, gurgling shriek, his legs buckling as the attacker tore a massive chunk of flesh from his throat. They fell to the asphalt together, the attacker instantly burrowing his face into the dying man's chest. 

Clara let out a piercing scream from the back seat, kicking the back of Frank's seat. "Oh my God! Oh my God! He's eating him!"

Frank's breath hitched, his stomach turning. He felt a cold dread settle in his chest. This wasn't a riot. 

"No, no, no," Frank muttered. 

He threw the gear selector into reverse, his foot slamming onto the accelerator. The taxi's tires screeched as he tried to back out of the lane, but the space behind him was already filled by a panicked SUV trying to do the same.

Chapter 5: The Collision

Crunch!

The taxi was hit from behind. A silver SUV, attempting to swerve out of the traffic lane, slammed into the rear left quarter panel of Frank's Ford Falcon. 

The impact was violent. Clara was thrown sideways, her head slamming against the door frame. The rear left window shattered, raining safety glass onto the back seat. The door itself buckled, the latch mechanism groaning as the frame bent. 

"Damn it!" Frank screamed, his head snapping back. 

The engine stalled, the dashboard lights flickering before going dark. Frank turned the key frantically, but the starter motor only let out a weak, dying click. The alternator or battery had been damaged in the crash. 

In the back seat, Clara blinked away tears, her head throbbing. She sat up, her bound hands feeling the seat behind her. 

Her fingers brushed against a long, jagged shard of heavy glass still wedged in the rubber seal of the shattered window frame. It was sharp, glinting in the red glow of the traffic lights. 

Clara squeezed her eyes shut. She slid her body back, positioning her bound wrists against the sharp edge of the glass. She began to rub the plastic zip-tie back and forth across the glass. The sharp edge sliced into her skin, and she felt hot blood run down her fingers, but she didn't stop. The plastic began to fray, notch by notch. 

Frank wasn't looking at her. He was staring through his side window, his jaw hanging open. 

Three cars ahead, the businessman who had just had his throat torn out was moving. 

The body twitched. The man's legs jerked, and then, slowly, he pushed himself up from the bloody asphalt. His head hung at a grotesque angle, his collar soaked in blood. His eyes, once bright and angry, were now milky and blank. He didn't look at the attacker who had moved on; instead, the businessman turned toward the nearest car, his hands raising as he let out a wet, raspy wheeze. 

"He was dead," Frank whispered, his voice cracking. "I saw him die. How is he standing?"

Chapter 6: The Shattered Window

Thud!

A heavy, bloody hand slammed against the driver's side window of the taxi. 

Frank jumped, letting out a sharp yelp. 

Standing right outside his door was a construction worker in a torn high-visibility vest. The left side of his face had been ripped away, exposing the jawbone and teeth beneath. His remaining eye was a milky, clouded white, staring directly at Frank through the glass. 

Thud! Thud!

The zombie slammed his forehead against the glass. A greasy streak of blood and grey hair smeared across the window. The glass creaked, a thin, white crack appearing near the lock pin. 

"Get back!" Frank screamed, scrambling toward the passenger side of the front seat. He grabbed the replica gun from the passenger seat, his hands shaking so hard he almost dropped it. He pointed it at the window, his finger squeezing the trigger, but there was no round to fire. The heavy metal slide remained silent. "Get the hell away from me!"

In the back seat, Clara felt the plastic of the zip-tie give way with a sharp snap. 

Her hands were free. She pulled them forward, her wrists raw and bleeding, covered in glass dust and sweat. She looked at the buckled door on her left. The window was gone, and the frame was bent outward from the SUV's impact. 

"Come on," she whispered, her heart pounding in her ears. 

Outside, a second zombie—a teenage girl in a school uniform, her throat torn open—joined the construction worker. They pressed their weight against the driver's door, the metal panel denting under their collective force. The crack in the driver's window spiderwebbed, tiny shards of safety glass falling onto Frank's lap. 

Frank was crying now, his back pressed against the passenger door, holding the useless replica gun out like a shield. 

"Don't look at me," Frank whimpered. "Don't look at me."

Chapter 7: The Hostage Escapes

Clara threw her weight against the buckled left rear door. The metal frame, already loosened by the SUV's impact, groaned. She kicked it with both feet, her leather boots hitting the inner panel. 

With a sharp, metallic clack, the bent latch sheared off. The door swung open, hanging by a single cracked hinge. 

Clara tumbled out of the taxi, landing hard on the cold, wet asphalt. She ignored the scrape on her knee, scrambling to her feet. 

Frank heard the crash of the door and spun around in the front seat. 

"Clara!" he yelled, his eyes wide. 

He looked at the empty back seat, then through the open door to see her running between a parked sedan and a shipping container fence. If she got away, he was dead anyway. The loan sharks wouldn't care about a zombie outbreak; they would find him and kill him. Clara was his only ticket out of debt. 

"Get back here!" Frank roared, lunging over the center console to grab her bag, but it was empty. Clara was already ten meters away, disappearing into the crowd of fleeing motorists. 

Before Frank could climb out after her, the driver's side window finally gave way. 

The construction worker's fist shattered the glass, a shower of sharp crystals raining onto the driver's seat. A gray, rotting arm reached through the opening, the fingers clawing at Frank's jacket. The smell of the creature—stagnant blood and old decay—filled the cab, making Frank gag. 

"Get off me!" Frank shrieked, kicking the arm. 

He scrambled across the console, pushed the passenger door open, and tumbled onto the street. He stood up, clutching the replica gun in his right hand, his eyes scanning the chaotic road for Clara's designer trench coat.

Chapter 8: The Chase

"Clara!" Frank screamed, pushing past a man who was desperately trying to pull a baby seat out of a parked car. 

The street was a corridor of panic. The red glow of brake lights illuminated a sea of terrified faces. People were running in all directions, their screams bouncing off the concrete warehouse walls. 

Fifty meters ahead, Frank caught a glimpse of Clara's beige trench coat. She was running toward a gap in the shipping container fence, heading toward the dark railway line. 

"Stop!" Frank yelled, raising the replica gun. "I'll shoot! I swear to God I'll shoot!"

Nobody listened. A woman running past shoved Frank aside, sending him stumbling into the side mirror of a utility van. By the time he recovered his footing, Clara had vanished through the gap in the fence. 

"Damn it!" Frank cursed, running to the gap. 

He stepped through the wire fence onto the gravel ballast of the railway line. The tracks were dark, stretching out toward the Botany port. It was empty. Clara was gone, lost in the shadows of the container yards. 

As Frank stopped, his chest heaving, he realized the sounds of the street had changed. 

The screaming of the crowd was getting further away, moving north. But behind him, in the dark service lane he had just left, a different sound arose. 

Drag-scrape. Drag-scrape.

It was the sound of dozens of boots shuffling in unison on the gravel. 

Frank turned around, his heart stopping. 

Emerging from the shadows of the warehouses were figures. Dozens of them. Some were clad in dockworker uniforms, others in civilian clothes. They moved with a slow, agonizing crawl, their heads tilted back, their eyes reflecting the red streetlights. They were ignoring the distant crowd; instead, they had locked onto Frank, the only living person left in the service lane. 

A zombie in a high-vis vest shuffled out of the dark, barely five meters away. Its jaw snapped, a wet, clicking sound. 

Frank backed away, his hand shaking as he pointed the fake gun. "Stay back. I'll shoot."

The zombie didn't blink. It kept shuffling forward, its hands reaching for his throat. Behind it, ten more dead stepped onto the gravel. 

Frank turned and ran back toward the yellow cab, his only shelter.

Chapter 9: Trapped in the Yellow Cab

Frank sprinted back through the gap in the fence, his boots crunching on the gravel. He reached the passenger side of the yellow cab and threw himself inside, slamming the door shut and sliding the lock pin down. 

He lay across the front seat, gasping for air, the replica gun cold against his palm. 

Smash!

A hand reached through the shattered driver's window, the fingers scratching the dashboard. It was the construction worker, his half-jaw snapping in a silent frenzy. 

Frank scrambled into the back seat to escape the reaching hand, only to remember with a jolt of terror that the rear left window was also shattered. 

He huddled in the center of the bench seat, his knees pulled to his chest. On his left, the broken window was a black void. On his right, a mummified-looking hand was already poking through the driver's window frame, reaching over the front headrests. 

"Start, you piece of garbage," Frank sobbed, lunging forward to turn the ignition key. 

The dashboard lights flared, then faded. 

Click.

The starter motor didn't even try to turn. The engine was dead. 

Frank pulled his hand back, retreating to the middle of the rear seat. He looked out the front windshield. 

The street was empty of living people now. The red brake lights of the abandoned cars cast a bloody, surreal glow over the scene. Emerging from the gaps between the cars, from the shipping yards, and from the dark warehouse alleys were more of the dead. 

They walked with the slow, relentless gait of shambling corpses. They converged on the yellow taxi, drawn by the sound of the starter motor and the smell of Frank's panic. 

Ten. Twenty. Fifty. 

Within minutes, the cab was surrounded. 

Cold, gray hands began to slap against the metal panels. Faces pressed against the glass of the unbroken windows. The taxi began to rock gently from side to side under the collective weight of the crowd. 

Frank sat in the center of the seat, his eyes darting from the broken driver's window to the shattered rear window. 

The first hand reached through the back window, the cold fingers barely scraping the sleeve of his jacket.

Chapter 10: The Meter is Running

The taxi rocked like a small boat in a rough sea. 

Frank pressed his back flat against the center of the seat, his legs drawn up, his breath coming in dry, rattling gasps. On both sides, hands were reaching through the shattered windows. A gray, fingernail-less finger caught the fabric of his trousers, pulling. 

Frank kicked out, his boot heel crushing the zombie's knuckles against the door frame. "Get off me! Get off!"

He raised the replica gun, aiming at the face of a zombie trying to squeeze through the rear left door. He pulled the trigger. 

Click.

The empty metal sound was a mockery in the dark cabin. Frank screamed, reversing the weapon and using the heavy steel butt to hammer the zombie's nose, breaking it into a bloody pulp. The creature didn't even flinch. It kept crawling forward, its teeth snapping in the air. 

On the hood of the Ford Falcon, three figures crawled. 

One was the businessman in the gray suit, his throat a gaping, red cavity, his silk tie soaked in blood. He dragged himself across the hood, his knees scraping the metal. He pressed his face against the front windshield, his milky eyes locked on Frank. 

Tink. Tink.

The glass began to crack under the weight. 

Frank watched in horror as a spiderweb pattern bloomed across the laminated glass. The weight of the three dead bodies pressing down caused the windshield to buckle, the center bowing inward. 

"Please," Frank wept, his hand reaching for the dashboard as if he could somehow push the glass back. "Please, no."

With a deafening, wet pop, the windshield gave way. 

A heavy shower of safety glass rained over the front seats. The businessman's body tumbled into the front footwell, instantly thrashing as he reached over the center console. 

At the same time, the two zombies at the rear window broke through the remaining frame. 

Dozens of cold, gray hands grabbed Frank's jacket, his legs, his hair. He was pulled forward, his body dragged over the front seats toward the gaping hole of the windshield. 

On the dashboard, the digital taximeter flickered, its green display casting a faint glow over the chaos. 

FARE: $24.50
STATUS: HIRED

Frank's scream was cut short as the first set of teeth sank into his shoulder, and the yellow cab was swallowed by the dark.

================================================================================

STORY 10: THE REEF
------------------

Chapter 1: The Floating Island

The Pacific Ocean was a vast, flat mirror of bruised purple and grey. Day sixty of the collapse, and the Great Barrier Reef felt less like a natural wonder and more like a floating cage. 

Liam stood on the top deck of the Reef Explorer pontoon, his hands resting on the fiberglass railing. He was twenty-three, wearing a faded blue rash guard and board shorts, his skin salt-crusted and dark from two months in the tropical sun. He looked west, toward where Cairns lay behind the horizon. There were no lights. No radio signals. Only a dark column of smoke that had finally burned out three weeks ago. 

"The barometer is still dropping, Liam," Dr. Helena Vance said, stepping onto the deck. She was forty, a marine biologist who had spent the last five years living on research vessels. Her hair was tied back in a messy bun, her face lined with exhaustion. "We're down to 965 millibars. The wind is starting to shift from the northeast. The cyclone is coming straight for us."

"How long do we have?" Liam asked, his eyes scanning the horizon. 

"Twelve hours before the outer bands hit. Twenty-four before we get the full swell," Helena replied, her voice tight. "This pontoon is rated for a Category 3 storm, but the anchors haven't been serviced in a year. If the swells hit ten meters, the chains will snap. The platform will disintegrate on the shallow reef."

"We have to use the catamaran," Liam said, pointing toward the Reef Queen, a twin-deck luxury tourist catamaran anchored fifty meters away in the deeper channel. It bobbed gently, dark and silent, its boarding ladder raised. 

"The catamaran has fuel, and its hull can handle the storm," Noah said, climbing up from the lower kitchen deck. He was twenty-five, a wealthy international tourist whose family owned hotels in Singapore. He still wore his gold watch, though his linen shirt was stained with fish blood and oil. "But how do we get to it? We can't swim. Not anymore."

Liam looked down at the crystal-clear water surrounding the pontoon. 

Ten meters down, the coral gardens of the reef were laid out in breathtaking detail—brain corals, staghorns, and giant clams. But nestled among the beautiful shapes of the reef were other, darker shapes. 

Figures. 

They lay on the sand, or were wedged between the coral branches, drifting lazily with the currents. In the warm, salt water, the bodies had bloated and peeled, their skin a pale, translucent white. They didn't sink to the bottom like stones; they hovered, their limbs waving as the ocean moved them. 

And they were staring upward. 

A dozen pale, waterlogged heads turned slowly toward the shadow of the pontoon. Their dead eyes, clouded by salt water, reflected the gray light of the storm clouds. Their jaws opened and closed in a silent, slow-motion rhythm. 

"The harbor was full of them when we evacuated," Liam muttered. "The current must have carried the drowned ones out to the reef. They're following the shelf."

"They don't drown, Noah," Helena said, her voice dropping. "They don't swim, either. They just walk along the bottom. And they're gathering."

Chapter 2: Shadows in the Coral

Helena descended the spiral metal stairs leading down to the underwater observatory—the reinforced steel viewing chamber bolted to the underside of the pontoon, sitting five meters below the surface. 

Usually, this room was a sanctuary of blue light and coral views, where tourists could sit in air-conditioned comfort and watch parrotfish and sea turtles. Now, it was a room of nightmares. 

She stood before the thick, curved acrylic window. The ocean below was a pale, shifting turquoise, illuminated by the fading daylight. 

On the white sand bed forty feet down, the shadows were moving. 

They weren't fish. They walked on two legs, their movements slow and heavy as they fought the density of the water. Dozens of drowned corpses were shuffling across the sand, their feet kicking up tiny plumes of white dust. They looked like divers without gear, their arms reaching out to grab coral heads and limestone ridges to pull themselves forward against the current. 

They were all heading in the same direction: toward the center of the pontoon's shadow. 

"Helena?" Liam's voice crackled over the intercom speaker on the wall. "You see anything down there?"

"They're gathering at the anchor bases, Liam," she said, her voice shaking as she pressed her forehead against the cool glass. "There are at least fifty of them on the sand. They're... they're searching."

Through the window, she watched a figure walk toward the massive steel clump block that anchored the pontoon's primary eastern chain. It was a tourist, a middle-aged man still wearing a blue snorkel mask pushed up on his forehead. His skin was a bloated, waxen gray, torn by reef fish. 

The drowned man reached the heavy, rusted metal links of the chain. His gray fingers wrapped around the iron link. 

Then, slowly, he began to pull himself up. 

He climbed with a slow, mechanical rhythm, hand over hand, his legs dangling in the current. Behind him, three more corpses approached the chain, their hands reaching for the lowest links. 

"Liam!" Helena screamed into the intercom. "They're climbing the chains! They're coming up the east anchor!"

Above, the wind began to howl, a sudden gust shaking the entire three-story pontoon, sending a low, metallic groan through the steel frame. The storm was coming, and so were the dead.

Chapter 3: The Anchor Chains

Liam sprinted down the stairs to the lower wet-deck, the grated steel platform where tourists usually donned their flippers and masks. Now, the platform sat barely two feet above the choppy, rising waves. 

Noah was already there, clutching a carbon-fiber speargun with a double-barbed steel tip. His hands were shaking, his knuckles white. 

"Which chain?" Noah yelled over the roar of the wind. 

"The east fairlead!" Liam pointed to the heavy steel collar where the massive, link-chain disappeared into the surging blue water. 

The water within the collar bubbled. A hand—bloated, gray, and covered in green slime—shot out of the froth, gripping the steel edge of the deck. 

A moment later, the head of the snorkel tourist broke the surface. The snorkel mask was still crooked on his forehead, his face swollen, his jaw snapping as he caught the scent of the living. He dragged his upper torso onto the wet-deck, his dead eyes locked on Liam's legs. 

"Get back!" Liam roared, stepping forward. 

In his hands, he carried a shark bang stick—a heavy aluminum pole tipped with a chamber loaded with a twelve-gauge shotgun shell. He thrust the stick forward, slamming the tip directly into the center of the zombie's forehead. 

BOOM.

The detonation was a sharp, flat crack that vibrated through the metal deck. The zombie's head shattered, a dark cloud of gray matter and black fluid spraying into the wash. The body went limp instantly, slipping back through the fairlead into the churning sea. 

"There's more!" Noah screamed, pointing his speargun into the water. 

Through the clear surface, illuminated by the pontoon's emergency floodlights, Liam saw three more figures climbing the thick metal chain, their bodies swaying in the surge like seaweed. Further down, in the blue-black depths, a dozen pale shapes were rising, ascending the chain link by link. 

"We can't hold them here," Liam said, his voice tense as a wave splashed over his boots. The wind was rising, tearing spray off the crests of the waves. The sky was a dark, bruised charcoal. "The storm is going to tear this place apart. We have to get to the catamaran now."

"How?" Noah gasped, firing his speargun. The steel shaft took a rising zombie in the throat, pinning its neck to the chain, but the creature didn't stop. It kept pulling itself upward, the steel bar sliding through its dead flesh. "The catamaran is fifty meters away! We'll drown in this swell!"

"The Zodiac," Liam said, pointing to the small, rigid-inflatable rescue boat secured to the davit on the dry side of the pontoon. "We drop it, we motor over, and we get on board."

Chapter 4: The Catamaran Run

The rain arrived not as a drizzle, but as a solid wall of water that stung their skin. The wind screamed through the pontoon's rigging, a high-pitched wail that made communication almost impossible. 

Liam lowered the Zodiac into the tossing swell. The small inflatable boat bounced violently against the pontoon's rubber bumpers, the waves threatening to capsize it before they could even board. 

"Get in! Go!" Liam yelled, holding the painter line tight. 

Helena scrambled down the ladder first, landing heavily in the center of the rubber boat. Noah followed, his wet sneakers slipping on the pontoon steps, almost falling into the sea before Liam grabbed his collar and threw him into the hull. Liam jumped last, releasing the line and grabbing the outboard motor's starter cord. 

He pulled it. The small engine sputtered, coughed, and died. 

"Liam!" Helena screamed, pointing toward the eastern platform. 

The first wave of the climbing dead had breached the deck. A man in a tattered floral shirt dragged his bloated legs onto the grated steel. Behind him, three more crawled through the fairlead, their pale skin glistening under the rain. They began to shuffle toward the ladder they had just used. 

Liam pulled the cord again, his muscles burning. 

The outboard roared to life, its prop churning the foam. Liam twisted the throttle, and the Zodiac surged away from the pontoon just as the lead zombie reached the edge, its gray fingers snapping at the empty air. 

The run to the catamaran was a nightmare. 

The fifty-meter gap felt like an ocean. The swells were three meters high now, lifting the light Zodiac to the crests before dropping it into deep, watery troughs where visibility was zero. Rain whipped their faces, blinding them. 

"There!" Noah yelled, wiping water from his eyes. 

The Reef Queen loomed out of the gray curtain. The luxury catamaran was rolling heavily in the swell, its twin fiberglass hulls rising and falling with the waves. 

But as they drew closer, Liam's stomach tightened. 

The water around the catamaran was not clear. It was clogged with a drifting, white mass. 

It was a field of floating bodies. Weeks ago, a crowded refugee boat fleeing the mainland had capered and sunk in the channel. The tide had dragged the bloated, drowned corpses into the catamaran's mooring zone. They floated face-down or face-up, their limbs tangled in the seaweed and drift-nets, bobbing like grotesque buoys in the rising storm. 

"Look out!" Helena cried. 

The Zodiac's bow hit a floating body with a soft, wet thump, sending a shudder through the rubber hull. A pale, bloated face rolled over in the wash, the eyes open and staring. 

Liam steered the boat through the floating minefield, guiding the Zodiac toward the catamaran's stern. The boarding ladder was lowered, dangling just above the water line. 

"Noah! Grab the ladder!" Liam yelled, pushing the Zodiac's bow against the catamaran's stern.

Chapter 5: Drowned and Floating

Noah leaped from the bouncing Zodiac, his hands catching the bottom rung of the catamaran's aluminum ladder. He scrambled upward, his feet kicking the air before he hauled himself onto the wet deck of the Reef Queen. 

"Helena! You're next!" Liam yelled, fighting the steering arm of the outboard. 

But before Helena could reach the bow, a massive wave lifted the Zodiac, slamming it against the catamaran's hull and pushing it three meters back into the floating field of corpses. 

The engine intake sucked in a tattered piece of clothing, and the outboard sputtered and died. 

"Liam!" Helena screamed, losing her footing and falling onto the wooden deck of the inflatable. 

The Zodiac was drifting, surrounded by the bloated, floating dead. 

Suddenly, the floating figures began to move. 

They weren't just drifting with the current. Under the dark surface, their arms were reaching out. Their bloated, wrinkled fingers clawed at the air, their legs kicking weakly. 

A hand—gray, waterlogged, its fingernails missing—wrapped around the rubber tube of the Zodiac. Another hand grabbed the stern, then three more. 

A zombie wearing a torn life jacket rolled over in the water, its head breaking the surface. Its jaw snapped, and it sank its teeth directly into the rubber inflation tube of the Zodiac. 

HISS.

A loud, violent rush of escaping air screamed from the puncture. The front chamber of the inflatable began to sag instantly, the rubber folding inward as the ocean water poured over the bow. 

"They're sinking us!" Helena shrieked, scrambling toward the stern as the bow dipped below the waterline. 

Liam grabbed a heavy fiberglass boat hook from the deck. He lunged forward, driving the blunt end of the pole into the eye socket of the zombie biting the tube. The skull collapsed, and the creature sank, but the air was gone. The Zodiac was deflating rapidly, turning into a floppy, waterlogged sheet of rubber. 

"Helena! Jump!" Liam yelled. 

He threw the boat hook aside and grabbed her arm, hauling her toward the catamaran's stern. The sagging Zodiac was barely floating now, the weight of the waterlogged dead pulling the rubber tubes down into the dark water. 

Helena reached out, her fingers catching the catamaran's ladder. Noah grabbed her wrists from above, pulling her up onto the deck. 

Liam scrambled behind her, his feet slipping on the deflating rubber. As he grabbed the ladder's bottom rung, a hand shot out of the foam, grabbing his ankle. 

The grip was cold, heavy, and wet. 

Liam looked down. A drowned woman, her hair tangled in sea-grass, was staring up at him through the wash, her mouth open in a silent, watery snarl. 

Liam kicked back violently with his free leg, his heavy deck shoe smashing into her face. The jaw shattered, and the grip released. He hauled himself up the ladder, collapsing onto the catamaran's deck just as the Zodiac disappeared completely beneath the black, foaming waves.

Chapter 6: The Engine Room

They scrambled inside the catamaran's main cabin, slamming the sliding glass door shut to block the wind. The cabin was luxury personified—leather booths, a polished teak bar, and large panoramic windows. But the windows now showed only sheets of gray rain and the violent, rolling motion of the sea. 

"The catamaran's engines are in the hulls," Liam said, his voice raspy as he wiped salt water from his face. "Separate engine bays in the port and starboard pontoons. I need to get them started. Noah, stay in the cabin. Watch the windows."

Liam opened a hatch in the cabin floor, revealing a steep metal ladder leading down into the cramped, hot interior of the starboard hull. He climbed down, the smell of diesel oil and hot fiberglass hitting him immediately. 

He reached the engine compartment. The massive Volvo Penta marine diesel sat in the center of the narrow space, surrounded by wire bundles and hydraulic hoses. 

The hull rolled violently, throwing Liam against the fuel tank. He recovered his footing, grabbing a flashlight from the bulkhead. 

"Sea strainers first," Liam muttered. 

The engines relied on seawater pulled through intake ports in the hull to cool the exhaust. If the strainers were blocked, the engine would overheat and seize within minutes. 

He shone the flashlight on the clear plastic cylinder of the starboard sea strainer. 

The mesh filter was completely packed. It wasn't seaweed. 

A thick, dark mass of human hair was tangled inside the filter, mixed with shreds of denim clothing and gray, waterlogged flesh. The suction of the intake had pulled in debris from the drowned refugees floating in the channel. 

"Oh, God," Liam whispered, gagging at the sight. 

He grabbed a bucket, closed the seacock valve, and unscrewed the strainer cap. The smell of stagnant, decaying flesh flared in the cramped space, trapped and concentrated. Liam pulled his shirt collar over his nose. He reached his gloved hand into the filter, pulling out the wet, tangled mass of hair and flesh, tossing it into the bucket. 

He cleaned the screen, screwed the cap back on, and reopened the valve. 

He turned the key on the local control panel. The starter motor whined, cranking the diesel. 

Crank-crank-crank—

The engine sputtered, then roared to life, a deep, powerful rumble that vibrated through the fiberglass hull. 

"One down," Liam breathed, a brief flash of relief crossing his face. 

But his relief was cut short by a muffled, high-pitched scream from the cabin directly above his head. 

It was Helena.

Chapter 7: The Pontoon Swarmed

Liam scrambled up the ladder from the engine bay, his heart hammering. He burst into the cabin. 

Noah and Helena were leaning against the glass door, their faces pale. Helena was pointing toward the bow. 

"The mooring line!" Helena shouted over the roar of the wind. "The catamaran's bow line is still tethered to the pontoon's main cleat. We're anchored, but we're also tied to the platform. If we don't cut or release that line, we can't steer. The catamaran will get dragged into the reef if the pontoon drifts!"

Liam looked through the window. The thick nylon mooring line was stretched taut between the catamaran's bow and the pontoon's lower deck cleat, vibrating like a guitar string under the immense tension of the waves. 

"The wind is pushing us apart," Liam said. "I can't reach the cleat from our deck. The line is under too much load to cut with a pocket knife—the tension will snap it and take someone's head off. Someone has to release it at the pontoon end where the quick-release pin is."

"I'll go," Helena said, grabbing a yellow life jacket from the cabin rack. "I know how the pontoon's release system works. Keep the catamaran's starboard engine in reverse to slacken the line."

"No, Helena, it's too dangerous!" Liam warned. 

"If we stay tied, both vessels sink," she said, her voice firm. 

She shoved the sliding door open, stepping out into the screaming wind. Liam ran to the helm station, shifting the starboard engine into reverse. The catamaran roared, backing up, and the thick nylon line slackened slightly. 

Helena crawled along the catamaran's wet bow, holding the safety rail. The catamaran was rolling violently. She reached the bow pulpit, stepped onto the rubber bumper of the pontoon's landing platform, and reached for the quick-release cleat. 

She pulled the safety pin. 

The mooring line snapped free with a loud thwack, flying through the air and splashing into the sea. The catamaran was free. 

But as the line released, a massive wave—a five-meter swell—struck the catamaran's hull. The boat was pushed violently away from the pontoon, the gap between the two hulls widening to ten meters in an instant. 

Helena lost her footing, falling onto the pontoon's wet deck. 

"Helena!" Liam screamed from the catamaran's helm. 

She scrambled to her feet, looking back at the gap. It was too wide to jump. 

Before she could run toward the Zodiac davit, a pack of the dead spilled out of the pontoon's upper lounge. They had climbed the west anchor chain. Ten mummified, waterlogged zombies stumbled onto the landing deck, their arms reaching for her. 

Helena looked behind her. The east anchor chain was also swarmed. She was surrounded. 

With no other way out, Helena turned and sprinted toward the metal hatch of the underwater observatory. She yanked the hatch open, slid down the spiral stairs, and slammed the heavy steel door shut, turning the iron wheel lock just as the first zombie face smashed against the small glass portal.

Chapter 8: Trapped Below

The wind had reached a sustained ninety knots. The rain was a horizontal sheet of gray needle-pricks that obscured the pontoon entirely, except for the occasional flash of its strobe lights. 

Inside the catamaran's cabin, Liam fought the wheel. The starboard engine was humming, and he had managed to start the port engine as well, but the catamaran was a toy in the massive swells. Every wave lifted the hulls, threatening to flip the vessel. 

"I can't get us closer!" Liam shouted, his face drenched in sweat. "The wind is blowing us toward the outer reef! If I try to dock, the swell will smash our fiberglass hulls against the pontoon's steel frame. We'll sink in seconds!"

"We can't leave her!" Noah yelled, holding onto the pilot's chair. 

Deep beneath the water, inside the steel cylinder of the observatory, Helena sat in the dark. 

The pontoon's generators had finally failed, drowned by the waves crashing over the upper deck. The bright, reassuring lights of the viewing room had gone out, replaced by the dull, pulsing red of the battery-powered emergency beacon. 

Clang. Clang.

Above her, at the top of the spiral stairs, the dead were hammering on the steel hatch. The sound traveled down the vertical metal tube, echoing in the cramped, circular chamber. She could hear the wet, muffled scraping of fingernails on the rubber seals, and the deep, hollow groans of the dead trying to force the iron wheel lock. 

Helena turned away from the stairs, facing the curved acrylic windows. 

Outside, the reef was in chaos. 

The storm surge had reached the seabed. The clear turquoise water was gone, replaced by a churning, dark soup of sand, ripped-up sea-grass, and shattered coral branches. The current was immense, tossing large fish like leaves in a gale. 

And drifting through the underwater storm were the dead. 

Dozens of bloated, drowned bodies were swept past the windows by the surge. Some were tumbled along the coral, their limbs breaking silently. Others were caught in the eddies, their pale, waterlogged faces pressed directly against the thick acrylic glass. 

A female zombie, her skin paper-thin and peeling in the saltwater, drifted to a stop right in front of Helena's window. Her hair floated around her head like a dark halo. She stared through the glass with cloudy, dead eyes, her mouth opening to release a stream of bubbles as her teeth clicked against the acrylic pane. 

Helena backed away, her hands trembling as she pressed her back against the steel stair column. 

"Liam," she whispered, though she knew the radio was dead. "Please."

Chapter 9: The Anchor Snaps

The wave was a black wall, rising twelve meters out of the dark ocean. 

Liam saw it coming and throttled both engines to full, turning the catamaran's bows directly into the mountain of water. The Reef Queen climbed, its hulls vertical, the twin propellers screaming in the air as they broke the surface. Then the boat crashed down the back of the swell, the hulls slamming into the trough with a violent shudder that shattered the cabin's rear sliding door. 

Directly behind them, the tourist pontoon was not so lucky. 

The massive swell lifted the floating steel island, pulling the anchor chains taut. The chains, rusted and unmaintained, reached their limit. 

BANG.

The sound was like a cannon shot, vibrating through the water. The primary eastern anchor chain snapped, the heavy links whipping through the air and tearing the steel landing platform to pieces. 

Seconds later, the western chain sheared. 

"The anchors are gone!" Noah screamed, pointing through the shattered cabin door. "The pontoon is drifting!"

Without its anchors, the massive three-story platform was at the mercy of the cyclone. The wind and waves caught it, pushing it rapidly toward the shallow coral reef. The structure tilted at a terrifying thirty-degree angle, its lower decks disappearing beneath the churning foam. 

Deep below, inside the observatory, Helena was thrown violently across the room. Her head hit the steel stair rail, and she fell to the floor, her vision swimming. 

The room was tilting. The red emergency light pulsed, illuminating a nightmare. 

The structural steel beams surrounding the chamber were groaning, bending under the immense pressure of the sinking platform. 

Tink.

Helena froze. 

In the center of the largest acrylic window, a thin, white fracture appeared. A split-second later, a needle-thin stream of high-pressure seawater squirted through the crack, slicing through the air like a knife and hitting the steel column with a sharp hiss. 

Outside the window, three drowned corpses were pressed against the cracked glass, their bloated bodies held there by the current. Their pale hands scratched at the spiderwebbing cracks, their empty eyes staring in at her. 

The water level in the observatory was already up to Helena's ankles, and the stream of high-pressure water was growing wider.

Chapter 10: The Deep Observatory

"Liam! The reef!" Noah screamed, pointing to the breaking white water ahead. 

The catamaran was drifting toward the shallow coral heads. Liam slammed the port engine throttle forward, turning the wheel hard to starboard. The boat groaned, its hulls scraping the top of a staghorn reef before slipping back into the deep water of the channel. 

He looked back through the rain-streaked cabin window. 

The Reef Explorer pontoon was gone. The massive three-story structure had rolled completely over, its yellow decks disappearing beneath the white foam. The steel platform was sinking, dragged down by the weight of its flooded hulls and the heavy metal machinery. 

"Helena..." Jase muttered, his hands shaking on the wheel. There was nothing they could do. The catamaran was barely surviving the storm, and the pontoon had vanished into the black depths of the Great Barrier Reef. 

Deep below the surface, the observatory was a steel tomb. 

The chamber was tilted almost upside down. The water had risen to Helena's chest, cold and dark. The air inside the sealed cylinder was compressed, the rising pressure causing her ears to pop and her lungs to ache. 

Above, the dead weight of the drowned zombies trapped on the upper deck kept the spiral staircase hatch locked shut. 

Creeeeak.

The curved acrylic window bowed inward, the white spiderweb of cracks spreading to the edges of the frame. 

Helena held her breath, her fingers gripping the metal rungs of the ladder. Outside the window, in the deep blue-black of the ocean, the drowned dead floated in the storm's wake. They didn't struggle; they simply drifted, their pale, bloated faces pressed against the cracked pane, watching her. 

A hand—gray, waterlogged, its skin peeling like paper—tapped the center of the window. 

The crack split. 

With a deafening, explosive crash, the acrylic window shattered. 

A wall of freezing, black seawater roared into the chamber, crushing Helena against the steel column. The rush of water brought the dead with it—bloated, pale bodies pouring through the shattered frame, their cold hands reaching out in the dark, turbulent currents. 

Helena's last breath was a trail of silver bubbles rising toward the steel ceiling as the deep observatory filled, sinking into the dark silent heart of the reef.

================================================================================

STORY 11: THE HILL
------------------

Chapter 1: The Hill

The white sails of the Parliament House flagpole stood silent against the pale Canberra sky. Day one hundred and twenty of the collapse, and Capital Hill—once the heart of the nation's democracy—was now a concrete fortress besieged by a quiet, crawling tide. 

Officer Dan leaned over the concrete parapet of the roof terrace, his hands gripping his Steyr assault rifle. He was twenty-five, wearing a dust-stained Australian Federal Police tactical vest over his dark blue uniform. His eyes were bloodshot, circled by deep bags of exhaustion. 

Below him, the grand, sweeping lawns that rose up the sides of Parliament House—designed to allow the public to walk over their politicians—were no longer green. They were a carpet of red dust, crushed grass, and shuffling figures. 

Hundreds of zombies were crawling and shuffling up the grassy ramps, their limbs dragging. Some wore business suits, others tattered civilian clothes, and a few were clad in the high-visibility vests of the parliamentary maintenance crew. They moved with a slow, agonizing crawl, their heads tilted back toward the grand building above. 

"They're getting closer, Dan," Evelyn said, stepping out of the glass pavilion onto the terrace. She was fifty-five, a senior parliamentary librarian with gray hair cut in a sharp bob. She wore a heavy tweed jacket, and a ring of master keys jangled on her belt. 

"The perimeter fences were breached days ago," Dan said, his voice raspy. "The concrete barriers at the entrance are the only thing keeping them from the main plaza. But they're just climbing the hill. The grass slopes are a perfect ramp."

"How much ammo do we have left?" Senator Mark asked, stepping onto the terrace. He was forty, a junior senator from Queensland who had refused to evacuate with the cabinet. He still wore his red tie and a pin of the Australian flag on his lapel, though his shirt was wrinkled and stained with sweat. 

"Three magazines for the Steyr, and two for my Glock," Dan said. "Not enough to clear a fraction of them."

"We don't need to clear them," Mark said, his voice filled with a desperate, feverish energy. "We just need to reach the broadcasting suite in the Ministerial Wing. The emergency transmitter is still connected to the national backup grid. If we can get there, I can send a message. A final address to the nation. We have to let the survivors know the government stands."

"The government is dead, Senator," Evelyn said quietly, looking down at the red-dusted city of Canberra. The lake, Lake Burley Griffin, was still, the bridges choked with abandoned cars. "But the transmitter has its own backup batteries. If we can broadcast, we might reach the regional holdouts."

"Then we go," Dan said, checking the bolt of his rifle. "But the public lobbies are swarmed. We'll have to go through the basement."

Chapter 2: Red and Green

They stepped back inside, sliding the heavy glass doors of the roof pavilion shut. The interior of Parliament House was a vast, silent tomb of marble and timber. 

They walked through the high-ceilinged corridors, their boots silent on the thick carpets. They crossed the threshold into the House of Representatives chamber. The room was a sea of green leather seats, the grand Speaker's Chair sitting empty at the front, bathed in the dim light filtering down from the high skylight. 

"The Senate chamber is sealed," Evelyn said as they huddled in the central aisle. "But the public lobbies are overrun. I heard them scraping against the glass doors of the main foyer when I went down to the library. There must be hundreds of them pressed against the entrance."

Creak.

A low, metallic groan echoed from above. 

Dan raised his rifle, aiming at the massive, geometric glass skylight that sat in the center of the chamber ceiling. 

Through the frosted glass panels, they could see the dark, pressed shapes of bodies. Several zombies had crawled onto the roof terrace above the chamber, drawn by the sound of their footsteps. They lay flat on the glass, their faces pressed against the panes, their fingers scratching at the metal frame. 

"They're on the glass," Senator Mark whispered, his face pale. 

"The frame is steel, but the panes aren't designed to hold that kind of weight," Dan said, backing away from the center of the room. "If one of those panels shatters, they'll rain down on us."

"We need to get to the archives," Evelyn said, gesturing toward the back door of the chamber. "The access stairs are in the Member's Library, just behind the Speaker's Lobby. It leads directly down to the basement vaults."

"Then let's move," Mark said, taking a step toward the lobby. 

Before they could reach the door, a sharp, deafening crack echoed through the chamber. 

One of the central glass panels of the skylight fractured, a long, white spiderweb line tearing across the frosted pane. A tiny shower of glass dust drifted down, glittering in the dim green light of the empty parliament. 

The weight of a zombie, a former parliamentary guard still wearing his blue shirt, pressed down as the glass bowed inward.

Chapter 3: The Subterranean Archives

They ran. 

Behind them, the glass panel shattered with a loud, ringing crash. The zombie guard plunged through the opening, landing heavily on the desks of the opposition benches below. The body hit the timber structure with a wet, bone-crushing thud, going limp for a second before its limbs began to twitch. 

Dan shoved the Senator through the Speaker's Lobby doors, slamming them shut behind them. Evelyn was already at the Member's Library, her keys rattling as she unlocked a heavy, unmarked wooden door in the corner. 

"In here! Quick!" she hissed. 

They scrambled through, Dan locking the door behind them. They stood on a narrow concrete landing. A spiral metal staircase descended into a dark, concrete throat. 

Evelyn clicked on a heavy tactical flashlight, its beam slicing through the damp air. "The parliamentary archives. It sits beneath the main hill. It's the most secure part of the building."

They walked down the stairs, their boots ringing on the metal steps. The air grew cold, smelling of dry paper, cardboard boxes, and old dust. 

At the bottom of the stairs lay a concrete corridor that stretched out into the darkness. Rows of towering mobile shelving units—the kind that roll on tracks on the floor—flanked the walls, filled with thousands of blue archive boxes containing over a century of Australian political history. 

"These tunnels connect the House and Senate sides to the executive wing," Evelyn whispered, her voice hushed by the dead silence of the basement. "The politicians used them to bypass the press in the public hallways. It'll lead us directly to the basement of the Ministerial Wing."

"It's like a maze down here," Senator Mark said, his flashlight beam dancing across the labels of the boxes: Cabinet Minutes 1975, Joint Committee on Foreign Affairs 1988. 

"Just stay close to me," Evelyn said. 

They began to walk down the narrow aisle, the concrete walls pressing in. 

Suddenly, Dan stopped. He held up a hand. 

From the darkness ahead, a low, rhythmic sound echoed through the metal shelving. 

Thump... thump... thump.

It was the sound of something heavy dragging itself slowly along the concrete floor.

Chapter 4: The Dead Ledger

Dan raised his rifle, the flashlight mounted on the barrel illuminating the narrow concrete aisle. 

The beam caught a figure shuffling between two rows of blue archive boxes. It was an elderly woman, her gray hair pinned back, wearing a tattered cardigan and a lanyard with a parliamentary security pass. Her throat had been torn open, the blood dried into a black crust on her collar. She dragged her left foot, her arms reaching out blindly. 

Behind her, two more figures emerged from the shadows. 

"Clerks," Evelyn whispered, her voice tight with grief. "They must have taken shelter down here when the building was breached."

To the right, a younger man in a dark suit was wedged between two of the mobile shelving units. The heavy steel racks had rolled together, crushing his lower torso. His legs were pinned, but his upper body was still active. He clawed the air, his head jerking back and forth as he clicked his yellow teeth in the dark. 

"Keep moving," Dan whispered. "Don't shoot unless you have to. The noise will echo in these concrete vaults."

He stepped forward, his tactical knife drawn. As the elderly clerk reached for him, Dan lunged. He drove the long blade upward through the soft bone beneath her chin, severing the brainstem. The body went limp. He caught her before she hit the concrete, sliding her quietly to the floor. 

He dispatched the second clerk with a swift, vertical strike to the temple. 

"This way," Evelyn said, leading them past the crushed man in the suit, who hissed weakly as they went by. 

But as they neared the end of the aisle, a loud, heavy clang echoed from behind them. 

The heavy steel door at the top of the stairs they had just descended had been forced open. A group of the dead—the ones that had fallen through the Representatives' skylight—had followed their scent. 

They were stumbling down the spiral metal stairs, their boots loud on the iron. 

"They're behind us!" Senator Mark panicked, pointing his flashlight back. 

From the front, three more shambling figures walked out of the darkness of the port corridor. They were trapped.

Chapter 5: The Skylight Shatters

"The emergency exit ladder!" Evelyn pointed toward a steel hatch in the ceiling of the utility alcove. "It leads up to the service closet behind the Members' Hall. Move!"

Dan fired two rapid shots from his Glock, the suppressed pops flat in the concrete room. Two of the approaching zombies in front of them went down, their foreheads shattered. Dan grabbed the Senator's collar and shoved him toward the ladder. 

"Climb, Senator!" Dan yelled. 

Mark scrambled up the iron rungs, his hands slick with sweat. Evelyn went next, her keys clinking. Dan fired two more rounds at the dead closing in from the archives stairs, then leaped onto the ladder, pulling the heavy steel hatch shut behind him. He threw the latch just as a cold, gray hand slapped against the metal underneath. 

They stood in a narrow cleaning closet. Dan pushed the door open, and they stepped out into the grand, cavernous expanse of the Members' Hall. 

The Hall was the center of Parliament House, a vast space of polished Australian timber and grey marble, situated directly beneath the towering, four-legged steel flagpole that dominated the Canberra skyline. The ceiling was a massive glass skylight that looked up at the flag mast above. 

"We're almost to the Ministerial Wing," Evelyn said, pointing across the empty hall toward a set of heavy double doors. 

But as they ran across the polished floor, a low, ominous vibration shook the building. 

The wind outside had picked up, but it wasn't the wind. 

Above them, on the roof terrace surrounding the flag mast, a massive crowd of the dead had gathered. They had crawled up the grassy slopes of the hill, drawn by the sound of the gunshots below. Hundreds of them pressed against the central glass skylight. 

SCREEECH.

The steel support beams holding the massive glass panes began to buckle. 

"Dan!" Mark screamed, looking up. 

With a sound like a bomb detonating, the entire central skylight collapsed. 

Thousands of shards of heavy glass rained down on the Members' Hall. Along with the glass, dozens of bodies fell through the opening, plummeting thirty feet onto the polished marble floor. Many of the dead shattered on impact, their limbs breaking, but under the Romero rules, they didn't feel pain. 

Within seconds, the piles of broken glass were moving. Bloated, gray figures were crawling out of the debris, their fingers scratching the marble as they scrambled to their feet. 

"Run!" Dan roared, aiming his rifle at the falling debris. "Go to the Ministerial corridor!"

Chapter 6: The Undercroft Retreat

The Ministerial Wing was supposed to be a secure corridor, but the building's automated fire systems had triggered. A heavy, security grille had descended across the main hallway, blocking their path to the broadcast studios. 

"It's locked!" Senator Mark yelled, rattling the thick steel bars of the gate. Behind them, the Members' Hall was a warzone of breaking glass and shuffling footsteps as the fallen zombies scrambled to their feet. 

"This way!" Dan directed, pulling the Senator toward a side door marked Maintenance Access. 

Dan raised his Glock and fired two rounds directly into the door's lock cylinder. The wood splintered, and he kicked the door open. 

Beyond lay a concrete landing leading down into the undercroft—the raw, industrial basement beneath the Senate wing where the building's massive heating, ventilation, and plumbing systems were housed. Unlike the carpeted halls above, the undercroft was a maze of unpainted concrete walls, thick steel pipes, and humming electrical conduits.
