They ran down the stairs, their flashlights casting long, frantic shadows across the concrete. 

"We need to find the service lift," Evelyn panted, her face flushed, her breath coming in ragged gasps. "It bypasses the security grilles. It leads straight up to the press gallery behind the Senate Chamber."

"Where is it?" Mark asked, looking around the dark chamber. The air was warm, vibrating with the low hum of the air conditioning units. 

Before Evelyn could answer, the door at the top of the stairs burst open. 

The first of the Members' Hall zombies tumble down the steps, their limbs snapping on the concrete before they drag themselves back up. 

"Go! Find the lift!" Dan yelled, turning to face the stairs. 

He raised his Steyr rifle, aligning the sight with the head of the lead zombie—a former security guard. He squeezed the trigger. 

Pop-pop-pop.

The rifle barked, three rounds striking the zombie in the chest and forehead. The body collapsed, blocking the stairs, but more were pushing behind it. Dan fired again, clearing three more, the empty brass cartridges clattering loudly on the concrete floor. 

"Dan! I found the service door!" Mark called out from the dark back section of the chamber. 

Dan fired his last three rounds, the bolt locking back on an empty chamber. He dropped the rifle, drew his Glock, and backed away from the stairs. 

"Lock the door!" Dan yelled as he ran through the heavy red fire door. 

Mark slid a heavy steel bar into the brackets, securing the door just as a mass of cold bodies slammed against the other side. The metal panel buckled slightly, but it held. 

They were safe, but they were trapped in the dark undercroft.

Chapter 7: Evelyn's Sacrifice

The undercroft was dark, lit only by the narrow beams of their flashlights. Evelyn led the way, her keys clinking softly. She stopped at the intersection of two concrete corridors, checking the labels on the electrical boards. 

"The service lift is just past the next junction," Evelyn whispered, her voice shaking. "We need to clear the main control room first. There might be site workers who—"

She stepped toward a heavy metal door marked Electrical Plant 2. 

As she pushed the door open, a figure lunged from the pitch-black room. 

It was a technician in overalls, his flesh gray and peeling. He grabbed Evelyn's jacket with waterlogged strength, pulling her forward. Evelyn shrieked, trying to push him away, but the zombie's head snapped forward. 

His teeth sank deep into her forearm. 

Crack!

Dan fired his Glock point-blank, the bullet shattering the technician's temple. The zombie went limp, collapsing onto the concrete, but Evelyn fell back against the wall, clutching her arm. 

Dan knelt beside her, his flashlight beam illuminating her wrist. The fabric of her sleeve was torn, exposing a deep, bleeding bite mark. The skin was already beginning to discolor around the wound. 

"Evelyn..." Dan's voice broke. 

Evelyn looked at her arm, her face going completely blank. She let out a long, slow breath. 

"The rules of the dead," she said quietly, her voice flat, devoid of emotion. "We've all seen it. In a few hours, the fever will take me. Then I'll turn. Any cause of death. Even if my heart stops from the shock, I'll turn."

"Evelyn, we can—" Senator Mark started. 

"No, Senator," Evelyn cut him off, looking him in the eye. "I'm not getting in that lift with you. If I turn in a closed steel box, both of you are dead. You won't make the broadcast."

She reached down to her belt, unclipped the heavy ring of master keys, and pulled a yellow security keycard from her pocket. She pressed them into Dan's hand. 

"This keycard overrides the broadcast studio locks," Evelyn said. "Get to the studio. Make the broadcast."

She stood up, her knees shaking, and walked back into the dark Electrical Plant 2 room. She looked at Dan, her eyes hollow but determined. 

"Close the door, Dan. Lock it from the outside."

Dan stared at her, the keys heavy in his hand. He swallowed the lump in his throat, slowly closed the heavy metal door, and slid the external bolt lock into place. 

From inside the dark room, there was only silence.

Chapter 8: The Red Carpet

The service lift rose with a low, hydraulic hum. The metal doors slid open, revealing the plush, deep red carpets of the Ministerial Wing. 

This was where the country's leaders had walked. The walls were lined with portraits of former prime ministers, their painted eyes staring down at a hall now littered with broken glass, scattered papers, and dried blood. 

"The studio is at the end of this corridor," Senator Mark whispered, his voice shaking. He was holding his right arm, his sleeve pulled down tight. 

"Stay behind me," Dan said, his Glock held ready. 

They walked down the red carpet, their boots silent. The air was thick with the copper smell of blood and the sour tang of decay. 

Suddenly, a door to the Prime Minister's suite swung open. 

A figure stumbled out. It was a man in a tailored charcoal suit, his silk tie askew. It was the Chief of Staff, his face half-eaten, his throat a ragged mess of dried black tissue. He lunged at Mark with surprising, silent speed. 

"Mark! Look out!" Dan yelled. 

Mark reacted, raising his arms to push the creature back. The two men crashed into the wall, a framed portrait of Gough Whitlam shattering on the carpet. Mark struggled, his fingers clawing at the zombie's throat as the snapping jaws came inches from his face. 

Crack!

Dan fired, the bullet taking the Chief of Staff in the side of the head. The body slumped, dragging Mark to the floor. 

Mark scrambled up, panting, his face flushed. He pulled his sleeve down quickly, holding his right forearm. 

"Are you hit?" Dan asked, his gun still aimed down the hallway as he scanned for more threats. 

"I'm fine," Mark said, his voice high-pitched, defensive. "Just... scraped it on the broken glass from the portrait. We need to go. Now."

Dan looked at him. He could see a dark, wet stain spreading through the fabric of Mark's shirt sleeve, but he didn't have time to argue. 

At the far end of the corridor, the heavy glass doors to the Members' Hall were starting to rattle. A crowd of the dead had seen the flash of the gun and were pushing against the glass. 

"The keycard, Dan!" Mark urged, pointing to the studio door. "Open it!"

Chapter 9: The Soundproof Booth

Dan swiped Evelyn's keycard through the reader on the broadcasting studio door. A green light flashed, and the heavy, soundproof door clicked open. 

They slipped inside, Dan throwing the deadbolt lock and sliding the safety bar into place. 

The studio fell into a deep, heavy silence. The soundproofing foam on the walls absorbed all ambient noise, shutting out the howling wind and the distant, muffled bangs of the dead against the corridor doors. 

The room was lit by the dim red glow of the emergency backup console. In the center of the room stood a glass-walled broadcast booth containing a microphone, a mixing desk, and the transmitter controls. 

"We made it," Senator Mark whispered. 

But as he spoke, his legs gave way. He collapsed onto the carpeted floor, his back sliding down the wall. He was shivering violently, his skin a pale, clammy gray, his forehead slick with cold sweat. 

Dan knelt beside him, pulling Mark's hand away from his right arm. 

He ripped the sleeve open. 

The scratch was deep, three angry red furrows that had already turned a bruised, swollen purple. The infection had spread rapidly through his bloodstream. 

"You lied to me," Dan said, his voice flat. 

"I... I had to get here," Mark shivered, his teeth chattering. "The broadcast... the country needs to hear... that someone is left."

He looked at the glass-walled booth, then at Dan. His eyes were already losing their focus, the pupils dilating. 

"I'm not going to make it, Dan," Mark whispered, a tear running down his nose. "The rules... I'm going to turn. Lock yourself in the booth. The controls are simple. Hit the master switch, and the microphone is live. You have to make the broadcast. Tell them... tell them we held the hill."

Mark began to convulse, his limbs jerking as the fever boiled his brain. 

Dan didn't hesitate. He stood up, grabbed his gun, and stepped inside the glass-walled broadcast booth. He closed the thick, double-glazed glass door and slid the brass latch into place. 

Through the glass, he watched the Senator. 

Mark's body lay still on the blue carpet for a long, agonizing minute. Then, his fingers twitched. His chest let out a sudden, violent gasp of trapped air. 

Slowly, the Senator stood up. 

His red tie was crooked, his suit wrinkled. His head hung low, then snapped up. His eyes were blank, milky white. He turned toward the glass booth, his nostrils flaring as he caught Dan's scent. He began to shuffle toward the window, his hands raising to claw at the glass.

Chapter 10: The Final Broadcast

Dan sat in the operator's leather chair, the console's green and red lights reflecting in the glass window. 

Outside the booth, the reanimated Senator Mark pressed his face against the pane. His nose flattened against the glass, leaving a greasy smear of decay. He slapped his palms against the double-glazed window, a dull, rhythmic thud-thud that barely carried through the soundproofing. 

Behind him, the heavy outer door of the studio was beginning to shake. The horde from the Ministerial Wing had arrived. The wood was solid, but under the weight of dozens of bodies, it was only a matter of time. 

Dan turned his back to the glass, facing the console. 

He flipped the three toggle switches marked TRANSMITTER, POWER, and MIC. A digital VU meter flickered, the green needle jumping to life. The main display screen flashed: 

EMERGENCY BROADCAST SYSTEM ACTIVE.
CARRIER: 100% (ALL FREQUENCIES).

He pulled the heavy broadcast microphone close to his mouth. He adjusted the volume slider, took a deep breath, and pressed the red ON AIR button. 

"This is Officer Dan of the Australian Federal Police," he said, his voice surprisingly steady, echoing in his headphones. "Broadcasting from Parliament House, Capital Hill, Canberra. If anyone is receiving this signal, listen carefully."

Behind him, a loud, splintering crash echoed through the glass. The outer studio door had split. 

Through the window, Dan saw the first of the dead stumble into the studio. They were political staff, security officers, and clerks. They filled the small room, instantly drawn to the glass booth where the lights were on. 

They swarmed the booth, their hands slapping against the glass. The structural frame groaned under the pressure. 

Dan didn't look back. He kept his eyes on the microphone. 

"The federal government has fallen," Dan continued, his voice calm, measured. "The evacuation flights did not succeed. Do not come to Canberra. The capital is overrun. The hill is lost. If you are surviving in the regions, stay hidden. Conserve your water. Destroy the brain. That is the only way to stop them."

A sharp tink sounded behind him. 

He looked over his shoulder. A web of cracks was spreading across the glass door of the booth. The Senator's face was right there, his teeth snapping against the fracture. 

Dan turned back to the microphone, speaking his final words into the dark, empty airwaves of a dying continent. 

"This is Officer Dan, AFP. We held the hill as long as we could. The meter is dead. God save the South."

The glass door shattered, a waterfall of sharp crystals raining onto the console, and the red light of the microphone went black.

================================================================================

STORY 12: THE NULLARBOR
-----------------------

Chapter 1: The Straight Line

The steel tracks of the Trans-Australian Railway stretched out ahead like two silver blades slicing through the flat, red earth. Day forty-five of the collapse, and the Nullarbor Plain was a vast, silent ocean of nothingness under a white-hot sky. 

Gary sat in the driver's seat of the NR-class diesel locomotive, his hand resting lightly on the throttle lever. He was fifty-eight, his face lined by decades of shifting schedules and long nights on the rails. His eyes were red-rimmed, staring at the straight line of the tracks that vanished into the shimmering heat haze of the horizon. 

Behind the two-thousand-horsepower engine, the Indian Pacific train trailed like a silver snake. Twenty steel carriages long, it carried over four hundred refugees who had boarded in Adelaide, fleeing the chaos of the eastern cities. They were heading west, toward Perth, hoping the isolation of the western capital would hold. 

But the train was a closed system, and the virus was already on board. 

"Any change in the engine parameters?" Mel asked, stepping into the cab from the narrow corridor of the power van behind them. She was twenty-eight, a passenger attendant who had spent the last three days managing the terrified, cramped coach class carriages. Her uniform shirt was wrinkled, her face smudged with soot. 

"Fuel is fine, Mel," Gary said without looking back. "We've got enough diesel in the tanks to reach Kalgoorlie. But we aren't stopping there. The last signal tower we passed was dead. If the tracks are blocked at the yard, we'll have to crash through."

"It's not the tracks I'm worried about," Ryan said, sitting on an upturned tool chest in the corner of the cab. He was thirty-two, a rugged hunter from the Flinders Ranges who had boarded with a pack and a bolt-action rifle. He was cleaning a smear of dust from the rifle's scope. "It's the passengers. Coach B has been silent for two hours. No one has come out of the toilets. The doors are locked."

"They're just exhausted, Ryan," Mel said, though her voice lacked conviction. 

"Exhaustion doesn't sound like that," Ryan said, gesturing toward the heavy steel fire door that separated the locomotive cabin from the power van and the passenger cars. 

Through the thick metal door, a faint, rhythmic sound was vibrating. It wasn't the thrum of the diesel engine or the clack of the wheels on the joints. 

It was a wet, muffled thudding, accompanied by a low, guttural scratching that grew louder as the train sped across the empty desert at one hundred and ten kilometers an hour.

Chapter 2: The Screams in Coach

Mel took a step toward the steel fire door. "I have to check on them, Gary. site rules. If there's a medical emergency in Coach B, I'm the first responder."

"Don't open that door, Mel," Ryan said, standing up and shifting his rifle. "Whatever is making that sound isn't looking for a first aid kit."

"I'm just going to look through the observation window," Mel said, ignoring him. She stepped through the narrow doorway into the power van—the noisy, windowless engine room that housed the train's electrical generators. The roar of the auxiliary diesel was deafening here, vibrating the metal floor under her feet. 

She walked to the far end of the van, where the heavy steel gangway door separated the crew area from Carriage A, the first passenger sleeper car. 

Mel raised her hand, wiping a layer of condensation from the small, thick glass window port in the center of the door. She shone her pocket flashlight through. 

The interior of Carriage A was a corridor of blood. 

The lights were flickering, casting long, erratic shadows down the narrow aisle of sleeper berths. The red carpet was soaked black with fluid. Standing in the aisle, hunched over a collapsed figure near Berth 4, was a man. He wore a striped passenger shirt, but his head was tilted back at an unnatural angle. 

He rose slowly, his face smeared with crimson. In his hands, he held a piece of torn flesh. 

Further down the carriage, three more figures emerged from the sleeper cabins. They walked with the slow, dragging gait of shambling corpses. A woman in a dressing gown, her throat torn open, tripped over a suitcase in the aisle. She didn't cry out; she simply pushed herself back up with her hands, her milky eyes locked on the door. 

The man in the striped shirt saw the beam of Mel's flashlight. 

He turned, letting out a dry, silent hiss through the glass. He began to shuffle toward the door, his boots dragging on the bloody carpet. Behind him, the other three joined, their faces pressed against the glass port. 

Thud.

Their pale hands hit the window, leaving red, greasy streaks on the glass. 

Mel gasped, her flashlight dropping from her fingers and clattering onto the metal floor. She backed away, her heart hammering against her ribs. 

"They're dead," she whispered, her voice choking. "They're all dead. They're... they're eating each other."

She turned and ran back through the roaring power van, slamming the inner steel door shut behind her and throwing the heavy iron bolt lock. She burst into the locomotive cab, her face white, looking at Gary. 

"It's overrun, Gary," she gasped. "The whole train. Carriage A is full of them. They're coming."

Chapter 3: The Decoupling Plan

"We can't stop the train," Gary said, his voice flat, his hand tightening on the throttle. "If we stop, the brakes on the passenger cars will lock up, and we'll be sitting ducks. Those glass windows on the carriages won't hold if four hundred of them start throwing their weight against them. They'll swarm the locomotive in minutes."

"Can we just run the engine all the way to Perth?" Ryan asked, his rifle resting on his knee. 

"Not with them on board," Gary said, shaking his head. "The passenger cars are connected to the locomotive's main air line. If they damage the air pipes in the vestibules, the train's brakes will trigger automatically. We'll stall in the middle of the desert."

"Then what do we do?" Mel asked, wiping a tear from her cheek. 

"We decouple them," Gary said. 

He pointed through the rear window of the cab toward the power van. "The Indian Pacific uses a heavy knuckle coupler between the power van and Carriage A. If we release the coupling, the passenger carriages will separate from the engine. The air line will disconnect, which will automatically trigger the fail-safe brakes on the passenger cars. They'll roll to a stop in the desert, and we'll keep moving."

"And the people in there?" Mel whispered, her face pale. 

"There are no people left, Mel," Ryan said, his voice cold. "Only them."

"How do we release the coupler?" Ryan asked Gary. 

"There's a manual release lever and a locking pin on the external gangway," Gary explained, looking at Ryan. "But you can't reach it from the inside. The gangway doors are locked, and Carriage A is full of zombies. Someone has to go outside. You'll have to climb out of the power van's side service door and crawl along the external maintenance catwalk to the rear coupler."

Ryan looked out the side window of the cab. 

The ground was a red blur of dirt and saltbush, rushing past at one hundred and ten kilometers an hour. The wind was a howling gale, screaming off the flat plain. 

"The catwalk is barely a foot wide," Ryan said, his throat dry. 

"And if you slip, you're dead," Gary added. "But if you don't do it, we're all dead."

Chapter 4: The Speeding Tube

The wind howling off the Nullarbor was a deafening, constant roar that threatened to tear the glass from the cab windows. Outside, the landscape was a blur of red sand and low bluebush under a blazing sun. 

Through the small rear window of the cab, they could see the vestibule of Carriage A. The glass gangway door was spiderwebbed with bloody handprints. Several zombies were crowded against it, their pale, bloated faces pressed tight to the pane. They could see the movement inside, the dead clawing at the frame, their jaws clicking in a silent frenzy. 

"The door is holding, but the frame is flexing," Mel said, her eyes locked on the window. "If they break that glass, they'll be in the power van. They'll reach the engine cab in minutes."

Ryan stood up, securing his rifle over his shoulder with a tight webbing strap. He reached into Gary's steel maintenance chest and pulled out a heavy, double-ended iron spanner. 

"To hammer the lock pin," Ryan said, weighing the heavy tool in his hand. "In case the coupling is jammed under the tension of the train."

Mel stepped forward, holding a yellow webbed safety harness. "Use this, Ryan. It's from the crew safety gear. I'll tether you to the internal frame of the power van."

"Will the line reach all the way to the rear coupler?" Ryan asked, strapping the harness over his chest. 

Mel checked the length of the steel cable. "No. The cable is only five meters. The catwalk is eight meters long. You'll have to unclip once you reach the halfway mark."

"Great," Ryan muttered. 

They walked back into the roaring, shaking engine room of the power van. The heat here was intense, radiating from the auxiliary generator. Ryan stood before the narrow side service door—a steel panel that slid open to the elements. 

"Ready?" Mel asked, her hand on the release lever. 

"Do it," Ryan said, checking his grip on the spanner. 

Mel pulled the lever and slid the door back. 

The wind hit them like a physical blow. A blast of hot, dusty desert air roared into the engine room, carrying the deafening scream of the wheels on the tracks and the whistle of the wind. Below, the gravel ballast was a gray, spinning blur, barely inches beneath the narrow steel catwalk. 

Ryan took a deep breath, gripped the cold metal handrail, and stepped out onto the ledge.

Chapter 5: The Gangway Run

The wind was a solid wall of pressure, hitting Ryan's chest and throwing him against the metal side panels of the power van. He closed his eyes against the stinging red dust, his fingers clawing for a grip on the narrow safety handrail. 

Below his boots, the gravel ballast of the Nullarbor track spun past like a grey conveyor belt at one hundred and ten kilometers an hour. If he fell, the steel wheels of the train would slice him in half before he even hit the sand. 

"Inch by inch," Ryan muttered to himself, his voice lost instantly in the wind. 

He slid his left foot forward along the wet, rusted steel catwalk. The metal was vibrating violently under the vibrations of the massive diesel engine. The sound of the wheels grinding against the rails was a high-pitched, metallic shriek that set his teeth on edge. 

He reached the halfway mark. 

Jerk.

The steel safety cable of his harness went taut, pulling against his chest. He could go no further. 

Ryan looked back toward the open service door. Mel was leaning out, her face pale, her hands gripping the frame as she watched him. 

Ryan reached for the carabiner hook on his harness. His fingers were stiff with cold and fear, his palms slick with sweat. He squeezed the release latch. 

Click.

The cable snapped back, sliding along the guide wire. 

Ryan was now completely unsecured. 

He crawled forward, his chest pressed flat against the metal side of the van to minimize his profile in the wind. The heat radiating from the engine vents was blistering, contrasting with the cold blast of the desert wind. 

He reached the end of the catwalk, stepping onto the small, metal transition plate above the heavy coupling mechanism. 

Directly in front of him, through the glass window of Carriage A's gangway door, a zombie was staring at him. It was a young woman, her face swollen, her mouth smeared with black, coagulated blood. She slammed her hands against the glass, leaving bloody palm prints inches from Ryan's face. 

Behind her, the aisle of the carriage was packed with shambling figures, their dead eyes reflecting the harsh glare of the desert sun. 

Ryan ignored them, looking down at his feet. 

The heavy knuckle coupler—two massive steel fists locked together—lay directly below, shifting and groaning under the immense weight of the speeding train.

Chapter 6: The Decoupling

The heavy steel coupling knuckle groaned and twisted as the train roared over a slight dip in the tracks. Ryan crouched on the transition plate, his hands gripping the freezing metal handrails of the power van to avoid being thrown off. 

Below his boots sat the manual release assembly—a thick steel lever connected to a vertical lock pin that kept the two heavy knuckles closed. 

Ryan reached down, gripped the release lever, and pulled. 

The lever didn't budge. Under the immense tension of the 1,500-ton train speeding at one hundred and ten kilometers an hour, the steel knuckles were locked together like jaws of iron. 

"Rusted," Ryan hissed, his throat dry. 

He pulled the heavy iron spanner from his belt. 

Clang!

He slammed the spanner down onto the safety lock pin that held the lever in place. The sound was a sharp, metallic ring that carried over the wail of the wind. 

Through the glass door of Carriage A, the zombies reacted to the sound. The young woman zombie slammed her forehead against the glass with a wet, bone-crushing crack. The glass port began to spiderweb, a white fracture lines spreading across the pane. Behind her, the crowd pushed harder, the steel frame of the door creaking as it bent inward. 

"Come on!" Ryan roared, raising the spanner again. 

Clang! Clang! Clang!

He hammered the pin with a series of brutal, rhythmic strikes. Sparks flew from the steel. The pin began to slide downward, notch by notch, through the slot. 

Tink.

The glass port of Carriage A shattered. 

A gray, waterlogged arm shot through the opening, the fingers clawing at the collar of Ryan's jacket. The smell of decay—stagnant blood and old sweat—blew through the gap, filling his nose. 

Ryan didn't pull away. He couldn't. 

With a final, desperate swing, he slammed the spanner down onto the head of the pin. The steel sheared off, flying into the desert. 

Ryan grabbed the release lever with both hands, throwing his entire body weight backward. 

The lever snapped back. 

With a deafening, high-pressure screeech, the main air brake lines disconnected. A massive blast of pressurized air exploded from the ruptured hoses, spraying dust and grit into Ryan's face, blinding him as the couplers began to separate.

Chapter 7: The Separation

The knuckle couplers separated with a heavy, grinding slide. 

The gap between the power van and Carriage A opened, exposing the raw, spinning gravel ballast directly below Ryan's feet. The ruptured air hoses whipped wildly, spraying high-pressure air and red dust into the gap. 

Without the air pressure from the locomotive, the automatic fail-safe emergency brakes on the passenger carriages triggered instantly. 

A screech of steel on steel erupted from the wheels of Carriage A, a massive cloud of grey smoke and friction sparks spraying from the undercarriage. The eighteen passenger cars began to slow down rapidly, their momentum dying as the brakes clamped tight. 

The locomotive, now free of its 1,500-ton load, lunged forward with a violent, sudden acceleration. 

The lurch was massive. 

Ryan was thrown off balance, his sneakers slipping on the grease-stained metal transition plate. He fell backward, his hands yanking free of the safety handrails. 

"Ryan!" Mel screamed from the open door of the power van, her arm reaching out in a desperate, futile gesture. 

Ryan tried to grab the handrail as he slid, but the acceleration was too fast. His fingers brushed the cold steel, unable to grip. He tumbled off the edge of the catwalk, falling toward the fast-moving ground. 

For a fraction of a second, he was airborne, the red desert sky and the silver side of the train spinning in his vision. 

Then he hit the ground. 

He struck the sharp limestone ballast beside the tracks at nearly eighty kilometers an hour, his body tumbling violently through the red dust and saltbush like a rag doll.

Chapter 8: Stranded on the Ballast

The world stopped spinning. 

Ryan lay on his back in the red dust, his chest heaving, his mouth filled with the bitter taste of iron and dirt. The glare of the sun was blinding, and the heat of the earth radiated through his clothes. 

He tried to sit up, but a hot, white-hot spike of agony shot up his left leg. He looked down. His left leg was bent at an unnatural angle, the bone fractured beneath his jeans, the fabric already staining dark red with blood. 

He let out a choked cry, collapsing back onto the dirt. He was covered in deep scrapes, his arms and chest bruised from the high-speed impact on the limestone ballast. 

He looked down the tracks. 

The NR-class locomotive and the power van were already a kilometer away. They were speeding west, a silver speck on the straight line of the railway, disappearing into the vast, shimmering heat haze of the Nullarbor. 

"Gary... Mel..." Ryan whispered, his voice dry. 

He turned his head to look in the other direction. 

The eighteen passenger carriages of the Indian Pacific were still rolling down the tracks, their wheels screaming as the automatic brakes ground them to a halt. They were throwing up a massive cloud of gray friction smoke and dust. 

Carriage A, the lead car, rolled past where Ryan lay. The screech of the brakes was deafening, the metal wheels protesting before they finally came to a complete, silent stop. 

The first carriage stood barely twenty meters from where Ryan sat helpless on the ground. 

Through the shattered window of the vestibule door, the young woman zombie was staring at him. Her face was bloody, her eyes blank. She pressed her nose against the broken frame, sniffing the air as she caught the scent of the fresh blood pooling on the ballast. 

Behind her, the passenger cars were a cage of movement. The dead were crowding the windows, their hands slapping against the glass.

Chapter 9: The Spill

The silence of the Nullarbor was a heavy weight, broken only by the ticking of the cooling brakes on the passenger cars. 

Creak. Pop.

The shattered vestibule door of Carriage A groaned. The frame, bent in the crash and under the collective pressure of the dead inside, finally gave way. The metal latch snapped, and the door swung wide open, hanging by a single hinge. 

The young woman zombie tumbled out, landing face-down on the sharp limestone ballast. She didn't make a sound. She slowly pushed herself up with her hands, her joints creaking in the dry heat. Her blank, milky eyes locked onto Ryan. 

Behind her, the rest of the carriage poured out. 

They fell from the door landing like a slow, gray waterfall, tumbling onto the gravel. One by one, they pushed themselves back up, their boots scraping on the stones. 

Along the length of the eighteen carriages, more doors were opening. The emergency exit releases inside the cabins, pulled by passengers before they turned, let the doors slide back. From the sleeper berths, the coach lounges, and the dining cars, the dead began to spill out onto the red sand of the desert. 

They were clad in travel gear—shorts, t-shirts, sun hats—now torn and stained with blood. In the scorching heat of the Nullarbor, their flesh was already starting to dry and tighten, turning a leathery gray. 

They shuffled down the gravel embankment, their feet kicking up tiny plumes of dust. 

"No," Ryan muttered, his heart freezing. 

He dug his elbows into the dirt, trying to drag his body backward. The pain in his broken leg was a white-hot hammer, making him scream, but he kept pulling himself inch by inch. His fingers tore on the sharp limestone ballast, leaving bloody tracks in the dust. 

But it was useless. 

From all sides, the dead were converging. Hundreds of shuffling figures were closing the distance, their hands raised, their dry jaws snapping in the hot wind. 

The circle was shrinking.

Chapter 10: The Horizon

The NR-class locomotive roared west, its twin diesel engines humming a steady, powerful song. The train was light now, only the engine and the power van, flying down the straight silver rails toward the Western Australian border. 

In the cab, Mel stood at the rear window, her forehead pressed against the warm glass. 

Behind them, the Nullarbor Plain stretched out for miles—a flat, featureless table of red earth under a setting sun that turned the sky a deep, bloody crimson. 

On the horizon, the eighteen passenger carriages of the Indian Pacific were a tiny, thin silver line, standing motionless on the track. Through the heat haze and the swirling red dust, the carriages looked like a toy train left in the sand. 

Mel squinted, her eyes filling with tears. 

She couldn't see Ryan. She could only see the silver line of the cars, and around it, a shifting, dark circle of tiny specks that seemed to crawl across the red earth, closing in on the tracks. 

"Gary," Mel whispered, her voice cracking. "He's still out there."

Gary didn't look back. His hand was steady on the throttle. "We couldn't stop, Mel. If we stopped, we'd be joining them. He knew the risk."

"He saved us," she said, a single tear running down her face. 

Gary stared at the straight line of the tracks ahead, vanishing into the dark. "The only way to pay him back is to keep this engine moving. We don't stop until Perth."

In the red desert, the circle had closed. 

Ryan lay on his back, his breath coming in dry, rattling gasps. His rifle had been lost in the fall, its stock broken on the ballast. He had no weapons. Only his hands. 

The young woman zombie crawled over his chest, her weight heavy and cold. Her milky eyes stared down at him, her snapped jaw clicking. Behind her, a dozen dry, leathery hands reached for his neck, his arms, his broken leg. 

Ryan looked past them, up at the grand, bloody sunset of the Nullarbor. 

The engine was gone, a tiny speck on the western horizon, its roar faded into the silence of the desert. 

The first set of teeth sank into his throat, and the red sky went black.

================================================================================

STORY 13: THE DAINTREE
----------------------

Chapter 1: The Green Wall

The air inside the Daintree Rainforest was a hot, liquid steam, thick with the smell of rotting leaves, wet clay, and sulfur from the swamps. Day twenty-one of the collapse, and the wilderness of Far North Queensland felt less like a sanctuary and more like a green furnace. 

Mick stood on the wooden deck of the stilt-supported research cabin, his hands resting on the rough timber railing. He was forty-five, wearing a faded khaki work shirt and a canvas hat, his skin tanned and weathered from a lifetime of handling reptiles. A heavy-duty machete hung in a leather sheath at his hip. 

Below him, the Daintree River was a wide, sluggish ribbon of brown mud, flowing silent and dark between two massive walls of ancient, green jungle. 

"The Cairns radio is completely dead, Mick," Jess said, stepping out of the small lab room. She was twenty-four, a veterinary assistant who had been doing her placement at the station when the city fell. Her hair was tied back in a messy braid, her face smudged with dried mud. She carried a bolt-action hunting rifle over her shoulder. "Nothing but static on all bands. The coastal ferry has been abandoned. We're completely cut off."

"We were cut off the moment the road bridge was blocked," Mick said, his voice low. He didn't look at her; his eyes were scanning the dense tangle of mangrove roots on the opposite bank. "The river is the only way out now."

"We can't take the research boat," Jess said, pointing toward the small aluminum dinghy tied to the wooden dock. "The outboard is too loud. It'll draw every one of them out of the bush."

"We aren't taking the dinghy," Mick said, pointing further down the dock where a larger, flat-bottomed steel airboat sat. A giant aircraft-style propeller, enclosed in a heavy wire cage, dominated the stern. "We take the airboat. It's fast enough to outrun anything in the water, and it can slide right over the shallow sandbars. But the fan shaft is jammed."

Before Jess could reply, a low, rustling sound emerged from the wall of green ferns at the edge of the clearing. 

The palm leaves parted. 

A figure stumbled out onto the muddy bank. It was a tourist, still wearing a colorful Hawaiian shirt, but the fabric was shredded, his chest a dark, hollow cavity. He dragged his right leg, his foot turned completely backward. He didn't look at the stilts; his head was lowered, his mouth opening to release a wet, dry rattle that carried across the water. 

Then, another figure emerged behind him. Then five more. 

They were covered in thick black mud, their skin gray and peeling in the tropical humidity. They began to shuffle toward the water's edge, their dull eyes locking onto the research cabin.

Chapter 2: The Swamped Dead

Jess raised her rifle, her fingers wrapping around the trigger guard, but Mick held up a hand. 

"Don't waste the lead," Mick said, his voice a whisper. "Look at what they're doing."

The lead zombie, the man in the Hawaiian shirt, reached the edge of the muddy bank. He didn't pause. He stepped straight off the mud into the deep, brown water of the Daintree River. 

The water rose to his chest, then his chin. He let out one final, gurgling breath, releasing a cluster of bubbles, before his head disappeared completely beneath the muddy surface. 

Behind him, the other five zombies walked into the river, their bodies sinking into the brown depths without a struggle. 

Jess stepped to the edge of the stilt platform, her eyes scanning the water. The river was silent, its surface smooth and opaque, hiding the muddy bottom. "Did they drown?"

"They don't breathe, Jess," Mick said, his face grim. "They don't drown. They just walk on the bottom. In this current, they'll crawl along the riverbed. They're heading for our dock."

A few meters from the wooden pylons of the dock, the surface of the water bubbled. 

A pale, bloated hand broke the surface, the fingers clawing the air for a second before disappearing back down. Then, a few feet away, a cluster of bubbles rose, carrying a dark smear of decay to the top. 

"They're invisible down there," Jess said, her throat tightening. "If we fall in, or if we have to wade out..."

"That's why we stay on the boat," Mick said, grabbing his machete and a heavy iron crowbar. "But we have to clear that propeller shaft first. The logs and the old nylon net wrapped around it are locking the transmission. If I can't cut it free, the engine will burn out the moment I start it."

He led the way down the wooden stilt stairs toward the dock, his eyes locked on the dark water beneath the planks. The wood groaned under their feet, and the river lapped gently against the pylons, hiding the legions crawling in the mud below.

Chapter 3: The Airboat

The airboat was a flat-bottomed steel hull, designed to slide over the shallow sandbars and weed beds of the estuary. It sat tied to the end of the wooden dock, its massive, cage-enclosed aircraft propeller rising three meters into the humid air. 

Mick stepped onto the deck, the boat rocking gently under his weight. Jess followed, her rifle held ready as she scanned the muddy water surrounding the slips. 

Mick walked to the stern, climbing up onto the raised engine platform. The massive Chevrolet V8 engine sat beneath a steel cowl, connected to the propeller shaft by a heavy belt drive. 

He leaned over the protective wire cage, looking down at the propeller. 

A thick, black nylon commercial fishing net was wrapped tightly around the shaft and the blades, bound together by river silt, thick mangrove branches, and rotting vegetation. It was a solid, fibrous knot, locked tight under the tension of the river's current. 

"Rusted net," Mick muttered, pulling his heavy serrated diving knife from his belt. "It's wrapped three times around the main bearing. If I start the V8 with this jam, the belt will snap or the drive shaft will twist."

"How long will it take to clear?" Jess asked, her eyes scanning the riverbank. 

"Ten, fifteen minutes," Mick said, looking down at the water at the stern. The water here was chest-deep, muddy, and silent. "But I can't reach the bearing from the deck. I have to go into the water."

Jess's face turned pale. "Mick, no. You just saw them walk into the river. They're down there. And... the crocs. The noise from Cairns must have driven them up the estuary."

"The airboat is our only ticket out, Jess," Mick said, his voice firm. "We don't have a choice. You stand on the engine deck. If anything breaks the surface near me, you put a bullet in its head. Headshots only."

Before Mick could lower himself over the transom, a loud, wet creak echoed from the dock behind them. 

At the middle of the wooden slip, a figure rose from the water. 

It was a former deckhand, his skin a bloated, translucent gray, covered in green river weed and thick black mud. His hands gripped the wooden planks of the dock, pulling his upper body up. His jaw snapped, a wet, clicking sound that broke the silence of the rainforest.

Chapter 4: The Swamp Walkers

Crack!

Jess's rifle barked. The bullet struck the mud-covered deckhand zombie in the temple. The head jerked, and the body slipped back into the brown water with a soft splash, leaving a thin trail of dark blood on the surface. 

The sound of the shot was deafening, a sharp slap that echoed back and forth between the high walls of the jungle canopy. For a second, the forest fell silent, the birds and insects silenced by the blast. 

Then the jungle came alive. 

From the dense mangroves on the riverbanks, the rustling began. The green wall of ferns and palm leaves shook as shapes began to move through the thick undergrowth. Dozens of mud-stained, tattered figures emerged, dragging their limbs through the clay as they headed toward the research station. 

In the water, the bubbles rose faster. 

The sound of the gunshot had traveled down the water column, alerting the submerged dead walking the riverbed. A cluster of bubbles broke the surface ten meters away, followed by the top of a gray, hairless head. 

"Mick! They're coming out of the trees!" Jess yelled, chambering another round. "And the water is bubbling!"

"I'm going in," Mick said, his face set. "Keep them off the dock, Jess. Don't let them reach the airboat."

He gripped the transom rail and swung his legs over the stern of the boat. He lowered himself slowly into the murky, brown water. 

His boots touched the riverbed, sinking six inches into the thick, soft clay. The water rose to his chest, warm and turbid. He couldn't see his own waist, let alone what was crawling in the mud beneath him. The river's current pushed against his back, threatening to sweep his feet out from under him. 

Mick reached up, grabbing the jammed propeller shaft. He pressed his body against the steel hull and began to cut the thick nylon net with his serrated diving knife, sawing at the tough, plastic fibers. 

"Hurry, Mick!" Jess called down, her rifle raised as she scanned the clearing. 

Through the trees, the first of the forest horde reached the mud bank, their blank eyes locked on the research station.

Chapter 5: The Feeding Frenzy

The water twenty meters out from the dock erupted in a violent spray of mud and foam. 

A massive, four-meter saltwater crocodile—a prehistoric giant with scales the color of wet coal—had surged from the deep channel. Its jaws clamped shut on the torso of a mud-covered zombie wading through the shallows. 

The crocodile rolled, a classic "death roll" designed to tear prey apart and drown it. The water churned, spray flying into the mangroves. 

But there was no scream. No struggle for air. 

As the crocodile rolled, the zombie's head broke the surface. Its mouth was open, snapping aimlessly. Its gray, mud-stained arms reached out, its fingers clawing violently at the crocodile's sensitive nostrils and the soft skin around its yellow eyes. 

From the muddy depths, three more zombies emerged, walking toward the thrashing reptile. They threw their bodies onto the crocodile's back, their teeth sinking into the thick leather hide, their hands pulling at its legs. 

The crocodile, confused and overwhelmed by prey that didn't bleed, didn't panic, and didn't die, thrashed wildly before being dragged down into the dark water by the collective weight of the dead. The surface bubbled violently, then went still. 

"The crocs..." Jess whispered, her rifle shaking in her hands. "They're swarming them."

"The rules of the dead, Jess," Mick said, his knife ripping through another layer of nylon. His forehead was slick with sweat, his shoulder muscles straining. "Animals don't stop them. Unless they crush the skull, the dead just keep clawing. The crocs are going to get overwhelmed."

Below his waist, Mick felt the water shift. 

The current seemed to eddy, a slow, heavy drag pulling at his trousers. He looked down, but the water was a solid brown wall. He couldn't see what was moving near his feet. 

"Mick, we have more on the bank!" Jess called out. 

Three zombies had stepped off the mud, their heads disappearing beneath the water. They were walking the riverbed, heading directly toward the stern where Mick stood. 

"I've got two layers left!" Mick yelled, his blade sawing frantically. "Keep your eyes on the wash!"

Chapter 6: Into the Shallows

The heat in the river was stifling, the humidity wrapping around Mick's chest like a wet blanket. He worked by touch, his fingers sliding along the cold metal of the propeller shaft, feeling the tight, plastic strands of the fishing net. 

His serrated knife sliced through another thick cluster of nylon. The net began to unravel, the tension releasing. 

"Mick! Bubbles! Right behind you!" Jess screamed, leaning over the engine cage. 

Mick didn't look. He kept his shoulder pressed against the steel hull, sawing at the final loop of rope wrapped around the main bearing. 

"How close?" he grunted. 

"Three meters! I can see the shape!" Jess raised her rifle, aiming into the brown water near the stern. 

The surface of the river bulged. A hand—bloated, yellowed, and covered in river silt—broke the water, the fingers spreading as they clawed toward Mick's shirt. 

Crack!

Jess fired. The bullet punched into the water, releasing a spray of brown mud. The shot hit the zombie's shoulder, tearing the flesh, but the figure didn't stop. It kept shuffling forward, the water rising to its chin as its face broke the surface. 

It was a former fisherman, his eyes gone, his nose eaten away by crabs. 

Mick kicked back with his right leg, his heavy leather boot striking the zombie's chest. The impact felt like hitting a bag of wet sand. The zombie stumbled back into the current, but its hands were still reaching. 

"Almost... done!" Mick roared, putting all his strength into a final, sawing motion. 

The last strand of the nylon net snapped. The heavy wood log that had been wedged against the blade slid free, drifting away in the current. The propeller shaft spun freely. 

"It's clear! Start the engine, Jess!" Mick yelled, grabbing the metal transom rail of the airboat to pull himself out of the water.

Chapter 7: The Mud Attack

Jess scrambled to the pilot's console, her wet fingers sliding on the plastic dashboard. She reached for the key, turning it. 

The V8 engine starter groaned. Wuh-wuh-wuh... 

"Come on, start!" Jess cried, pumping the foot throttle. 

On the dock, the rustling had turned into a swarm. Five zombies had climbed onto the wooden planks, their mud-slicked feet making a wet, slapping sound as they shuffled toward the bow of the airboat. They were led by a tourist woman in a torn sun hat, her face a mask of dried clay and dried blood. 

Jess abandoned the ignition key, grabbing her rifle. She ran to the bow, aiming at the lead zombie. 

Crack!

The shot caught the woman in the throat, tearing a chunk of flesh away, but she kept shuffling forward. 

"The brain, Jess!" Mick yelled from the stern, his hands slipping on the wet steel transom. 

Jess chambered another round, her hands shaking. She fired again, the bullet shattering the woman's temple. The zombie collapsed, blocking the narrow deck, but the four behind her kept coming. 

At the stern, Mick was trying to pull his hips over the high transom rail. 

Suddenly, a cold, heavy grip wrapped around his left ankle beneath the muddy surface. 

Mick's breath hitched. Before he could kick free, the force pulled downward. The grip was immensely strong, the dead weight of a submerged zombie dragging him down into the clay. 

Mick's fingers slipped from the steel rail. He went under, his chin dipping beneath the brown, sulfur-smelling water. He swallowed a mouthful of the muddy river, coughing and choking as his hands clawed desperately for the transom.

Chapter 8: The Propeller Cleared

Jess heard the splash and Mick's choked cry. She spun around, leaving the bow undefended, and sprinted back to the stern. 

Mick's head broke the surface, his face covered in brown mud. He was gasping, coughing up river water, his fingers clawing at the smooth steel transom. 

"Something's... got my leg!" Mick choked out, his eyes wide. 

Jess dropped her rifle on the deck, leaning over the rail. She grabbed Mick's wrists, her muscles straining as she tried to pull his forty-five-kilogram frame out of the river's mud. The current was pushing against him, adding to the drag. 

Beside Mick's head, the water bubbled. 

A face broke the surface—a teenage boy, his skin a swollen, translucent gray, his nose missing. His jaw snapped, his teeth clicking inches from Mick's ear. 

Jess screamed. She released her right hand, grabbed the rifle from the deck, and pointed the barrel down into the water, pressing it directly against the teenager's temple. 

Thud.

The shot was a muffled thump under the water. The zombie's head collapsed, and the body sank back into the mud. 

The grip on Mick's ankle went slack. 

With a final, desperate heave, Jess pulled Mick upward. Mick grabbed the transom rail, dragged his hips over the edge, and rolled onto the steel deck of the airboat. He lay there, coughing up brown river silt, his chest heaving. 

"Mick! Are you okay?" Jess asked, kneeling beside him. 

Mick didn't answer. He was staring at his left leg. 

The cuff of his khaki trousers was torn. A deep, jagged crescent of red was spreading through the fabric. Mick reached down, his hand shaking as he pulled the cloth back. 

A bite mark—deep, blue-rimmed, and bleeding heavily—was carved into the flesh just above his ankle. 

Jess stared at it, her breath catching. 

Mick closed his eyes, his head falling back against the deck. He knew the rules. Everyone knew the rules.

Chapter 9: The Bite

"Mick, we have to cauterize it, we have to—" Jess panicked, her voice high and erratic. 

"There's no time," Mick grunted, his face turning pale as the shock and the heat of the fever began to set in. "They're on the deck."

The tourist zombies had cleared the obstacle at the bow. They stepped onto the airboat's metal deck, their hands reaching out, their jaws snapping. 

Mick dragged himself to the pilot's console. He gripped the steering lever, turned the ignition key, and slammed his foot down on the throttle pedal. 

Wuh-wuh—ROAR.

The V8 engine caught, roaring to life with a deafening, thunderous scream that shook the steel hull. 

The massive, cage-enclosed propeller at the stern spun into a blur, howling like a cyclone. The blast of air from the giant fan was a physical force, blowing a gale of exhaust, wind, and river spray backward. 

The airboat surged forward. 

The rotten hemp mooring lines snapped with a loud crack. The flat-bottomed boat shot away from the dock, the sudden acceleration throwing the zombies at the bow off balance. They slid across the metal deck, tumbling over the side into the churning brown foam of the prop wash. 

Jess fell onto the passenger bench, her ears ringing from the engine's roar. 

The airboat planed, rising onto the surface of the water as it accelerated down the narrow river channel. 

Mick steered the boat into the center of the river, his hand shaking on the control lever. His forehead was slick with sweat, his eyes starting to glaze over as the infection's fever began to climb. He looked at Jess, his chest heaving. 

"Jess," Mick gasped, his voice raspy. "You... you have to drive. I'm losing my grip."

He slid out of the pilot's seat, collapsing onto the metal deck. Jess scrambled to the helm, grabbing the steering lever and stabilizing the boat as it sped down the winding, mangrove-bordered channel.

Chapter 10: The Estuary

The Daintree River was widening, the dense green walls of the rainforest slowly giving way to the salt-marshes of the estuary. The sun was setting, casting long, orange beams of light that turned the brown water into a sheet of liquid copper. 

Jess stood at the helm, her hands white on the steering lever. The wind from the airboat's V8 fan whipped her hair across her face, carrying the salty tang of the nearby ocean. 

Mick lay on the deck behind her, his back resting against the engine cowling. His shivers had turned into violent convulsions, his skin burning hot to the touch. His eyes were open but glassy, staring up at the orange sky. 

"Jess," Mick whispered, his voice dry. 

Jess looked back, tears streaming down her dusty face. "I'm here, Mick. We're almost to the mouth. We're almost at the ocean."

"Listen to me," Mick gasped, his teeth clicking. "The rules... you know the rules. Any cause of death. The fever will kill me in a few minutes, and then I'll get back up. If I turn in this boat, you won't be able to drive. You'll crash."

"Mick, please," she sobbed. 

"The estuary is full of crocs," Mick said, a faint, rattle-like laugh escaping his throat. "Better to feed them than to join the shamblers. At least they'll tear the brain."

He began to drag his body toward the low steel gunwale of the airboat, his boots scraping on the metal floor. 

"Mick! No! Stay there!" Jess screamed, trying to reach back with one hand, but the boat hit a small sandbar, the hull sliding violently. She had to grab the steering lever with both hands to avoid flipping the speeding vessel. 

Mick reached the edge. He looked back at her, his eyes clouded, the pupils dilating into black pools. His face was a mask of cold sweat, but there was a quiet determination in his gaze. 

"Don't stop the boat, Jess," Mick said. "Get to the ocean. Find the charter. If... if you see me again, destroy the brain."

He rolled his body over the low gunwale. 

He fell into the wake, a brief splash of white foam before the dark water of the estuary swallowed him. Jess watched the spot in the mirror, but there was nothing—only the wide, copper-colored river and the dark shadows of the mangroves. 

She turned back to the helm, her throat tight, her chest heaving with dry, silent sobs. 

The airboat shot out of the mouth of the Daintree River, entering the vast, empty gray of the Coral Sea as the last light of the sun vanished behind the mountains, leaving her alone in the dark.

================================================================================

STORY 14: THE VINTAGE
---------------------

Chapter 1: The Stone Cellar

The air inside the underground stone cellar of the Barossa Valley winery was cool, damp, and thick with the heavy, sweet scent of fermenting Shiraz grapes. Day thirty of the collapse, and the limestone walls felt like a medieval fortress, shielding them from the horrors of the scorched plains above. 

Julian stood on the oak walkway above a massive, five-thousand-liter fermentation vat, holding a long stainless-steel plunger. He was thirty-eight, wearing a wine-stained canvas apron over a gray shirt, his face lined with dark circles and stubble. He slowly plunged the cap of grape skins back down into the bubbling, purple juice. 

"The fermentation is active, Tess," Julian said, his voice flat, echoing in the vaulted stone chamber. "The yeast is eating the sugar. But active fermentation releases carbon dioxide. Without the exhaust fans, this room will turn into a gas chamber."

Tess sat on a stack of oak barrels in the corner, her knees pulled to her chest. She was twenty-four, a French seasonal worker from Bordeaux who had been stranded here when the airports closed. Her dark hair was tied back, and a worn wool blanket was wrapped around her shoulders. 

"How much fuel is left in the generator?" she asked, her voice quiet. 

"Less than ten liters," Julian said, setting the plunger aside. He walked to the control panel on the wall. A green light illuminated the switch for the ventilation system, its steady hum vibrating through the stone floor. "That's maybe four hours of run time. If the generator dies, the fans stop. The CO2 from these tanks will settle on the floor, and eventually, it will rise past our heads."

"We can't just open the doors," Tess said, looking toward the heavy, iron-strapped oak double doors that led up to the courtyard. 

"No," Julian agreed. 

Through the thick timber, a faint, dry scratching sound was constant—the sound of fingernails dragging across the wood, accompanied by low, guttural rasping. 

The dead were waiting in the courtyard. The winery's generator had been drawing them out of the surrounding vineyards for weeks, a slow, shambling circle closing in on the old stone estate. 

"We need to get to the fuel shed," Julian said, grabbing a heavy iron wrench and a five-gallon plastic jerrycan. "If we don't bring back enough diesel to keep the generator running, we won't survive the night in this cellar."

Chapter 2: The Vines

Julian climbed the narrow stone steps to the high gallery, where a row of horizontal ventilation slits looked out over the estate at ground level. He pressed his face to the cool limestone, looking out through the iron bars. 

The afternoon sun was hot, baking the red clay of the Barossa Valley. The rolling hills were lined with neat, parallel rows of ancient Shiraz grapevines, their green leaves thick and heavy with unharvested fruit. 

But the beauty of the landscape was ruined by the figures. 

Dotted between the rows of vines were dozens of sun-baked, dried zombies. Under the dry, hot South Australian sun, their skin had turned a leathery, dark parchment, stretched tight over their cheekbones. They shambled slowly, their heads turning toward the slight hum of the winery's diesel generator. The thick leaves of the vines hid many of them, making it impossible to know how many were lurking in the shadows of the rows. 

"They're everywhere," Julian whispered. 

Tess climbed up behind him, peering through the adjacent slit. "The courtyard has at least ten of them. If we walk out the main cellar doors, they'll surround us in seconds."

"We don't go through the courtyard," Julian said, pointing to the eastern corner of the cellar wall. "There's an old cellar window that opens directly into the first row of the vineyard. The vines are high enough to cover us. We can use the rows as a corridor to reach the machine shed and the fuel tank. It's about eighty meters."

"But the dead are in the rows too," Tess pointed out, her hand tightening on her wool blanket. 

"We move slow," Julian said. "They don't see well. If we stay low and don't make noise, they'll shuffle past. We get the diesel, we fill the jerrycan, and we slip back."

He descended the stairs, walking to the back of the cellar. He pulled back a heavy iron grating, revealing a low, narrow stone chute used for venting air. It opened directly into the dirt at the base of the first row of grapevines. 

"I'll go first," Julian said, shoving the plastic jerrycan through the opening.

Chapter 3: The Generator Room

Julian squeezed through the narrow stone chute, landing on his elbows in the hot, red Barossa dirt. The dust rose in a tiny cloud, smelling of iron and dry clay. He reached back, helping Tess slide out of the opening. 

They lay flat in the dirt, the low-hanging branches of the grapevines forming a dense, green canopy above their heads. The hum of the diesel generator was louder here, a steady, mechanical thrum that vibrated through the earth. 

"Stay low," Julian whispered, grabbing the plastic handle of the empty jerrycan. 

They began to crawl on their hands and knees along the dusty red path between the vine rows. The grapevines were old, their gnarled, thick trunks rising like twisted wooden pillars every few feet. The green leaves and clusters of dark purple grapes hung low, blocking the sun but also reducing their line of sight to a few meters. 

Rustle. Rustle.

Julian froze, his hand dropping to the dirt. Tess stopped right behind him, her breath catching. 

On the other side of the grapevine row, barely two meters away, a figure was shuffling. Through the gaps in the leaves, Julian could see the faded fabric of a blue polo shirt and a leathery, sun-baked arm. The zombie's feet dragged in the red dirt, making a dry, scraping sound. 

It stopped, its head turning toward the hum of the generator. It stood there for a long, agonizing minute, its gray nostrils flaring as if trying to catch a scent in the hot wind. 

Tess pressed her face into the dirt, her fingers clawing the earth as she fought the urge to cough. 

Finally, the zombie took a slow step forward, its boots dragging as it shuffled down the row away from them. 

Julian let out a silent breath. He gestured to Tess, and they resumed their crawl, keeping their heads below the leaf line. 

They reached the end of the row. The back wall of the fuel shed—a corrugated iron outbuilding next to the main brick generator house—stood barely five meters away across an open gravel path. 

"The door is around the side," Julian whispered, his forehead slick with sweat. "We have to cross the open."

Chapter 4: Row by Row

They dashed across the five meters of open gravel, their boots crunching softly on the stones. Julian reached the side door of the fuel shed—a simple latch on a corrugated iron panel. He slid the metal bar back, the iron squeaking with a sharp, dry scratch that made them both wince. 

They slipped inside, Julian sliding the door shut behind them. 

The interior of the shed was a dark, baking oven. The heat was intense, the air thick with the fumes of gasoline, kerosene, and diesel. In the corner sat a massive, blue 200-liter steel drum. 

Julian set the jerrycan down under the drum's manual hand-pump nozzle. He gripped the lever and began to pump, moving it in a steady, rhythmic cycle. 

Clack-clack. Clack-clack.

The sound of the pump was metallic and sharp, vibrating inside the hollow iron shed. 

"Julian," Tess whispered, her face pressed to a gap in the corrugated iron wall. "Two of them. In the vineyard row. They heard the pump."

Julian looked through the gap. Two zombies—one wearing a torn straw sun hat, the other in grease-stained overalls—had stopped their aimless wandering. They turned their heads toward the shed, their dried, leathery ears catching the rhythmic sound. They began to shuffle toward the door, their feet dragging in the gravel. 

"I'm half full," Julian muttered, sweat dripping from his chin onto the jerrycan. He pumped faster, his forearm muscles burning. 

Clack-clack-clack.

Outside, the hum of the diesel generator suddenly shifted. 

The steady, low rumble of the V8 engine began to cough, the pitch fluctuating as the fuel line sucked in the last drops of diesel from the primary tank. The engine shuddered, restarted, then coughed again. 

The change in the sound was like a signal. 

The two zombies outside accelerated their crawl, their dry hands slapping against the corrugated iron wall of the shed. The metal panels rattled, a hollow, booming sound that echoed inside the vault.

Chapter 5: The Fuel Shed

Sputter... shudder... silent.

The generator let out one final, gasping cough and died. 

The hum that had vibrated through the winery for weeks vanished, replaced by a sudden, heavy silence. In the engine room next door, the cooling fans spun down, the high-pitched whistle fading to nothing. 

"The fans are off," Julian said, his voice dropping. "The CO2 is starting to collect on the cellar floor. We have to get back. Now."

He tightened the plastic cap on the jerrycan, the fuel sloshing inside. It weighed twenty kilograms, a heavy, awkward load. 

Bam!

A hand slammed against the door latch from the outside. The metal bar rattled. The two zombies were right outside, their dried jaws clicking as they smelled the living through the gaps in the sheet metal. 

Julian grabbed a heavy steel crescent wrench from a workbench. "Tess, when I open the latch, we push the door hard. Don't stop."

Tess nodded, her face slick with sweat, her hands gripping the wooden frame of the door. 

"One... two... three!" Julian roared. 

He threw the latch, and they both slammed their shoulders against the door. 

The corrugated iron door burst outward, hitting the first zombie—the man in the overalls—square in the chest. The creature fell backward into the gravel. The second zombie, the woman in the straw hat, lunged through the opening, her gray fingers clawing for Tess's face. 

Julian swung the heavy steel wrench in a tight, brutal arc. 

Crack.

The metal bar caught the zombie in the side of the skull. The leathery skin split, and the bone caved in like a dry gourd. She collapsed instantly, going limp on the gravel. 

"Go!" Julian yelled, grabbing the jerrycan. 

They ran out of the shed, heading back toward the vineyard rows. But the sudden silence of the generator had changed the behavior of the horde. 

Dozens of zombies that had been shuffling toward the outbuilding now stopped. They turned, their blank, milky eyes scanning the open gravel yard. They saw Julian and Tess. 

A red-dusted wave of the dead began to spill out of the grapevine rows, blocking their path back to the cellar chute.

Chapter 6: The Retreat

"This row!" Julian yelled, shoving Tess into a gap between the vines. 

They ran, their boots kicking up clouds of red dust. Behind them, the dry, scraping sound of dozens of feet was loud on the gravel. The dead were shuffling into the vineyard rows, their arms tearing through the leaves to grab them. 

A hand caught Julian's shoulder, the nails ripping his shirt. Julian spun, driving the wrench into the zombie's nose, breaking the bone and sending it stumbling back into the vines. 

He scrambled forward, the heavy jerrycan straining his arm. 

They reached the eastern cellar wall. The low stone chute was open, a dark hole in the limestone. 

"In! Tess, go!" Julian gasped, tossing the jerrycan through the opening. 

Tess threw herself onto the dirt, crawling headfirst into the narrow stone tunnel. 

Behind them, a zombie—a former harvester wearing a torn flannel shirt—lunged out of the vines. His gray hand shot forward, grabbing Tess's ankle. 

"Mick!" she shrieked, her body jerking to a stop. 

Julian didn't hesitate. He brought the heavy wrench down onto the zombie's forearm, breaking the bone. The grip released, and Tess slid down into the dark cellar with a soft crash. 

Julian scrambled into the chute, his knees scraping the stone. He pulled his legs inside and grabbed the heavy iron grating. 

Clang.

He slammed the grate shut, sliding the locking bolt into place. 

Outside, a dozen gray, dried hands slammed against the bars, their fingers clawing at the empty space. Their faces pressed against the grate, their yellow teeth clicking in the light. 

Julian slid down the chute, landing in the pitch-black cellar. 

"Tess?" he panted, his hands feeling the cold stone floor. 

"I'm here," her voice came from the dark, shaking. 

Julian clicked on his pocket flashlight. The beam illuminated the concrete floor. The silence inside the cellar was absolute now, and the air was still—too still. 

He looked at the jerrycan of diesel sitting on the floor. 

"We got the fuel," Tess said, a trace of hope in her voice. 

"It doesn't matter," Julian said, his voice flat. "The generator is in the outbuilding. The courtyard is swarmed. We can't go back out to fill the tank. We're locked in. And the gas is rising."

Chapter 7: The Heavy Gas

The cellar was silent, except for a soft, rhythmic sound that filled the darkness. 

Pop... pop... pop.

It was the sound of carbon dioxide bubbles breaking the surface of the active fermentation vats. The yeast was still working in the dark, converting the grape sugars into alcohol, releasing tons of invisible, odorless, and heavy gas. 

Julian sat on the bottom step of the stone gallery stairs, his flashlight turned off to conserve the battery. 

"The air," Tess said, her voice raspy. She let out a long, dry breath. "It feels... heavy. Like trying to breathe warm syrup."

"It's the carbon dioxide," Julian said, his voice echoing. "It's heavier than oxygen. It pools on the floor first, filling the lower corners like water in a tank. Then it rises."

He clicked his flashlight on, shining the beam toward the floor. 

The air looked clear. You couldn't see the gas, but Julian knew it was there. He reached down, holding his hand near the floor. He could feel it—a subtle, heavy warmth that lacked the cool draft of normal air. 

"How much gas do those tanks release?" Tess asked, her breathing noticeably faster now, her chest rising and falling in a rapid, shallow pattern. 

"During peak fermentation? Hundreds of liters an hour," Julian said. "Normally, the exhaust fans pull it out and vent it through the roof. But now... it has nowhere to go. It's trapped in this vault with us."

"We need to go higher," Tess said, standing up. 

"The gallery stairs," Julian agreed. 

They climbed the narrow stone steps to the high gallery platform, six meters above the cellar floor, near the ventilation slits. Up here, the air was slightly cooler, but the wind blowing through the iron grates was hot, carrying the smell of the dead waiting outside. 

Julian sat on the top step, his back against the stone wall. His head was already throbbing—a dull, pulsing headache at the temples, the first sign of carbon dioxide poisoning. 

Beside him, Tess shivered, her hand clutching her throat. Her breathing was getting louder, a soft, wheezing rattle in her chest.

Chapter 8: The Silent Killer

The headache was a blinding white light behind Julian's eyes. His heart raced, his pulse drumming a frantic rhythm in his ears. His lungs burned, demanding oxygen that wasn't there. 

Beside him, Tess was in agony. 

She lay on her side on the narrow stone platform, her hands clawing at the collar of her jacket. Her wheezing was a sharp, whistle-like rattle that cut through the silence of the vault. 

"Tess," Julian gasped, his own voice faint. "The inhaler... where is it?"

"Empty," she choked out, her lips turning a dark, bruised blue. "Two days... ago."

Julian clicked his flashlight on. The beam caught her face. Her skin was a pale, waxen white, her lips and fingernails showing the deep blue of cyanosis—oxygen starvation. Her chest was heaving, her throat muscles straining as she tried to force air into her lungs, but the rising carbon dioxide layer had reached the gallery height. 

"We need... to break... the slits," Julian muttered, his vision tunneling, his hands shaking as he reached for the heavy iron wrench on his belt. 

He tried to stand, but his legs were lead. He fell back onto the stone steps, the wrench slipping from his fingers and clattering down the stairs, landing with a hollow ring in the dark below. 

"Julian..." Tess gasped. 

She let out one long, whistling breath. Her eyes rolled back, and her hands went limp, falling to the stone. Her chest stopped moving. 

"Tess! No!" Julian dragged himself toward her, his hands checking her neck. 

There was no pulse. Her skin was already turning cold. 

She was dead, suffocated by the rising gas. 

Julian lay beside her, his head spinning, his vision dissolving into gray static. He looked at her face, his heart freezing as he remembered the rules of the dead. 

Universal reanimation.

No bite was needed. If you die of any cause, you get back up, unless the brain is destroyed. 

He needed to find the wrench. He needed to smash her skull before it was too late. But he couldn't move. He lay flat on the stone, his limbs paralyzed by the carbon dioxide, watching her in the dim glow of the flashlight.

Chapter 9: The Reanimation

Julian lay paralyzed, his cheek pressed against the cold limestone step. The flashlight had rolled, its beam casting a long, bright corridor of light across Tess's limp body. 

His lungs burned. His body was shutting down, the carbon dioxide level in his blood reaching a lethal concentration. He was slipping into unconsciousness, his mind drifting. 

Twitch.

Julian's eyes widened. 

Tess's right hand, lying limp in the dust, twitched. The fingers curled inward. 

Then, a sudden, wet gasp of trapped air escaped her throat—a dry, mechanical rattle. Her head jerked sideways. 

Slowly, the reanimated girl sat up. 

Her dark hair fell over her face, but through the strands, Julian could see her eyes. They were wide, blank, and milky white, reflecting the beam of the flashlight. She didn't look at the sky, or the stone walls; she turned her head toward him, her nostrils flaring as she caught the scent of his living flesh. 

She lunged. 

Her cold, gray fingers wrapped around Julian's throat, squeezing with terrifying, dead strength. 

The shock of the cold flesh and the constriction of his airway sent a sudden, desperate surge of adrenaline through Julian's body. The survival instinct overrode the CO2 paralysis for a fraction of a second. 

"Get... off!" Julian choked, his hands clawing at her wrists. 

They rolled on the narrow stone platform, a silent, desperate struggle at the edge of the abyss. Tess's teeth snapped inches from his throat, her jaws clicking in the dark. 

With a final, desperate heave, Julian shoved his boots into her chest. 

He pushed. 

Tess slid backward, her hand losing its grip on his collar. She tumbled over the concrete edge of the gallery railing. 

She fell silently through the dark. 

A heavy, bone-crushing thud echoed from the concrete floor six meters below, followed by the sound of breaking wood as she hit a rack of empty bottles. 

Julian lay on the edge of the platform, gasping, his throat raw. He looked down into the black void. 

She was down there. Her legs were probably broken, but she didn't feel pain. She would crawl. She would find the stairs.

Chapter 10: The Vault

Julian knew he had minutes. The carbon dioxide was rising, a silent tide that would soon submerge the gallery platform. He had to escape, even if it meant facing the courtyard. 

He took one deep breath of the thin, burning air at the ceiling level, held it, and scrambled down the stone steps into the pitch black of the cellar floor. 

His boots splashed into the invisible, heavy gas layer. The air down here was freezing, devoid of oxygen. 

He ran toward the main double doors, his flashlight beam slicing through the darkness. 

Scritch. Scritch.

To his right, near the collapsed rack of bottles, Tess was moving. Her legs were broken, bent backward at the knees, but she didn't care. She was crawling across the concrete floor, her gray hands dragging her body forward, her milky eyes locked on his flashlight. 

Julian ignored her, reaching the oak doors. He grabbed the heavy iron bolt and yanked it back. It slid with a metallic shriek. 

He threw his weight against the doors to push them open. 

They didn't budge. 

Julian slammed his shoulder into the wood, his chest burning as he held his breath. The doors were locked from the outside—the external security bars had been dropped by the escaping staff weeks ago, and the collective weight of the zombies in the courtyard, pressed tight against the timber, had jammed the latch. 

Thud. Thud.

From the other side of the oak, the scratching was frantic. The dead heard his hammering. They were pressing their bodies against the wood, sealing him in. 

Julian's lungs screamed. He couldn't hold his breath any longer. He gasped, taking in a deep breath of the heavy, sweet air. 

His vision exploded in black spots. His knees buckled, and he collapsed onto the concrete floor, the jerrycan of diesel sitting uselessly beside him. He lay on his side, his hands clawing at his throat as the carbon dioxide suffocated him. 

The flashlight fell from his hand, rolling across the floor. 

In the dim, angled light, a shadow crawled. 

Tess was barely two meters away now. She dragged her broken legs through the pools of purple wine, her gray fingers reaching for his boots. Her jaw snapped, a wet, hollow sound in the silent cellar. 

Julian lay in the dark, his vision fading to black, listening to the soft, rhythmic pop-pop-pop of the fermenting Shiraz as the crawling hands reached his ankles.

================================================================================

STORY 15: THE LOOKOUT
---------------------

Chapter 1: The Orange Sky

The sky above the Adelaide Hills was no longer blue. It was a bruised, heavy shade of ochre, bleeding into a toxic orange where the sun struggled to pierce the canopy of smoke. Clint stood on the wooden gallery of the Mount Lofty lookout tower, his calloused hands gripping the green steel railing. Below him, the valley was a sea of grey haze, the dense eucalyptus forest of Stirling and Crafers swallowed by the creeping front of a massive bushfire.

"Wind’s shifting, Clint," Meg said, her voice muffled by the damp CFS bandana tied over her nose and mouth. She stepped out of the glass-walled cabin, holding a pair of heavy binoculars. "It's coming straight up the eastern ridge. How long until the road is completely cut?"

Clint didn't answer immediately. He adjusted his yellow firefighting helmet, squinting into the hot, ash-laden wind. The heat was already rising, carrying the scent of vaporized eucalypt oil and burning resin. The utility grid had failed three days ago, taking the municipal water pumps and mobile towers with it. Out here, they were on their own.

"It's already cut," Clint said, his voice gravelly. "Saw the radio tower road go under twenty minutes ago. The gums along the cutting are crowning. Nothin' gets through that."

Meg lowered the binoculars. Her eyes, red-rimmed from the soot, widened. "So we're stuck here. In a wooden box fifty feet off the deck."

"We've got the pump, Meg," Clint replied, pointing down to the steel tank at the base of the tower structure. "And the roof sprinklers. If we keep the wet-down running, the firefront will pass over us. We just need to stay clean, stay low, and keep that diesel motor humming."

He turned back to the horizon, but as he did, his eyes caught a movement in the grey haze at the foot of the ridge. A shape, slow and mechanical, was staggering out of the burning scrub.

Then another. And another.

Chapter 2: The Ash Rain

The wind howled through the steel lattice of the tower, carrying with it a silent, choking rain of grey ash. It settled on Clint’s shoulders, on the glass panes of the cabin, and on Meg’s outstretched hands. The smell of burning leaves was thick enough to taste, a dry, bitter coat on the tongue.

Meg raised the binoculars again, her hands shaking slightly. "Clint... what are they doing? Why are they coming up here? The fire is right behind them."

Clint took the binoculars from her. Through the magnifying lenses, the shapes resolved into terrifying clarity. They weren't tourists or firefighters fleeing the flames. Their clothes were charred, hanging in blackened tatters, but they didn't run. They shambled, their movements sluggish and jerky, stepping over burning logs and hot coals without a flinch. 

"They're not running from it," Clint whispered, his heart hammering against his ribs. "They don't feel it."
