One of the figures, a man in a scorched business suit, looked up. His skin was blistered and peeling, but his milky, dead eyes were locked onto the tower. He dragged a broken leg behind him, leaving a trail of ash and dark, viscous fluid on the rocks. Behind him, dozens more crawled out of the smoke-filled gully.

"We need to get the pump running," Meg said, her voice rising in panic. "If they reach the base, they'll trap us up here, and the fire... Clint, the fire is coming!"

Clint looked from the shuffling horde to the massive column of black smoke rising from the trees below. The air was growing hot, the convection currents tossing burning bark onto the gallery deck.

"Right," Clint said, checking the bolt-action rifle slung over his shoulder. "Grab the jerrycan and the hoses from the cabin. We're going down."

Chapter 3: The Pump

The descent was a nightmare of heat and noise. The wind screamed through the structural steel, and the roar of the bushfire in the valley was a deep, guttural bass that vibrated through Clint's boots. They climbed down the open steel stairs, the metal handrails already uncomfortably hot to the touch. 

At the base of the tower, the air was thick with drifting embers. The water storage tank—a massive, corrugated iron cylinder—sat next to a small, weatherboard pump shed. 

"Get the fuel!" Clint yelled over the roar of the wind. He pointed to the heavy green plastic jerrycan sitting in the corner of the shed. 

Meg scrambled inside, her boots kicking up dust and dry pine needles. The pump shed was small, smelling of grease and stale petrol. The engine itself, a red Honda four-stroke connected to a high-pressure water pump, sat bolted to a concrete slab. 

Clint knelt beside it, his hands moving with practiced efficiency. He checked the oil, then reached for the starter cord. The firefront was less than half a kilometer away now, a wall of shifting orange light visible through the dense canopy of the surrounding stringybark trees. 

"Here's the petrol!" Meg gasped, dragging the heavy jerrycan out. She unscrewed the cap, and the sharp, volatile scent of fuel hit the hot air.

As Clint took the can, a low, dry rasp sounded from the shadows behind the water tank. 

Clint froze. Through the drifting smoke, a charred hand gripped the edge of the corrugated iron.

Chapter 4: The Smoldering Horde

The figure that shuffled from behind the tank was unrecognizable as human. Its clothes had melted into its flesh, forming a black, bubbled armor of charred fabric and burnt skin. Small pockets of fire still flickered along its shoulders, fed by the dry cotton of its shirt. It didn't scream. It didn't run. It just kept coming, its jaw hanging open to reveal blackened, soot-stained teeth.

"Clint!" Meg screamed, backing into the pump shed.

Clint dropped the jerrycan, his hand flying to the .303 rifle slung across his back. He brought it up, aiming for the center of the charred face, and pulled the trigger. The crack of the rifle was swallowed by the roar of the wind, but the bullet hit true. The back of the skull blew outward in a shower of black ash and bone. The thing collapsed, hitting the dry dirt with a heavy, dead thud.

But the shot was only a brief reprieve. 

Out of the smoke-filled scrub, more figures appeared. The fire had swept over them, burning away their hair and skin, leaving them as skeletal, smoldering marionettes. One was a former CFS volunteer, his yellow jacket melted and fused to his chest, the plastic helmet melted over his ears. Flames licked at his trousers as he stepped over the body of the first zombie.

"They're carrying the fire," Clint muttered in horror. "They're literal torches."

The smoldering CFS volunteer stumbled toward the open door of the pump shed. Clint cycled the bolt of his rifle, but the chamber jammed, a piece of hot grit wedging the brass casing tight.

Chapter 5: The Fuel Run

"Get back!" Meg yelled, pushing past Clint. She held a heavy steel crowbar she’d grabbed from the tool rack. With a grunt, she swung it in a wide arc, the heavy iron head smashing into the temple of the burning CFS volunteer. The skull cracked with a wet pop, and the creature fell sideways.

But as it fell, the flames engulfing its clothes brushed against the dry, splintered weatherboards of the pump shed. In the desiccated summer heat, the wood caught instantly. A sheet of yellow flame raced up the wall, fed by the volatile fumes of grease and fuel.

"Meg, get the fuel! Now!" Clint roared, hammering at the jammed bolt of his rifle.

Meg lunged into the smoke-filled shed. The heat inside was already searing, the ceiling bubbling with black paint. She grabbed the green jerrycan, but a charred, smoldering hand reached out from the rising flames by the doorway, grabbing the sleeve of her heavy canvas jacket. 

The fabric caught fire, the flames licking hungrily toward her neck.

"Meg!" Clint screamed, lunging forward.

Meg didn't panic. With a desperate wrench, she unzipped the heavy jacket and tore it off her shoulders, leaving the burning garment in the zombie's grip. She stumbled backward out of the shed, clutching the jerrycan to her chest, her shirt singed but her skin intact. Behind her, the pump shed erupted into a roaring inferno, the heat forcing them both back toward the base of the tower.

Chapter 6: The Steel Ladder

"Up! Get up the tower!" Clint ordered, shoving Meg toward the vertical steel ladder that climbed the center of the lattice structure. 

The pump shed was a pillar of fire now, and the intense heat was already starting to blister the paint on the tower's lower struts. The wind was carrying burning embers directly onto the wooden landing above. Clint looked back. The road and the surrounding clearing were crawling with the dead—dozens of charred, smoldering shapes shuffling out of the smoke, drawn by the light of the fire and the sound of their voices.

Meg scrambled up the ladder, the heavy jerrycan clanking against the steel rungs. Clint climbed right behind her, his rifle slung over his back. 

The vertical climb was exhausting. The smoke was thicker here, rising in greasy black plumes from the burning valley. Clint’s lungs burned with every breath. He looked down through the open metal grid of the steps.

Directly below them, the dead had reached the ladder.

A charred figure, its skin burnt to a glossy black, grabbed the bottom rung. It began to climb. It was slow, its movements clumsy, but it didn't hesitate. Behind it, another zombie reached up, its blackened fingers slipping through the open steel mesh of the steps just inches below Clint's boots. Clint kicked down, his heavy work boot crushing the thing's fingers, but another hand reached through the landing grid, grabbing the cuff of his trousers.

Chapter 7: Priming the Lines

Clint kicked backward, the steel-capped heel of his boot shattering the zombie's wrist bones. The hand let go, and the charred figure tumbled backward, crashing through the lattice girders to the ground fifty feet below. Clint scrambled up the remaining rungs, pulling himself onto the wooden gallery deck just behind Meg.

She had already reached the auxiliary pump controls mounted on the external wall of the glass cabin. The small diesel generator that powered the backup pressure pump was housed in a steel locker on the deck. Clint rushed to it, cracked open the fuel tank, and poured the petrol from the jerrycan inside, the fumes whipping away in the howling gale.

"Start it!" Meg gasped, her face pale, soot streaking her forehead.

Clint yanked the starter cord. The engine sputtered, coughed, and died. He pulled again, harder, throwing his weight into the cord. The motor roared to life, a steady, rattling thrum that vibrated through the deck. 

A second later, the overhead sprinkler lines hissed. Jets of mist and water erupted from the pipes lining the roof, washing over the wooden deck and the glass walls of the cabin. The temperature dropped instantly, the cool spray creating a temporary shield against the radiant heat.

But the relief was short-lived. 

The engine's roar suddenly faltered. The sprinkler spray weakened, turning into a pathetic drip. Clint knelt by the pump housing, wiping water from his eyes. Through the spray, he saw a steady stream of dark fuel spraying from a split rubber line near the carburetor.

"The pressure line’s split!" Clint yelled. "It's spraying fuel all over the manifold. If it hits the hot exhaust, the whole deck will go up!"

Chapter 8: The Red Wall

The firefront reached the crest of the ridge. 

It was a wall of pure, roaring red, a hundred feet high, consuming the tall stringybarks in seconds. The sound was deafening, like a freight train passing inches from their ears, accompanied by the sharp, explosive cracks of expanding tree trunks. The wind, whipped into a fire-tornado by the convection currents, screamed through the steel lattice of the tower.

"Get inside, Meg! Now!" Clint screamed, pushing her toward the cabin door. 

He didn't wait for her to answer. He grabbed a roll of self-amalgamating silicone tape from the maintenance locker and climbed onto the exposed gantry platform beside the rattling engine. The heat was immense, a physical weight that blistered the back of his neck despite the dying sprinkler mist. 

Inside the cabin, Meg slammed the heavy wooden door shut. Through the thick glass, she watched Clint working. The smoke was a solid, grey wall now, swallowing the landscape. Inside the sealed room, the air was quickly turning toxic. She began to cough, a deep, hacking sound that tore at her chest. She fell to her knees, clutching her throat.

On the gantry, Clint fought the wind and the heat. His hands shook as he wrapped the tape around the leaking fuel line, the fuel slick and cold against his burnt fingers. He pulled the tape tight, layer after layer, sealing the split. 

The engine's pitch changed, rising back to a steady, confident roar. The overhead sprinklers hissed back to life, drenching the cabin in a thick protective shroud of water.

Clint let out a ragged breath, turning toward the cabin door. But through the wet glass, he saw Meg.

She was lying face down on the floor, motionless.

Chapter 9: The Last Breath

Clint scrambled to the cabin door, threw his weight against the latch, and fell inside. He slammed the door behind him, locking out the worst of the heat, though the room was already filled with a dense, choking haze of grey smoke.

"Meg! Meg, talk to me!" Clint gasped, dropping his rifle and crawling to her side.

He rolled her over. Her skin was a pale, bluish-grey beneath the soot, her lips white. Her chest wasn't moving. Clint grabbed the small, green oxygen cylinder from the wall bracket, cracked the valve, and pressed the mask to her face. 

"Come on, Meg. Breathe. Just breathe," he pleaded, his own lungs burning from the smoke. 

He pressed two fingers to her carotid artery. Nothing. No pulse. He began chest compressions, the glass panels of the cabin vibrating violently as the fire roared outside. He blew air into her lungs, but there was no response. Her body remained limp, her eyes fixed and dilated.

Meg was dead. She had suffocated from the intense smoke and carbon monoxide trapped in the cabin.

Clint sat back on his heels, the oxygen mask slipping from his fingers. A heavy, hollow despair washed over him, louder than the fire storm outside. 

Then, Meg's fingers twitched.

Clint gasped, reaching for her hand. "Meg?"

But her fingers didn't curl in a warm grip. They locked onto his wrist like a cold steel clamp. Meg’s eyes snapped open. The pupils were gone, replaced by a milky, bloodshot glaze. Her jaw unlocked with a dry, clicking sound, and she lunged upward, her teeth snapping inches from his face.

Chapter 10: The Tower of Fire

Clint threw his weight backward, breaking Meg's grip, but he lost his footing on the slick wooden floor and crashed into the metal wall bracket. The oxygen cylinder rolled away, clattering against the glass panes.

Meg scrambled to her feet. The coordination was gone, replaced by the jerky, relentless drive of the dead. She stepped over the oxygen tank, her teeth snapping, her bloodshot eyes locked onto him. 

A sudden, massive shudder ran through the floor. Clint looked out the window. The pump shed below had collapsed, and the fire had caught the main wooden support pillars of the lookout tower. Flames were racing up the dry timber beams, turning the entire structure into a vertical chimney of fire. The air inside the cabin grew hotter, the floorboards starting to smolder.

Clint scrambled to the floor hatch, the only way down to the ladder. He grabbed the handle and yanked it up, but a blast of pure flame and black smoke roared up from the shaft. The ladder below was a column of fire. The exit was blocked.

He was trapped.

Meg lunged again, her cold hands reaching for his collar, pinning him against the glass wall. Through the wet window behind her, Clint could see the Adelaide Hills burning, a sea of fire stretching to the horizon. The glass behind his back began to crack from the heat, the spiderweb lines spreading rapidly.

Clint gripped Meg’s throat, pushing her snapping jaws away as the floor beneath them began to sag and burn.

================================================================================

STORY 16: THE RIG
-----------------

Chapter 1: The Sanctuary

The Halibut oil platform stood like a steel spider in the middle of the Bass Strait, its massive legs anchored deep into the ocean floor. Around it, the sea was a churning mass of dark grey swells, whipped by a freezing wind that blew straight from the Antarctic. 

Inside the primary control room, Damon watched the digital pressure gauge of the main extraction pipe. He was a broad-shouldered man, his face weathered by years of working offshore, his jumpsuits smelling of grease and crude oil. 

"Pressure’s holding steady at three hundred bar, Chloe," Damon said, tapping the screen. "Generators are purring. We've got enough diesel and dry rations to last another six months out here."

Chloe, the rig's safety manager, looked up from her console. She was staring at a static-filled computer monitor. "Any word from the mainland?"

"Nothing but static for three weeks," Damon replied quietly. "Melbourne radio went silent first. Then the Hobart transmitter. The last transmission was some bloke in Geelong screaming about the docks being overrun."

Chloe shivered, wrapping her arms around her heavy CFS-issue windbreaker. The mainland was a graveyard. But out here, eighty kilometers off the coast of Victoria, the undead couldn't reach them. The ocean was their moat, and the steel platform was their fortress. They had twenty-two crew members left, all healthy, all safe.

"It's a sanctuary, Damon," she murmured, looking out the reinforced glass window. "As long as we keep the drill running and the flare burning, we're safe."

But as she spoke, a low, pulsing alarm began to chime on the navigation radar.

Chapter 2: The Ghost Ship

Chloe’s fingers flew across the keyboard. The radar sweep showed a large, solid return moving slow and heavy through the storm. 

"Damon, we've got a contact. Twelve miles out, bearing south-southwest," she said, her voice tight. "It's moving at six knots. Straight toward us."

Damon leaned over her shoulder, his brow furrowed. "That's not a shipping lane. Did you hail them?"

"I've tried every marine channel, UHF, VHF, even distress frequencies," Chloe said, shaking her head. "No response. It's a ghost."

The storm outside was worsening, rain lashing against the glass panes like gravel. Chloe stood up and walked to the window, raising a marine spotlight and aiming it into the darkness. The sea was wild, ten-meter waves crashing against the rig's structural pillars, sending white spray flying forty feet into the air.

Through the sheets of driving rain, a massive shape slowly materialized. It was a container ship, a towering wall of rusted iron, riding low in the water. It was completely dark—no running lights, no cabin illumination, no signs of life on the bridge. 

The ship drifted sideways, pushed by the massive swells and the gale-force wind. It was on a direct collision course with the Halibut platform.

"She’s caught in the current," Damon muttered, his face turning pale. "And she's coming in broadside. If she hits us, she'll tear the northern leg right off."

"Evacuate the lower deck!" Chloe yelled, reaching for the PA microphone. "All hands, brace for impact!"

Chapter 3: The Collision

The collision was not a single crash, but a long, agonizing shriek of tearing steel. 

The bow of the container ship, the Southern Cross, slammed into the northern lattice leg of the Halibut platform. The force of the impact threw Damon and Chloe to the floor. The ceiling panels popped out, raining insulation and plastic clips down upon them. The primary lights flickered and died, replaced instantly by the spinning red glare of the emergency beacons.

"Status!" Damon roared, dragging himself up by the console.

"Structural alarms on Leg A!" Chloe coughed, her hand covering a cut on her forehead. "The hull of the ship is wedged right into the main support. It's... it's not bouncing off, Damon. The ship is pinned against us by the swells."

Outside, the ship rose and fell with the massive waves, grinding its rusted iron sides against the platform's steel columns. The sound was like a giant hammering on the metal structure. 

"We need to check the cellar deck," Damon said, grabbing a heavy flashlight from the wall charger. "If the main riser is damaged, we'll have a blowout. The gas pressure will turn this place into a torch."

"I'm coming with you," Chloe said, grabbing a radio and a steel spanner from the tool rack. 

They ran out of the control room into the howling storm, descending the wet steel grating stairs toward the lower cellar deck. Below them, the bow of the wedged container ship rose and fell in the dark, a rusted monster locked in a death grip with their home.

Chapter 4: The Cargo

The cellar deck was a wind tunnel of freezing spray and noise. The bow of the Southern Cross was wedged tightly into the lattice steel of Leg A, only ten feet below the lower walkway. 

Damon shone his flashlight onto the ship's main deck. Several of the stacked cargo containers had been crushed in the collision, their corrugated steel walls split open like tin cans.

"Look at that," Chloe whispered, her spotlight beam dancing over the wreckage. "What was she carrying?"

The containers weren't filled with machinery or consumer goods. They were packed with human belongings—clothes, bags, and rows of makeshift wooden bunks. It had been a refugee ship, fleeing the collapse of the mainland. 

But there was no movement of survivors. Instead, the split containers were filled with a mass of pale, waterlogged bodies, piled on top of one another. The sea water, washing over the ship's deck, swirled around them.

"They must have suffocated," Damon said, his stomach turning. "Locked in those steel boxes when the ship lost power."

Then, a body at the front of the split container moved. 

It rolled over, its pale, bloated face turning toward the light. Its eyes were milky, its mouth opening to release a stream of dark ocean water. Beside it, another body began to crawl out of the wreckage, its limbs stiff and blue. 

One by one, the waterlogged dead began to stand up on the deck of the container ship, their eyes locking onto the steel walkways of the oil rig just above them.

Chapter 5: The Ascent

"They're getting up," Chloe gasped, backing away from the railing.

The container ship’s deck was level with the rig’s lower maintenance ladders. The drowned dead, their skin swollen and white from weeks in the sea, shuffled forward. They didn't feel the freezing wind or the stinging spray. They climbed. 

Blackened, bloated hands gripped the yellow steel rungs of the ladders. They climbed slowly, their wet clothes dragging against the metal. The wind was so loud, howling through the girders, that it completely drowned out the sound of their scraping fingernails and wet, shuffling footsteps.

"Damon, we have to cut the ladders!" Chloe yelled.

"They're welded steel, Chloe! We can't cut them without a torch, and there’s gas in the air!" Damon pointed to the control room level above. "We need to get to the drilling floor and close the blast doors. If they reach the living quarters, we're done."

They scrambled back up the stairs, the wet metal slick under their boots. 

Damon looked back. A zombie in a waterlogged life jacket had already reached the lower walkway. It dragged itself over the safety rail, its pale eyes locking onto them. Behind it, a dozen more hands reached over the deck edge. The moat had failed. The ocean had brought the dead straight to their door.

Chapter 6: The Blast Wall

They reached the drilling floor. The heavy steel blast doors—designed to isolate the quarters from a gas explosion—stood wide open. 

"Get the manual release!" Chloe yelled, running to the red control box on the wall. 

She opened the box and yanked the lever. The heavy hydraulic doors began to slide shut, moving slowly on their massive steel tracks. 

But from the main corridor, a group of five crew members appeared, running in blind panic. They were covered in soot, their faces twisted in terror. 

"Stop! Don't close them!" one of the men, a roughneck named Mick, screamed. "The lower decks are full of them! They're coming up the service lift!"

"Mick, get inside! The doors are closing!" Chloe warned.

Mick lunged through the narrowing gap, but in his panic, he tripped, dropping a heavy steel toolbox he was carrying. The box wedged directly into the door’s threshold. The heavy hydraulic blast door slammed into the steel box, bent it, and jammed on its track with a loud, mechanical groan. The motor hissed, steam venting from the overloaded line.

"It's jammed!" Damon yelled, running to the door. He tried to pull the box free, but the pressure had locked it tight.

From the dark corridor behind Mick, the first of the waterlogged dead appeared, their wet footprints marking the steel floor.

Chapter 7: The Pressure Build

"Help me!" Mick screamed as a pale, bloated hand grabbed his ankle, dragging him back into the dark corridor. 

Damon swung his heavy steel spanner, crushing the skull of the zombie holding Mick. The creature went limp, but three more pressed forward, their cold fingers clawing at the gap in the blast door. Damon grabbed Mick's arm and hauled him through the opening, but they couldn't clear the jammed toolbox. The door remained cracked open, a four-inch gap that the dead were already trying to squeeze through.

Suddenly, a high-pitched whistle echoed from the drilling floor behind them.

Chloe ran back to the main console, her face turning pale in the red emergency light. "Damon! The collision ruptured the primary choke manifold. Gas pressure in the main riser is spiking. It's at four hundred bar and rising."

"The automatic vent valve must have seized when the power cut," Damon said, running to her side. 

"If it hits five hundred, the casing will fracture," Chloe said, her voice shaking. "The gas will ignite on the hot generator engines. The whole rig will blow."

Damon looked back at the jammed blast door. More waterlogged faces were pressing against the gap, their cold, dead eyes staring at him. 

"There's a manual bypass valve on the flare boom," Damon said, his jaw tightening. "We have to go out onto the catwalk and open it by hand. It's the only way to dump the pressure."

Chapter 8: The Flare Boom

The flare boom was a narrow, triangular steel truss that projected one hundred and fifty feet out over the ocean, away from the main structure of the rig.

Damon stepped out onto the open metal grating of the catwalk. The wind immediately hit him like a physical blow, nearly throwing him over the low safety rail. The rain was deafening, stinging his eyes and freezing his face. Below him, the ocean was a churning, white-capped void, the waves crashing against the rig's support structure far below.

"I'll hold the hatch!" Chloe yelled, standing at the entrance of the catwalk. She held the spanner, her knuckles white. 

Damon began to crawl. He had to keep his center of gravity low, his fingers gripping the wet, icy steel of the grating. The metal was slick with a mixture of salt water and drilling mud. Every time a massive swell hit the rig's leg, the entire boom vibrated, swaying side to side in the gale.

He looked down through the metal floor. 

In the dark water below, the spotlights from the rig illuminated dozens of pale, floating shapes. The zombies that had fallen off the container ship were drifting in the currents, their limbs moving aimlessly. Some were clawing at the structural cables that anchored the flare boom to the rig's main deck.

Damon forced himself to look away. He crawled faster, reaching the end of the boom where the massive, yellow manual release wheel sat.

Chapter 9: The Fracture

Damon grabbed the manual wheel. It was rusted tight, the salt air having seized the threads. He threw his weight against it, but it wouldn't budge.

Suddenly, a massive shudder ran through the platform. The container ship Southern Cross, battered by a fifteen-meter swell, shifted its position. It ground deeper into Leg A, the impact sending a violent shockwave up the entire structure. 

The flare boom whipped violently. Damon’s boots slipped on the icy grating, and he tumbled over the low rail. He screamed, his hands catching the edge of the catwalk as he fell. He hung suspended in the air, his boots dangling over the roaring ocean a hundred feet below.

"Damon!" Chloe screamed. 

She abandoned her post at the hatch and sprinted out onto the narrow catwalk. She threw herself flat on the grating, reaching down to grab his wrists. 

"I've got you! Don't let go!" she gasped, her face pale with effort.

As she pulled, a pale, waterlogged arm reached through the steel lattice of the boom from below. One of the zombies had climbed the structural cables from the deck level. The creature’s blue, bloated face appeared between the steel struts, its teeth bared. 

Before Damon could warn her, the thing lunged. Its teeth sank deep into Chloe's exposed forearm, tearing through the fabric of her windbreaker.

Chloe screamed in pain, but she didn't let go of Damon. With a final, desperate heave, she pulled him up onto the grating.

Chapter 10: The Flare

Damon scrambled to his feet, kicking the climbing zombie in the head until it lost its grip and fell into the sea. He grabbed the manual bypass wheel, using the spanner as a lever, and pulled with all his strength. 

The wheel turned with a loud, metallic crack. 

A split second later, a deafening roar shook the boom. High-pressure natural gas rushed through the pipes, venting from the tip of the boom. The automatic igniter sparked, and a massive column of orange flame erupted into the storm, lighting up the churning ocean for miles. The pressure gauge began to fall. The rig was saved from explosion.

Damon turned back to Chloe. She was sitting on the catwalk, clutching her bleeding arm. Her eyes were already growing cloudy, the fever from the waterlogged pathogen taking hold with terrifying speed.

"Damon..." she whispered, her voice weak. "They're... they're through the blast door. I heard them on the radio."

She stood up, staggering back toward the hatch that led to the main platform. She stepped inside the control room foyer, her movements already stiffening.

"Chloe, wait!" Damon yelled, running toward her.

"No, Damon. I'm not bringing it back," she said, her face pale, a thin trickle of dark blood running from her nose. 

She grabbed the heavy steel hatch handle from the inside, pulled it shut, and threw the manual locking bolts. 

Through the small reinforced glass port, Damon watched her collapse onto the floor. Within seconds, her chest stopped moving. Then, her eyes snapped open, a milky white glaze covering her pupils. She stood back up, scratching uselessly at the glass from the inside, her dead eyes staring at him.

Damon stood alone on the wind-swept catwalk, trapped between the roaring fire of the flare boom behind him and the reanimated safety manager blocking his only way inside.

================================================================================

STORY 17: THE PASS
------------------

Chapter 1: The White Out

The window panes of the Charlotte Pass Ski Lodge rattled in their wooden frames, encrusted in a thick layer of white frost. Outside, the blizzard was a roaring, blinding wall of snow, reducing visibility to less than a meter. The towering snowgums of the Kosciuszko main range were buried to their lower branches, their twisted trunks frozen grey.

Inside the cellar lounge, Sean stood next to the massive cast-iron combustion heater. He rubbed his hands together, his breath coming in visible, white puffs. He was wearing his heavy, oil-stained grooming jacket, the fleece collar pulled high. 

"Generator’s chattering, Zoe," Sean said, looking at the small control panel on the wall. "The RPMs are fluctuating. We’ve got less than fifty liters of diesel in the primary tank. Once that goes, the heating loops will freeze."

Zoe, sitting on a heap of wool rugs near the hearth, wrapped a heavy thermal blanket tighter around herself. Her lips had a slight, pale tint. "And the auxiliary lines?"

"Frozen solid last night," Sean replied, his voice flat. "The fuel lines gelled up when it hit minus twelve. Nothin's flowin' from the bulk tanks on the hill. We've got to run down to the transport depot in the snowcat if we want to survive the night."

Zoe looked around the dim room. In the corners, the remaining guests—six of them—huddled under piles of ski jackets and bedding, their faces pale and hollow. They had fled the lowlands when the cities fell, thinking the freezing isolation of the Snowy Mountains would keep them safe. And it had, for five months. But now, the cold itself was the threat.

"Can the snowcat make it in this whiteout?" she asked.

"She’s got the V8 and the tracks, Zoe. If anyone can navigate the drift, it's her," Sean said, checking the straps on his snow goggles. "But we need to go now, before the wind packs the Pass shut."

As he spoke, the lights overhead flickered, dimmed to a dull orange filament, and went completely black. The comforting hum of the floor vents died, replaced by the howling of the wind outside.

Chapter 2: The Blue Dead

Zoe stood up, her limbs stiff from the cold. She grabbed a heavy LED flashlight, clicking it on. The beam cut through the dark lounge, illuminating the frost patterns on the large bay windows. 

Sean took a steps toward the glass, scraping at the ice layer with the edge of his plastic ski pass. He peered out into the swirling white storm. The snow was falling in dense, heavy flakes, driven horizontally by the wind.

"Sean," Zoe whispered, joining him at the glass. "Look at the main run. Under the chairlift tower."

Sean focused his eyes. Through the shifting sheets of white, shapes were moving. They were slow—almost impossibly slow—staggering through drifts that came up to their hips. They were blue-skinned, their flesh hard and frostbitten, their hair coated in icicles. One was wearing a ski patrol jacket, the fabric stiff and frozen.

"They're still out there," Sean muttered. "The cold hasn't stopped them."

"No," Zoe said, her voice trembling. "But it's slowed them down. Look at them. They look like statues."

One of the blue figures stumbled, its leg catching on a buried snow-drift. It fell forward, hitting the snow with a dull, wooden sound. It didn't try to break its fall. Instead, it slowly, mechanically began to push its frozen arms into the snow to haul itself back up, its jaw opening to reveal a mouth packed with ice.

"The frost preserves them," Sean said, adjusting the rifle strap on his shoulder. "They don't rot up here. They just freeze. Like meat in a freezer. Come on. The maintenance hangar is two hundred meters down the track. Let's move."

Chapter 3: The Snowcat

The air outside hit them like a wall of needles. 

The wind chill was well below minus twenty. Sean and Zoe pushed through the heavy wooden door of the lodge, stepping directly into a snowdrift that reached Zoe’s knees. The blizzard roared, the sound a constant, deafening roar in their ears. They held onto a yellow guide rope that Sean had strung between the lodge and the maintenance hangar before the storm hit.

"Keep your head down!" Sean yelled over the wind. 

They staggered forward, their boots sinking deep into the fresh powder. The light was almost gone, the sky a dark, heavy grey. Through the blowing snow, the corrugated iron walls of the maintenance hangar appeared like a shadow. 

Sean reached the small side door of the hangar. He pulled his thick leather gloves off with his teeth, his fingers immediately turning red and stiff in the biting cold. He grabbed the padlock, but it was encased in a thick shell of ice. 

"It's frozen solid!" he yelled, shaking the lock.

Behind them, a low, scraping sound came from the darkness. Through the curtain of white, a shape emerged. It was a frozen zombie, its face a mask of blue ice, its hands frozen into stiff, claw-like shapes. It was shuffling along the side of the hangar, its frozen boots clinking against the metal walls.

Chapter 4: The Hangar Gate

"Get the crowbar from my pack!" Sean yelled, turning to face the approaching blue walker. 

He unslung his rifle, his frozen fingers struggling to find the trigger guard. The zombie was slow, its frozen knee joints clicking with every step, but it was relentless. Sean brought the rifle up, lined up the scope on the blue forehead, and pulled the trigger. The shot was a muffled pop in the storm. The bullet shattered the skull, sending a spray of frozen blood and bone into the snow. The creature fell backward, stiff as a log.

Zoe dragged the heavy steel crowbar out of Sean's backpack. She jammed the tip into the ice-encrusted padlock, throwing her weight against it. With a loud, sharp crack, the ice shattered, and the lock popped open. 

"I've got the door!" she yelled, throwing it open. 

But as they stepped into the dark hangar, a low rumble sounded from the steep slope of the ski run above. 

"Look out!" Sean pulled her back.

A small snowbank had collapsed, sliding down the metal roof of the hangar. With it came three frozen bodies, their limbs tangled, sliding down the roof and crashing into the snowdrift right in front of the open door. They didn't lie still. The impact shattered their frozen joints, but they immediately began to crawl toward the open doorway, their blue hands clawing at the metal threshold.

Chapter 5: The Frozen Fuel

Sean slammed the side door shut, slide-bolting it from the inside. The wind outside howled against the corrugated walls, but inside, the air was still and freezing. 

In the center of the hangar sat the snowcat—a massive, red tractor with wide steel tracks and a heavy hydraulic blade. 

"Zoe, watch the window," Sean said, running to the back of the machine. "I need to get this engine started."

He climbed onto the steel deck, popping open the engine cowling. The smell of cold metal and old grease met him. He checked the fuel filter. The diesel inside was no longer liquid; it had turned into a cloudy, waxy gel, completely blocking the lines. 

"It's gelled," Sean muttered. 

He scrambled down, grabbed a small blue propane torch from the workbench, and clicked the igniter. A steady, blue flame hissed into the dark. He climbed back up, carefully sweeping the flame along the steel fuel lines and the metal filter housing, trying to heat them without igniting any stray fumes.

"Sean, they're at the roller doors," Zoe called out. She was standing by the glass window of the main bay. 

Outside, several blue-skinned figures were slamming their frozen fists against the thin metal paneling of the large hangar doors. The metal drummed like a giant sheet of tin. 

"Just a minute more!" Sean yelled, the torch flame heating the filter until the wax turned back to liquid. He tossed the torch aside, jumped into the cab, and turned the key. 

The starter motor groaned, turned over slowly twice, and died.

Chapter 6: Clearing the Run

Sean turned the key again, pumping the glow-plug switch. The V8 engine coughed, a cloud of thick black soot bursting from the exhaust stack, and then it roared to life with a deafening, metallic rumble. 

"Get in!" Sean yelled.

Zoe scrambled into the passenger seat, slamming the heavy cab door shut. The cabin was cold, but the vibration of the engine was comforting. Sean engaged the hydraulics, raising the front plow blade. He slammed the gear lever forward and hit the throttle. 

The snowcat lurched forward, its massive tracks chewing through the ice on the hangar floor. It rammed the frozen roller doors, the thin metal buckling and tearing open as the machine burst out into the blizzard.

The snowcat crawled up the steep ski run, its headlights cutting two bright beams through the whiteout. Sean steered by memory, keeping the machine aligned with the invisible slope. 

Ahead of them, several blue-skinned figures stood on the run. The snowcat hit them at fifteen kilometers an hour. The massive steel plow blade smashed them aside, their frozen bodies shattering like glass under the heavy steel tracks.

But as they reached the crest of the first ridge, a loud, metallic grinding sound came from the left track. The engine bogged down, and the snowcat veered sharply to the left, heading toward a steep drop-off.

"Sean!" Zoe screamed.

Sean hit the brakes, the machine stopping inches from the edge of the ravine. The left track was locked solid.

Chapter 7: Under the Tracks

Sean grabbed the heavy steel crowbar from the cab floor. "I have to go under. Something's jammed in the drive sprocket."

"The wind's coming up, Sean," Zoe said, her face tight. "And we've got company."

Through the swirling snow, several blue shapes were emerging from the tree line of the surrounding snowgums. They were slow, but the headlights of the snowcat had drawn them like a beacon.

"Keep them off the cab!" Sean yelled, throwing open his door and stepping down into the freezing wind.

He crawled under the massive red chassis, the steel plates cold against his back. He shone his headlamp onto the left drive sprocket. A frozen leg, clad in a thick ski boot, had been sucked into the gears, the bone and boot jammed tightly between the steel track teeth and the sprocket wheel. 

"Damn it," Sean muttered, positioning the crowbar. He hammered the end of the bar, trying to pry the frozen limb free.

Above him, on the deck of the snowcat, Zoe stood with the rifle. She fired down the slope, the crack of the .303 swallowed by the blizzard. A blue walker collapsed, its head shattered. She cycled the bolt, the brass casing hitting the snow with a hiss. 

Underneath, Sean gave a massive heave on the crowbar. The frozen bone snapped with a loud crack, and the boot tumbled out onto the snow.

"Got it!" Sean yelled. "Start backing up!"

But as he started to crawl out from under the chassis, a blue, frozen hand reached through the track links, grabbing his collar.

Chapter 8: The Cold Sleep

Sean slammed the crowbar into the frozen forearm. The ice-brittle bones shattered, and the grip released. He scrambled out from under the tracks, climbed back into the cab, and slammed the door just as a blue hand brushed against the window. 

He threw the snowcat into reverse, the tracks clearing, and they surged back down the mountain toward the lodge, the heavy fuel drums secured in the rear tray.

They reached the lodge fifteen minutes later. The building was completely dark, its windows frosted over. 

Sean and Zoe dragged the heavy fuel jerrycans through the back door, entering the freezing lobby. The indoor temperature had dropped below zero. The air was dead and silent.

"Zoe?" Sean called out, his voice echoing in the empty hallway.

They hurried down to the cellar lounge. The fire had died, and the room was a tomb of cold. The remaining guests were huddled together under a mound of blankets on the sofa, shivering violently. 

"Where’s Arthur?" Zoe asked, looking around.

One of the guests, a young woman named Sarah, pointed toward the back cellar stairs. "He... he went down to the wine cellar to look for more firewood. He hasn't come back up. We were too cold to go look."

Zoe ran down the narrow concrete stairs, Sean following with the flashlight. 

At the bottom of the stairs, lying face down on the cold concrete floor, was Arthur. His body was stiff, his face pale and blue, his eyelashes coated in frost. He had collapsed from the extreme cold.

Chapter 9: The Reanimation

They carried Arthur's stiff body up the stairs and laid him on the rug near the cold hearth. Zoe knelt beside him, rubbing his frozen hands, while Sean rushed out to pour the diesel into the generator tank. 

"Arthur, can you hear me?" Zoe pleaded, checking his neck for a pulse. His skin felt like marble. 

There was no heartbeat. Arthur had frozen to death in the unheated cellar. 

"Arthur..." Zoe whispered, her eyes filling with tears. He had been a kind man, a retired schoolteacher who had helped keep the lodge organized. 

Suddenly, Arthur's chest gasped. 

Zoe jumped back, hope flaring in her chest. "Arthur! You're alive!"

But the gasp was not the return of life. It was a dry, hollow rattle. Arthur’s eyelids fluttered open, revealing pupils that were completely white, the iris faded into a milky glaze. His jaw opened, his frozen joints popping loudly, and a stream of grey, icy condensation drifted from his mouth.

"Zoe, get back!" Sean yelled, running into the room from the generator bay.

But it was too late. Arthur’s hands, stiff but incredibly strong, shot out. He grabbed Zoe’s shoulder, pulling her down toward him. Before Sean could reach them, Arthur’s teeth sank into the flesh of Zoe’s collarbone, tearing through her ski vest.

Chapter 10: The Ice Tomb

Sean lunged forward, the butt of his rifle crashing into Arthur's temple. The frozen skull cracked, and Arthur collapsed back onto the rug, static and dead. 

Sean pulled Zoe away. She was clutching her shoulder, dark blood staining her yellow ski jacket. The bite was deep, the teeth having torn through the muscle. 

"Sean... it's cold," she whispered, her teeth chattering violently, her face turning a deathly pale. The fever was already taking hold, but the sub-zero room was accelerating her shock.

Suddenly, a deafening crash echoed through the lounge. 

A massive gust of wind, carrying a heavy drift of snow, shattered the large bay window. The wooden frame gave way, and a torrent of freezing air and white snow blasted into the room. The curtains ripped free, whipping wildly in the gale. The temperature in the room plummeted instantly.

Through the shattered window, the blue, frozen dead of Charlotte Pass began to appear, drawn by the sound of the glass breaking. They crawled over the window sill, their blue faces covered in snow, their milky eyes locking onto the survivors.

"We have to go," Sean said, his voice cracking. He tried to lift Zoe, but she was too weak to stand. 

"Leave me, Sean," she whispered, her eyes starting to turn cloudy, her breath slowing in the freezing draft. "I'm already... I'm already cold."

Sean backed away toward the rear exit, his rifle raised, as the first of the blue-skinned walkers stepped into the room, their frozen boots crunching on the shattered glass.

================================================================================

STORY 18: THE FERRY
-------------------

Chapter 1: The Last Boarding

The sirens in downtown Sydney were a continuous, rising wall of sound, punctuated by the occasional dull thump of an explosion. Circular Quay was a scene of unmitigated panic. The glass facades of the office towers along Alfred Street reflected the orange glow of burning transit buses, and a thick cloud of oily black smoke rose from the direction of the Harbour Bridge.

Toby stood at the gangway of the Queenscliff, one of the iconic green-and-yellow double-ended passenger ferries. His hands shook as he held the safety rope. He was wearing his blue transit uniform, the collar soaked with sweat in the humid harbor air.

"Hurry up! Watch your step!" Toby shouted, his voice cracking. 

A wave of desperate commuters pressed forward, shoving and clawing to get past the turnstiles. Women carried crying children, businessmen dragged heavy suitcases, and tourists clutched whatever bags they could carry. The wharf was black with a shifting mass of thousands more, all trying to reach the water.

Fiona, a young woman in practical canvas trousers and a backpack, pushed through the crowd, her hand gripped firmly around the wrist of a terrified teenage girl. They made it across the gangway just as the crowd behind them surged forward, nearly knocking them into the dark, oily water below.

"That's enough, Toby!" a voice barked from the upper deck. It was Arthur, the veteran ferry captain, leaning over the bridge wing. His face was pale and drawn, his white hair plastering to his forehead. "Cast off the lines! We're sitting too low in the water already. If we take any more, we'll capsize."

"But Captain, there's still—" Toby started, looking at the screaming faces on the dock.

"Now, Toby!" Arthur roared. 

Toby dropped the gangway latch. He ran to the heavy bollards, throwing the thick mooring lines off and letting them splash into the harbor. The ferry’s twin diesel engines groaned, the propellers churning the water into a white foam as the Queenscliff slowly backed away from the swarmed wharf.

Chapter 2: The Harbour

The Queenscliff cleared the cove, turning eastward into the main shipping channel of Sydney Harbour. 

Toby walked along the narrow external deck, squeezing past passengers who were pressed against the wooden benches and the steel rails. The ferry was rated for eleven hundred passengers, but Toby estimated there were nearly fifteen hundred on board. People were sitting on the stairs, on the floor, and in every available corner.

Fiona stood near the starboard rail, her arm wrapped around the teenage girl, her eyes fixed on the city behind them. The Opera House passed by, its white sails stark against the black smoke rising from the central business district. A military helicopter roared low overhead, heading toward the western suburbs.

"Is Manly safe?" the girl asked, her voice trembling.

"It will be, Clara," Fiona said, trying to sound confident. "My sister has a place near the cove. It's isolated. We just need to get out of the city center."

Toby approached them, wiping grease and sweat from his forehead. "You two alright?"

"We're on the boat. That's more than most," Fiona said, nodding toward the city. "But the air in the saloon is terrible. It's packed in there, and the air conditioning is failing."

Toby looked through the large glass windows of the main passenger saloon. Inside, the cabin was a sea of sweaty, panicked faces. The air was thick and humid, the passenger density creating a greenhouse effect. 

Suddenly, a disturbance flared in the middle of the crowded saloon. A man's voice rose in a panicked yell, followed by the sharp scrape of plastic chairs sliding across the floor.

Chapter 3: The Fever

Toby pushed open the heavy saloon door, the wall of hot, stale air hitting him immediately. Fiona followed closely behind, her medical instincts kicking in.

"Make way! Transit staff!" Toby called out, pushing through the dense crowd.

In the center of the cabin, a man in his late thirties had collapsed onto the linoleum floor. He was convulsing, his body shaking violently, his skin a flush, bright red. A thick, dark sweat soaked his business shirt. 

"Give him air! Back off!" Fiona ordered, kneeling beside the man. 

She pulled his collar open. His neck veins were distended, his skin burning hot to the touch. As she did, she noticed a crude bandage wrapped around his right forearm, dark fluid soaking through the gauze. 

"He's got a bite," Fiona muttered to Toby, her eyes widening. "He must have gotten it at the Quay and hid it to get on the boat."

The man's convulsions suddenly stopped. His head fell back, his jaw dropping open. Fiona pressed two fingers to his neck, then leaned down to check for breathing. 

"His heart’s stopped," she said, starting chest compressions. "He's in arrest. Toby, get the first-aid kit and the defibrillator from the locker!"

Toby turned to fight his way back through the crowd, but the passenger saloon was too packed. People were pushing forward, trying to see what was happening, while others, realizing the man was bitten, were screaming and trying to back away. 

"Fiona," Toby called out, his voice rising in panic. "He's... he's not breathing."

"I know!" Fiona yelled, pressing down on the chest. 

But beneath her hands, the man's chest suddenly went stiff.

Chapter 4: The Saloon Panic

The collapsed man’s eyes snapped open. 

They were not the eyes of a recovering patient. The pupils had shrunk to pinpricks, surrounded by a milky, bloodshot haze. His jaw unlocked with a wet, clicking sound. Before Fiona could pull her hands back, the man lunged upward, his teeth snapping shut on the wrist of a nearby passenger who had bent down to help.

The bitten passenger—a middle-aged woman—screamed in agony as the man tore a chunk of flesh from her arm.

"Get back!" Fiona yelled, scrambling backward on the floor. 

Panic swept the saloon like a physical wave. The crowd erupted into a chaotic stampede. Hundreds of passengers rushed for the double doors leading to the external decks, their weight pressing against one another in the narrow aisles. Chairs were overturned, luggage was abandoned, and people were trampled underfoot.

"Clear the doors!" Toby yelled, trying to guide the crowd, but he was swept aside by the rushing tide of bodies.

In the middle of the saloon, the woman who had been bitten collapsed, her skin turning pale, her chest rising and falling in rapid, shallow gasps. She died within seconds, the toxic fever destroying her nervous system. 

Under the universal rules of the dead, she sat back up before the first screams had even cleared the room. Her milky eyes locked onto a teenager trapped in the crush by the exit, and she lunged forward, her teeth bared.

Chapter 5: Locked In

The heavy double doors leading to the external deck were designed to swing outward, but the sheer weight of the panicked crowd pressed so tightly against them that they couldn't be opened. People were jammed shoulder to shoulder, their faces pressed against the glass panels, screaming for the passengers on the deck to move back. 

But on the outer deck, there was no room to move. The ferry was too full.

"Toby! The crew hatch!" Fiona shouted, pulling Clara behind her. 

Toby ran to the small steel hatch in the floor of the saloon, which led down to the steering flat and engine room corridors. He grabbed the flush ring-pull and yanked upward, but the hatch only cracked open an inch before hitting something solid and heavy from below.

"It's blocked!" Toby yelled, straining against the metal handle. "Someone must have piled the spare life jacket crates against the stairs to block the engine room!"

Behind them, the saloon had become a slaughterhouse. 

The two reanimated passengers had turned the center of the cabin into a bloodbath, their slow, mechanical movements making them no less deadly in the confined space. Every person they bit collapsed, died of the fever, and stood back up. The number of the dead was growing exponentially, their blue, bloated hands reaching into the screaming crowd.

"The stairs!" Fiona pointed to the crew-only access stairway leading up to the wheelhouse. "Toby, the key!"

Chapter 6: The Wheelhouse

Toby scrambled to the crew door, his fingers slick with sweat as he sorted through the keys on his belt. He found the brass master key, jammed it into the lock, and turned it. The steel latch clicked open. 

"Get in! Move!" Toby yelled, shoving Clara and Fiona through the door. 

He followed, slamming the heavy door shut behind them just as a bloody hand slammed against the glass viewing pane. Toby threw the deadbolt, the lock clicking home with a solid, metallic thud.

They climbed the narrow, steep stairs to the upper deck, emerging into the soundproof quiet of the wheelhouse. The cabin was surrounded by glass, offering a panoramic view of the harbor. 

But there was no one at the helm.

Captain Arthur was slumped over the wooden steering console, his forehead resting against the steel rim of the wheel. His hand was still clutching his chest, his fingers white and stiff. 

"Arthur?" Toby approached slowly, his heart in his throat.

Fiona checked the captain’s neck. "He's gone, Toby. Heart attack. The stress... his heart must have given out."

Outside, the Queenscliff had drifted past Fort Denison, its engines still turning at idle speed, but there was no one steering. The massive, double-ended ferry was heading straight toward the shallow reefs near Bradleys Head.

"We're running out of room," Toby said, looking at the radar screen. "If we hit the rocks at this speed, the hull will split."

Chapter 7: Runaway Vessel

"Toby, take the wheel!" Fiona yelled, stepping toward the engine throttles. "We need to reverse the propellers to slow us down!"

Toby reached for the steering wheel, but as he did, Captain Arthur’s head slowly lifted from the console. 

The captain’s eyes had gone milky, and his skin was a bruised, grey shade. Under the universal rules of the dead, his heart attack had not been the end. He opened his mouth, a dry, wheezing gasp escaping his throat, and lunged sideways at Toby, his cold hands clawing for Toby's throat.

"Captain, no!" Toby screamed, backing away.

Arthur was a large man, and his dead weight pinned Toby against the navigation chart table. The table’s wooden edge dug into Toby's back as he struggled to keep the captain's snapping jaws away from his face.

"Fiona! Help!" Toby choked out.

Fiona grabbed the heavy brass fire extinguisher from the wall bracket. She swung it with both hands, the heavy metal cylinder striking the side of Arthur's head with a dull, hollow crack. The captain's skull fractured, and he went limp, collapsing onto the floor in a heap.

At the same moment, the ferry shuddered violently. 

"Toby!" Clara screamed, pointing out the forward window. "We're going to hit the island!"

Through the glass, the stone jetty of Fort Denison was rushing toward them.

Chapter 8: The Fort

The impact was a grinding, crushing roar that shook the Queenscliff from bow to stern. 

The ferry’s heavy steel sponson slammed into the ancient sandstone jetty of Fort Denison. The force of the crash threw Toby and Fiona against the instrument console. Down below, the lower passenger saloon windows shattered, sending showers of glass onto the rocks. The wooden benches on the outer deck splintered, throwing passengers into the shallow water and onto the stone wharf.

"Is everyone alright?" Toby groaned, shaking his head to clear the ringing.

"Clara’s okay," Fiona said, helping the girl up. 

But as Toby looked out the side window, his stomach dropped. 

The crash had breached the saloon cabin. The broken glass windows allowed the panicked survivors to scramble out onto the jetty—but it also released the infected horde. 

Dozens of reanimated passengers, their clothes bloodied and their eyes milky, crawled through the broken window frames. They shuffled onto the narrow sandstone paths of the tiny island, their teeth snapping as they chased the fleeing commuters. 

Fort Denison was a tiny speck of stone in the middle of the harbor, less than eighty meters long. There was nowhere to run. The island was quickly becoming a trap.

"They're coming up the service stairs!" Clara pointed to the door they had locked. The wood was already beginning to shudder under the weight of the dead.

Chapter 9: The Landing

"We need to get to the Martello tower!" Toby yelled, pointing to the massive, round stone fort at the far end of the island. "It's the highest point. We can lock ourselves in!"

He threw open the external wheelhouse door, and the cold harbor wind hit them. They scrambled out onto the top deck walkway, descending the steel stairs to the main deck. 

The scene on the jetty was chaos. Fleeing passengers were being cornered against the stone walls of the fort, their screams echoing over the water. Toby, Fiona, and Clara ran along the narrow sandstone path, dodging staggering, waterlogged zombies that had crawled out of the shallow water where the ferry had crashed.

They reached the heavy timber door of the Martello tower. 

Toby threw his shoulder against it. It was locked, but the wood was old. With a desperate grunt, he kicked the lock, the old wood splintering open. They scrambled inside, entering the cool, damp dark of the historic tower. 

"Up the stairs!" Fiona ordered. 

They climbed the narrow, spiral stone staircase that wound around the central pillar. Behind them, through the open door, the shuffling sound of the dead began to echo in the damp stone vault. The horde had followed them.

Chapter 10: The Martello Tower

They reached the top gun platform of the tower. The open air met them, the Sydney Harbour Bridge visible in the distance, a dark silhouette against the burning city. The harbor waters around the fort were empty now, the ferry Queenscliff slowly drifting away, its engines silent.

Toby slammed the heavy iron hatch at the top of the stairs shut, but the latch was rusted away. He had to hold it down with his weight. 

"Fiona," Toby gasped, his face pale and covered in sweat. "Help me block it."

Fiona looked around, but there was nothing on the stone deck but the old cannons. "There's nothing to block it with, Toby!"

Below them, the dead began to beat against the underside of the hatch. The iron plate shuddered under their fists, the metal ringing. 

Toby sat on the hatch, his legs shaking. As he wiped his forehead, the sleeve of his uniform slid back, revealing a deep, bloody scratch and bite mark on his wrist. 

Fiona saw it and took a step back, pulling Clara behind her. "Toby... your arm."

Toby looked down at the wound. The skin around the bite was already turning a dark, bruised shade, and he could feel a burning fever starting to spread up his forearm. 

"I got it when I pulled Mick through the door," Toby said, his voice flat. "I'm sorry, Fiona."

The hatch beneath him gave a massive shudder, rising an inch as the hands of the dead pressed from below. Toby held it down, but he knew his strength was fading as the fever rose. 

The story ended on a grim cliffhanger: Toby was dying of the bite on the hatch, while the dead clawed their way up the spiral stairs, and the burning ruins of Sydney looked on from across the dark water.

================================================================================

STORY 19: THE PARK
------------------

Chapter 1: The Cheerful Music

The sound that filled the hot Gold Coast afternoon was a tinny, synthetic pop song, repeating on a loop that had been running for ten days straight. It blared from the fake rock-shaped speakers hidden in the manicured gardens of the park, a cheerful, upbeat melody that now felt like a mockery of the quiet horror below.

Inside the glass-walled control booth of the G-Force roller coaster, Ryan wiped his greasy hands on a rag. His blue maintenance jumpsuit was stained with hydraulic oil, and his eyes were hollow with exhaustion.

"We have to shut that music off, Ryan," Tess said, not taking her eyes off the security monitors. She was dressed in her black security uniform, a heavy maglite flashlight hanging from her duty belt. "It's driving me mental. And it's drawing them in. Every walker within five miles of the Pacific Highway has shuffled through those broken turnstiles."

Ryan sighed, looking at the massive control board. "The main PA switch is in the guest services office, Tess. And that's right in the middle of the central plaza. We go down there, we're meat. The park's main power grid is still running on the solar field backup, but we have no control over individual zones from here."

Through the reinforced glass of the booth, Ryan looked out at the roller coaster's towering steel loop. The track, painted a bright neon yellow, rose eighty feet into the clear Queensland sky. Below, the concrete avenues of the park—normally crowded with screaming kids and families—were dotted with slow, aimless shapes.

"How many do you reckon?" Ryan asked.

"Hundreds," Tess said, pointing to a monitor that showed the Splash Mountain queue area. "They're just wandering. Following the music. But once the solar batteries drain tonight and the park goes dark... they'll start looking for us."

"We need the truck," Ryan said, nodding toward the VIP parking lot visible on the far edge of the park fence. "If we get that Toyota Hilux, we can ram the back gates and reach the coast."

"Yeah," Tess replied. "But the keys are in the maintenance workshop. On the other side of the park."

Chapter 2: The Avenue of the Dead

Tess tapped the screen of the security console, switching the feeds. 

The cameras showed the main avenue, Celebrity Boulevard, which was lined with souvenir shops and retro diners. Shuffling down the middle of the road was a bizarre, nightmarish parade. Several of the zombies were wearing massive, oversized mascot costumes—a faded pink koala with its head lolling sideways, and a blue kangaroo with its ears torn. 

"They look ridiculous," Ryan muttered, a dark humor in his voice. "Imagine getting bitten by a giant koala."

"It's not funny, Ryan," Tess said, her voice sharp. "Look at the gate camera."

She switched to Camera 4, which showed the main entrance. The heavy wrought-iron gates, adorned with the park's friendly cartoon logo, had been pulled off their tracks. A massive pileup of vehicles—SUVs, caravans, and police cars—blocked the highway outside. From the gridlocked lanes of the Pacific Motorway, a steady stream of figures was shuffling through the gap, drawn by the blaring music.

"They're coming from the highway," Tess said. "If we go down to the streets, we won't make it ten yards before we're cornered. The lanes are too narrow, and there are too many blind spots behind the churro stands."

Ryan leaned against the console, his eyes tracing the yellow roller coaster track that rose from their station and climbed high above the park.

"We don't go down," Ryan said.

Tess looked at him. "What do you mean?"

"The G-Force track runs from here, straight over the center plaza, and terminates right above the maintenance hangar roof," Ryan said, pointing to the structural schematic on the wall. "The service catwalk runs the entire length of the track. We cross the park forty feet in the air."

Chapter 3: The Rollercoaster

Tess stared at the schematic, then out the window at the towering structure. "The catwalk is less than two feet wide, Ryan. And the wind up there is always gusty. If we slip..."

"We don't slip," Ryan said, grabbing a pair of safety harnesses and two heavy-duty carabiners from the maintenance locker. "We clip into the safety line. It’s what my crew uses when we inspect the track. As long as we're clipped, we can't fall."

He threw a harness to Tess, who caught it and began to strap it over her security uniform. Ryan checked the bolt-action rifle he had brought from home, securing it to his shoulder. 

They stepped out of the control booth onto the loading platform. A three-car coaster train sat on the launch rails, its orange fiberglass bodies dusty. The safety harnesses were all open, raised like claws.

Ryan walked to the end of the platform where the yellow track began its steep, sixty-degree climb up the lift hill. Next to the track rails ran the narrow steel grating of the maintenance catwalk, equipped with a thick steel cable for safety harnesses.

"I'll lead," Ryan said, clipping his carabiner onto the cable. The metal-on-metal clang was sharp, briefly echoing over the cheerful pop music playing from the speakers below.

Tess clipped in behind him. She looked down through the open metal grid of the platform. Forty feet below, a group of five zombies—including one in a faded, dirty mascot suit—had gathered at the base of the coaster’s support pillars, their pale faces turning upward as the cable clanged.

Chapter 4: The Loop

The climb up the lift hill was a slow, muscle-straining grind. The wind was warm, carrying the scent of salt water from the nearby beach and the bitter smell of ozone. Below them, the park looked like a miniature model, the streets dotted with moving figures.

They reached the top of the hill, where the track curved sharply before plunging into the main loop. 

"Watch your step here," Ryan called back, sliding his carabiner along the safety cable. "The track transitions to a steel plate platform. It can get slippery."

As Ryan stepped onto the high maintenance platform, a shadow shifted in the small alcove where the emergency brake system was housed. 

A figure in a matching blue maintenance jumpsuit stepped out. It was Dave, one of Ryan's coworkers. His face was a pale, bloated grey, and a thick, dried crust of blood covered his throat. Dave was still clipped into the safety cable, his short tether restricting him to the small platform. He had died up here, trapped on the high track when the world ended.

Dave lunged at Ryan, the safety tether snapping tight with a sharp metal clang. 

"Dave, get back!" Ryan yelled, his boot slipping on the metal plate. 

The reanimated mechanic grabbed Ryan’s collar, pulling him toward the edge of the platform. The safety rail was low, only waist-high, and below them was a seventy-foot drop onto the concrete plaza. Ryan struggled, his hands gripping Dave's wrists, but the zombie’s strength was mechanical and tireless.

"Ryan! Keep still!" Tess yelled, fighting to slide her harness carabiner past the track support so she could reach him.

Chapter 5: The Hangar

Tess lunged forward, her heavy Maglite flashlight swung in a short, powerful arc. The thick aluminum casing struck Dave in the temple with a dull crack. The reanimated mechanic’s grip failed, and he fell backward, his safety harness catching him and leaving his body dangling over the edge of the high platform like a puppet.

Ryan gasped for air, his hand checking his collar. "Thanks. That was close."

"Let's get off this track," Tess said, her heart hammering. "Before another colleague shows up."

They unclipped from the main line and scrambled down the service ladder that led from the high platform to the flat roof of the coaster’s storage hangar. A small skylight stood open, a metal ladder leading down into the dark workshop below.

The hangar was a massive, concrete-walled room. Inside, two auxiliary coaster trains sat on elevated rails, their steel wheels suspended over pit-bays. The room was dark, the only light filtering through the dusty skylight. The air smelled of heavy grease, chain oil, and something else—a sweet, cloying scent of decay.

Ryan descended the ladder first, his boots hitting the concrete floor with a quiet thud. He raised his flashlight, the beam cutting through the shadows. 

The hangar was silent, but the floor below the elevated rails was littered with discarded tools, split oil drums, and several pools of dark, dried blood. 

"The keys are in the back office," Ryan whispered, pointing to a small glass-walled room at the far end of the hangar. "Let's make it quick."

Chapter 6: The Keys

They entered the maintenance office. Ryan shone his light on the large key board behind the desk. Dozens of keyrings hung from metal hooks, labeled with the park’s various utility vehicles. 

"Here it is," Ryan said, grabbing a set labeled Toyota Hilux - Utility 4.

As the keys clinked together, a low, scraping sound came from the main hangar floor outside the office glass. 

Tess spun around, her flashlight beam illuminating the area beneath the elevated rails. Figures were crawling out from the dark maintenance pits. They were former park mechanics and cleaners, their uniforms torn and bloodied. The noise of their entry through the skylight and the keys had drawn them out.

"Ryan, we've got a problem," Tess whispered. "They're blocking the exit doors."

A group of six zombies shuffled toward the office, their arms outstretched. More were emerging from the dark corners of the hangar.

"Up!" Ryan said, pointing to the stairs that led to the coaster train's loading platform. "We can get onto the train on the rails. They can't climb the smooth fiberglass cars easily."

They bolted out of the office, running up the steel steps to the elevated platform. The dead followed, their slow, relentless footsteps echoing in the large room. Ryan and Tess climbed onto the top of the stationary coaster train, standing on the orange seats and holding onto the steel safety harness frames. Below them, the dead gathered around the train, their pale hands clawing at the sleek fiberglass sides.

Chapter 7: The Manual Release

"We're trapped up here," Tess said, shining her light onto the mass of reaching hands below. "The hangar doors are closed, and we can't get down without stepping right into them."

Ryan looked at the track. The coaster train sat on a section of track that sloped slightly downward toward the hangar exit. The exit doors were simple, light-gauge aluminum rollers that were designed to open automatically when a train approached.

"The manual brake release," Ryan said, pointing to a large yellow lever mounted on the wall near the front of the train. "If I pull that, the air brakes will bleed, and the train will roll out of here on gravity. It'll smash right through those roller doors."

"But the lever is on the wall," Tess said. "It's surrounded."

"I'll jump for it," Ryan said, securing the keys in his pocket. "You cover me from up here."

Ryan took a deep breath, stepped onto the front nose of the orange coaster car, and leaped across the six-foot gap toward the wall platform. He landed hard, his boots slamming onto the narrow metal shelf. 

He lunged for the yellow lever, grabbing it with both hands, and pulled downward. 

The lever didn't move. It was locked in place by a mechanical safety pin. 

"It's pinned!" Ryan yelled, his hands slipping on the cold metal. 

Below him, the dead turned toward the sound of his landing. A zombie in a dirty mascot suit reached up, its pale, bloated fingers clawing at the heel of his boot.

Chapter 8: The Ride of the Dead

"Ryan, kick it!" Tess screamed, leaning over the rail of the coaster car. She fired her security issue pistol, the loud report echoing like thunder in the concrete hangar. The bullet shattered the skull of the mascot zombie, sending it sprawling.

Ryan raised his heavy work boot and slammed it down onto the steel safety pin. The pin sheared off with a sharp crack, and the yellow lever clicked downward. 

A loud, hissing gasp of escaping compressed air echoed through the hangar as the track brakes bled off. 

The coaster train groaned, its heavy steel wheels starting to roll forward on the declining rails. Ryan lunged across the gap, throwing himself onto the front car of the moving train just as it cleared the platform. 

The train accelerated, its weight carrying it down the track. It hit the aluminum roller doors at twenty kilometers an hour, the thin metal panels tearing open with a deafening screech of ripping metal. 

They burst out into the bright Queensland sunlight, the coaster train rolling silently along the outdoor track that wound through the park.

But their relief was short-lived. 

The track ahead curved downward before heading into the main loop. At the lowest point of the curve, where the track ran close to the ground, a massive cluster of zombies had gathered, drawn by the music. They were packed onto the track structure, their bodies blocking the rails.

Chapter 9: The Collision

"Hold on!" Ryan roared, ducking into the footwell of the front car.

The unpowered coaster train slammed into the cluster of bodies at thirty kilometers an hour. The impact was a horrific storm of thuds, cracking bones, and wet splatters. The front car plowed through the first few walkers, but the sheer volume of flesh and bone wedged beneath the steel wheels. 

The train shuddered, its wheels jumping the yellow steel pipe track. With a screech of grinding metal, the front car derailed, tilting sideways and crashing into the decorative plastic bushes that lined the ride's boundary.

The car came to a sudden, violent stop. 

Ryan was thrown forward against the fiberglass nose, his forehead hitting the frame. He groaned, shaking his head. Beside him, the second car had flipped onto its side.

"Tess!" he gasped, crawling over the seat back.

Tess was pinned in the middle row. Her left leg was wedged beneath the collapsed fiberglass side panel of the car, her face twisted in pain. "Ryan... I'm stuck. The frame's bent."

As Ryan scrambled to help her, a sound came from the third car of the train, which was still upright on the track. 

Sitting in the middle seat of the third car was a passenger—a teenage guest who had been trapped in the heavy, over-the-shoulder safety harness when the ride stopped ten days ago. He had died of dehydration, locked in the seat. 

Under the universal rules, the sudden impact had jostled his body, and his dead eyes snapped open. He began to thrash against the padded harness, his mouth snapping hungrily at Tess’s shoulder, only inches away.

Chapter 10: The Harness

The reanimated teen, locked in the thick rubber harness, could not escape his seat, but his arms reached out, his fingernails scratching at Tess's uniform. His teeth snapped together with a dry, clicking sound, missing her cheek by a fraction of an inch.

"Ryan, get me out!" Tess panicked, trying to pull her leg free, but the bent fiberglass panel held her fast. 

Ryan scrambled to the side of the car, throwing his shoulder against the heavy frame. He grunted, his boots slipping on the bloody grass, but he couldn't lift the multi-ton vehicle. "It's too heavy, Tess! I need a lever!"

The synthetic pop music from the park's speakers continued to blare, a cheerful brass melody looping over the screams. 

Ryan looked back toward the main avenue. The noise of the collision and the gunshots had cut through the music. The horde was no longer wandering aimlessly. Dozens of figures—including the pink koala mascot and a crowd of bloated tourists—had turned and were shuffling toward the derailed coaster car, their dead eyes locked onto the survivors.

Ryan grabbed the crowbar from his belt, trying to wedge it under the fiberglass panel, but the metal bent under the strain. 

The first of the walkers reached the outer perimeter fence of the ride, their pale hands gripping the wire mesh and pulling it down as the loop of the cheerful music started again. The story ended on a grim cliffhanger: Tess was pinned next to the snapping jaws of the harnessed zombie, while Ryan struggled with the wreckage as the wider horde closed in.

================================================================================

STORY 20: THE STATION
---------------------

Chapter 1: The Red Sky

The sky above the Kimberley was bleeding. 

A towering, miles-wide wall of dust—a red outback haboob—marched across the flat, scrubby landscape, swallowing the afternoon sun. The horizon had vanished behind a dense, churning barrier of rust-colored sand that rose thousands of feet into the atmosphere. The wind carried a static charge, crackling through the dry spinifex grass and making the hair on Dan's arms stand up.

Dan stood on the wide wooden verandah of the Kimberley downs homestead, his hand shielding his eyes. Next to him, Lachlan, a grizzled musterer wearing a sweat-stained Akubra hat, spit a stream of dark tobacco juice over the railing.

"She’s a big one, Dan," Lachlan said, his voice raspy. "I haven't seen a blow this thick since the dry of '18. It's going to swallow the station in twenty minutes."

From the generator shed behind the house, a low, mechanical cough sounded. The diesel motor faltered, its steady thrum dropping to a ragged stutter before recovering. 

"Dust is already getting into the air intakes," Dan said, looking at the grey-painted shed. "If the generator chokes, we lose the water pumps and the electric fence. We'll be sitting in the dark, in the middle of a red-out."

"We won't be just sitting, mate," Lachlan said, pointing a calloused finger toward the fence line. 

Through the rising red haze of the approaching storm, shapes were moving. They were slow, mechanical, and silent, staggering through the dry red dirt. The outback sun had baked them, drying their flesh until it was as hard and grey as old fence posts.

"They're coming out of the scrub," Dan whispered, his grip tightening on his rifle. "They're moving with the wind."

Chapter 2: The Mummified Horde

The wind was rising, bringing with it the first grit of red sand that stung their faces and coated their lips. 

Lachlan raised his binoculars, squinting into the growing gloom. Through the magnification, the staggering figures were terrifyingly clear. The dry, blistering heat of the Kimberley had preserved them, mummifying their bodies. Their skin was stretched tight over bone, leathery and dark, cracks showing around their joints. Many were missing ears and noses, dried away by the sun, their teeth permanently exposed in skeletal grins.

"They're completely dried out," Lachlan muttered, lowering the binoculars. "Classic walkers. Slow but tough. The bullet won't expand in 'em if they're this hard. Have to hit 'em clean in the skull."

"They're heading for the helipad," Dan said, pointing to the flat concrete circle fifty yards from the verandah. 

The station’s Robinson R44 helicopter—a small, four-seat piston machine with high landing skids—sat tied down on the pad. It was their only way out of this remote sector. The roads had been blocked by abandoned road-trains since month two, and the nearest town, Fitzroy Crossing, was a dead zone.

"The R44's got less than a quarter tank, Lachlan," Dan said, his voice tense. "I haven't flown her in a week. We need to fuel her up if we want to beat this storm."

"Fuel's in the machinery hangar," Lachlan said, adjusting the rifle slung over his back. "Let's grab the jerrycans and the siphon pump. The storm's coming, and once the red-out hits, we won't see our own hands."

Chapter 3: The Hangar Run

They ran down the verandah steps, their boots kicking up clouds of fine red dust. The wind was a hot, dry blast now, carrying enough grit to make breathing difficult. The sky had turned a dark, bruised violet, the top of the dust wall blocking the late afternoon sun.

The machinery hangar was a large, arched corrugated iron structure, its massive sliding doors closed. 

"Keep an eye on the scrub!" Dan yelled, his hand on his rifle. 

As they approached the side entrance of the hangar, three mummified figures shuffled out from the shadow of a parked tractor. They were wearing faded denim shirts, their skin shrunk around their ribs. They moved slowly, their dry joints clicking in the wind, but their milky, dead eyes were locked on Dan.

Lachlan raised his lever-action Winchester. He fired three quick shots, the cracks sharp and loud in the hot air.
