Two of the mummified heads exploded into grey dust and bone shards, the bodies collapsing into the dirt. The third zombie was hit in the shoulder, the bullet tearing through the leathery skin but barely slowing it down. 

Lachlan stepped forward, using the butt of the rifle to smash the thing’s forehead, sending it crashing to the ground.

"Door's open!" Dan yelled, sliding the hangar's side door open. 

They slipped inside, pulling the heavy door shut behind them just as the wind gave a massive howl, rattling the iron walls of the structure.

Chapter 4: Siphoning the Drum

The hangar was a vault of shadows and heat. The corrugated iron roof groaned under the force of the wind, and a fine red dust was already filtering down from the rafters, coating the stored machinery in a rusty film.

"Over here," Dan said, running to the back of the hangar where several blue steel drums of Avgas sat on wooden pallets. 

Lachlan dragged two empty green plastic jerrycans from the workbench. He unscrewed the caps while Dan inserted the plastic hose of the manual siphon pump into the drum's bunghole. 

"Start pumping," Dan said, holding the nozzle.

Lachlan worked the plastic handle of the pump. With a wet, sucking sound, the clear, volatile fuel began to flow through the hose, filling the first jerrycan. The smell of high-octane gasoline was sharp and strong, mixing with the dusty air.

Outside, the storm had arrived in earnest. 

The wind shrieked through the gaps in the iron sheets, and the sound of fine gravel pelting the metal was a constant, deafening roar. Through the noise, a new sound began to echo along the northern wall of the hangar. 

Scritch. Scritch.

It was the sound of dried, leathery fingernails scraping against the corrugated iron sheets. Dozens of them. The mummified horde had reached the building, drawn by the sound of the gunshots, and they were surrounding the hangar in the red-out.

Chapter 5: The Storm Hits

The hangar shuddered as a massive gust of wind hit, the sheet-metal walls buckling inward before popping back out with a loud boom. The red dust was now a thick fog inside the building, reducing their visibility to ten feet. Dan coughed, his eyes watering from the dust and gasoline fumes.

"Second can is full!" Lachlan yelled, screwing the cap on tight. 

Before Dan could pull the pump hose out, the side door of the hangar gave a loud, metallic screech. The latch had failed under the combined force of the wind and the weight of the dead. The door slid open two feet, letting in a blinding blast of red sand and wind.

With the dust came a mummified zombie. Its face was a dried skull, its lips gone, exposing its teeth. It was wearing a dirty, torn CFS shirt. It stumbled through the opening, its skeletal hands reaching out.

"Lachlan!" Dan shouted.

Lachlan spun, bringing the Winchester up, and fired. The bullet blew the top of the walker's skull off, the body collapsing into the open doorway. But the shot was a mistake. 

The gunshot’s report was carried by the wind, echoing through the station yard. 

Through the open doorway, in the red, howling storm, more shapes appeared. Dozens of dry, mummified walkers were crawling and stumbling toward the gap, their dead eyes fixed on the light inside.

Chapter 6: The Run to the Pad

"We have to go! Now!" Dan screamed, grabbing one of the heavy jerrycans. 

Lachlan grabbed the other, his rifle secured by its sling. They ran toward the main sliding doors. Dan reached the release lever, pulling it down, and threw his weight against the metal door. It slid open three feet, letting in a wall of pure, blinding red dust.

They stepped out into the teeth of the haboob. 

The wind was a physical weight, eighty kilometers an hour of hot, grit-laden air that stung their skin and filled their eyes. The sky was completely gone, replaced by a dark, glowing orange fog. Dan couldn't see the helipad. He had to navigate by the silhouette of the homestead roof line.

"Lachlan! Keep close!" Dan choked out, his throat dry and raw.

They stumbled forward, their boots sinking into the shifting red sand. The dust was so thick that they couldn't see the ground. Twice, Dan tripped over buried spinifex roots, nearly dropping the heavy fuel can. 

Through the red haze, a figure appeared right in front of him. It was a mummified stockman, his skin dried to a dark mahogany. Dan didn't have time to shoot. He swung the heavy twenty-liter jerrycan, the plastic corner smashing into the zombie's face. The dried skull cracked, and the walker tumbled back into the dust.

They kept running, their lungs screaming for clean air, until the high skids of the Robinson R44 materialized in the red gloom.

Chapter 7: Fueling the R44

"Open the cap!" Dan yelled, handing the jerrycan to Lachlan before climbing onto the helicopter’s landing skid.

The R44’s fuel tank was located high on the fuselage, right behind the main rotor mast. Dan reached up, unscrewed the cap, and took the jerrycan from Lachlan. The wind was buffeting the light helicopter, the rotor blades flexing and groaning in the storm.

Lachlan stood at the base of the helipad, his Winchester at his shoulder. The red dust was so thick that he could only see the silhouettes of the dead when they were within ten yards. 

"Hurry up, Dan!" Lachlan called out, firing a shot into the dust. A mummified figure collapsed, its chest cavity dry and empty. 

Dan poured the avgas into the tank, his eyes squinted shut against the stinging spray of fuel and red sand. The wind threatened to blow the fuel out of the nozzle, the sweet, volatile smell whipped away instantly. He finished the first can and dropped it, reaching down for the second.

"They're coming from the garden!" Lachlan fired again, then cycled the lever. The rifle was starting to grind, the fine red dust getting into the action. 

A mummified zombie, its skin peeled back by the outback sun, lunged out of the red haze by the steps. Lachlan pulled the trigger, but the lever jammed halfway. The rifle was clogged with grit.

Lachlan swore, dropping the gun and drawing his heavy hunting knife.

Chapter 8: The Asthma Attack

Lachlan lunged, the heavy steel blade of his knife driving upward through the jaw of the mummified stockman. The head clicked, and the zombie fell limp. He pulled the knife free, wiping the black, dry fluid on his trousers.

On the skid, Dan poured the last of the second jerrycan into the tank. He slammed the cap shut, locking it in place. "That's it! We've got enough to reach the coast!"

Suddenly, Lachlan's belt radio crackled. Through the static and the howl of the wind, a wet, gasping cough sounded.

"Lachlan... Dan..." a voice wheezed. It was George, the station's elderly cook, who was supposed to be hunkered down in the sealed kitchen. "The dust... it's through the... vents. Can't... breathe. My inhaler... it's empty..."

The transmission ended in a long, wet gasp, followed by the sound of the radio falling onto the floor.

"George!" Lachlan yelled into his mic. Nothing but static answered.

"Lachlan, we need to leave!" Dan yelled, jumping down from the skid. "The storm's crowning! We won't be able to lift off if the wind gets any higher!"

"I'm not leaving him, Dan!" Lachlan said, his jaw set. "He's been here thirty years. I'm going in."

Lachlan turned and ran toward the homestead, disappearing into the red wall of dust before Dan could stop him.

Chapter 9: Reanimation in the Dust

Lachlan kicked open the back screen door of the homestead. The interior of the house was filled with a dense, floating red haze. The air was hot and suffocating, the floor covered in a layer of red sand. 

"George!" Lachlan called out, his hand covering his mouth. 

He ran into the kitchen. Lying on the linoleum floor next to the stove was George. The elderly man was motionless, his eyes open and staring at the ceiling, his skin a pale blue under the dust. His chest wasn't moving. 

Lachlan knelt beside him, his hands shaking as he checked his neck. "George, mate. Come on."

There was no pulse. George had suffocated, his lungs failing under the intense concentration of fine outback dust.

But under the universal rules of the dead, death was only a brief transition. 

Before Lachlan could pull his hand back, George’s head snapped toward him. The milky, bloodshot eyes locked onto Lachlan's face. With a sudden, violent lurch, George grabbed Lachlan’s leg, his teeth sinking deep into the calf muscle through the heavy denim of his trousers.

Lachlan screamed in pain. He raised his heavy boot and kicked George in the throat, breaking the grip. He scrambled backward, drew his knife, and drove it down through the temple of the reanimated cook. George went limp, his body hitting the floor.

Lachlan sat back, his hand clutching his bleeding leg. The bite was deep and wide. He could already feel the burning fever starting to rise.

Chapter 10: The Takeoff

Lachlan dragged himself out of the kitchen, his leg trailing a line of dark blood in the red dust. He pushed through the back door and staggered toward the helipad, the wind nearly throwing him off his feet.

On the helipad, the R44’s main rotors were already spinning, the engine's roar swallowed by the wind. Dan was in the pilot's seat, his hands on the controls, his eyes wide as he watched the red storm close in.

"Get in! We're lifting!" Dan yelled, throwing open the passenger door.

Lachlan reached the cabin, but he didn't climb onto the skid. He leaned against the door frame, his face pale, his forehead beaded with sweat. He looked down at his leg, where the dark blood was soaking through the denim.

"Lachlan, what are you doing? Get in!" Dan roared.

"I can't, Dan," Lachlan said, his voice quiet. "George got me. Deep. I'll be turned before we hit the coast."

Dan stared at the wound, his heart freezing. "No. No, Lachlan. We can... we can cut it, we can—"

"In a four-seat chopper? You want to be at a thousand feet when I get back up?" Lachlan shook his head, a grim smile on his face. "Take off, mate. Save yourself."

He slammed the passenger door shut and stepped back, his hand raising in a final salute. 

Dan looked from the bleeding station hand to the red wall of dust closing in on the helipad. The wind was buffeting the helicopter, the skids lifting off the concrete. He pulled the collective, and the Robinson R44 rose into the red, howling storm, leaving Lachlan behind.

The story ended on a grim cliffhanger: Dan was flying blind into the howling haboob, while Lachlan stood on the helipad, his knife drawn, as the mummified dead crawled out of the dust toward him.

================================================================================

STORY 21: THE BRIDGE
--------------------

Chapter 1: The High Arch

The wind, blowing fresh off the Tasman Sea, whistled through the massive steel lattice of the Sydney Harbour Bridge. 

Vince stood on the narrow maintenance catwalk that curved along the very top of the southern arch, a hundred and thirty meters above the blue water. He adjusted his safety harness, the heavy steel clip clinking against the green-painted steel girder. He had a paint roller in one hand and a bucket of gray primer secured to the railing.

"Looks like a right mess down there, Sarah," Vince said, pointing down to the road deck. 

Sarah, a young structural engineer, was kneeling a few meters away, her clipboard secured to her thigh as she inspected a massive steel pin joint. She stood up, shielding her eyes from the bright morning sun, and looked down at the eight traffic lanes below.

The bridge was in a state of absolute, motionless gridlock. 

Hundreds of cars were packed bumper-to-bumper, extending from the toll plaza at Milsons Point all the way across the harbor to the city tunnels. Drivers were out of their vehicles, pacing the lanes, pointing toward the CBD skyline. A thick column of black smoke was rising from the direction of Circular Quay, and the distant, tinny wail of police sirens drifted up through the steel girders.

"They've blocked the bridge," Sarah said, her voice tight. "The radio was saying something about a quarantine line at the Bradfield Highway exit. They're not letting anyone into the city."

"It's not just a block, love," Vince said, squinting through his safety goggles at a silver Mercedes near the center pylon. "Look at that. People are running."

Chapter 2: The Gridlock Panic

Sarah’s UHF radio, clipped to her safety vest, crackled with static, a voice cutting through the noise.

"...all units, be advised, the Bradfield Highway is compromised. Commuters are abandoning vehicles. We have... we have multiple active assaults on the road deck. Repeat, subjects are biting. Do not attempt to—" 

The transmission dissolved into a high-pitched squeal of feedback and went silent.

"Active assaults?" Sarah muttered, her face turning pale. "Vince, what is going on down there?"

Vince didn't answer. He was staring at the silver Mercedes. A man in a torn suit had smashed the driver's side window of a red sedan next to him, dragging a screaming woman out onto the hot asphalt. He bit her shoulder, her blood spraying across the red paint. 

Within seconds, the road deck erupted into absolute chaos. 

Drivers abandoned their cars, leaving doors open, and began to run in a panicked stampede toward the north pylon. The sound of their screams, muffled by the height, still carried a chilling terror. People were climbing over hoods, trampling one another, desperate to escape.

"We need to get down," Vince said, his voice flat and hard. He unclipped his safety harness from the arch cable, his hands moving with rapid efficiency. 

"Down?" Sarah gasped. "To that?"

"We can't stay on the arch, Sarah," Vince said, pointing to the dark clouds rolling in from the west. "The wind is coming up, and if we get stuck up here with no power and no way down, we'll freeze tonight. The maintenance stairs lead to the south pylon pylon chamber. It's secure. We go there."

Chapter 3: The Descent

The descent down the massive steel arch was a test of nerve. 

The maintenance walkway was a narrow, open-grid steel stair structure that ran down the inner slope of the arch. Every step Vince and Sarah took vibrated with the wind, and through the open grating under their boots, the drop to the harbor below was a dizzying void. 

"Keep your eyes on the steel, Sarah," Vince called back, his hand sliding along the cold safety rail. "Don't look down."

The noise from the road deck was growing louder, rising through the steel frame. The stampede had thinned out, leaving behind a graveyard of hundreds of abandoned, idling cars. But the streets weren't empty. 

Figures were shuffling between the vehicles. They moved slowly, their limbs stiff, their clothes stained with blood. The woman who had been bitten by the Mercedes was already back up, dragging her leg as she wandered between the open car doors.

"They're... they're standing back up," Sarah whispered, her boot catching on a steel step. She recovered her balance, her knuckles white as she gripped the rail. "Vince, those people were dead. I saw them."

"They're not people anymore, love," Vince said, his voice grim. "My brother was in the police. He called me last night. Said the dead are rising. Told me to stay off the streets."

They reached the lower junction of the arch, where the steel structure met the massive stone pylon. A narrow, vertical ladder led down to the gantry level just above the road deck.

Chapter 4: The Gantry

The air at the gantry level was thick with the acrid smell of burning rubber and gasoline. A black SUV had crashed into the bridge railing directly below them, its engine bay engulfed in flames that licked the steel beams. The wind, whipped into a gale by the harbor, blew the greasy black smoke directly across the catwalk.

"Put your bandana over your mouth!" Vince yelled, his eyes watering from the smoke. 

They crawled along the steel beams of the lower gantry, their bodies low to avoid being spotted by the dead on the road deck only fifteen feet below. The heat from the burning SUV was intense, the steel under Vince's gloves hot enough to blister.

"Vince, the ladder to the pylon deck is just ahead," Sarah coughed, her throat raw. 

Through the shifting smoke, the vertical steel ladder appeared, leading down to the maintenance deck of the massive granite pylon. 

But as Vince reached the top of the ladder, a face appeared at the bottom. 

It was a driver—a man in a blood-stained business shirt, his eyes wide with panic. He was climbing the ladder, his hands grabbing the rungs with frantic strength. 

"Help me!" the man gasped, his voice raspy. "They're... they're right behind me!"

Behind the man, at the base of the pylon, several slow, shuffling figures emerged from the gridlock, their bloody hands reaching for the ladder.

Chapter 5: The Ladder Trap

"Get up! Move!" Vince yelled down, reaching out to help the driver.

The man scrambled up the rungs, but his movements were clumsy. As he reached the middle of the ladder, a hand shot out from the smoke at the base. One of the dead—a woman in a torn yellow dress—grabbed his ankle, her fingers locking onto his boot.

The man screamed, his grip slipping from the rungs. 

"Vince!" Sarah yelled, pointing to the base. 

More panicked commuters had noticed the maintenance ladder and were rushing toward it, shoving the slow-moving dead aside to climb. It was a bottleneck of human desperation. One man, carrying a heavy leather bag, began to climb over the woman, his boots kicking her face as he fought for space.

"Hold on!" Vince leaned over the gantry, grabbing the first driver's collar. 

But as he pulled the man up, the climber's sleeve slid back. A deep, bloody bite mark was visible on his forearm, the flesh torn and bleeding. The man’s eyes were already losing their focus, a thin layer of sweat covering his forehead.

"He's bitten, Vince!" Sarah screamed, grabbing Vince's arm and pulling him back. "He's going to turn!"

The bitten driver reached the gantry platform, but his strength suddenly failed. He collapsed onto the metal grating, his body convulsing as the fever took hold.

Chapter 6: The Fall

The man who had climbed over the woman was right behind the collapsed driver. He reached the top rungs of the ladder, his face pale with terror. 

"Get him off the platform!" the climber screamed, trying to push past the convulsing man. 

But as he did, the collapsed driver’s body went stiff, his chest stopping. A second later, his eyes snapped open—milky and bloodshot. He lunged upward, his teeth snapping shut on the climber's hand. 

The climber screamed, losing his footing on the slick metal rungs. He fell backward, his arms flailing, and plummeted thirty feet onto the asphalt road deck below. 

The sound of his impact—a heavy, wet crash—was clear even over the roar of the wind. He lay motionless on the road, his neck bent at an impossible angle, blood pooling around his head.

"Oh my god," Sarah gasped, covering her mouth.

But under the universal rules of the dead, there was no peace. 

Within seconds, the fallen climber's fingers began to twitch. His broken limbs moved mechanically, and he slowly, awkwardly pushed himself up from the tarmac, his neck still bent. He stood back up, his dead eyes turning toward the ladder, and began to shuffle back toward the pylon.

"Vince, the door to the pylon pylon chamber," Sarah said, pointing to the heavy steel door on the concrete deck. "We need to get inside."

Chapter 7: The Pylon Chamber

Vince ran to the heavy steel pylon door, grabbing the lever handle. It was unlocked. He threw it open, and they scrambled inside the cold, stone chamber of the south pylon. 

Vince slammed the door shut, throwing the heavy brass deadbolt. 

The silence inside was immediate, the wind and the screams of the road deck cut off by the three-foot-thick granite walls. The pylon chamber was a massive, concrete-walled room, containing the structural anchor cables of the bridge and a spiral staircase that descended to the maintenance offices below.

"It's dark," Sarah said, clicking on her headlamp. The beam cut through the dusty air, illuminating the concrete arches and the steel cable conduits.

From the depths of the pylon below, a heavy, metallic clang echoed. 

Someone was trying to open the road-deck level doors. The sound was followed by a series of muffled shouts and the sharp, rapid cracks of a handgun. 

"Someone's down there," Vince whispered, his hand on his pocket knife. "AFP, likely. They have a security post in the base of the pylon."

"We should go down," Sarah said. "They might have a radio. Or a way off the bridge."

They began to descend the spiral stairs, the concrete steps cold under their boots, their lights casting long, shifting shadows on the curved walls.

Chapter 8: The Heart Attack

They reached the pylon’s maintenance office level. The door was cracked open, a sliver of light coming from a battery-powered lantern on the desk inside.

Vince pushed the door open. 

Sitting on the floor, his back pressed against a filing cabinet, was an Australian Federal Police (AFP) officer. His blue uniform was covered in soot, and his tactical vest was open. He was clutching his chest, his face pale and slick with sweat. His breathing was a rapid, shallow gasp.

"Help..." the officer wheezed, his eyes wide with panic. "My chest... it's like a vice..."

Sarah ran to his side, kneeling in the dust. "He's having a heart attack, Vince. Do we have a first-aid kit here?"

"The crew locker is down on the pier level," Vince said, searching the desk drawers. "Nothing in here but office gear."

"Sir, try to stay calm. Breathe slowly," Sarah said, checking his pulse. It was weak and irregular. 

The officer’s eyes suddenly rolled back. His head fell sideways, his body going completely limp. Sarah pressed two fingers to his neck, waiting, but there was no pulse. 

"Arthur... I mean, sir?" Sarah checked his airway, but he had stopped breathing. "He's gone, Vince. His heart gave out."

Vince stood back, his flashlight beam illuminating the officer's still face. The room was silent, save for the hum of the emergency lantern. 

"We need to get his keycard," Vince said, reaching down. "It's the only way to open the security gates to the pedestrian walkway."

As his hand touched the officer's tactical belt, the dead man's fingers twitched.

Chapter 9: The Reanimation

The officer's eyes snapped open. 

They were covered in a milky, bloodshot glaze, the pupils dilated. With a sudden, mechanical jerk, he grabbed Vince’s wrist. The grip was cold and incredibly strong, the fingers locking like iron clamps. 

"Vince!" Sarah screamed, backing away.

The reanimated officer lunged upward, his teeth snapping shut. Vince threw his weight backward, his free hand shoving the zombie's face away, but the officer’s fingernails tore through Vince's sleeve, scratching his forearm deep enough to draw blood.

"Sarah, the door!" Vince yelled, his boot slamming into the officer's chest. 

The kick knocked the zombie back against the filing cabinet. Vince scrambled out of the office, Sarah pulling the heavy oak door shut behind him. Vince threw his weight against the wood, and Sarah turned the key in the lock. 

Inside, the reanimated officer began to slam his body against the door, the wood groaning under the impact.

Vince leaned against the wall, panting. He looked down at his arm. A three-inch scratch ran down his forearm, the skin red and starting to bleed.

Sarah saw it, her breath catching. "Vince... you're scratched."

Vince covered the wound with his hand, his face turning pale in the beam of her headlamp. "It's just a scratch, Sarah. Let's get to the walkway. We need to get off this bridge."

Chapter 10: The Steel Ridge

They climbed back up the spiral stairs, exiting the stone pylon onto the external maintenance deck. The cold wind hit them immediately, carrying the smell of smoke from the burning city.

Vince ran to the safety rail, looking down at the pedestrian walkway. It was blocked by a wire gate, and below, the stairs to the harbour pool were swarmed by walkers. The city was a grid of fires, the streetlights flickering.

Vince sat down on the edge of the steel beam, his legs dangling over the hundred-foot drop to the water. He pulled his sleeve back, looking at the red, inflamed scratch. He could already feel a dry, burning heat starting to creep up his arm, his fingers beginning to stiffen.

"Sarah," Vince said, his voice quiet in the wind. "You need to take the keycard and climb down the eastern pylon ladder. The dead are mostly on the west side."

"I'm not leaving you, Vince," Sarah said, her eyes filling with tears.

"You have to, love," Vince said, looking out at the burning silhouette of the Sydney Opera House. "I can feel it. The fever's rising. In a few minutes, I won't be Vince anymore. I don't want to be up here when I turn."

Behind them, the heavy steel pylon door began to rattle, the reanimated officer and the walkers from the road deck pounding on the metal. The steel hinges groaned under the pressure.

Vince sat on the steel ridge, looking at the red sky, while Sarah stood at the ladder, the dead hammering on the door.

================================================================================

STORY 22: THE PORTAL
--------------------

Chapter 1: The Black Seam

The continuous miner machine roared, its heavy steel drum studded with carbide teeth shearing coal from the face of the Hunter Valley coal seam. Gavin sat in the steel cab of the shuttle car, the headlights of his vehicle illuminating the thick black dust that hung in the air. He had worked the seam for fifteen years, six hundred meters beneath the rolling vineyards of the surface.

To his left, Jemma, the safety manager, stood checking a hand-held digital gas monitor. Her face was streaked with black dust, her bright orange safety vest the only spot of color in the dark seam.

"Methane’s at zero-point-five, Gavin," she said, her voice carrying over the roar of the machinery. "Ventilation’s running good. Let's get this load back to the boot."

Suddenly, the roar of the shearer died. 

The primary lights lining the coal seam flickered once and went completely black. The mechanical ventilation ducts, which had been hissing with a steady flow of fresh air, fell silent. The only remaining light came from the LED beams on their helmets and the headlights of Gavin's battery-powered shuttle car.

"What happened?" Gavin asked, popping his safety harness.

"Power cut," Jemma said, looking at her black monitor screen. "The surface sub-station must have gone down. That means the primary exhaust fans are off."

In the silence of the mine, the only sound was the drip of water from the roof and the creaking of the timber supports. The air, which had been cool and moving, immediately began to grow stagnant and hot. 

"We need to get out, Gavin," Jemma said, her voice tight. "Without the fans, the firedamp will settle. If we get a build-up, we're done."

Chapter 2: The Stagnant Air

The air in the seam was growing thick, tasting of old dust and sulphur. 

Jemma tapped her gas monitor, the screen lighting up. The methane level had risen to one-point-two percent. In the enclosed workings, the gas was seeping from the newly sheared coal face, accumulating in the roof arches where the ventilation could no longer clear it.

"Methane's rising, Gavin," Jemma said, her voice shaking slightly. "It's at one-point-five. If it hits five percent, we're in the explosive range. Any spark from the battery contacts or a steel tool will trigger a blast."

"Where's the rest of the crew?" Gavin asked, grabbing a non-sparking brass wrench from the shuttle car's tool rack.

"They were working the western drift," Jemma said, looking down the dark tunnel. "I tried to call them on the mine-phone, but the line’s dead. We can't wait. The drift portal is three miles of incline to the surface. If we walk it, we'll run out of oxygen before we hit the light."

"We're not walking," Gavin said, stepping into the cab of the electric shuttle car. "This buggy runs on a seventy-two-volt battery pack. She doesn't need air to run. Climb in. We're riding the drift."

Jemma climbed onto the passenger bench, her light scanning the dark tunnel. Behind them, the coal seam was silent, a black vault filling with invisible, volatile gas. 

Gavin engaged the drive motor. The shuttle car hummed, its wide rubber tires gripping the wet stone floor as they headed into the main drift tunnel.

Chapter 3: The Shuttle Car

The shuttle car hummed along the dark drift tunnel, its heavy-duty headlights cutting through the coal dust. The incline was steep, rising at a ten-degree angle toward the surface.

"How far to the second level?" Jemma asked, holding onto the steel safety rail.

"About a mile," Gavin said, his hands steady on the steering wheel. "But the battery's running warm. The incline's drawing too much current. If the thermal cutout trips, we're stranded."

The tunnel was a concrete-lined tube, supported by massive steel arches. Every hundred yards, a small emergency alcove was carved into the stone. 

As they passed one of the alcoves, Gavin’s headlights illuminated a shape sitting on a wooden bench. It was a miner, his overalls torn, his head slumped forward. 

"Stop, Gavin!" Jemma said. "It's Terry."

Gavin brought the shuttle car to a halt, the brakes squealing in the quiet. Terry was an older musterer who had transitioned to the mines, a stubble-chinned bloke from Muswellbrook.

"Terry, you alright mate?" Gavin called out.

Terry didn't answer. He slowly lifted his head. His skin was a pale, bruised grey, and a thick smear of dried blood covered his temple. His eyes were completely milky, the pupils gone. He let out a dry, rattling hiss, his hands reaching for the shuttle car's tires.

"Gavin, look at his head," Jemma gasped, her light illuminating a deep skull fracture. "He's... he's dead."

Chapter 4: The Drift Portal

"Get back, Terry!" Gavin yelled, slamming the gear lever forward. 

The shuttle car surged forward, its wide tires rolling past the reaching hands of the reanimated miner. Terry fell sideways, his leathery skin scraping against the concrete floor, but he didn't stop. He dragged himself onto the track behind them, his jaw clicking in the dark.

"Gavin, if Terry was down here like that, what happened on the surface?" Jemma asked, her voice cracking. 

"The outbreak," Gavin said, his jaw tight. "The manager was talking about it before we went down. Said the towns in the valley were being quarantined. I thought he was full of it."

They drove deeper into the incline tunnel. The air was growing hot, the lack of ventilation making it difficult to breathe. 

Through the haze of coal dust, more shapes appeared on the tracks ahead. They were surface workers—washery hands and office staff—their high-vis shirts torn and bloodied. They had fled down the drift tunnel to escape the chaos on the surface, only to turn in the dark.

They shuffled down the slope, their milky eyes locking onto the shuttle car's headlights.

"We can't stop, Gavin!" Jemma yelled, checking her gas monitor. "Methane's at three percent! If we hit a pocket of gas, any collision could spark it!"

Gavin hit the throttle, the electric motor screaming as the car plowed into the first group of walkers.

Chapter 5: The Crushed Cage

The shuttle car rammed through the walkers, the heavy steel chassis crushing them against the concrete walls. The wet, slapping sounds of the impact were loud in the confined space, but the electric motor kept hummering, pushing them up the incline.

But a mile from the surface, Gavin slammed the brakes on, the tires sliding on the wet stone. 

"What is it?" Jemma asked, her hand on her safety belt.

Ahead of them, the main shaft cage—the massive steel elevator used to haul coal and machinery to the surface—had crashed. The steel support cables had snapped at the surface, dropping the cage hundreds of meters down the shaft. It had wedged itself into the drift tunnel junction, a crumpled pile of steel, cables, and structural beams that completely blocked the tracks.

Inside the collapsed cage, a dozen figures were thrashing. 

They were the morning shift miners who had been in the elevator when it fell. Their bodies were broken, legs and arms bent at impossible angles, but under the universal rules of the dead, the impact had not stopped them. They clawed at the steel mesh of the cage, their teeth snapping through the gaps.

"We're blocked," Gavin said, his headlights reflecting off the crumpled steel. "The shuttle car can't get past that. The rails are torn up."

"We have to clear it," Jemma said, looking back down the dark tunnel. "The methane is at three-point-five percent. We don't have long before the air becomes toxic."

Chapter 6: Clearing the Rails

"Grab the hydraulic jack from the back utility bed," Gavin said, stepping out of the cab. 

He approached the crumpled elevator cage, his light scanning the wreckage. The steel frame was wedged between the floor and the roof arch, but the main guide rails were still intact. If they could push the lower corner of the cage three feet to the left, the shuttle car could squeeze past.

Jemma dragged the heavy, ten-ton hydraulic hand-jack from the utility bed. She positioned it between the concrete tunnel wall and the crumpled steel corner of the cage.

"Watch the gaps," Gavin warned, pointing to the thrashing arms of the turned miners inside the cage. "They're still reaching."

Lachlan... wait, Gavin grabbed the jack lever, his calloused hands pumping the handle. The hydraulic piston slowly extended, the steel frame of the cage groaning under the pressure. The metal shrieked as it slid across the stone floor.

"Keep pumping, Gavin!" Jemma said, her flashlight beam scanning the dark tunnel behind them. 

The sound of the grinding steel was loud, echoing down the shaft. From the dark incline below, a low, dry rasp sounded. Through the haze of coal dust, several blue-skinned figures were climbing the slope toward them, drawn by the noise.

Chapter 7: The Methane Rise

Jemma’s gas monitor began to chirp, a high-pitched, steady alarm. 

"Methane's at four percent, Gavin!" she yelled, her voice trembling. "No shots! If you fire that rifle, the muzzle flash will blow this tunnel sky-high!"

Gavin swore, dropping his hands from the jack. He couldn't use his rifle. They had to rely on manual tools. He grabbed the brass wrench from his belt, his heart hammer-beating.

At that moment, the hydraulic jack gave a sharp hiss, the release valve popping under the extreme load. The cage shifted, and a section of the collapsed steel mesh tore open. 

A turned miner—his legs crushed, but his upper body intact—crawled through the gap. He dragged himself along the floor, his blackened, bloody hands lunging forward and grabbing Gavin's ankle.

"Gavin!" Jemma screamed.

She lunged forward, her heavy surveyors' steel trip-pin held like a spear. She drove the pointed end down through the zombie's skull. The skull cracked, and the miner went limp, releasing Gavin's boot.

"Get back to the car!" Gavin yelled, prying the jack free. The cage had cleared the rails by just enough.

Chapter 8: The Silent Asphyxia

They scrambled back into the shuttle car. Gavin engaged the motor, and the buggy squeezed past the shifted elevator cage, its tires scraping against the steel mesh before clearing the bottleneck.

The incline became steeper, the air growing hot and thin. 

In the utility bed of the shuttle car, Bill, an older shearer operator who had been riding with them since the power cut, was breathing in shallow, raspy gasps. He had been quiet, his head resting against the side panel.

"Bill, you alright back there?" Jemma called out, turning in her seat.

Bill didn't answer. His head lolled to the side. 

Jemma reached over the seat back, checking his pulse. His skin was cold, his lips a dark shade of blue. He wasn't breathing. The rising concentration of methane and carbon monoxide in the unventilated tunnel had silently suffocated him. His heart had stopped.

"Gavin, Bill's dead," Jemma said, her voice hollow. "He's suffocated."

"We're almost at the portal gate, Jemma," Gavin said, his eyes fixed on the tunnel ahead. "Hold on. We just need to hit the surface."

But behind them, in the dark utility bed, Bill’s fingers began to twitch.

Chapter 9: The Turn in the Dark

"Bill?" Jemma whispered, reaching back toward him.

But Bill’s hand shot out, his fingers locking onto her collar. His eyes snapped open, a milky, bloodshot glaze covering his pupils. Under the universal rules of the dead, the asphyxiation had not been the end. 

He lunged forward over the seat back, his jaw clicking as he snapped at her face. Jemma threw her head back, but the space in the cab was too small. Bill’s teeth sank deep into her shoulder, tearing through her safety vest and shirts into the flesh below.

Jemma screamed in agony, the sound echoing off the concrete walls of the tunnel.

"Jemma!" Gavin yelled. 

He couldn't look away from the steep, curving track. He reached blindly with his left hand, grabbing the brass wrench from the console, and swung it backward. The heavy tool struck Bill in the temple. The skull fractured, and the reanimated miner went limp, releasing Jemma and falling back into the utility bed.

Jemma collapsed against the dashboard, clutching her shoulder, her fingers quickly staining with dark, hot blood.

"I've got the gate in sight!" Gavin yelled, pointing ahead. 

Through the haze, the heavy steel security doors of the drift portal exit appeared, lit by the shuttle car's headlights.

Chapter 10: The Portal Gate

Gavin slammed the shuttle car to a halt in front of the drift portal exit. 

The heavy steel security gates, designed to keep out trespassers, were closed and bolted. Through the iron bars, Gavin could see the surface—a bright, sun-drenched yard of the Hunter Valley mine. But the yard was a scene of wreckage. A massive Caterpillar loader had crashed into the gate from the outside, its steel bucket jammed into the frame, welding the doors shut under the weight.

"It's blocked," Gavin whispered, his hands dropping from the steering wheel. "We can't get the buggy out. The loader is wedged."

Beside him, Jemma’s breathing was growing shallow. Her skin was a deathly pale, sweat beading on her forehead as the toxic fever from the bite took hold. 

"Gavin... it's hot," she murmured, her eyes starting to glaze over. 

Gavin looked back down the dark incline. The headlights of the shuttle car reflected off dozens of shapes crawling and shuffling up the tracks toward them. The horde they had run through below had followed the sound of the electric motor, and they were closing the gap.

"We have to climb through the loader," Gavin said, reaching for Jemma. 

But as he did, Jemma's chest stopped moving. Her hand fell limp from her shoulder, her eyes fixed and dilated. 

The story ended on a grim cliffhanger: Gavin was trapped at the blocked gate, with the reanimating Jemma beside him in the cab and the crawling horde of turned miners closing in from the dark tunnel behind.

================================================================================

STORY 23: MAWSON STATION
------------------------

Chapter 1: The Dark Winter

The wind that shrieked across the Antarctic ice shelf was a physical force, a ninety-knot blizzard that whipped the dry, powdery snow into a solid wall of white. Mawson Station, a small cluster of red and yellow insulated shelters built on the rocky shore of Holme Bay, was completely swallowed by the long polar night. Outside, the temperature had dropped to minus thirty-six degrees.

Inside the green-painted generator building, Alistair stood over the master control board. He was wearing his heavy polar parka, the hood trimmed with synthetic fur, his face lined and pale.

"Generator number two is running rough, Elena," Alistair said, checking the fuel flow meter. "The fuel pressure is dropping. I think the intake lines from the external tank farm are starting to ice up."

Elena, the station's chief mechanic, stood at the workbench, her fingers wrapped in greasy thermal gloves as she cleaned a fuel injector. "The heating trace line must have failed. If the diesel gets too cold, it'll wax up and choke the engines. Once the generators go, the heating loops in the living quarters will freeze in twenty minutes."

She walked to the triple-paned window, rubbing a circle in the frost to peer into the howling darkness. The station's external sodium lights cast a dim, yellow glow on the surrounding ice ridges, but the visibility was near zero.

"We have to go out, Alistair," she said, her voice grim. "If we don't clear the intake filter at the tank farm, we're dead. The wind is rising, and the storm's only getting worse."

Alistair adjusted his snow goggles, his chest tightening. The isolation of Mawson was absolute. They were thousands of kilometers from any help, and the world on the other side of the Southern Ocean had fallen apart months ago. 

"Right," Alistair said, checking the bolt-action rifle slung over his shoulder. "Get the guide line. Let's move."

Chapter 2: The Locked Ship

"Elena, look at the screen before we head out," Alistair said, pointing to the station’s main navigation console. 

The digital map showed Holme Bay, its waters locked in a six-foot layer of solid winter sea ice. Five miles offshore, a static green icon represented the Aurora Australis, the Australian Antarctic division’s red-hulled supply ship. It had been trapped in the pack ice since the start of winter, unable to return to Hobart before the ice closed in.

"Any response from the crew?" Elena asked, zip-tying her thermal boots.

"Nothing but static for three weeks," Alistair said, his voice quiet. "The last radio call from the captain said the flu had swept the entire crew. He was the last one standing, and he was burning up. He said they were locking the hatches."

Alistair switched the monitor feed to the station’s high-powered infrared camera, which was mounted on the main radio mast. The screen showed the vast, cold white of the sea ice in shades of blue. 

But near the coast, only half a mile from the station's perimeter fence, several warm, bright yellow shapes appeared on the screen. 

They were figures, moving in a slow, mechanical line across the frozen sea. They didn't have the rapid heat signatures of living humans; they were cold, their extremities blue, but they were walking. 

"They're off the ship," Elena whispered, her eyes wide. "They walked across the ice."

"The cold didn't stop them," Alistair said, his hand sliding down to the rifle stock. "Under the rules, anyone who dies on that ship... they get back up. They're coming for the station's heat."

Chapter 3: The Ice Walkers

The cold outside was a physical weight, instantly freezing any exposed skin. 

Alistair and Elena pushed through the heavy insulated door of the generator building, stepping onto the snow-packed deck. The wind shrieked, ninety knots of freezing air carrying a blinding drift of white snow. The sodium lights of the station cast a long, dim cone of yellow light into the blizzard.

Alistair raised his binoculars, wiping the frost from the lenses. 

Through the swirling whiteout, the figures were visible. They had reached the rocky perimeter of the station, their boots crunching on the frozen shale. They were the crew of the Aurora Australis, still wearing their heavy orange survival parkas. Their faces were swollen and blue, their skin frozen to a marble-like hardness. Icicles hung from their eyelashes and chin straps, and their mouths were frozen open in silent, stiff gasps.

"They're coming up the service ramp!" Elena yelled over the roar of the wind.

The frozen dead moved slowly, their joints popping and clicking like dry twigs as they forced their stiff limbs through the snowdrifts. The extreme cold had slowed them further, but they didn't stop. They didn't feel the frostbite or the wind. 

"Get the line to the fuel farm!" Alistair shouted, his breath freezing on his woolen collar. "We need to clear those valves before they trap us in the main block!"

He unslung his rifle, his gloved fingers shaking as he aimed at the lead figure—a turned deckhand whose orange parka was split, exposing a frozen, blue ribcage.

Chapter 4: The Fuel Farm

The crack of Alistair's rifle was a flat pop, instantly swallowed by the howling wind. 

The bullet hit the turned deckhand in the forehead. The frozen skull shattered like a hollow ice sculpture, and the body fell backward, hitting the hard-packed snow with a heavy, solid thud. It didn't slide; it was as stiff as a block of wood.

"Keep moving!" Elena yelled, pulling the yellow guide rope from her pack. She clipped the carabiner to the steel post of the generator deck, the metal cold enough to skin her fingers if she hadn't been wearing gloves.

They stepped off the wooden deck into the deep snow, holding onto the guide line as they fought their way toward the fuel farm. The fuel farm consisted of six massive, insulated steel tanks sitting on concrete plinths fifty yards away, connected to the generator building by a series of insulated pipe loops.

The blizzard was a blinding wall, reducing their world to the yellow line of the rope and the white sand of the drift. 

Elena reached the first tank's valve box. She threw open the steel cover, her flashlight beam illuminating the primary filter housing. The fuel lines were coated in a thick layer of white rime, and a steady drip of gelled, cloudy diesel was leaking from the main gasket.

"The trace line’s dead!" Elena yelled, her breath freezing on the valve wheel. "The heating loop must have shorted out. I need to clear the screen before the engine starves!"

Chapter 5: The Blizzard Run

Elena pulled a heavy steel spanner from her tool bag, her hands shaking from the cold. She began to loosen the nuts of the filter housing, the metal screaming under the force of her wrench. 

"Watch the line, Elena!" Alistair called out, his rifle raised as he scanned the swirling whiteout.

Through the sheets of blowing snow, shapes emerged. They were no longer walking in a neat line; the wind had scattered them, and they emerged from the drifts like ghosts. One was a female researcher from the ship, her long hair frozen into a stiff, white fan behind her head. Her face was a mask of blue frost, her fingers missing their nails, worn down by scratching at the ship's steel hatches before she died.

She shambled toward the valve box, her boots crunching on the frozen shale.

"They're coming from the south, too!" Alistair yelled, firing another shot. 

The bullet tore through the researcher's neck, but she didn't slow down. Without a brain hit, she was immune. Alistair cycled the bolt, his gloves stiff, and fired again. The second shot shattered the temple, and the body dropped into the drift.

"Got the filter clean!" Elena gasped, slamming the housing shut. She grabbed the valve wheel, throwing her weight against the iron spokes. With a grinding crunch of ice, the valve turned, and the clear, warm diesel began to flow through the line.

"The line’s open!" she yelled. "Let's get back to the melt room!"

Chapter 6: The Melt Room

They scrambled back along the guide rope, the wind howling behind them like a siren. 

The melt room—a small, insulated shelter housing the massive snow-melting tanks that supplied the station’s water—sat halfway between the fuel farm and the main quarters block. Its heavy, refrigerator-style door was their closest refuge from the storm.

Elena reached the door, grabbing the steel handle. It wouldn't budge. A thick sheet of blue ice had formed over the frame, sealing the rubber gaskets tight.

"It's iced shut!" she screamed, the wind tearing the words from her mouth.

"Use the pry-bar!" Alistair yelled, backing against the wall to guard the ramp.

Elena grabbed the steel bar from her belt and began to hammer at the ice around the hinges, sending frozen chips flying. 

From the shadow of the melt room tank, a figure lunged out of the whiteout. It was the ship’s first mate, his face a swollen, blue mask, his hands frozen into stiff claws. He didn't make a sound. Before Elena could turn, the first mate’s frozen hands grabbed her shoulders, his icy weight slamming her against the steel door.

Elena screamed, the cold of his grip penetrating her heavy parka. The first mate's jaw clicked, his teeth snapping at her neck.

Chapter 7: The Blowtorch

Alistair swung his rifle, the steel barrel slamming into the first mate's temple. The frozen head cracked with a wet pop, and the body fell sideways, sliding down the icy ramp. 

Elena scrambled back, coughing, her hands shaking as she grabbed the diesel blowtorch from her maintenance pack. She clicked the igniter, and a steady, bright orange flame hissed into the freezing air. 

She swept the flame along the door frame, the heat melting the blue ice in seconds. The steam rose in white plumes, instantly freezing into ice crystals in the wind. She threw the latch, and the heavy door creaked open.

But as the heat from the torch swept across the surrounding deck, it melted the edge of a massive, ten-foot snowdrift that had accumulated against the side of the melt room. 

The snow wall collapsed, sliding down onto the ramp. 

With the snow came three more bodies, their survival suits frozen and dirty. They had been buried in the drift, preserved by the ice. The heat from the torch had thawed them enough to move, and their milky, glazed eyes snapped open. They immediately began to crawl out of the wet snow toward the door, their frozen fingers scratching at the steel ramp.

"Get inside!" Alistair yelled, pushing Elena through the door.

Chapter 8: The Red Beacon

They slammed the melt room door shut, throwing the latch. Inside, the hum of the melting tanks was loud, but it was warmer than the storm outside. 

"We can't stay here, Elena," Alistair said, checking the power board. "The quarters block is another eighty yards. The visibility has dropped to zero. We've lost the guide line. If we step out there without a beacon, we'll wander onto the sea ice and freeze."

"The radio mast," Elena said, pointing to the schematic on the wall. "The emergency beacon controls are at the base of the mast. If we turn on the red flasher, we can see the light through the whiteout from here."

"I'll go," Alistair said. "The mast ladder is exposed. I've got the harness."

He stepped back out into the blizzard, the wind immediately hitting him. The radio mast rose like a skeletal tower into the dark sky, its steel struts covered in a thick layer of rime ice.

Alistair climbed the steel ladder, his hands freezing through his gloves. The wind was a ninety-knot roar, threatening to pull him off the steel with every step. He climbed twenty feet, reaching the emergency control box. 

He threw the switch. 

A second later, a bright, pulsing red light erupted from the top of the mast. It cut through the whiteout, a blood-red glow that illuminated the swirling snow. Alistair let out a breath, starting his descent.

Chapter 9: The Cold Hand

As Alistair descended the ladder, a sudden, violent gust of wind whipped the mast. His frozen boot slipped on the ice-covered rung, and he fell.

"Alistair!" Elena screamed, lunging out of the melt room doorway. 

She caught him by the harness strap as he swung off the ladder, her boots sliding on the icy deck. She held him, her muscles straining, as he dangled ten feet above the rocky ground. 

But below them, a shape emerged from the shadow of the pylon. 

It was a turned cook from the ship, his apron frozen stiff over his survival suit. He had climbed the lower struts of the mast, his frozen, blue hands reaching up through the steel trellis. Before Alistair could pull his legs up, the zombie’s teeth snapped shut on his ankle, tearing through the thick leather of his boot.

Alistair screamed, the pain sharp and burning even in the sub-zero air. 

Elena pulled with all her strength, hauling Alistair onto the deck. He kicked back, his boot smashing the zombie’s face, sending the frozen body tumbling into the drift.

"He got me, Elena," Alistair gasped, clutching his ankle. "He bit through the leather."

Chapter 10: The White Tomb

They staggered toward the main quarters block, guided by the pulsing red beacon light. The wind was a roaring wall, and the cold was seeped deep into Alistair's bones. He dragged his bitten leg, the ankle already turning a dark, bruised shade.

They reached the heavy, yellow insulated door of the quarters. Elena grabbed the digital keypad lock, but the screen was dead, coated in a thick layer of frost. She tried the mechanical override key, but the lock cylinder was frozen solid.

"It's frozen!" Elena yelled, hammering the metal handle with her spanner.

At that moment, the red beacon light on the mast flickered and died. The station’s backup generator had finally choked, the gelled fuel blocking the lines. The sodium lights went black, plunging the platform into total, freezing polar darkness.

The wind howled in the dark. 

Alistair fell against the door, his breathing shallow. He could feel the burning fever of the bite rising, his body beginning to go stiff in the sub-zero night. 

Through the dark whiteout, the crunch of frozen boots on the shale sounded from the ramp below. The ice walkers had reached the deck, their milky eyes invisible in the blackness, but their cold hands reaching out.

The story ended on a grim cliffhanger: Alistair was freezing and turning in the dark polar night, while Elena hammered on the frozen quarters door as the dead closed in.

================================================================================

STORY 24: THE LIGHTHOUSE
------------------------

Chapter 1: The Gathering Gale

The wind at Cape Leeuwin did not merely blow; it roared, a physical weight that pressed against the granite blocks of the lighthouse. Here, at the extreme southwestern tip of Australia, the Indian Ocean met the Southern Ocean in a churning collision of slate-grey water and white foam. 

Inside the lantern room, seventy feet above the surging tide, Arthur polished the heavy glass panels of the lens. His hands were calloused, joints stiff with arthritis, but his movements were methodical. He had kept this light for thirty years. He wasn't about to stop now, even if the world on the mainland had gone quiet.

"The barometric pressure is plummeting, Arthur," Maya said, not looking up from the weather station terminal. Her voice was taut. She was twenty-four, a bureau meteorologist who had fled the panic in Perth weeks ago. "We're looking at a category three system rolling in from the west. Sustained winds over a hundred and fifty kilometers an hour."

"The lens doesn't care about the wind," Arthur replied, his voice dry as kelp. "Just the generator. Did you check the fuel day-tank?"

"It's full. But the main storage tank down at the cottages is low. We only have enough diesel to run the light for another three days," she said, finally turning to face him. "And the radio is still dead. No broadcasts from the HMAS Choules. If they don't arrive tonight, we're out of options."

Arthur paused, his rag hovering over the glass. The Choules, an amphibious landing ship, was supposed to be evacuating survivors from the coastal pockets. The lighthouse was their designated beacon, the only landmark visible along this treacherous, reef-strewn coast in a storm.

"They'll come," Arthur said. "They have to."

Maya walked to the curved glass, looking down into the darkness. The surf below was a frothing, chaotic mess, throwing spray high up the cliffs. 

Then she froze, her breath fogging the pane. "Arthur. Look at the beach."

Chapter 2: The Rising Surf

Arthur joined her at the glass, squinting through the salt spray and the gathering gloom. The tide was coming in, throwing massive swells against the jagged granite rocks at the base of the cape.

In the foaming shallows, something was moving against the pull of the undertow. It wasn't seaweed or driftwood.

"Is that... people?" Maya whispered, her knuckles white against the brass railing.

Arthur reached for the heavy binoculars on the shelf and adjusted the focus. Through the rain-speckled lenses, the pale, bloated forms became clear. They were dressed in lifejackets, tattered clothes, and oilskins—refugees from some foundered vessel that had tried to run the gauntlet of the reefs.

But they weren't floating.

A man in a torn yellow slicker dragged himself out of the white foam, his limbs moving with a slow, mechanical jerk. He fell onto the sharp rocks, his knees scraping against the granite, but rose again without a sound. Behind him, more figures emerged from the churning surf, their bodies grey and waterlogged, eyes milky and fixed.

"A shipwreck," Arthur murmured, his chest tightening. "They must have hit the Geographe Reef out in the bay. None of them survived the water."

"Then why are they walking?" Maya’s voice cracked.

Under the rules of the dead, it didn't matter if they drowned or bled out. Once the lungs filled with water and the heart stopped, they rose. The ocean didn't wash away the infection; it only preserved the bodies, keeping them cold and intact.

"They're coming up the path," Arthur said, pointing to the gravel track that wound from the old boat ramp up the cliff face.

Through the rain, the first of the drowned dead began their slow, shambling ascent toward the yellow light of the keep.

Chapter 3: The Ascent

The wind shrieked through the ventilator grilles of the lantern room, a high-pitched wail that competed with the deep rumble of the surf. Down on the gravel path, the waterlogged dead moved in a ragged, unstoppable line. There were dozens of them now, their clothes dripping sea water, skin bleached white and wrinkled from the ocean.

"We have to bolt the bottom door," Arthur said, reaching for the heavy iron key hanging by the desk. "The main door is solid jarrah wood, but the lock is old."

"I'll come with you," Maya said, her eyes darting to the trapdoor.

"No. You stay here. Monitor the radio. If the Choules calls, you need to tell them the harbor channel is blocked by the dead." Arthur slung his old twelve-gauge shotgun over his shoulder. It was a relic, but it worked.

He climbed down the narrow iron ladder, his boots ringing out in the hollow concrete cylinder of the tower. The air grew colder, damp with the smell of wet stone and salt. By the time he reached the ground floor landing, the sound of the storm was muffled, replaced by a rhythmic, heavy thudding against the outside of the door.

Thump. Thump.

They were already there.

Arthur stepped to the door, his heart hammering against his ribs. He looked through the narrow iron peephole. A bloated face was pressed against the glass on the other side, its lips peeled back, eyes glazed with a blue film. Behind it, more grey hands reached for the heavy brass handle.

He jammed the key into the lock and turned it, the iron deadbolt sliding home with a heavy click. 

At that exact moment, the hum of the electrical lights flickered and died. The tower was plunged into near-total darkness, save for the weak, grey light filtering through the high slits.

Upstairs, the great lens ground to a halt.

Chapter 4: The Generator Room

"Arthur!" Maya’s voice echoed down the spiral stairwell, thin and panicked. "The light’s gone! The rotation motor stopped!"

"I know!" Arthur shouted back, his hand touching the cold stone wall to guide himself. "The generator's tripped. I'm going into the engine house."

The engine house was a low, limestone building connected to the tower by a short, covered passageway. Arthur walked through the connecting archway, his flashlight beam cutting through the damp air. The smell of diesel and hot oil was strong. The twin Lister generator engines sat like sleeping metal beasts in the center of the concrete floor.

Arthur shone his light on the control panel. The red warning light for fuel starvation was glowing.

"Clear the filters," he muttered to himself, proping the flashlight on a pipe. 

He grabbed a wrench and loosened the fuel line valve. The diesel that trickled out was thick, cloudy, and mixed with water. The heavy rain must have breached the seals on the external day-tank during the storm.

Outside, the wind slammed against the metal roof of the engine house, a deafening rattle. But underneath the sound of the wind, there was another noise—a scraping sound on the corrugated iron sheets of the outer wall.

Arthur froze, the wrench in his hand.

The generator house had two small glass windows high up on the wall. Through the rain-washed panes, he saw the dark, wet shapes of several drowned walkers. They were moving along the side of the building, drawn by the heat of the cooling engine blocks.

One of them turned, its head tilted, and slammed its forehead against the glass. The pane cracked.

Chapter 5: The Outer Gallery

High above, Maya climbed out of the lantern room hatch onto the circular outer gallery. The storm hit her like a physical blow, nearly ripping the yellow raincoat from her shoulders. Rain stung her face like needles, and the wind threatened to throw her over the waist-high iron railing.

She gripped the cold metal, squinting into the black void of the ocean. 

A flash of lightning illuminated the sea, and for a fraction of a second, she saw it—a dark, low silhouette riding the massive swells two miles out. It was the HMAS Choules. A sweeping beam of a searchlight cut through the driving rain, searching for the Cape Leeuwin beacon.

But without the light, the ship would have no way of navigating the inner reef channel. It would either turn back or rip its hull open on the Geographe reefs.

"Come on, Arthur," Maya whispered, her teeth chattering from the freezing wind.

She looked down toward the base of the tower. The ground below was a shifting mass of dark shapes. The drowned crew and passengers of the shipwreck had surrounded the base of the lighthouse. 

Suddenly, she heard a metal clatter directly below her.

She looked over the edge. A rusted iron service ladder ran up the exterior of the tower, used for maintenance. Clinging to the rungs, thirty feet up and slowly climbing, was a zombie in a shredded navy blue sweater. Its fingers were raw, the nails torn off, but it gripped the iron rungs with mechanical, tireless strength.

And behind it, another was starting the climb.

Chapter 6: The Basal Door

In the engine house, the glass window shattered. 

A waterlogged arm, grey and bloated, reached through the broken frame, claws scratching at the stone wall. Arthur ignored it, his hands flying as he bled the fuel lines. He jammed the manual crank handle into the flywheel of the backup generator.

"Start, you bastard," he growled, throwing his weight against the heavy iron wheel.

He spun the wheel once, twice. The compression release valve hissed, but the engine died with a wet gurgle. The arm at the window was followed by a second. A zombie was dragging its shoulders through the narrow frame, the sharp glass tearing its cold flesh without drawing blood.

Arthur gritted his teeth, grabbed the crank again, and threw all his fading strength into the spin.

The diesel engine sputtered, coughed a cloud of black smoke, and then roared to life. The high-pitched whine of the alternator filled the room, and the work lights flickered back on. 

Arthur pulled the crank out just as the zombie tumbled through the window, landing heavily on the concrete floor. It scrambled up, its milky eyes locking onto him.

Arthur didn't waste a shell. He turned and ran through the covered passageway back into the tower, slamming the heavy iron connecting door behind him. He threw the latch just as a wet shoulder hit the other side.

He leaned against the metal, breathing heavily, but the relief was short-lived. 

From the base of the tower stairs, he heard the splintering sound of wood. The main jarrah door was buckling under the weight of the horde outside.

Chapter 7: The Lantern Room

The power surge from the generator flashed through the tower. Upstairs in the lantern room, the rotating brass gears began to turn with a low hum. But as the electrical current hit the massive thousand-watt bulb in the center of the lens, there was a sharp, loud pop. 

A white flash illuminated the room, followed by the smell of ozone. The filament had snapped.

"No!" Maya cried out, stepping back into the lantern room from the windy gallery. 

The backup bulb was stored in a wooden case in the cupboard beneath the watch desk. She ran to it, her fingers trembling as she unscrewed the wooden latches. Inside, nestled in thick foam, was the spare glass globe, the size of a melon.

As she lifted the bulb, a heavy blow struck the glass panel of the lantern room.

She spun around. The zombie in the Navy sweater had reached the top of the external ladder. It was standing on the narrow gallery platform, its grey face pressed against the thick curved window pane. It raised a waterlogged fist and slammed it against the glass.

The double-glazed safety glass held, but a web of fine cracks spread across the outer layer.

"Arthur!" Maya screamed, clutching the delicate spare bulb to her chest.

From the spiral staircase below, she heard the echo of a shotgun blast, the sound booming like thunder in the concrete cylinder.

Chapter 8: The Spiral Climb

Arthur stood on the narrow spiral steps, thirty feet above the ground floor, his shotgun pointed down into the dark well. The jarrah wood door at the base had completely shattered, and the dead were pouring through the gap like a slow, grey tide.

He fired. The blast shredded the head of the lead walker, sending the body tumbling down the steps to trip the others. But they didn't feel fear or hesitation. They simply crawled over the fallen, their wet boots squeaking on the iron stairs.

"Get to the top, Maya!" Arthur yelled, his voice cracking. "Lock the trapdoor!"

He pumped the shotgun, ejecting a smoking red shell, and fired again. Another walker fell, but his flashlight beam revealed dozens more crowding the lower stairwell. The damp air was freezing, and the smell of rot and sea water was overwhelming.

Arthur took three steps back, his boots slipping on the wet metal. He fired his last shell into the mass.

As he turned to run up the spiral, a cold, wet hand shot through the open metal grating of the steps from below, grabbing his ankle. 

Arthur screamed as he was pulled down, his chin slamming against the hard iron edge of the stair. The shotgun slipped from his grasp, clattering down into the dark. He kicked wildly with his free leg, his boot heel smashing the face of the zombie reaching through the grates, but another hand joined the first, cold fingers digging into his flesh.

Chapter 9: The Final Spare

Maya ran to the iron trapdoor, looking down the spiral stairs. 

Arthur was dragging himself up, his face covered in blood, his left leg limp. Behind him, the grey hands of the dead were reaching through the rails, their fingers clutching at his trousers.

"Arthur!" Maya reached down, grabbing his coat collar to pull him through the hatch.

With a final, agonizing effort, Arthur scrambled onto the floor of the watch room. Maya slammed the heavy iron hatch shut, sliding the locking bars into place. Almost immediately, the sound of fists hammering against the iron began from below.

Arthur lay on the floor, gasping for air. His trousers were torn at the ankle, revealing a deep, ragged bite mark. The blood that oozed from the wound was dark, and the skin around it was already turning a bruised, ash-grey color.

"No," Maya whispered, tears burning her eyes.

"It's... it's alright," Arthur panted, clutching his chest. "The rules of the dead... we know how this ends. You have to change the bulb."

He pointed to the glass lens assembly above them. 

"I can feel the heat rising," Arthur said, his eyes rolling back slightly as the fever began its rapid climb. "Don't let them turn the light off. Keep it burning."

Maya wiped her face, picked up the spare bulb, and climbed the small ladder into the lens compartment.

Chapter 10: The Last Beam

Maya’s hands shook as she unscrewed the housing of the main lamp. The heat of the dead bulb burned her skin, but she ignored it, lifting the cracked glass globe out and replacing it with the fresh, clear sphere. She tightened the collar screws, her breath coming in ragged gasps.

"Rotate," she whispered, flipping the master switch.

The current surged. The bulb erupted into a brilliant, blinding white light. The massive prisms of the Fresnel lens caught the beam, magnifying it into a concentrated spear of light that cut through the black storm. 

The beam swept across the ocean.

Maya crawled back to the outer gallery, shielding her eyes. The sweeping light illuminated the raging waters of the bay. 

The HMAS Choules had attempted to enter the channel, but without the light, they had drifted too far east. The sweeping beam revealed the massive navy vessel listing heavily, its hull impaled on the jagged black rocks of the outer reef. Huge waves crashed over the deck, washing vehicles and containers into the sea. Through her binoculars, she saw the lifeboats swarmed by pale, bloated shapes waiting in the water.

There was no rescue. The ship was dying.

Behind her, the hammering on the iron trapdoor suddenly stopped.

Maya turned slowly. The watch room below was silent, save for the hum of the rotating gears. 

Arthur lay still on the floor, his breathing finished. But as she watched, his hand twitched. His head rolled back, and his eyes opened—devoid of life, clouded with a cold, blue film. He began to crawl toward the ladder.

================================================================================

STORY 25: THE SALT LAKE
-----------------------

Chapter 1: The White Horizon

The glare off Kati Thanda–Lake Eyre was blinding, a flat, endless expanse of crystalline white salt that stretched in every direction to meet a pale, heat-washed sky. In the mid-afternoon sun, the temperature on the salt bed hovered near forty-five degrees. The air was bone-dry, tasting faintly of salt and dust.

"Keep her at twenty kilometers an hour, Marcus," Brody said, squinting through polarized sunglasses at the laptop screen mounted on the dashboard. "Any faster and we're drawing too much current from the battery bank. The cell temperatures are already in the red."

Marcus, his hands light on the modified steering yoke, nodded. "The surface is soft here. We're breaking through the crust into the wet mud below. That's adding drag."

They were inside The Sandlark, a custom-built, three-wheeled solar buggy they had salvaged from the university workshop in Adelaide. Its body was a lightweight carbon-fiber shell, covered in high-efficiency silicon solar cells. In a world without fuel, solar was their only hope of crossing the vast, empty interior to reach the rumored survival community in Alice Springs.

Brody checked the rear-facing camera feed. The buggy’s single rear tyre left a narrow, grey track in the shimmering white salt. 

"We've been on the salt for six hours," Marcus muttered, wiping sweat from his forehead with a dirty sleeve. "No signs of life. No road blocks. No nothing."

"That's because there's nothing out here to survive on," Brody replied. "No water, no shade. Just salt."

He glanced back at the camera monitor again, about to shut it down to save power. 

But a pixelated shape caught his eye. Far behind them, emerging from the shimmering heat mirage where the white salt met the blue sky, several small, grey dots were moving. They were walking in their track.

Chapter 2: The Mirage

Marcus slowed the buggy, the electric hub motor whining as the speed dropped. He adjusted his sunglasses, staring at the rear-view mirror.

"Are those... kangaroos?" Marcus asked, his voice dry.

"Not out here," Brody said, adjusting the contrast on the camera monitor. "And they're walking on two legs. Too slow for a man, but they keep coming."

Through the heat haze, the figures looked warped, stretched vertically by the thermal columns rising from the salt. They appeared like thin, grey giants dancing in the mirage. But as the buggy slowed, the distance between them began to shrink.

"The solar arrays are dropping output," Brody noted, tapping the battery gauge. "Look at the panels. The salt crust we're kicked up has settled as a fine, white film over the cells. We're only pulling forty percent power."

"Should I stop to wipe them?"

"No," Brody said, his eyes fixed on the monitor. "If we stop, we're sitting ducks. Those things... they don't look right."

The figures were closer now, perhaps half a kilometer back. They were shambling, their limbs moving with the unmistakable, jerky gait of the dead. But these were different from the wet, bloated bodies of the coast. These were mummified by the dry desert air and coated in a crust of white salt. They looked like walking statues carved from salt and dust, their tattered clothes stiffened by brine.

"How are they even moving?" Marcus whispered. "There's no water in those bodies. They're like beef jerky."

"The rules of the dead don't care about dehydration," Brody said grimly. "They just keep walking."

Suddenly, the buggy's front wheel dipped violently, the steering yoke snapping out of Marcus's hands.

Chapter 3: The Desert Heat

The buggy ground to a halt, the electric motor hum turning into a high-pitched, strained squeal before cutting out completely. A warning alarm began to blare from the dashboard.

MOTOR OVERTEMP. FAULT.

"The front wheel is bogged," Marcus said, trying to reverse, but the tyre only spun, kicking up a spray of damp, black mud from beneath the white salt crust. "It's like clay under here. The crust was too thin."

"Turn it off! You'll burn out the windings," Brody snapped, unlatching his harness. "We have to push. And we need to clean those solar panels, or the battery will be dead before the motor cools down."

They stepped out of the carbon-fiber shell. The heat hit them like an oven door opening, a dry, baking force that made their skin tighten instantly. The white glare was painful, even through their sunglasses.

Brody grabbed a microfibre cloth and began wiping the salt dust off the solar array, the glass hot enough to burn his fingertips. 

"Marcus, get the recovery boards from the side," Brody called out.

Marcus didn't answer. He was standing by the bogged front tyre, staring back across the salt flats. 

The heat mirage was playing tricks on their eyes, but the shapes were unmistakable now. There were five of them, their bodies encrusted in a glittering coat of salt crystals that caught the harsh sunlight. Their skin was dark brown, dried and stretched tight over their skulls. 

They were less than three hundred meters away, shambling forward in a silent, straight line.

"Brody," Marcus said, his voice trembling. "They're gaining on us. The mud's slowing them down, but not enough."

Chapter 4: The Dried Dead

"Forget the panels for a second," Brody yelled, sliding the plastic recovery boards under the bogged front tyre. "Get behind the wheel! When I say go, apply gentle throttle. Don't spin it!"

Marcus scrambled back into the driver's seat. 

Brody got behind the buggy, his boots sinking into the black, salty mud. He pressed his shoulders against the carbon-fiber frame, the heat radiating off the panels baking his face. 

"Go!" he roared.

The electric motor whined. The front tyre caught the plastic ridges of the board, throwing up a shower of salt crystals and grey mud. Brody pushed with all his strength, his legs straining. The buggy shifted, rolling forward a few inches, then slipped off the side of the board and sank even deeper into the black ooze.

"Stop! Stop!" Brody gasped, leaning over the buggy, his chest heaving. The salt air burned his throat.

He looked up. The nearest zombie was barely eighty meters away now. 

It had been a tourist once, wearing a faded blue polo shirt and khaki shorts, now stiff with salt. Its skin was dry as parchment, mummified by the sun. Its eyes were gone, the empty sockets filled with white salt crystals that sparkled in the light. As it walked, the dried joints of its knees and ankles made a dry, crackling sound, like breaking twigs.

It opened its mouth, but no sound came out—just a puff of dry dust.

"We need a jack," Marcus said, his face pale. "The frame is resting on the salt. We're high-centered."

"We don't have a jack, Marcus," Brody said, reaching into the buggy for the heavy metal tyre iron. "We have to clear the mud by hand."

Chapter 5: The Broken Axle

Brody dropped to his knees in the salt, clawing at the thick, sticky mud beneath the front wheel. The salt crust scraped his knuckles, the brine stinging the cuts instantly. 

"The motor's cooled down," Marcus called out, checking the dash. "Temp's back in the green. I'm going to try one more hard push on the battery."

"Wait!" Brody yelled, scrambling out from under the nose. "Don't—"

But Marcus had already pressed the manual override switch. 

The hub motor engaged with a violent, electric roar, drawing maximum current from the batteries. The tyre bit into the edge of the recovery board. The buggy lurked forward, lifting out of the hole, and then—

CLANG.

A sharp, metallic snap echoed across the silent salt flats. The front end of the buggy collapsed, the nose slamming hard into the white salt crust. The single front wheel folded completely under the frame, the steel suspension arm snapped clean in two.

Marcus cut the power, the cabin going silent. He stared through the windshield at the crooked bonnet.

"The axle," Marcus whispered. "It's snapped."

"I told you to wait!" Brody screamed, throwing the tyre iron onto the salt. "We're stuck. We're completely done."

He looked back. The salt-crusted tourist zombie was fifty meters away. Behind it, three more mummified figures were closing the distance, their dry joints crackling in the hot wind.

Brody picked up the tyre iron, his hands shaking. "Get out of the cab, Marcus. We have to fight."

Chapter 6: The Welding Arc

"We can weld it," Brody said, his eyes darting from the broken steel arm to the main battery compartment. "The lithium bank runs at forty-eight volts. If we strip the main power cable and use the copper wire, we can arc-weld the suspension bracket back together."

"Are you crazy?" Marcus scrambled out of the cab. "We don't have welding rods!"

"We have the steel tent pegs from the survival kit. They're zinc-coated steel. It'll be a messy weld, but it'll hold long enough to get us off the lake." Brody reached for his toolkit, his movements frantic. "I need ten minutes. Keep them away from me."

The lead zombie, the salt-crusted tourist, reached the front of the buggy. Its dry, stiff hand clutched at the solar panels, its fingernails scratching the glass with a terrible, dry squeal.

Marcus stepped forward, gripping the tyre iron with both hands. He swung, hitting the side of the zombie's head. 

The impact sounded like hitting dry timber. The zombie's head snapped sideways, a shower of dried skin and salt crystals breaking off, but it didn't fall. It simply turned its empty, salt-filled sockets back toward Marcus, its jaw hanging loose as it reached for his throat.
