Marcus swung again, harder this time, bringing the tyre iron down onto the top of the skull. The bone cracked, and the tourist collapsed into the salty mud.

"One down!" Marcus yelled, his chest heaving as the heat began to dizzy him.

Behind the tourist, three more salt-mummies shambled forward. One was wearing a tattered high-vis work shirt, its arms covered in deep, white-crusted gashes. 

Beneath the buggy's nose, Brody stripped the heavy copper cables, his face covered in sweat as he prepared to spark the arc.

Chapter 7: The Dust Devil

Brody touched the stripped copper wire to the steel tent peg, holding the makeshift electrode against the broken suspension arm. 

SPARK.

A brilliant, blue-white flash erupted under the buggy’s nose, illuminating the white salt bed. The intense heat of the electrical arc began to melt the steel, joining the fractured suspension bracket. Brody shielded his eyes with his hand, squinting through the sparks.

The electrical crackle and the flashing light acted like a beacon in the flat desert. In the distance, more figures began to emerge from the shimmering mirage, their salt-crusted bodies drawn to the noise.

"Hurry, Brody!" Marcus shouted, backing away as two more zombies approached.

Before Brody could answer, the hot wind shifted violently. A high-pitched whistling sound grew louder. 

A dust devil—a localized whirlwind of hot air—swept across the salt flats. It hit them directly, a spinning column of wind that whipped up loose salt crystals and dried mud, turning the air into a blinding, stinging vortex.

"My eyes!" Marcus screamed, dropping the tyre iron. The sharp salt crystals pelted his face, scratching his corneas and leaving him temporarily blinded. He stumbled backward, rubbing his eyes frantically as the wind roared.

In his blindness, he stepped right into the path of the high-vis zombie. 

A cold, dried hand grabbed Marcus's shoulder, the fingernails digging through his shirt. Marcus gasped, trying to pull away, but the zombie's grip was like an iron vise.

Chapter 8: The Quiet Camp

Brody broke the electrical contact, dropping the hot wire. He rolled out from under the chassis, grabbing the heavy zinc-coated tent peg. With a roar, he drove the sharp metal spike directly into the temple of the high-vis zombie. 

The skull cracked with a dry pop, and the zombie slumped onto the chassis. Brody yanked the spike out, grabbed the blinded, sobbing Marcus, and dragged him toward the buggy's cabin.

"Get in! Get in!" Brody yelled, pushing Marcus through the side gull-wing door.

He scrambled in after him, slamming the carbon-fiber hatch shut and locking the latches. The dust devil passed, leaving the salt flats quiet again, but the heat inside the unventilated cabin was immediately stifling. It was like sitting in a plastic box under the sun.

"I can't see, Brody," Marcus groaned, clutching his face. His eyes were red, swollen, and weeping tears that dried into white salt crusts on his cheeks.

"Keep them closed. Don't rub them," Brody said, his own breathing ragged. He reached for the five-litre water bladder stored in the footwell. 

When he lifted it, the bladder felt light. Too light.

He looked down. A sharp corner of the metal battery frame had punctured the plastic skin. The bladder was bone-dry. The water had slowly drained out into the absorbent black mud beneath the floorboards hours ago.

Brody unscrewed the cap, tipping it upside down. A single, tepid drop of water fell onto his cracked tongue.

Outside, the first dry hand slapped against the side glass.

Chapter 9: The Salt Sleep

The sun went down, a blazing red disc that bled across the white horizon before leaving the desert in a cold, starlit silence. But the temperature inside the carbon-fiber cabin did not drop. The heat was trapped, thick and suffocating.

Marcus lay back against the passenger seat, his skin dry and hot to the touch. His breathing was shallow, a dry rattling in his throat.

"Brody," Marcus whispered, his lips split and bleeding. "The water... is there any left in the lines?"

"No," Brody said, his own tongue swollen, making it hard to speak. He had tried to suck moisture from the windshield condensation, but it was bitter and salty. "Just rest, mate. The sun will be down. We'll fix the wheel at first light."

Brody closed his eyes, his mind drifting into a feverish, half-conscious state. The silence of the desert was absolute, broken only by the occasional, soft scrape of dry fingernails against the outer shell of the buggy. The dead were still out there, waiting.

Hours passed. 

Brody woke to the sound of a sudden, sharp gasp. He turned his head in the dark cabin.

Marcus had gone quiet. Brody reached out, his hand touching Marcus's neck. There was no pulse. The skin was cold now, the chest still. Dehydration and heatstroke had taken him in the dark.

"Marcus?" Brody whispered, a knot of dread tightening in his stomach.

Before he could reach for his flashlight, Marcus’s chest chest expanded with a sudden, violent inhalation. The body sat up, the head snapping toward Brody. In the dim starlight filtering through the hatch, Marcus's eyes opened—dull, clouded, and empty of life.

Chapter 10: The Horizon's End

Marcus lunged across the narrow console, his teeth clicking together as he snapped at Brody’s face. 

The tight cabin was a trap. There was no room to swing a weapon, no space to run. Brody jammed his forearm against Marcus's throat, holding the reanimated driver back as Marcus's cold, stiff hands clawed at his shirt.

"Marcus, stop!" Brody croaked, his dry throat screaming in pain. 

But there was no Marcus left. The entity in front of him was just a biological machine, driven by the singular urge to feed. The grey skin of Marcus's neck crackled like dry paper under Brody's arm.

Outside, the noise began.

Drawn by the struggle inside, the salt-mummies began to press against the buggy. The light carbon-fiber shell creaked under their collective weight. One by one, dry, salt-crusted faces filled the windows, their empty sockets staring in.

A crack appeared in the gull-wing hatch glass.

Brody’s strength was fading. Dehydration had left his muscles weak, his vision tunneling. He looked past the snarling face of his friend, out through the windshield at the vast, white salt lake. The morning sun was rising, a bright, cruel light that illuminated the endless flats.

Far on the horizon, more salt-crusted figures were emerging from the mirages, walking toward the buggy.

The glass shattered. Cold, dried hands reached through the broken window, grabbing Brody's hair from behind. 

He was pinned in the seat, caught between the dead teammate in front of him and the horde clawing through the broken glass, as the red sun rose over the white tomb of Kati Thanda.

================================================================================

STORY 26: THE SUBSTATION
------------------------

Chapter 1: The Grid's Edge

The control room of Loy Yang Power Station was a cavernous space filled with the low, steady hum of turbine controls, electrical hums, and the gentle clicking of printers. Wall-sized digital mimics showed the Victorian electrical transmission grid, a vast web of green lines connecting Melbourne to the Latrobe Valley. 

Now, half of those green lines were flickering orange or dead grey.

"The Melbourne-Ringwood transmission line just tripped," Sarah said, her fingers flying over the control keyboard. She was thirty-two, the shift supervisor, her eyes bloodshot from a double shift. "That's another five hundred megawatts dropped. We're losing the frequency, Keith. It's down to forty-nine point two hertz."

Keith, a senior electrician in grease-stained blue overalls, leaned over her shoulder. His face was lined with deep fatigue. "The frequency goes below forty-eight, and the automated load shedding will isolate the entire eastern state. We'll black out the whole valley."

"It's already blacked out, Keith," Sarah said, pointing to the secondary monitors. The live security feeds from the outer switchyard were grey static. "The transmission lines aren't failing because of electrical faults. It's the suburbs. The substations are being overrun. No one is left to reset the breakers."

Outside, the Latrobe Valley was silent, a cold Victorian drizzle falling over the open-cut coal mine. But within the concrete walls of the station, the hum of the remaining five-hundred-megawatt generator was a deep vibration that shook the soles of their boots.

Suddenly, a red alarm banner flashed across the main console.

UNIT 1 BOILER FEED PUMP FAULT. FREQUENCY DEVIATION TRIP.

A deafening, metal-rending whine began to echo through the walls as the massive steam turbine began its emergency shutdown sequence.

Chapter 2: The Blackout

The vibrations in the concrete floor escalated, a violent shuddering as the massive turbine blades spun down. Then, with a sound like a distant artillery shell exploding, the generator's main circuit breaker tripped.

The bank of monitors on the control wall died instantly. The fluorescent ceiling lights flickered and went dark, plunging the cavernous room into blackness. 

A second later, the low-voltage emergency lighting system engaged. Weak, amber LEDs flickered along the baseboards, casting long, distorted shadows across the row of dead control desks.

"Sarah?" Keith’s voice was muffled in the sudden, heavy silence. The roaring hum that had filled the building for decades was gone, replaced by the hiss of high-pressure steam venting from the roof stacks outside.

"I'm here," Sarah said, her flashlight beam cutting through the darkness. The beam trembled. "The station auxiliary power is gone. The batteries will keep the control systems online for another hour, but the main lub-oil pumps for the turbine shaft will stop. If the shaft cools without turning, it will warp under its own weight. We'll destroy the turbine."

"We need the diesel generators," Keith said, checking his own torch. "They should have started automatically."

"They didn't," Sarah replied, checking the system status screen. "The links to the generator yard are showing offline. No signal. Someone must have isolated them manually at the field switches."

"Or something broke the cables," Keith muttered.

From the hallway outside the heavy security door, a faint, rhythmic sound began. It was the scraping of boots on vinyl tile, accompanied by the wet, heavy breathing of a crowd.

Chapter 3: The Breach

The security door of the control room was reinforced steel, but the electronic lock began to beep as the battery backup power fluctuated. Outside, the scraping grew louder, followed by a sudden, heavy slam against the metal.

BANG.

"They're in the admin wing," Sarah whispered, shining her light on the thick glass observation window. 

Through the double-paned glass, the weak amber light of the corridor revealed a cluster of figures. They were wearing office lanyard badges, safety vests, and hardhats—the station staff who had been bitten during the initial outbreak in the canteen. Their skin was pale grey, eyes clouded and milky. 

One of them, the head of planning, slammed his forehead against the observation window. A smear of dark blood left a wet streak on the glass.

"They're trying to reach the warmth of the control room," Keith said, adjusting his grip on a heavy copper busbar he’d taken from the tool chest. "Once the battery dies, this door will fail-safe open. We can't stay here."

"The black-start diesel generator is in the coal yards, five hundred meters north," Sarah said, pulling up a map of the station layout on her tablet. "If we can reach the yard, bypass the main floor, and manual start the generator, we can black-start the station's auxiliaries. We can keep the shaft turning and restore the control room."

"How do we get out?"

Sarah pointed to the rear of the room, where a steel floor hatch led down to the cable spreader room. "The cable galleries. They run directly under the turbine hall to the coal conveyor tunnels."

Chapter 4: The Coal Yard

Brody—no, Keith lowered himself through the hatch, his boots landing on a steel mesh catwalk in the cable spreader room. Sarah followed, pulling the heavy steel hatch shut above them. 

The air down here was hot, thick with the smell of scorched rubber insulation and grease. Hundreds of thick, black electrical cables ran along steel trays overhead, resembling giant, sleeping vines in the flashlight beam.

"Keep your head down," Keith whispered, his light reflecting off the low ceiling beams. "The ceiling is barely five feet high here."

They crawled along the catwalk, the silence broken only by the drip of condensation and the distant, muffled thumping from the control room above. The dead were trying to break the glass.

They reached the end of the tray gallery, where the cable trays dipped down into the conveyor tunnels that brought brown coal from the open-cut pit. 

Keith stopped, his flashlight beam illuminating a figure standing in the narrow junction. 

It was wearing a high-vis jacket, its back to them, its head tilted at an unnatural angle. Its shoulders twitched as the flashlight beam hit it.

"Quiet," Sarah whispered, her hand gripping Keith’s shoulder.

The figure turned slowly, its work boots scraping on the metal grate. It was a conveyor technician. Its throat had been torn out, a dried, black cavity in the center of its neck. It saw them, its jaw dropping open, and began to shuffle forward, its boots clanging loudly on the steel floor.

Behind it, from the dark depths of the conveyor tunnel, more clanging sounds began to echo.

Chapter 5: The Conveyor Belt

Keith swung the copper busbar, the heavy metal bar striking the side of the technician's head with a dull clack. The zombie stumbled, its hardhat falling off, but it lunged again, its grey hands clawing at Keith's face.

Sarah stepped forward, her heavy industrial flashlight striking the zombie's temple. The skull fractured, and the body fell forward, its head pinning Keith's boots to the grate.

"Come on!" Sarah gasped, pulling Keith up. 

They scrambled onto the massive rubber coal conveyor belt that rose out of the tunnel at a thirty-degree angle. The belt was covered in a thick layer of brown coal dust, making their boots slip. In the dark, the structure felt like a hollow steel tube suspended over the dark yard.

They climbed the belt, their flashlights illuminating the open yard below through the gaps in the corrugated iron walls.

Outside, the cold rain was turning the coal dust on the ground into a black slime. In the yard below, dozens of walkers were shuffling between the heavy mining machinery and coal hoppers. They had been drawn by the steam vents and the sudden silence of the station.

At the top of the conveyor structure, the belt led into the distribution bin house. 

As Keith reached the platform, a figure fell from the upper gantry, landing heavily on the rubber belt directly in front of Sarah. It was a turned security guard, his gun belt still strapped to his waist. He scrambled up on all fours, snarling.

Chapter 6: The High Voltage Yard

Sarah dodged the security guard, her boot slipping on the coal dust. Keith caught her arm, pulling her past the snarling walker and down the metal emergency stairs that ran down the exterior of the bin house. 

The cold rain hit them, washing the black coal dust into streaks on their faces. 

They ran across the gravel-lined high-voltage switchyard, the massive transmission towers rising into the grey sky like skeletal giants. The air hummed with static electricity from the active lines, but the main transformers below were silent and cold.

"The generator is in the switch house!" Keith yelled, pointing to a long, concrete building on the northern perimeter.

They made a run for the door, but the gravel made their movements loud. 

From behind the massive, three-story porcelain bushings of the main transformer banks, shapes began to move. They were turned switchyard technicians, their overalls covered in grease and dirt. They emerged from the shadow of the cooling radiators, their movement slow but relentless.

"We're cut off!" Sarah called out, her flashlight beam sweeping the fence.

The main switchyard gate was locked, and the dead were converging on the gravel pathway from both sides. Keith backed up, his hand touching the cold steel fence of the transformer enclosure. 

A zombie in a high-vis jacket shambled out of the rain, blocking their path to the switch house. Its hand was missing, the wrist bone exposed and grey.

Chapter 7: The Black Start

Sarah squeezed past the transformer support beams, her small frame allowing her to reach the concrete platform of the switch house. She threw her weight against the metal door, finding it unlocked. 

She slipped inside the generator room.

The smell of diesel was strong. The massive twelve-cylinder black-start engine sat in the middle of the concrete bay. Sarah ran to the local control board, her hands flying over the manual start switches.

"Keith! I'm at the panel!" she screamed through the open doorway.

Outside, Keith stood at the foot of the concrete ramp, his heavy copper bar raised. "Just start it! They're on the ramp!"

Sarah flipped the fuel pump toggle. The electric pumps whined, priming the lines. She pressed the start button.

The engine starter motor engaged with a heavy, metallic clink. Chug... chug... chug... The massive pistons moved, but the cold diesel did not catch. 

Through the open door, Keith saw three zombies reach the top of the ramp. He swung the busbar, striking the lead zombie's collarbone. The bone shattered, but the walker continued forward, its teeth snapping inches from his neck.

"Come on, Sarah!" Keith roared, his boots slipping on the wet concrete.

Sarah pressed the emergency ether override, injecting starting fluid directly into the air intake, and hit the start button again.

Chapter 8: The Steam Release

The twelve-cylinder diesel engine roared to life with a deafening, smoke-belching rumble that shook the concrete building. The generator hummed, the voltage indicator on Sarah's panel rising to four hundred and fifteen volts.

But the noise was a dinner bell.

From the dark corners of the switchyard and the open-cut mine beyond the fence, dozens of walkers began to turn toward the switch house, their shambling movements accelerating.

"We have to clear the yard, Sarah!" Keith shouted, his voice barely audible over the engine's roar. He ran to the auxiliary steam header pipe that ran along the outside wall of the turbine hall. "If they block the door, we'll never get back to the control room!"

Keith grabbed the iron wheel of the manual blowdown valve. It was rusted, hot to the touch. He threw his weight against it, turning the wheel three times.

With a high-pitched, screaming roar, superheated steam erupted from the vent stack above the yard. A massive, white cloud of boiling moisture rolled across the gravel, blinding the approaching dead and filling the switchyard with a thick, choking fog.

The steam scalded the flesh of the zombies, but they did not scream. They only kept walking, their shapes moving like ghosts through the white mist.

But the roaring sound of the steam vent was deafening. It echoed across the Latrobe Valley, drawing every reanimated soul for kilometers.

Keith turned to run back to the switch house, but a figure emerged from the steam cloud directly in front of him.

Chapter 9: The Cable Duct

The figure in the steam was a turned maintenance welder, his heavy leather apron scorched. Before Keith could raise the copper bar, the welder lunged through the mist, his teeth sinking deep into Keith's shoulder.

Keith screamed, a sound swallowed by the roar of the steam vent. He slammed the copper bar into the welder's temple, the skull fracturing with a wet crunch. The zombie fell, but the damage was done. 

Keith stumbled back, his hand clutching his bleeding shoulder. The skin was torn, the blood warm against the cold rain. 

He didn't run for the main door. The switchyard was completely swarmed now. Instead, he dropped into the concrete cable trench that ran along the ground, pulling the steel mesh cover over himself. The trench was barely two feet wide, filled with water and mud, but it led directly under the switch house wall.

He crawled through the dark, tight conduit, dragging his injured shoulder, his breath coming in ragged gasps as the fever began its rapid climb.

Sarah was waiting at the internal trench hatch. She pulled the grate open, helping the shivering, mud-covered Keith up into the generator bay.

"Keith! You made it!" she cried, looking at the mud on his overalls. "The auxiliary power is routed. The control room pumps should be running."

Keith collapsed against the diesel generator's frame, his face ash-grey, sweat beads forming on his forehead. He kept his hand clamped tightly over his shoulder.

"We have to... go," he panted, his eyes rolling. "The path to the tower is open. But I can't... I can't run, Sarah."

Chapter 10: The Power Return

They made it back to the control room through the cable spreader hatch, Sarah dragging Keith's dead weight up the ladder. 

As they rolled onto the floor, the master control board suddenly hummed. The massive digital mimics on the wall flashed back to life, green and orange lines lighting up the dark room with a clinical, neon glow. The auxiliary pumps were running, the turbine shaft saved.

But the monitors also showed the truth.

The live camera feeds from the perimeter fences, coal loaders, and offices were active again. They showed the entire Loy Yang site swarmed by thousands of walkers, their grey bodies crawling over the coal heaps and surrounding the control building. 

The eastern grid map was almost entirely grey. Melbourne was dark. The valley was gone.

"We did it," Sarah whispered, her voice hollow as she stared at the screens. "We saved the turbines. But there's no grid left to power."

A low groan sounded from the floor.

Sarah turned. Keith was lying by the main desk. His hand had fallen away from his shoulder, revealing the dark, fever-blackened bite mark. His breathing had stopped, his skin cold and waxy. 

Then, his fingers twitched.

Keith's head snapped up. His eyes opened, the pupils gone, replaced by a milky, white film. He began to drag himself toward Sarah, his heavy work boots scraping on the vinyl floor.

At the same time, the heavy glass of the observation window cracked under the weight of the crowd outside.
