STORY 27: THE SANCTUARY
-----------------------

Chapter 1: The Aviary

The walk-in aviary at Healesville Sanctuary was normally a place of bright color and birdsong, a massive steel-mesh dome enclosing a small pocket of mountain ash forest and ferns. Now, it was a silent vault. The misting nozzles on the ceiling were dry, and the only sound was the rustle of leaves as a solitary superb lyrebird dug through the damp mulch.

"The radio from Lilydale went silent at noon, Clara," Owen whispered, his hands resting on the wooden rail of the boardwalk. He was fifty-two, the sanctuary’s senior keeper, wearing his green uniform. "The highway’s blocked with cars all the way back to Melbourne. They say the town center is burning."

Dr. Clara, the sanctuary's veterinarian, adjusted her medical bag. She was thirty-six, her face pale. "We have twenty-four hours of antibiotic supply left in the clinic, Owen. We can't treat the injured koalas or the raptors if we don't get the medicine from the pharmacy donga in the visitor center."

"It's too dangerous to leave the dome," Owen said, pointing through the thin wire mesh of the aviary walls. "Look at the main path. They're already at the platypus exhibit."

Through the mesh, Clara saw a figure shuffling along the timber boardwalk. It was a tourist, wearing a faded Melbourne FC cap, his shirt torn and stained with dark, dried blood. His jaw was slack, his head tilted back as his milky eyes scanned the forest floor. 

He was followed by three more, their dragging steps rustling the dry eucalyptus leaves. 

"They're moving along the creek line," Clara whispered, her hand tightening on the wooden rail. "The perimeter fence on the Badger Creek side must have buckled. The water levels are low, they just walked right through the creek bed."

Suddenly, a loud, metallic screech echoed from the eastern gate of the aviary. The heavy double doors of the double-entry airlock began to rattle violently.

Chapter 2: The Perimeter Fence

The steel mesh door of the aviary airlock buckled with a sharp, metallic ping. A bloated, grey hand reached through the gap, the fingers clawing at the empty air. Behind the outer door, a dozen more walkers pressed against the wire, their collective weight warping the frame.

"We have to leave the dome, Clara," Owen said, reaching for his keys. "The emergency exit behind the cassowary enclosure leads to the service road. It's our only way out before they block the airlock."

Clara followed him down the sloping boardwalk, the lyrebird fluttering into the high branches above. They reached the small, green metal door at the back of the aviary. Owen unlocked it, and they stepped out into the cool, damp forest air of the sanctuary.

The smell of wet eucalyptus was strong, but beneath it lay the faint, sickly sweet scent of decay.

They walked along the service road, their steps muffled by the thick bark mulch. To their right, the high chain-link fence of the cassowary enclosure was still intact, the large, flightless bird watching them silently from the ferns, its blue neck bright in the shadows.

But to their left, where the sanctuary bordered Badger Creek, the main perimeter fence was gone.

A massive tree trunk had fallen during a storm weeks ago, crushing a thirty-meter section of the chain-link fence into the mud. The creek bed was dry, and a well-worn path had been beaten through the grass. 

Through the gap, a slow, continuous line of walkers was shuffling into the sanctuary grounds. They were tourists, hikers, and local residents from the surrounding Yarra Valley, their bodies grey, dry, and silent. They moved like sleepwalkers, drawn by the smell of life inside the sanctuary.

Chapter 3: The Reptile House

"The main path is swarmed," Owen whispered, peering around the corner of the feed shed. "We'll have to go through the reptile house. It's concrete, windowless, and has heavy doors at both ends. We can cut straight through the middle of the park."

"The power's been out for days, Owen," Clara said. "It'll be pitch black inside."

"I know the layout by heart," Owen replied, pulling out his flashlight. "Just keep close."

They stepped into the concrete pavilion of the reptile house. The heavy, insulated doors closed behind them, cutting off the grey light of the afternoon and plunging them into near-total darkness. The air was cold and stagnant, smelling of damp soil and peat.

Keith—no, Owen clicked on his flashlight, the narrow beam illuminating the glass enclosures that lined the walls. 

Inside the tanks, the snakes and lizards were still, their cold-blooded bodies slowed by the lack of heating lamps. A massive lace monitor lay coiled on a log, its black eyes reflecting the light but its body motionless.

"Watch the floor," Clara whispered, pointing her light down. "The glass on the carpet python enclosure is shattered."

The thick glass pane had been broken from the outside. A tattered, blood-stained jacket was draped over the bottom frame. 

Scrape.

From the dark corridor ahead, the sound of dragging rubber soles echoed. The flashlight beam swept the passage, revealing a figure standing near the venomous snake tanks. It was wearing a volunteer keeper's shirt, its neck bent, its mouth opening to reveal black, dried blood.

It began to shuffle toward them, its hands scraping against the glass displays.

Chapter 4: The Quiet Path

Owen grabbed Clara's shoulder, pulling her back into a side maintenance alcove as the turned volunteer shuffled past their hiding spot. The zombie's milky eyes stared straight ahead, its fingers scratching the glass of a taipan exhibit as it went.

Once the sound of its dragging feet faded, they slipped out of the exit doors into the grey light of the eucalyptus forest. 

The paths here were quiet, carpeted with thick bark and fallen gum leaves. A light rain began to fall, pattering against the high canopy of mountain ash. The smell of wet earth and damp wood was clean, a stark contrast to the stagnant air of the reptile house.

"The clinic is just past the koala enclosure," Clara whispered, pointing through the trees.

They walked quickly, their movements cautious. To their left, in the nocturnal house yard, several koalas sat high in the forks of the gum trees, watching the paths with wide, dark eyes. 

But down on the ground, the paths were no longer safe.

A family of tourists—a mother, father, and a young girl in a pink rain jacket—shambled along the boardwalk near the dingo enclosure. They were walking in a tight, silent group, their clothes shredded, their faces grey and dry. The girl was holding a tattered stuffed platypus, her fingers frozen around the toy.

"They're everywhere," Owen muttered, his hand gripping the heavy iron crowbar he had retrieved from the tool shed. "We can't go down the main boardwalk. We'll have to use the elevated canopy walk."

Chapter 5: The Enclosure

They descended from the high boardwalk to reach the platypus ponds, a series of outdoor concrete tanks nestled among ferns. The water was murky, covered in floating green duckweed.

As they passed the low stone wall of the main tank, a figure lunged from the shadow of the pump house.

It was wearing a green keeper's shirt, the name tag Gary pinned to the chest. Gary’s throat was torn open, his face bloated and grey. He slammed Owen against the thick glass pane of the platypus tank, the water inside sloshing against the glass.

"Owen!" Clara cried, reaching for her scalpel from her bag.

Owen gripped Gary’s wrists, keeping the snapping teeth inches from his face. Gary’s strength was mechanical, fueled by the dead muscles, his fingers digging into Owen's shoulders. The glass behind Owen's head creaked under the pressure.

"Get him... off me!" Owen gasped, his boots slipping on the wet moss of the pathway.

Clara stepped forward, her hand shaking. She drove the long, thin surgical scalpel directly into Gary’s ear canal, pressing with all her weight. The steel blade sliced through the bone and into the brainstem.

Gary’s body went limp instantly, his jaw dropping open as he fell forward. 

Owen pushed the corpse off himself, stumbling back against the stone wall. He was breathing heavily, his hands shaking as he looked at the dead keeper.

"Gary was here when the blockade fell," Owen muttered, his voice cracking. "He tried to stay and feed the animals."

"We have to move, Owen," Clara said, her eyes fixed on the path behind them. "The noise from the struggle... they've heard us."

Chapter 6: The Veterinary Clinic

The veterinary clinic was a modern, green-painted building set back from the main tourist paths, surrounded by a wire fence. The glass entrance door was locked, but inside, they could see the metal cabinets that held the sanctuary's surgical supplies and drugs.

But the walkway leading to the clinic door was blocked.

Five walkers—three adult tourists in matching sunhats and two teenagers—were standing on the gravel path. They had been drawn to the clinic by the smell of animal food stored in the bins outside. They were shuffling around the entrance, their grey faces turned toward the glass doors.

"We can't fight five of them in the open," Clara whispered, crouching behind a low wattle bush. "Not with a scalpel and a crowbar."

"The service window at the side," Owen said, pointing to a small, sliding glass panel used for dropping off injured wildlife. "It's elevated. If we can reach the shipping crates behind the clinic, we can climb through."

"What about the ones at the door?"

"I'll draw them away," Owen said, tapping his crowbar against a metal rubbish bin nearby. The sharp, clanging sound rang out through the quiet forest.

The five zombies turned their heads in unison, their milky eyes locking onto the source of the noise. They began to shuffle toward the bin, leaving the clinic entrance clear.

"Go, Clara!" Owen whispered, stepping out from the wattle bush.

Chapter 7: The Sedative

Clara scrambled onto the wooden shipping crates, slid the small glass service window open, and squeezed her shoulders through. She fell onto the stainless steel counter of the triage room, her medical bag clattering to the floor.

She got to her feet, her heart hammering. The clinic was silent, smelling of disinfectant and animal feed. 

She ran to the pharmacy cabinet, keying in the entry code she knew by heart. The metal doors swung open, revealing rows of sterile saline, antibiotics, and surgical tools. She packed the bottles of penicillin and pain medication into her bag.

Then her eyes fell on the red cabinet at the back.

Inside were the high-strength veterinary sedatives—carfentanil and etorphine—drugs so potent that a single drop on the skin could kill a human in minutes. They were used only for tranquilizing the large kangaroos and emus.

"Just in case," she muttered, grabbing two pre-loaded syringes of etorphine and slipping them into her coat pocket.

Behind her, the clinic’s rear fire exit door rattled.

A walker in a dirty yellow rain jacket had pushed the bar handle open from the outside. It shuffled into the clean triage room, its grey hands outstretched, a guttural groan rising from its throat. 

Clara backed away, her heel hitting the steel counter. She had no weapon except the syringes in her pocket.

Chapter 8: The Tree Walk

Clara drew the syringe of etorphine and drove the needle deep into the zombie's neck, plunging the black liquid into its flesh. 

"Die, you bastard," she gasped, stepping back.

But the walker didn't collapse. It didn't even slow down. The etorphine—a drug ten thousand times more potent than morphine—was useless. Under the rules of the dead, there was no active blood circulation, no nervous system to depress, no breathing to suppress. The chemical simply sat inert in the cold, dead muscle tissue.

The zombie lunged forward.

Clara scrambled sideways, throwing a metal tray of surgical tools at its head. The tray clattered to the floor, distracting the walker long enough for her to dive through the open service window and tumble back onto the shipping crates outside.

Owen was waiting, his face pale, the group of five zombies pursuing him from the gravel path.

"The canopy walk! Now!" Owen yelled, pointing to the spiral wooden staircase that wound up the trunk of a massive mountain ash.

They sprinted to the stairs and began to climb, their boots pounding the wooden steps. Thirty feet above the forest floor, the elevated canopy walk ran as a series of suspended timber bridges connecting the high tree trunks.

They reached the first platform just as the first zombie reached the base of the stairs, its grey face looking up.

Chapter 9: The Rotten Branch

The suspension bridge swayed in the wind, the wire cables groaning under their feet. Below, the forest floor was a grey sea of shuffling heads, the zombies drawn by the sound of the metal steps.

"Keep moving, Clara," Owen said, his hand on the rope railing. "The next platform has the exit slide to the car park."

They reached the middle of the bridge, when a sudden crack echoed.

A rotted timber plank beneath Owen's feet snapped clean in two. He gasped, his boots slipping through the gap as his body plummeted. He caught the thick wire handrail with both hands, his legs swinging in the empty air thirty feet above the concrete paths.

"Owen!" Clara screamed, dropping her bag and lunging to grab his wrists.

She pulled with all her strength, her knees pressed against the wooden decking. But as Owen struggled to find purchase, a hand reached out from the trunk of the mountain ash.

A turned keeper, who had climbed the structural support tower earlier, was clinging to the trunk platform directly below the bridge. It reached through the wooden slats of the walkway and sank its teeth deep into the leather of Owen's boot, the bite tearing through the ankle collar into his flesh.

Owen cried out, a sharp yell of pain. 

Clara pulled him up with a final, desperate heave, dragging his bleeding body onto the wooden platform. The keeper below snarled, its mouth covered in fresh, dark red blood.

Chapter 10: The Nocturnal House

Clara dragged Owen down the final spiral ramp and through the heavy, padded doors of the nocturnal house. 

The building was designed for night-active animals, completely sealed from the daylight. Inside, the only light was the dim, red LEDs of the empty displays. The air was cold, smelling of straw and eucalyptus.

She locked the heavy iron fire doors behind them, sliding the security bolts home.

Owen fell against a concrete display wall, his breathing a rattling gasp. The fever had taken him quickly, his skin burning, his eyes bloodshot and unfocused. The bite on his ankle was black, the tissue around it turning grey and dead.

"Clara," he whispered, his lips dry. "The... the keys. Lock the nocturnal chambers. Don't... don't let me..."

He didn't finish. His head fell back against the concrete, his chest stopping. 

Clara stood in the red darkness, her breath hitching as she held her scalpel.

Outside, the first heavy thuds began to hit the steel fire doors. The horde had followed them down from the canopy walk. Under their collective pressure, the steel frame began to groan, the iron bolts warping.

A low, guttural hiss rose from the floor.

Owen's body shifted, his limbs straightening with a stiff, mechanical jerk. His head rolled toward Clara, and his eyes opened—empty, clouded with a blue film, reflecting the red light of the nocturnal tanks. He began to crawl toward her, his fingers scratching the concrete floor.

In the dark behind her, the glass of a glider enclosure cracked as the dead pressed against the outer wall.

================================================================================

STORY 28: THE PORT
------------------

Chapter 1: The Maze

The Port of Melbourne container terminal was a towering, multi-colored labyrinth of steel. Shipping containers—red, blue, green, and rusty orange—were stacked five high in long, geometric avenues that stretched across the asphalt docks. Under the low, grey Victorian sky, the port was dead silent, the massive gantry cranes along the pier standing like dormant steel dinosaurs.

"Jack, do you copy?" Minh's voice crackled through the UHF radio, thin and tense. "The gatehouse is overrun. A whole pack of them just came through the main gate from Footscray Road. They're heading into the stack lanes."

High above the asphalt, Jack sat in the glass cab of a forty-ton straddle carrier. The vehicle was a skeletal, four-legged giant, its cabin suspended twenty feet in the air above the container-lifting frame. 

"I copy, Minh," Jack said, his hand resting on the hydraulic joy-sticks. "I'm in Lane Four. How's the pier?"

"I'm at the base of Crane Three," Minh replied. "The evacuation tug is supposed to be at the fuel wharf in half an hour. But the path to the pier is blocked. Someone dropped a refrigerated container in the middle of the main avenue. It's blocked solid."

Jack looked out of his cab window, looking down the narrow, asphalt lane between two five-story walls of steel containers. The perspective was dizzying, a narrow canyon of metal. 

Down at the end of the lane, emerging from the shadow of a stack of Maersk containers, a figure shuffled into the light. It was wearing a high-vis vest and a hardhat, its shoulders slumped, arms dragging. 

Then another walked out, and another. They were dockworkers who had been trapped during the first week of the quarantine, their skin now a waxy grey, their faces silent.

"Minh, they're in my lane," Jack said, his pulse accelerating. "I'm starting the engine."

Chapter 2: The Straddle Carrier

The straddle carrier's six-cylinder Cummins engine roared to life with a puff of black exhaust soot, the cabin vibrating violently as the hydraulic pumps pressurized. Jack pushed the drive lever forward. The massive machine moved, its six huge rubber wheels rolling slowly down the asphalt lane, the chassis straddling the containers below.

Through his floor window, Jack watched the ground pass. The grey, bloated walkers in the lane looked up at the sound of the engine, their milky eyes locking onto the glass cab. They raised their hands, their fingers scratching uselessly against the massive rubber tyres as the carrier rolled over them, the weight crushing bones with a series of wet thuds.

"Jack! The gate pack is heading your way," Minh's voice came through the radio. "They're following the engine noise."

"I'm going to block the access lane," Jack said, his hands moving the joysticks. "There's a forty-foot container sitting in Lane Four. I'm going to drop it across the intersection."

He lined the carrier up over the red container, lowering the lifting spreader beam until the twistlocks aligned with the corner castings of the steel box. He hit the lock switch.

CLACK.

The hydraulic pins locked home. Jack pulled the lift lever, the winch motors groaning as the forty-foot steel container rose ten feet into the air. 

He drove the carrier forward toward the intersection of the main avenue. If he could drop the container crosswise between the stacks, he could seal off the main horde.

But as he reached the turn, he saw that the intersection was already a sea of grey heads.

Chapter 3: The Gantry Crane

From his position at the base of Crane Three, Minh looked up the steel ladder that rose eighty feet to the main machinery platform. The cold wind off Port Phillip Bay was rising, whipping spray over the concrete pier.

"They're on the dock, Jack," Minh said into his mic, his boot stepping onto the first metal rung. "I'm climbing."

He hauled himself up, his muscles burning. Below him, the concrete pier was a grid of yellow lines and shipping lanes. Through the gaps in the steel stairs, he saw walkers shuffling out of the warehouse doors, their wet clothes dripping.

Minh reached the high operator cabin, sliding the glass door open and stepping into the control seat. The view from here was vast. He could see the entire Yarra River mouth and the West Gate Bridge rising in the distance, its spans choked with abandoned, burnt-out cars.

He grabbed the binoculars, training them on the shipping channel.

A small, white pilot boat was riding the swells half a mile out, its diesel engines throwing up white spray as it headed toward the fuel wharf. It was the evacuation boat.

"The boat's coming, Jack!" Minh called out. "But the pier deck is crawling. There must be a hundred of them around the mooring bollards."

He leaned over the console, looking down at the crane's legs. 

From the base of Crane Three, a line of grey figures was starting the climb. The lead zombie, a turned security officer, was already twenty feet up the ladder, his cold fingers gripping the steel rungs with tireless, mechanical rhythm.

Chapter 4: The Gridlock

Jack swung the straddle carrier around the corner, the forty-foot red container swinging slightly beneath the frame. The dead in the intersection looked up, their grey hands reaching for the steel box as it passed over their heads.

"I'm dropping it now," Jack said, aligning the carrier perpendicular to the stack walls.

He hit the lowering valve. The heavy container began to descend, but as it reached three feet from the ground, one of the walkers stepped directly under the corner casting. The weight of the box crushed the zombie, but the uneven impact tilted the container.

SNAP.

One of the carrier's main lifting cables snapped under the sudden, unbalanced tension. The red container slipped, falling at an angle and wedging itself tightly between the five-story steel walls of the stacks. 

The carrier jolted violently, the cabin glass cracking as the frame twisted. 

Jack tried to reverse, but the snapped steel cable had wrapped around the rear wheel assembly, jamming the gears. The carrier was stuck, wedged in the narrow lane directly behind the fallen container.

"Minh! The cable snapped!" Jack yelled, his voice rising in panic. "The carrier is jammed. I'm stuck in the lane."

Below him, the dead in the intersection began to crawl over the wedged red container. Their grey, wet faces appeared over the top edge of the steel box, their eyes locking onto the glass cab suspended above the lane.

Chapter 5: The Asphalt Walkers

The asphalt below the carrier was a shifting carpet of grey bodies. The walkers swarmed the six massive rubber tyres, their hands scratching against the steel wheel hubs with a dry, metallic rasp. 

Several of them began to climb the structural legs of the machine, using the hydraulic hoses and steel braces as handholds.

"They're climbing, Minh!" Jack said, his voice trembling as he looked through the floor window. A face was pressed against the glass from below, a zombie clinging to the hydraulic oil tank. "The cabin doors aren't sealed. Once they reach the platform, I'm dead."

From the operator seat of Crane Three, Minh looked down at the lane where Jack’s carrier was wedged. The crane's massive gantry trolley sat on the high rails directly above the container stacks.

"I can reach you with the crane spreader," Minh said, his hands moving the master controls of the ship-to-shore gantry. "If I trolley back, I can drop the spreader over the container stack next to you. You can climb onto the container top, and I'll lift you out."

"The wind is rising, Minh," Jack warned. "The crane's swaying."

"I have no choice," Minh muttered, pushing the trolley joystick.

High above, the massive steel trolley began to roll along the crane boom, its steel wheels squealing in the wind. The crane's heavy, rectangular spreader beam swung wildly as it moved toward the narrow stack lane.

Chapter 6: The Ladder Run

Minh aligned the crane's spreader beam over the stack of blue containers immediately adjacent to Jack’s trapped straddle carrier. He hit the winch lever. The heavy steel frame descended, the four steel cables humming under the tension.

CLACK.

The spreader landed on the top container.

"Jack! It's down!" Minh called out. "Climb out of the cab onto the blue container. Step onto the spreader frame. I'll trolley you back to the pier deck."

Jack unlocked the cab hatch. A blast of cold rain hit him, nearly throwing him off the small steel platform. He climbed out onto the frame of the carrier, his boots slipping on the wet steel. Below him, the fingers of the climbing dead reached through the platform grating.

He took a breath and leaped across the four-foot gap, landing heavily on the top of the blue shipping container. He scrambled onto the crane's spreader beam, his fingers wrapping around the thick steel cables.

"Got him!" Minh said, his hands moving to lift the winch.

But a sudden, heavy slam against the cabin door of the crane made Minh spin around.

Through the rain-streaked window of the operator cabin, he saw the face of the turned security officer. The zombie had climbed the eighty-foot ladder, its fingers raw and bleeding from the steel rungs. Behind it, two more walkers were standing on the narrow catwalk platform outside the cab.

The door latch began to rattle.

Chapter 7: The Spreader Beam

Minh threw his shoulder against the glass door of the crane cab, holding it shut as the turned security officer slammed his weight against the latch. The glass creaked under the impact.

With his free hand, Minh reached for the crane's joystick, pulling it back.

"Hold on, Jack!" Minh screamed, his voice strained.

High above the stacks, the trolley began to roll toward the pier, carrying the spreader beam and the clinging Jack. But the wind off the bay was now a gale-force howl, catching the massive container stacks and the crane boom.

The spreader beam began to swing like a pendulum, forty feet in the air. 

Clank.

A sudden shudder ran through the crane's cables. The trolley stopped, the warning screen on Minh's console flashing red.

TROLLEY CABLE BIND. LIMIT OVERRIDE.

The high wind had twisted the lifting cables, jamming the trolley wheels on the gantry rail. Jack was suspended forty feet above the concrete dock, clinging to the swinging spreader frame, unable to move forward or back.

Below him, the walkers on the pier gathered under the swinging beam, their faces turned upward, waiting for him to fall.

Chapter 8: The Diesel Leak

The glass of the crane cab door shattered with a sharp crash. 

The turned officer's arm thrust through the opening, the fingers scratching at Minh’s neck. Minh dodged, his hand hitting the safety override switch on the side panel. 

He rammed the trolley lever forward, bypassing the limit sensors.

The electric motors screamed, forcing the jammed trolley forward. With a metallic screech that echoed across the harbor, the cable freed itself. The trolley surged forward, carrying the swinging spreader beam toward the concrete pier.

But the sudden, violent movement was too much for the old crane. 

Under the chassis of the crane cabin, a high-pressure hydraulic line burst. A stream of hot, pressurized oil sprayed across the steel catwalk and down onto the concrete dock below. The oil ignited against the hot resistor grids of the crane motor, sending a sheet of orange flame down the side of the steel tower.

The fire cut off the stairs, trapping Minh in the burning cabin. 

"Minh! The crane's on fire!" Jack yelled, the spreader beam finally lowering him toward the edge of the fuel wharf.

Through the broken window, Minh wrestled with the officer, the zombie’s teeth scraping against his leather sleeve.

Chapter 9: The Slip

In the burning cab, the officer's teeth broke through Minh’s sleeve, sinking deep into the muscle of his forearm. 

Minh gasped, the pain a hot needle. With his free hand, he grabbed a heavy metal fire extinguisher and smashed it into the officer's skull. The zombie collapsed, but the fire was already filling the cabin with thick, black plastic smoke.

Minh slumped over the console, his fingers locking the lower lever in place before he lost consciousness.

On the wharf below, the spreader beam dropped the last ten feet, slamming hard onto the concrete. Jack let go of the cables, falling onto the wet pier.

"Minh! Minh, answer me!" Jack yelled, his radio dead.

He scrambled to his feet, but the concrete deck was slick with rain and the black hydraulic oil spraying from the burning crane. Jack's boot slipped. He fell heavily, his left arm folding under his body. 

SNAP.

The bone in his forearm broke, a sharp white pain making him scream. 

As he tried to drag himself up with his one good hand, a figure emerged from the shadow of the fuel tanks. It was a turned deckhand, his skin pale and bloated from the river. Before Jack could move, the zombie lunged, its teeth sinking deep into his right wrist.

Chapter 10: The Open Bay

Jack lay on the cold, oil-slicked concrete of the wharf, his broken arm clutched to his chest. The bite on his wrist burned, a fire that was rapidly climbing his arm toward his heart.

Through the rain and the thick black smoke of the burning crane, a low horn sounded from the river.

The white pilot boat emerged from the fog, its searchlight sweeping the pier. It pulled within fifty meters of the fuel wharf, the crew on the deck looking at the flames and the swarmed concrete docks. 

Jack raised his good hand, waving it weakly, but his voice was swallowed by the roar of the fire.

The pilot boat's captain took one look at the burning crane, the swarmed paths, and the shambling figures surrounding the wharf. He turned the wheel. The boat's engines roared, the vessel pivoting and accelerating away into the grey waters of the bay, leaving a wide, white wake.

"No," Jack whispered, his hand falling back into the oily water. "Wait..."

There was no rescue. The boat was gone, disappearing into the mist of Port Phillip Bay.

Above him, the crane cabin exploded in a ball of orange flame, the metal structure groaning as it began to warp. 

Jack fell back against the concrete, his breathing slowing. The cold of the rain faded, replaced by the heavy, burning fever. He closed his eyes. 

When they opened again, the pain in his arm was gone. The white lights of the burning crane reflected in his empty, grey eyes, and he began to drag his body toward the water's edge.

================================================================================

STORY 29: THE PEARLER
---------------------

Chapter 1: Roebuck Bay

The heat of the Kimberley night hung heavy over Roebuck Bay, a thick, salty humidity that made their skin feel greasy. Five miles to the east, the town of Broome was a jagged silhouette against a red, pulsing glow. The historic pearling port was burning, the smoke smudging the Southern Cross in the night sky.

"The wind's coming from the land, Pete," Kaelen said, leaning over the wooden gunwale of the Marilyn. She was a thirty-five-foot pearling lugger, her wooden hull creaking as the tide began to shift. "I can smell the ash. And the diesel from the burning jetty."

Pete, his skin tanned and wrinkled from decades of diving, checked the pressure gauges on the low-pressure compressor mounted on the deck. "The tide is a king tide tonight, Kaelen. A ten-meter drop and rise. The currents in the bay are going to be wild. We're anchored in the deep channel, but if the wind shifts, the mudflats will be exposed."

"We should have run for the open ocean," Kaelen muttered, her eyes fixed on the burning shoreline.

"In a wooden boat with a failing gearbox?" Pete shook his head. "We'd have ended up wrecked on the Dampier Peninsula. The bay is deep enough to keep the land-crawlers away. They can't swim."

He walked to the deck hatch that led to the small cabin below. 

From the dark interior, the sound of low, wet coughing echoed. It was Frank, their lead diver, who had been injured during their escape from the Broome wharf two days ago. He had a deep gash on his shoulder, which Pete had stitched and wrapped, but the fever had started that morning.

Suddenly, a heavy thud struck the wooden hull of the lugger near the bow.

Chapter 2: The Anchor Chain

Kaelen walked to the bow, her flashlight beam cutting through the humid darkness. She shone the light down the thick, rusted iron anchor chain that ran into the black water.

The tide was running fast, a massive volume of water sweeping out of the bay toward the Indian Ocean. Floating in the current, caught against the chain, were three bloated, grey forms. They were dressed in tattered tourist clothes and boardshorts.

But they weren't dead—at least, not in the normal sense.

One of them, a woman whose skin had been bleached white and wrinkled by the sea, raised her head. Her lips were peeled back from her teeth, her nose cartilage eaten away by crabs. Her milky, pupil-less eyes locked onto the flashlight beam. She reached out with a grey, waterlogged hand and grabbed the chain, her fingers slipping on the rust but finding purchase.

Slowly, with a heavy, mechanical pull, she began to haul herself up the links.

"Pete!" Kaelen gasped, backing away. "They're on the chain! They're climbing!"

Pete ran to the bow, a heavy wooden boat hook in his hand. He looked over the gunwale. The woman was already halfway to the deck, her wet clothes dripping onto the timber hull. Behind her, the other two drowned walkers were starting their climb, their grey limbs moving against the rush of the current.

Pete lunged, the metal spike of the boat hook striking the woman's forehead. 

The skull was soft, waterlogged, and the spike went clean through. She let go of the chain, falling back into the dark water with a heavy splash, but the current immediately swept the next climber into her place.

Chapter 3: The Deck Hatch

"Get the deck hatches locked!" Pete yelled, shoving the second climber off the anchor chain with the boat hook. The waterlogged zombie fell into the current, but a third one was already grasping the lower links.

Kaelen ran along the narrow deck, her boots slipping on the wet teak. She slammed the forward forecastle hatch shut, sliding the iron bolts into place. 

From the stern, a splashing sound began.

The tide was lifting the lugger, the stern sitting low in the water. Several drowned walkers, swept along by the current, had caught the rudder assembly and the low dive platform at the transom. They were crawling onto the deck, their wet fingers clutching the wooden cleats.

"They're coming over the stern!" Kaelen screamed, running to the main cabin hatch. She slammed the heavy timber doors shut and locked the padlock.

"Where's Frank?" Pete called out, backing away from the bow as a bloated walker crawled over the rail, its mouth snapping.

"He's in the chamber," Kaelen said, pointing to the rusted, steel decompression chamber bolted to the port side deck. It was a relic from the old pearling days, a two-meter-long steel cylinder with a heavy, circular hatch and a small acrylic viewport, used for treating the bends.

Frank had been sealed inside the chamber that morning to keep him quiet and safe during the fever, the air supply connected to the deck compressor.

Through the small glass viewport, the red emergency light inside the chamber showed Frank's face, his skin covered in sweat, his eyes closed.

Chapter 4: The Decompression Chamber

Kaelen stepped to the decompression chamber, shining her light through the circular acrylic viewport. The air inside the steel cylinder was thick, the glass fogged with Frank's heavy, hot breath.

"Frank?" she called out, tapping the steel wall with her knuckles.

Inside, Frank’s body twitched, his head rolling back. The sheet covering his shoulders had slipped. 

In the beam of the flashlight, the bandage on his left shoulder was soaked with dark, foul-smelling fluid. As he shifted, the edge of the wrap peeled away, revealing the skin beneath. It wasn't a clean metal tear. 

It was a curved, jagged line of teeth marks, the flesh around it black, swollen, and putrid.

"Pete," Kaelen said, her voice dropping to a whisper. "He didn't hit a metal bracket on the wharf. He was bitten."

Pete joined her at the glass, his face falling in the light. "The fever... it's not a wound infection. He's turning."

Inside the chamber, Frank’s eyes flew open. They were bloodshot, the pupils dilated, but as they watched, the color faded, replaced by a cold, milky white film. He began to convulse, his limbs thrashing against the steel walls of the cylinder, his fingernails clawing at the view port.

Scratch. Scratch.

The sound of his nails on the acrylic was a terrible, sharp squeal. He opened his mouth, his jaw locking, slamming his head against the viewport.

Chapter 5: The King Tide

The Marilyn lurched violently as the full force of the king tide hit the bay. The current was a rushing river of black water, flowing at over eight knots. The thick rope anchor line was pulled taut as a steel cable, groaning under the strain.

"The tide's lifting us, but the current is dragging more of those things from the harbor," Pete said, pointing to the dark water.

Through the ripples, they saw pale shapes passing beneath the boat—drowned bodies swept along by the current. Some of them caught the keel, their hands clawing at the wooden planks, their weight adding to the drag on the anchor.

Inside the steel decompression chamber, the clawing grew louder. 

Frank was no longer thrashing; he was pressing his face against the viewport, his mouth opening in a silent snarl as he watched Kaelen. Under the rules of the dead, he was trapped in the steel tomb, but his weight was shifting, making the lugger list to port.

"We have to cut the anchor, Pete!" Kaelen yelled, grabbing the fire axe from the cabin wall. "If we don't, the line's going to snap, or the bow will be pulled under!"

"If we cut the anchor, we're drifting," Pete said, looking at the dark mudflats exposed in the distance. "And the engine's gearbox is shot. We won't have control."

Before Kaelen could swing the axe, a sudden, heavy vibration shook the deck. 

A loud, metallic clanking sounded from under the hull. The engine shaft had spun, the propeller catching something in the water. The diesel engine sputtered and died with a loud cough.

Chapter 6: The Chamber Turn

Inside the steel cylinder, the pressure gauge began to climb. The diesel compressor on deck, still running on its battery before the main engine died, was pumping high-pressure air into the chamber. 

"The relief valve is shut!" Kaelen yelled, pointing to the brass gauge. "The pressure's at six atmospheres! Frank's going to get crushed, or the glass will blow!"

"He's already dead, Kaelen!" Pete said, struggling to push a zombie back over the starboard rail with the boat hook. "Just shut off the compressor!"

Kaelen ran to the compressor controls near the engine hatch. 

But as she reached for the kill switch, a waterlogged zombie—a turned tourist in a floral shirt—rose from the bilge well. It had crawled through the open steering port at the stern, its body covered in black mud from the bay floor. 

It lunged, its grey hands catching Kaelen's ankle.

Kaelen fell, her chin hitting the deck timber, the flashlight flying out of her hand. She scrambled back, kicking at the zombie's face. The soft, waterlogged flesh of its nose tore under her boot, but it didn't let go of her leg.

"Pete!" she screamed, her hand reaching for the compressor switch, but it was just out of reach.

Inside the chamber, the reanimated Frank pressed his chest against the glass, his mouth opening in a silent snarl as the air pressure hissed, his eardrums ruptured by the pressure, but his dead body completely unaffected by the bends.

Chapter 7: The Propeller Net

Pete ran down the deck, the boat hook swinging. He drove the metal spike into the skull of the tourist zombie grabbing Kaelen. The walker went limp, and Pete helped Kaelen up, his hand instantly flipping the compressor kill switch.

The high-pressure hiss faded, but the bay was still a chaos of rushing water.

"The propeller is jammed," Pete said, checking the steering quadrant. "The shaft is bound tight. A body or a net must have wrapped around the blades. If we don't clear it, we can't start the engine to run when the anchor snaps."

"I'll go down," Kaelen said, reaching for the dive mask.

"No. You don't know the hull layout in the dark," Pete said, grabbing his old diving regulator and a heavy steel knife from the rack. "Keep the deck clear. I'll cut us free."

He slipped the regulator mouth-piece between his teeth, adjusted his mask, and sat on the gunwale.

The water of Roebuck Bay was pitch black, a warm, thick soup that smelled of salt and decaying mangroves. Pete took a breath of compressed air, his flashlight beam cutting a weak, green cone into the dark water, and rolled backward into the bay.

The current hit him instantly, a powerful force that tried to rip him away from the hull. He gripped the wooden keel, hand over hand, pulling himself toward the stern.

Chapter 8: The Dive

Pete reached the stern, the propeller shaft vibrating in the heavy current. He shone his light on the brass blades. 

A bloated, waterlogged body was wrapped around the shaft, its limbs tangled in a nylon fishing net that had caught the propeller. The skin of the walker was grey, peeling off in strips where the current rubbed it against the metal.

Pete pulled his dive knife from his leg sheath, his breathing loud and metallic in the regulator.

He began cutting the thick nylon ropes, his fingers numb from the pressure. The current swung the body back and forth, the cold limbs brushing against his face. It was a turned deckhand, his eyes gone, his mouth open to reveal white teeth.

As the last rope snapped, the body suddenly shifted.

It wasn't just the current. The drowned walker’s head snapped toward Pete, its grey arms reaching through the tangled net. Before Pete could back away, the zombie's waterlogged fingers wrapped around his double-hose regulator, pulling the rubber mouthpiece out of his mouth.

A stream of silver bubbles erupted into the dark.

Pete gasped, swallowing a mouthful of salty, warm bay water. He thrashed, his flashlight falling into the dark depths of the channel. He stabbed blindly with his knife, the blade sinking into the zombie's chest, but the dead hands held his head, keeping him pinned against the wooden keel.

Chapter 9: The Deep Grab

On the deck, Kaelen watched the safety line. It was jerking violently, the bubbles rising in a chaotic froth from beneath the stern.

"Pete!" she screamed, hauling the rope with both hands.

The current was heavy, and it felt like she was pulling a lead weight. She wrapped the rope around a brass winch and cranked it, her muscles screaming. With a final, desperate heave, she pulled Pete’s head out of the black water.

He was limp, his mask gone, his mouth empty of the regulator.

Kaelen dragged his heavy body onto the deck, the water logging his heavy canvas dive trousers. She fell to her knees beside him, checking his neck. There was no pulse. His face was blue, his lungs filled with the salty water of the bay.

"No, no, no," she sobbed, pressing her hands to his chest, starting rhythmic CPR compressions. "Don't do this, Pete. Wake up."

Press. Press. Press.

She leaned down, pinching his nose and blowing air into his cold mouth. A mix of water and foam bubbled out of his lips. She pumped his chest again, her tears mixing with the salt water on his face.

Under the universal rules, the cause of death did not matter. Once the oxygen left the brain, the transition was instant.

Pete’s chest suddenly expanded with a sharp, gasping breath. But his hand rose, not to touch his face, but to grab Kaelen’s hair.

Chapter 10: The Turning Tide

Pete's eyes opened. They were no longer the warm, blue eyes of the veteran diver who had saved her life; they were dull, clouded with a cold, milky film. 

He lunged, his teeth snapping shut inches from Kaelen’s nose.

Kaelen screamed, pulling back, but her hair was caught in his grip. She grabbed the dive knife from the deck and sliced through her own hair, leaving a clump in his cold fingers as she scrambled backward toward the port rail.

At that exact moment, a sharp ping echoed from the bow.

The iron anchor chain, unable to bear the combined weight of the current and the walkers clinging below, snapped clean. The tension released, throwing the bow of the Marilyn sideways. 

Without an anchor, the lugger was swept away by the king tide, drifting rapidly down the dark channel.

Kaelen looked over the rail. The tide was sweeping them toward the shallow mudflats of the bay. The receding water had exposed the wide, grey expanse of mud, where dozens of figures were already standing, their legs sunk to the knees, waiting for the boat to drift ashore.

Behind her, the steel decompression chamber began to shake.

Frank was pounding the viewport with his forehead, a web of fine cracks appearing in the acrylic glass. Pete scrambled up from the deck, his wet boots squeaking on the wood, his hands outstretched as he shuffled toward her.

Kaelen stood backed against the wooden gunwale, the knife in her hand, as the lugger drifted silently into the mudflats of the dead.

================================================================================

STORY 30: THE OUTBACK MAIL
--------------------------

Chapter 1: The Track

The Birdsville Track was a ribbon of red dirt and gibber stones that cut through the empty heart of the Sturt Stony Desert. The sun was a white, blinding eye in a cloudless sky, baking the stony plains to a shimmering fifty degrees. The air was dry as ash, tasting of dust and heat.

Inside the air-conditioned cab of the modified Isuzu 4x4 mail truck, Clay kept his eyes locked on the faint wheel ruts ahead. His hands were gloved against the hot vinyl of the steering wheel, his face lined with dust-caked sweat.

"We should cross the South Australian border by sundown, Daisy," Clay said, his voice a gravelly rasp. "If the track stays clear. The gibbers are hard on the tyres."

Daisy, sitting in the passenger seat with a plastic crate of medical vials clutched in her lap, nodded. She was twenty-eight, her shirt stained with red dust. "The clinic in Marree needs these insulin packs, Clay. They've been out of supplies for three weeks. The heat's going to spoil them if the truck's cooling fails."

"The Isuzu doesn't fail," Clay said, tapping the steering wheel. "She's built for the track. Two spare tyres, a bullbar, and a locked rear diff. We've done this run for ten years."

He checked the side mirror. The red dust plume behind the truck stretched for a kilometer, a thick cloud that hung in the still air. 

Suddenly, Clay tapped the brakes, the truck slowing as the tires crunched on the loose stones. 

A mile ahead, where the track wound between two low, red sand dunes, the path was blocked. A massive sand drift had blown across the claypan, a wave of loose, powdery red sand three feet deep.

Chapter 2: The Sand Drift

Clay shifted the Isuzu into low-range four-wheel drive, engaging the differential locks. The transmission clanked, a heavy, solid sound.

"Hold on," Clay said, pressing the accelerator.

The truck hit the red sand drift at thirty kilometers an hour. The heavy bullbar plowed through the crest, throwing a wave of fine, red dust over the windshield. For a second, the tyres found traction, the Isuzu clawing its way up the slope. 

Then the momentum died. The wheels began to spin, their deep treads digging into the loose, powdery sand. The chassis sank, the differential housing landing hard on the crest of the drift with a dull whump.

The wheels spun freely, throwing up a spray of hot red sand. The truck was bogged.

"Turn it off, Clay!" Daisy said, her hand on the dash. "You're just burying us."

Clay cut the engine, the sudden silence of the desert heavy and baking. The air conditioner clicked off, and the heat in the cab began to rise instantly. 

"We'll have to use the shovel," Clay said, reaching behind the seat for his gloves. "We need to clear the diff and lower the tyre pressure to ten PSI."

He opened the driver's door, stepping out onto the hot sand. The heat was a physical force, a dry wave that made his eyes water. 

He walked to the rear of the truck to get the shovel from the tray. As he lifted it, he glanced back down the track.

Through the shimmering heat haze where the red track met the horizon, three shapes were moving. They were walking slowly, their silhouettes warped and dancing in the mirage.

Chapter 3: The Mirage Dead

Daisy climbed out of the passenger side, her hand shielding her eyes from the white glare. She raised her binoculars, focusing on the distant shapes.

Through the heat ripples, the figures became clear. 

They were desiccated, dried to leather by the outback sun. One was wearing a tattered Akubra hat, the chin strap caught around its jawbone, its clothes stiffened with dried mud and red dust. Their eye sockets were empty, dark hollows. They moved with a slow, mechanical crawl, their boots crunching on the gibbers.

"They're walkers, Clay," Daisy said, her voice dropping. "Three of them. They're coming down the track."

"They're a mile off," Clay said, his shovel biting into the hot red sand beneath the rear axle. "They'll take twenty minutes to reach us. I just need to clear the diff housing."

He threw a shovelful of sand over his shoulder, his shirt already soaked with sweat. The heat off the engine block was adding to the desert oven.

"There's more," Daisy said, her binoculars sweeping the low sand dunes to the west.

Through the red spinifex grass, more shapes were emerging. They were walkers from some abandoned station homestead or camp, their bodies dried and mummified, walking in a loose, converging line toward the smell of the truck’s cooling engine.

"Clay," Daisy said, her voice shaking. "We don't have twenty minutes."

Chapter 4: The Shovel

Clay gritted his teeth, his shovel flying. The red sand was hot enough to bake bread, the dust filling his throat and nose.

"Deflate the tyres, Daisy!" he roared. "Use the valve tool from the glove box!"

Daisy ran to the front wheel, her hands shaking as she unscrewed the valve cap. 

As she pressed the metal pin, releasing a loud hiss of air, a sudden rustle sounded from the spinifex mound directly above her.

A walker—a turned station hand in a shredded flannel shirt—tumbled down the side of the dune. It fell onto the sand, its joints crackling, but scrambled up on all fours, its mouth open to reveal a dry, blackened tongue. It lunged for Daisy’s arm.

"Daisy! Watch out!" Clay yelled.

He dropped the shovel's blade onto the sand and swung the heavy iron tool like a club. The flat of the blade struck the walker's skull with a dull whack. 

The bone cracked, a shower of dried skin and red dust breaking off, but the zombie didn't stop. It grabbed the wooden handle of the shovel, its grip like an iron clamp. Clay twisted the handle, his boots slipping in the sand, and drove the sharp metal tip of the blade directly through the zombie's eye socket.

The walker went limp, falling onto the sand.

Clay stood panting, his hand shaking on the handle. But as he looked up, the hiss of the deflating tyre was joined by a new sound. 

From the dunes on both sides of the track, dozens of dried, red-dusted dead were sliding down the sandy slopes, drawn by the struggle and the noise.

Chapter 5: The Swarm

"In the cab! Now!" Clay yelled, grabbing Daisy's arm and dragging her away from the half-deflated tyre.

They scrambled up the metal side-steps of the Isuzu. Clay threw the door open, pushing Daisy inside before climbing in after her. He slammed the heavy steel door shut, the latch sliding home with a solid, reassuring click.

Almost immediately, the first dry hands began to hit the passenger side window.

Tap. Tap. Scrape.

The walkers were surrounding the truck. There were dozens of them now, their mummified, red-dusted bodies pressing against the steel doors and the bullbar. Their skin was dry as leather, eyes gone, but their weight was a physical force that made the heavy Isuzu sway on its suspension.

"Clay, start the engine," Daisy gasped, clutching the insulin crate to her chest. "Try to reverse. Maybe the diff lock will catch."

Clay turned the key. The starter motor engaged, but the engine only coughed and died.

He tried again, the starter clicking rapidly. The dashboard screen flickered and went dark.

LOW BATTERY. ALTERNATOR FAILURE.

The heat of the desert day, combined with the strain of the bogged wheels, had cooked the alternator windings. Without the engine running, the air conditioning vents went silent. 

The cab began to warm up, a tight, steel box under the blazing outback sun.

Chapter 6: The Cab Shelter

The heat in the cab was a heavy, suffocating weight. Within ten minutes, the temperature rose past forty degrees, the metal dashboard hot enough to burn their skin. Sweat ran in streams down Clay's face, stinging his eyes.

Outside, the walkers crowded the windows. 

A turned tourist, her sun-bleached hair tattered, pressed her face against the passenger side glass. Her nose was flattened against the window, her mouth opening and closing in a silent, rhythmic snarl. Her dried fingers scratched the glass, leaving dusty, red streaks that blocked the light.

"We have two litres of water in the flask, Daisy," Clay said, his voice dry. He reached for the insulated thermos between the seats. "We have to ration it. A sip every hour."

"And the insulin?" Daisy looked down at the plastic crate. Without the truck's refrigerator running, the cold packs were already starting to sweat. "It won't last the night in this heat. It'll spoil."

"The insulin doesn't matter if we don't get out of this cab," Clay said, unscrewing the lid of the thermos. He handed it to her. "Drink. Just a sip."

Daisy took the flask, her hands shaking. She took a tiny mouthful of the tepid water, holding it in her mouth before swallowing.

Behind them, a heavy blow struck the rear window of the cab.

A walker had climbed onto the tray of the truck, its boots clanging on the metal floor. It was looking through the small rear glass, its milky eyes fixed on the back of Clay's head.

Chapter 7: The Heat Stroke

The sun began to sink, a blood-red ball that hovered over the stony desert, but the twilight brought no relief. The metal body of the Isuzu had absorbed the baking heat of the day, acting like a brick oven. 

Inside the closed cab, the air was stagnant, smelling of hot plastic, sweat, and the faint, sweet scent of rotting flesh from the zombies outside.

Daisy’s skin was flushed, hot and bone-dry to the touch. Her breathing was fast, shallow gasps.

"Daisy? Stay with me, mate," Clay said, his own head spinning, his vision blurred by dehydration. He unscrewed the thermos, but when he tipped it, only a few warm drops trickled onto his fingers. 

The flask was empty.

"Clay..." Daisy whispered, her eyes unfocused, her head rolling against the headrest. "It's... it's cold. Why is it so cold?"

"It's just the wind, Daisy," Clay lied, his voice a dry whisper. He took his damp shirt off and tried to fan her, but the air he moved was hot as a hair dryer.

Outside, the scratching on the windows was a continuous, rhythmic hiss. The red dust on the glass was thick now, blocking the starlight, leaving the cab in a red, claustrophobic gloom.

Daisy’s body suddenly went rigid. Her eyes rolled back, and she began to convulse, the plastic crate of insulin slipping from her lap and crashing into the footwell.

Chapter 8: The Windows

The convulsions ceased, and Daisy’s body went completely limp against the seat. Her head slumped forward, her chin resting on her chest.

"Daisy?" Clay reached out, his trembling fingers pressing against her neck.

The skin was hot, dry, and silent. There was no pulse. Her chest did not rise. The heat stroke had boiled her brain, her organs failing in the fifty-degree steel tomb.

"No," Clay whispered, his forehead resting against the hot steering wheel. He felt a tear rise, but his body was too dehydrated to shed it. "No..."

Outside, the scratching on the windows did not stop.

A mummified hand with long, dried fingernails scraped against the driver's side glass, leaving a series of parallel lines in the red dust. Clay looked up, his face inches from the glass. The zombie on the other side was staring in, its empty sockets seeming to mock him.

Clay looked down at the floorboards where the insulin vials had shattered, the clear liquid mixing with the red dust on the rubber mat.

He could feel his own head spinning, the fever of dehydration rising in his body. His throat was swollen shut, his tongue dry as sandpaper. He knew he didn't have much time left.

Suddenly, a soft rustle sounded from the passenger seat.

Chapter 9: The Dehydration

Daisy’s head snapped up. 

In the dim red light of the cab, her eyes opened—milky, white, and completely devoid of life. She lunged across the console, her teeth clicking together as she snapped at Clay’s face.

Clay tried to push her back, but his dehydrated muscles were weak, his limbs heavy. His arm felt like lead as he pressed his hand against her chest.

"Daisy... stop," Clay croaked, but the words were just a dry rasp.

Daisy’s fingers, cold and stiff, grabbed his collar. She pulled herself forward, her teeth sinking deep into the side of his neck. 

Clay gasped, a sharp, white-hot pain slicing through his throat. The blood that trickled down his neck was thick, dark, and warm. He didn't have the strength to fight her off. He slumped back against the driver's door, his hand falling away from her chest.

Daisy continued to tear at his neck, her growls low and wet in the silent cab.

Outside, the walkers on the sand drift heard the movement. They began to hammer on the steel panels of the doors with renewed frenzy, the Isuzu rocking on its bogged suspension.

Clay’s vision began to fade to black, the red dust on the windshield the last thing he saw.

Chapter 10: The Red Dust

The wind began to rise as the red sun finally slipped below the horizon, a howling desert wind that whipped the loose sand off the dunes. The red dust swirled in thick, choking sheets, blotting out the stars and burying the ruts of the Birdsville Track.

Inside the bogged Isuzu cab, the struggle was over. 

Clay’s body sat still in the driver's seat, his chin resting on his chest, the wound on his neck no longer bleeding. Beside him, Daisy sat quiet, her face smeared with dried blood and red dust.

Outside, the wind howled, a deafening roar that carried the sand. 

The loose sand piled against the tyres, rising slowly to cover the wheels, then the chassis, and finally the hood of the truck. The walkers surrounding the vehicle were slowly swept away by the wind or buried in the drifts, their dried bodies becoming one with the landscape.

Inside the cab, Clay’s fingers twitched.

His head rolled back against the headrest, and his eyes opened—dull, grey, and clouded with the blue film of the dead. He turned his head slowly toward Daisy. She turned her head toward him, her empty eyes reflecting the faint green glow of the dying dashboard clock.

The two reanimated souls sat side-by-side in the locked steel cab, their mouths opening in silent, synchronous snarls.

Outside, the red sand rose, covering the windows, then the roof, until the Isuzu was completely swallowed, a red mound in the middle of the empty, stony desert. The track was gone. The mail would never be delivered.
